# Front Cover of the Volume

The manuscript is encased in a <term>half-leather binding<note>A binding style where the spine and corners are covered in leather for durability, while the sides are covered in paper or cloth.</note></term>. The spine and corners are made of a dark brown textured leather, which is accented by delicate <term>gold tooling<note>The process of pressing decorative designs into leather using heated metal tools and gold leaf.</note></term> in the form of thin lines.

The central panels of the cover are decorated with <term>marbled paper<note>Paper featuring a swirled, stone-like pattern created by floating pigments on a liquid surface.</note></term> in a <term>nonpareil pattern<note>A classic, intricate marbling style characterized by very fine, combed horizontal waves.</note></term>. This decorative paper features a complex arrangement of colors, including:
* Deep red
* Purple
* Cream
* Light blue
* Yellow

On the far left, the spine of the book is visible. It features <term>raised bands<note>The horizontal ridges on a book's spine created by the cords used to sew the pages together.</note></term> and traces of gold-stamped lettering that are currently <unclear>illegible</unclear> due to wear.

<summary>This page describes the physical appearance of the manuscript's cover, noting its professional half-leather binding and fine nonpareil marbled paper. The high-quality materials suggest the volume was intended for a significant library or collection.</summary>

<keywords>bookbinding, marbled paper, nonpareil pattern, half-leather binding, gold tooling, spine, conservation</keywords>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This is the front cover of the manuscript volume. As the exterior of the book, it serves as the physical introduction to the work, though it contains no legible text.</meta>

# Front Cover

->**Physical Description of the Volume**<-

The manuscript is encased in a **half-leather binding**. The spine and the outer corners of the boards are protected by brown leather, which is decorated with fine **gold-tooled** lines where the leather meets the decorative paper.

The boards themselves are covered in a vibrant **marbled paper** featuring a **peacock pattern** (also known as a "comb" pattern). This design consists of flowing, horizontal wavy bands in shades of maroon, cream, and yellow, with occasional accents of blue.

The leather spine on the left edge shows evidence of raised bands—structural ridges used in traditional bookbinding—and traces of gold-tooled text that is now <unclear>illegible</unclear> due to wear.

<note>Half-leather binding was a popular style that balanced durability and cost by using expensive leather only on the spine and corners, which receive the most wear. The peacock marbled pattern was a hallmark of 18th and 19th-century book production.</note>

<term>half-leather (binding): a bookbinding technique where the spine and corners are covered in leather, while the rest of the cover is paper or cloth.</term>

<term>gold tooling: the process of pressing decorative designs or lettering into leather using heated metal tools and gold leaf.</term>

<summary>The front cover of the manuscript, showcasing a traditional half-leather binding with a decorative peacock-patterned marbled paper and gold-tooled accents.</summary>

<keywords>binding, marbled paper, peacock pattern, half-leather, gold tooling, manuscript cover</keywords>

The illustration is an elaborate <term>frontispiece<note>An illustration facing the title page of a book, often summarizing its themes symbolically.</note></term> or certificate related to <term>Freemasonry<note>A fraternal organization that uses the tools and customs of stonemasonry as metaphors for moral and intellectual development.</note></term>. At the top of a stone arch, a square and compass emblem surrounds the letter "G," flanked by two flaming torches. Below, a double-headed eagle wearing a crown holds a sword and supports a <term>patriarchal cross<note>A cross with two horizontal bars; in this context, it is a symbol used within high-degree Masonry.</note></term>. 

The scene is framed by two columns: one supports a <term>terrestrial globe<note>A map of the Earth.</note></term> and the other a <term>celestial globe<note>A map of the stars and heavens.</note></term>. The central chamber features a black-and-white checkered floor. In the middle, a crucifix stands on a three-tiered altar, positioned beneath a triangle containing the "All-Seeing Eye." To the left is a wooden ladder, and to the right stands a skeleton—a <term>memento mori<note>Latin for "remember you must die," a symbolic reminder of human mortality.</note></term>. Large three-branched candelabras sit on ornate pedestals. At the base of the altar, a radiating triangle contains the number "33," surrounded by swords and a crown. The entire scene rests upon a large stone plinth.

<meta>This page serves as the formal title page for Georgius Rostius’s work. It follows page 275, which was a blank flyleaf serving as a structural buffer within the manuscript.</meta>

# Theological Prognostication
<note>original: "Prognosticon Theologicon"</note>
### Or
## Theological Prophecy/
# Concerning the Last Day/

### In which various beautiful, lovely, and pleasant questions concerning the final dealings of this world
are discussed; what is to be held regarding the **calculation** <note>original: "Computation"</note> of the **Rosicrucians** <note>A secretive cultural and spiritual movement that emerged in early 17th-century Germany, often associated with alchemy and mysticism.</note> and **M. Paul Nagel's Astrological-Cabalistic Prognostication**, especially concerning the **Third Age** <note>original: "Tertio seculo," a reference to a predicted golden age or a millennial kingdom of Christ on earth.</note>, that **Christ shall return in the year 1623** and begin His Kingdom on Earth; what wonders and signs, what war and rumors of war shall precede the Last Day; concerning the resurrection of the dead and how it shall occur; also what state the souls of the deceased are in; and what process shall be held at the Last Judgment, in what place and in what manner it shall happen, and what shall follow thereafter.

### For the special information,
->instruction, and warning of all pious Christians, diligently<-
->described and published from the Word of God<-
->By<-
# GEORGIUS ROSTIUS
### Court Preacher of Mecklenburg at Lübz.

<image-desc>A horizontal decorative band consisting of repeating floral or heart-shaped ornaments within arched frames.</image-desc>

->Sirach 38.<-
->Think of the end and do not forget.<-

# To the Honorable /
### Respectable, Highly and Well-Learned, Highly and Well-Wise Lords, Mayors and Council of the Laudable Hanseatic and Maritime City of Wismar, My great-favoring Lords and Patrons.

->Grace and peace from God our Father and the Lord Jesus Christ, alongside my dear prayer at all times:<-

Honorable, Respectable, Highly and Well-Learned, Highly and Well-Wise Lords: what the spirit-illuminated Doctor of the Gentiles, Paul <note>original: "Doctor Paulus"</note>, and the Evangelical seer <note>original: "vident Joannes"; referring to John the Apostle as the author of Revelation</note> John, prophesy <note>original: "vaticinirn"</note> and foretell regarding the sorrowful state of the end times, the very same we must now painfully experience in deed. For the riders on the red, black, and pale horses <note>A reference to the Four Horsemen of the Apocalypse representing war, famine, and death.</note> roam about with violence, and from the seven bowls all kinds of plagues and punishments are poured out (Revelation <note>original: "Apoc."</note> 6 and 16). Because of this, the people become anxious and fearful, and men faint for fear and for expectation of the things which are to come upon the earth (Luke <note>original: "Luc."</note> 21). But moreover, all kinds of sins are in full motion and swing, so that the heavens must be horrified and the earth must shake at them; for the light of faith is extinguished in the majority of men, regarding which we not unjustly complain and say, when the

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of the book to the city council of Wismar, using apocalyptic imagery from the Bible to frame the current era as the "last days."</summary>
<keywords>Wismar, dedication, Saint Paul, Saint John, Book of Revelation, Four Horsemen, end times, patronage</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Council of Wismar. It transitions from describing the apocalyptic signs of the times to a call for repentance and spiritual readiness. The signature "A ij" indicates this is the second leaf of the first gathering of the printed book.</meta>

Son of Man comes, do you think he will also find faith on Earth? (Luke 18:8). The fire of love has grown cold, and almost no one deals faithfully with another anymore, just as Micah prophesies in chapter 7. Greed and usury, gluttony and drunkenness, pride and decadence <note>original: "Vppigkeit"; refers to excessive luxury or moral loose-living</note> are in full bloom, and there is no one who is sorry for his wickedness or says, "What have I done?" They all run their course like a fierce stallion in battle (Jeremiah 8). And even though they are punished for it according to God’s command, they nevertheless harden their hearts and let their ears be heavy, and they blind their eyes, so that they do not see with their eyes, nor hear with their ears, nor understand with their hearts, and turn and be healed (Isaiah 6). They let the angels of peace weep in vain (Isaiah 33) and are still hostile toward those who rebuke them in the gate, and they abhor the one who teaches what is healthy <note>original: "heilsam lehret"; teaching that leads to salvation</note> (Amos 5).

But all these things are harbingers and signs of the Last Day, from which we should perceive that the Lord has clothed himself with zeal as with a robe, and set a helmet of salvation upon his head, and will now break in with his majesty and glory to hold the Last Judgment over all people. There, we shall give an account of all that we have done during our bodily life, whether it be good or evil (Ecclesiastes 12; 1 Corinthians 5 <note>The author likely intended 2 Corinthians 5:10, which discusses the judgment seat of Christ.</note>). So that we may stand on such a Day of Judgment and be placed as obedient little sheep at the right hand of the Chief Shepherd <term>Arch-Shepherd Christ</term>, we should prepare ourselves for it with repentant hearts and remain watchful at all times. Thus, when the Lord comes, we may be caught up with joy to meet him in the air (1 Thessalonians 4). For this reason, Christ has hidden from us the time and hour of death and of his final coming, so that we should be found in constant readiness: *one day is hidden so that all may be observed* <note>original: "latet unus dies ut observentur omnes"; a common theological maxim attributed to St. Augustine, meaning God conceals the specific date of the end to encourage daily piety.</note>. To be sure, men have often presumed to calculate the time and hour, year and

A ij <meta>catchword: Day</meta>

<summary>The author describes a world filled with sin and apathy as evidence that the Last Judgment is imminent, urging readers to seek repentance. He explains that the exact timing of the end is hidden by God to ensure believers remain perpetually vigilant.</summary>
<keywords>Last Judgment, Second Coming, repentance, social decay, signs of the times, Chief Shepherd, spiritual watchfulness, Wismar dedication</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Council of Wismar from the previous page. The sentence fragment from the previous page concludes here, addressing the folly of trying to predict the exact date of the end of the world.</meta>

[the right hand. Because many people attempt] to calculate the Day of Judgment <note>original: "Jüngsten Tages"; literally "The Last Day"</note>, just as Nagelius <note>Paul Nagel (died 1624) was a mathematician and astronomer who used "cabalistic" calculations and celestial observations to predict that the end of the world would occur in 1624.</note> undertook to do in his *Astrological-Cabalistic Prognostication* <note>original: "Prognostico Astrologo-Cabalistico"</note>: But because this is directed against God’s revealed Word, and since many people might be misled and fooled by it, I have wished to publish this *Theological Prognostication* <note>original: "Theologicum Prognosticon"</note> under the highly praiseworthy names of Your Honorable Wisdoms and Graces <note>original: "E. E. W. und G."; an abbreviation for "Eure Edle Weisheiten und Gunsten," a formal address to a city council or group of magistrates.</note>, and to bring it to light as a warning and instruction for everyone. This is not because Your Honorable Wisdoms and Graces need my report, but rather so that I may publicly demonstrate my inclined and grateful spirit:

Therefore, I humbly and diligently pray that Your Honorable Wisdoms and Graces will be most graciously pleased to accept this little work and dedication in good part. With this, I most faithfully commend Your Honorable Wisdoms and Graces to Divine protection, for long-lasting health and for your temporal and eternal welfare in body and soul. Lübz <note>original: "Lüps"; a town in Mecklenburg, Germany.</note>, the 20th of February, in the year 1620.

->Your Honorable Wisdoms and Graces,<-

->Your always willing servant,<-

# Georgius Rostius

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page begins the primary text of the treatise. The previous page concluded the author's dedication to the Council of Wismar, signed by Georgius Rostius in February 1620.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal band of floral or fleur-de-lis ornaments at the top of the page.</image-desc>

->A Theological Prognostication,<-

# Of the Last Day.

## The First Chapter.

### That the Last Day will certainly come, and that the Lord will soon appear again to hold judgment over the living and the dead.

<image-desc>Large ornamental drop cap 'O' with interlaced vine and leaf patterns.</image-desc>
Although the Lord Christ is with us every day until the end of the world (Matthew 28) and walks among the candlesticks of His Christendom (Revelation 1), so that He may rule us with the spirit of His mouth, be our shepherd and host, and make us drunk from the rich goods of His house (Psalm 36); nevertheless, we only perceive the actual effect of His true presence <note>original: "præsentz"</note> and cannot see Him with our fleshly eyes in this state of mortality. As Saint Ambrose <note>A 4th-century Bishop of Milan and one of the four original Doctors of the Church.</note> also says in his tenth book on Luke: "standing in our midst, He is not perceived by us" <note>original Latin: "stans in medio nostrum non cernitur à nobis"</note>. Therefore, He comforts us throughout the writings of the prophets and apostles regarding His visible return and majestic appearance, when we shall behold Him face to face as He is (1 Corinthians 13, 1 John 3). For when Christ returns in His great power and glory, He will make everything straight and right and fully fulfill whatever remains unaccomplished in the Scriptures and within His dearly earned and purchased congregation. He will establish a universal World Reformation <note>The term "Reformation" here refers to a divine restoration or renewal of the entire world at the end of time.</note>, and God will judge the circle of the earth with justice through a Man in whom He has decreed it, and present the faith to everyone (Acts 17). Then, everything that is hidden will be uncovered and brought before God’s judgment seat <note>original: "Schöpffenbanck"; a legal term referring to the bench where lay judges sat, here used for the bar of divine judgment.</note>, and each person will

<summary>This page introduces the theological certainty of the Second Coming of Christ and the Final Judgment, transitioning from the introductory dedication to the main doctrinal argument.</summary>
<keywords>Last Day, Judgment Day, Second Coming, World Reformation, Presence of Christ, St. Ambrose, Biblical prophecy</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening chapter regarding the certainty of the Last Day. The previous page ended with the statement that God would judge the circle of the earth...</meta>

...receive according to what he has done during his bodily life, whether it be good or evil (Ecclesiastes 12, 2 Corinthians 5).

However, such a final Advent and visible return of Christ does not have only one name in Scripture; rather, it is called—specifically with respect to the judge <note>original Latin: "respectu judicis"</note>—the Day of the Lord (Amos 5:18, 2 Thessalonians 2:2); the Day of Jesus Christ (Philippians 1:7); the judgment of the great day (Epistle of Jude verse 6); the coming <note>original Greek: παρουσία (parousia)</note> of God (2 Peter 3:4); and the manifestation and appearing <note>original Greek: Φανέρωσις κ̀ Ἐπιφάνεια (phanerōsis kai epiphaneia)</note>, the appearance of the coming of Christ (2 Thessalonians 2:8, Titus 2:13). This is because Christ, the Lord of Glory (Psalm 24), will be the Judge on this great Day of Judgment. For the Father judges no one, but has committed all judgment to the Son, so that all might honor the Son just as they honor the Father (John 5). Furthermore, Christ's return is called the Day of Redemption—and indeed with respect to the pious <note>original Latin: "respectu piorum"</note> (Luke 21:28, Romans 8:23)—the time of refreshment (Acts 3:19), because the redeemed and chosen little children of God will be delivered from all evil, and enthroned and led into the eternal kingdom of glory, joy, and majesty. The Day of Wrath—with respect to the ungodly <note>original Latin: "respectu impiorum"</note> (Romans 2:5)—a day of trouble and distress, a day of storm and tempest, a day of darkness and gloominess (Zephaniah 1), a cloudy day, a misty day (Joel 2). For the fierce anger of the Lord will be poured out upon the godless and unbelieving, so that they pass away like chaff (Zephaniah 2) and are cast into the eternal hellfire like stinking goats (Matthew 25).

We commonly call it the Last Day <note>The German "Jüngster Tag" literally means "the youngest day," signifying the final, most recent day after which no other follows.</note> or the Final Judgment, as such names are also incorporated and noted in Holy Scripture (John 11:24, 1 John 2). We are thereby reminded that after this Last Day and Judgment, there will be no other day or judgment in the world, for God will create a new heaven and a new earth, so that the former things will no longer be remembered or come to heart (Isaiah 65, Revelation 21). And in such a new heaven and new earth, things will be far different and better than here in this world; for it will no longer be said of the righteous and pious that they pass through the valley of tears <note>original: "Jammerthal" — a common biblical metaphor for the suffering and sorrow of earthly life.</note>, as David says (Psalm 84). Instead, they shall have fullness of joy and a lovely existence at God's right hand forever (Psalm 16). For there we shall dwell <note>original Latin: "ibi enim habitabimus"</note>.

<summary>This page explains the various biblical names for the Day of Judgment, contrasting its role as a day of redemption for the faithful with a day of wrath for the wicked. It concludes by describing the "Last Day" as the transition to a new, joyful heaven and earth.</summary>
<keywords>Last Day, Final Judgment, Advent of Christ, Redemption, Wrath of God, New Heaven and New Earth, Valley of Tears, Salvation</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the end of the world and the final judgment. The previous page ended with a description of the Day of Wrath and the fate of the godless, who are cast into hellfire.</meta>

...we shall love <note>The Latin text likely began with *amabimus* on the previous page</note> without fear, says Bernard in his 33rd sermon on the Song of Songs; we shall abound without want, and we shall feast without distaste. There, the voice of weeping shall no longer be heard, nor the voice of lamentation; there shall no longer be children who do not reach their full days, nor old men who do not complete their years; rather, the youth of a hundred years shall die, and the sinner of a hundred years shall be cursed (Isaiah 65).

The teachers of the ancient schools and churches also **commemorate and recount the four last things** <note>original Latin: "Quatuor novissima commemorirt"</note>: **Death, judgment, hell, and eternal life** <note>original Latin: "Mors, judicium, infernus & vita æterna"</note>. Whoever wishes to rightly ponder and consider these things will not willingly or wantonly sink into sin, nor look downward and cling with his heart to earthly things, but will instead soar upward and strive for what is above (Colossians 3, Hebrews 13). As the wise man Sirach <note>Referring to the Book of Sirach, or Ecclesiasticus, in the Apocrypha</note> teaches in chapter 7: whatever you do, remember the end, and you will never do evil. Likewise in chapter 28: Remember the end and let enmity go, which seeks death and destruction, and remain in the commandments. For nothing so recalls men from sins, says Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian of Western Christianity</note> in the second book of *On Genesis Against the Manichaeans*, chapter 28, as the thought of imminent death. 

To this end, the ancient teacher Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic</note> also gives us very fine instruction and occasion in his sermon regarding our beginnings and our ends <note>original Latin: "de primord. & noviss. nostris"</note>: Consider your beginnings, attend to your middle, and remember your final things; these bring shame, those inflict pain, and the others strike fear. Think from whence you came and blush; where you are and groan; where you are going and tremble <note>original Latin: "Recole primordia, attende media, memorare novissima tua, hæc pudorem adducunt, ista dolorem ingerunt, illa metum incutiunt: Cogita unde veneris & erubesce, ubi sis & ingemisce, quò vadas & contremisce."</note>. 

For what does it profit a man if he scrapes together great wealth, much money and land, and is miserly? He must still die and leave everything behind, and his silver and gold will not rescue him on the day of wrath (Zephaniah 1, Proverbs 11). What does it matter if a man lives in Epicurean <note>Living only for pleasure and physical comfort, named after the Greek philosopher Epicurus whose teachings were often misinterpreted in this period as mere hedonism</note> security, in gluttony and gorging, in pride and vanity? When he is dead, the snakes and worms shall devour the stinking maggot-sack of his body. And how will such a fatted pig of Sardanapalus <note>A reference to Sardanapalus, a legendary Assyrian king famed in the Renaissance for his extreme decadence, effeminacy, and self-indulgence</note> stand before God and His judgment? Therefore, the final trumpet blast of Christ should forever ring and penetrate our hearts and ears: Arise, you dead, and come to judgment! <note>original Latin: "Surgite mortui & venite ad judicium"</note> As also Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a scholar and archbishop</note> writes <margin>writes</margin> of this in a Christian and lovely manner—

<summary>This page explores the "Four Last Things"—Death, Judgment, Hell, and Eternal Life—as a meditative tool to discourage sin and vanity. It draws on various authorities like St. Augustine and St. Bernard to emphasize that the thought of one's own mortality and the coming judgment should lead to a holy life.</summary>
<keywords>Four Last Things, Memento Mori, St. Bernard of Clairvaux, St. Augustine, Final Judgment, vanity, Sardanapalus, Isaiah 65</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of preparing for the end of the world, following the previous page's mention of the teacher Bernard of Clairvaux.</meta>

...writes in Book 3 of *On the Supreme Good*, chapter 66, folio 28: every person ought to live solicitously and always consider the end of their life, so that from the contemplation of it, the soul may always raise itself to higher things and beware the allurements of this world with fear <note>original Latin: "sollicitè debet unusquisq; vivere & semper terminum vitæ suæ considerare, ut de contemplatione illius animus se semper ad alta sustollat, & hujus seculi blanditias cum timore caveat"</note>. And indeed, in these last times, everyone should rightly consider this well, because the great Day of the Lord is near; it is near and hurries greatly (Zephaniah 1). Therefore, seek the Lord, all you humble of the land, you who keep his laws; seek justice, seek humility, so that on the Day of the Lord's Wrath you may be hidden (Zephaniah 2). To this also Fulgentius <note>Saint Fulgentius of Ruspe (c. 462–533 AD), an African bishop and theologian</note> admonishes in *On Stewards* on page 619: Let us place our treasure in heaven, so that we may also have our heart in heaven <note>original Latin: "Thesaurum nostrum in cœlis ponamus, ut etiam cor nostrum in cœlis habere possimus"</note>. And Christ himself says in Matthew 6: Seek first the kingdom of God and his righteousness, and all these other things will be added to you.

When we now look at these various names for the **Last Day** <term>Jüngster Tag: Literally the "Youngest Day," the common German term for the Day of Judgment</term> from Scripture in the fear of the Lord, and consider them thoroughly alongside the preceding signs, they remind us that the Last Day will certainly come and the Lord will soon break in to hold his final judgment. For even the voices of secular prophets, agreeing with the heavenly ones, announce the end of things and the sunset of the world after a short time, as Lactantius <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD), an early Christian author who advised Emperor Constantine</note> writes in book 7 of *On the Divine Reward*, chapter 14, page 313 <note>original Latin: "nam etiam secularium prophetarum congruentes cum cœlestibus voces, finem rerum & occasum post breve tempus adnunciant"</note>. For this reason, we should prepare ourselves for such **Advent** and the coming of Christ, so that at last we may be secure from future punishment, according to Ambrose <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397 AD), the influential Bishop of Milan</note>, Volume 3, folio 202 <note>original Latin: "ut tandem simus securi de pœnâ futurâ"</note>, so that we may escape the painful punishment of wrath and win eternal glory. We should lift up our heads, according to the instruction of Christ in Luke 21, and say with the Christian Church out of heartfelt longing: "Yes, come Lord Jesus" (Revelation 22). In the same way, Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD) known for his "Apology"</note> shines before us with such little sighs <note>original German: "Seuffkerlein," referring to brief, fervent prayers</note> in *Apology against the Gentiles*, chapter 39: "We pray," he says, "for the delay of the end" <note>original Latin: "oramus, inquit, pro morâ finis"</note>.

But regardless of this, despisers and mockers have been found at all times, as Peter prophesies in his second epistle, chapter 3 <note>The OCR reads "2. Eph. 3," but the context clearly refers to 2 Peter 3</note>, who walked according to their own lusts and said: "Where is the promise of his coming? For since the fathers fell asleep, everything remains as it has been from the beginning of creation." Thus, they willfully do not want to know that the heavens existed long ago, and that the earth stood out of water and in water by the Word of God; nevertheless, at that time the world was destroyed by the same with the **Great Flood**— <note>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword "derbet," continuing "verderbet" (destroyed) on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page exhorts believers to remain vigilant and prayerful as the Last Judgment approaches, citing both Church Fathers and Scripture to counter skeptics who doubt the end of the world. It emphasizes that just as the world was once destroyed by the Flood, it faces a certain final judgment.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the end of the world and the final judgment, transitioning from the signs of the end to the specific fate of the heavens and the earth.</meta>

...is corrupted: So also the heaven and the earth are now kept by his word, that they may be reserved for the fire on the Day of Judgment and the damnation of ungodly people.

For not only the pagans, but also many heretics and fanatics have very fiercely contested and attacked this article <note>The "article" refers to the Resurrection of the Dead.</note>, so that Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers.</note> may well lament and say in Sermon 2 on Psalm 88: In no matter is the Christian faith so vehemently, so stubbornly, so obstinately and contentiously contradicted as regarding the resurrection of the flesh <note>original Latin: "in nulla re tam vehementer, tam pertinaciter, tam obnixè & contentiosè contradicitur fidei Christianæ sicut de carnis resurrectione"</note>. Concerning the pagans, it is not so much to be wondered at, because it is an article of faith and not of sight, which cannot be measured by reason. For who can comprehend how it may happen that the dead body of a human, which has become rot and decay, dust and ash, shall rise again in glory? Taurellus <note>Nicolaus Taurellus (1547–1606), a German philosopher and theologian known for trying to use reason to defend faith.</note> wishes to devise and investigate such things according to reason in his preface to *On Eternal Things*, pages 17 and 18, just as the **Sacramentarian Rationalists** <term>Sacramentarian Rationalists: A derogatory term used by Lutherans for those (often followers of Zwingli or Calvin) who used logical reasoning to argue against the real, physical presence of Christ in the Bread and Wine.</term> or "Masters of Reason" otherwise presume to judge other articles of faith according to their own reason and wisdom: Mornæus in *On the Truth of Religion*, chapter 5; Keckermann in Book 1 of his *System of Theology*, chapter 3; Timpler in *Metaphysics*, chapter 2, questions 8 and 9; Bilsten in the preface to his *Dialogues*, chapters 3 and 4; just as Piscator <note>Johannes Piscator (1546–1625), a prominent Reformed (Calvinist) theologian.</note> boldly writes in his *Refutation of Buscher*, chapter 14: It is not true that theology often transcends the rules of logic <note>original Latin: "Non est verum Theologiam sæpè transcendere regulas logicas"</note>. 

But the articles of faith will surely remain unsearchable; flesh and blood will not reveal them to us (Matthew 16). For it is a Divine wisdom that lies hidden in mystery (1 Corinthians 2). Reason and the world can attain none of these points through themselves, nor grasp and understand them, says Luther (Volume 6 of the Jena edition, my folio 81); rather, they do the exact opposite, taking offense at such teaching and holding it for sheer great foolishness. Therefore, we must take off the "coarse shoes of reason" as did Moses (Exodus 3) and Joshua (chapter 5) when we wish to read the Scripture and simply believe what God allows to be presented to us therein. For He is Wisdom itself, who well knows what He should say; He is Truth itself, who cannot and will not deceive us. Therefore, let us simply cling to it because of the authority of the speaking Scripture, and let us not resist God, even if what is said seems absurd to our senses and thoughts; for we cannot be defrauded by the words of Jehovah <note>original Latin: "simpliciter igitur propter autoritatem dicentis scripturæ inhæreamus, nec repugnemus Deo, licet sensui & cogitationi nostræ absurdum esse videatur, quod dicitur, nam verbis Jehovæ defraudari non possumus"</note>.

<summary>This page defends the doctrine of the resurrection of the body against pagan and rationalist critics, arguing that faith must transcend human logic and reason.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection of the Flesh, Reason vs. Faith, Augustine, Luther, Rationalism, Sacramentarians, Scholasticism, Biblical Authority</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, specifically targeting the "Masters of Reason" who attempt to use logic to explain divine truths that are beyond human comprehension. It ends mid-sentence leading into the next page.</meta>

...[we] are, but our sense is very easily deceived <note>original Latin: "sumus, sensus autem noster deceptui facillimus est"</note>, Chrysostom <note>St. John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Archbishop of Constantinople known for his preaching.</note>, Homily 83 on Chapter 26 of Matthew.

For even if we search and ponder the Divine mysteries for a long time, we will still not reach them, because there is no knowledge of infinite things <note>original Latin: "quia infinitorum nulla est cognitio"</note>, Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), a Roman philosopher who bridged classical philosophy and Christian theology.</note>, commentary on the Categories, page 19, and we must even be brought to mockery and shame over it, and perhaps even suffer shipwreck of faith and salvation, as it is written: I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and I will reject the understanding of the prudent. Where are the clever? Where are the scribes? Where are the worldly-wise? Has God not made the wisdom of this world into foolishness? For since the world through its wisdom did not know God in His wisdom, it pleased God well through the foolishness of preaching to save those who believe in it, Isaiah 29, 1 Corinthians 1. Therefore Gualterus <note>Rudolf Gualther (1519–1586), a Swiss Reformed theologian and author of many popular homilies.</note> himself writes in Homily 6 on Chapter 3 of the first epistle to the Corinthians, page 43: It is the cause of extreme folly to reduce those things which surpass our understanding to the calculation of our reason <note>original Latin: "Extremæ stultitiæ causa est, quæ nostrum captum superant ad nostræ rationis calculum revocare"</note>. For we hardly guess at what is on Earth, and find with difficulty what is close at hand, Wisdom of Solomon 9 <note>A book from the Biblical Apocrypha emphasized in the 16th and 17th centuries for its focus on divine wisdom.</note>. How should the natural man understand anything of that which is of the Spirit of God? It is foolishness to him; he cannot comprehend it, 1 Corinthians 2, 2 Corinthians 3. 

This is confessed by Aristotle, the wise pagan, himself, who says in *Metaphysics*, Book 2, Chapter 1: Our intellect is blind even in the most manifest things <note>original Latin: "intellectus noster etiam in manifestissimis cæcutit"</note>. Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a celebrated Renaissance scholar and physician.</note> also writes in Exercise 20, page 29: There are many things in the sublunary world <note>The Earthly realm below the sphere of the moon, which in traditional cosmology was subject to change and decay.</note> which cannot be investigated by the sharpness of the human mind; for it is part of human wisdom to be willing to remain ignorant of some things with a calm mind <note>original Latin: "Multa sunt in orbe sublunari, quæ acie humanæ mentis pervestigari nequeunt, humanæ enim sapientiæ est, quædam æquo animo nescire velle."</note>. Therefore we say rightly with Luther, from the *Church Postil* <note>A collection of sermons meant for clergy to read to their congregations.</note> on the Trinity Epistle, page 139: Shame on you, you disgraceful reason! How do we miserable, poor humans come to this—we who do not even know how our own speaking, laughing, or sleeping happens (natural works we do and feel daily)—and yet we want to speak of God, of how He stands in His Divine essence, without God's Word, solely out of our own heads? Is that not blindness above all blindness? For there must and shall remain a detectable difference between seeing and trusting <note>original Latin: "inter videre, & fidere"</note>, as Christ says in John 20: Blessed are those who do not see and yet believe. Durandus <note>William Durand (c. 1230–1296), a medieval French liturgist and philosopher.</note> also recognizes this in Book 4 of the *Rationale of the Divine Offices*, page 67: Faith, he says, has no merit where human reason provides the proof <note>original Latin: "Fides inquit non habet meritum, ubi ratio humana præbet experimentum"</note>. <meta>This page follows a discussion on the limitations of human reason regarding divine mysteries. Accordingly, let everyone guide their reason in divine mysteries and take it captive under the obedience of faith (2 Corinthians 10). Let them praise God and give Him the honor alone; thus they will be found righteous on that Great Day <note>The "Great Day" refers to the Day of Judgment or the Last Day.</note> and be able to give a correct account of everything they have done.

Since the heathens judge by reason alone, it is no wonder that they do not believe the mysteries of God regarding the Last Day, but speak mockingly of them, as Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman naturalist who expressed skepticism about life after death in his *Natural History*.</note> does in Book 7, chapter 55, page 58; also Aeschylus <note>The Greek playwright; in his play *The Eumenides*, he suggests that once a man dies, there is no resurrection.</note> in *The Eumenides*. For that which is given to no one to experience, says Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic.</note> in Sermon 4 for All Saints, let no one attempt to express <note>original Latin: "Quod enim nulli datum est experiri... nullus conetur effari"</note>. Of all these things, Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most important Church Fathers.</note> writes more extensively in *The City of God*, Book 13, chapters 20 and 27. But this is certainly worth considering and wondering at: that the Sadducean swine-teachers <note>original German: "Sewlehrer" – a highly pejorative term used by the author to dismiss the Sadducees as false or "filthy" teachers.</note> in Jerusalem believed in neither the resurrection nor angels and spirits (Acts 23), even though they had heard Christ Himself and were convinced and overcome by Him (Matthew 22). 

But as the master is, so too are his disciples. For according to the opinion of Philastrius <note>Saint Philastrius (died c. 397), Bishop of Brescia, who wrote a catalog of 156 heresies.</note>, they take their name from Sadoc, who was a disciple of the heretic Dositheus. However, Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), whose *Etymologiae* was the standard encyclopedia of the Middle Ages.</note> in Book 8 of his *Etymologies*, chapter 4, as well as Carion <note>Johannes Carion (1499–1537), author of a famous world chronicle used widely in the Reformation.</note> in Book 2 of his *Chronicle*, and many other <term>early-modern theologians</term> <note>original Latin: "Palæneoterici Theologi" – scholars who interpreted ancient texts using the newer methods of the Renaissance.</note>, derive their name from <term>Zaddik</term> <note>Hebrew: צדיק (Tzadik), meaning "righteous one."</note>, which means "a righteous person." They likely used this name because it is the way of people in this desolate and base world that they can adorn the worst vices and decorate them finely with the cloak of virtue. 

Next to these, Hymenaeus and Philetus distinguished themselves; they missed the truth and said the resurrection had already happened, and they overturned the faith of some (2 Timothy 2). They themselves, however, suffered shipwreck of their faith (2 Timothy 2 [referencing 1 Timothy 1:19]). Since then, from one time to another, such heretical people have appeared and denied the resurrection of the dead and what will occur on the Last Day according to the prophecy of the prophets and apostles. These include Simon Magus (Epiphanius, Heresy 21; Irenaeus, Book 1, ch. 24), Carpocrates, Marcion, Valentinus, and Apelles (Irenaeus, Book 1, ch. 29; Epiphanius, Heresies 24 & 31; Tertullian, *On the Prescription of Heretics*), and Hierax, who believed in nothing but a spiritual resurrection <note>original Latin: "qui non nisi spiritualem resurrectionem credidit"</note> (Epiphanius, Heresy 67).

Although the Jews believed and still believe that they will rise again from the dead, they nevertheless deny this

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of those who deny the physical resurrection of the body. [distinguis]hes them from all other nations, and also from us Christians, as Rabbi Bechai teaches in the book he calls *Kad Hakkemach* <note>A work on Jewish ethics and philosophy by Bahya ben Asher (13th century).</note> page 8; also Aben Ezra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a significant Spanish Jewish scholar.</note> in his *Perusch* or interpretation of Daniel; likewise Rabbi David Kimchi <note>A medieval Jewish biblical commentator and grammarian (1160–1235).</note> on Psalms 1 and 26. Similarly, at this time in Poland and Lithuania, we have the Photinians <note>Followers of the teachings of Photinus, here used as a label for Socinians or Unitarians who denied the pre-existence of Christ and certain aspects of the resurrection.</note>, who are particularly given to "Judaizing" and believe only in the resurrection of the chosen—yet not with their [actual] bodies, but with spiritual and heavenly bodies; that is, those essentially transformed into spirits. But for the damned, they say it follows the little heathen verse: *Et redit in nihilum quod fuit ante nihil* <note>Latin: "And that which was nothing before returns to nothing."</note>. As all this may be found in their books: Faustus Socinus <note>A leading Italian theologian of the Radical Reformation.</note> against Andreas Volanus, part 2, folio 106; in the book *On the Savior* (*de Servatore*), part 3, chapter 8; Ostorod in his *Instruction*, chapters 14, 41, and 42; Smalcius in the book *On the Divinity of Christ*, chapter 24, folio 146; Eniedinus, Craterus, and other Photinians.

The ancient teacher Origen <note>An early Christian scholar (c. 184–253 AD) whose complex views on the soul often sparked controversy.</note> did not, it is true, dare to go so far as to deny the resurrection entirely; nevertheless, he taught quite absurdly and recklessly that *corpus non idem numero, sed specie resurrecturum* <note>Latin: "the body will rise not the same in number [identical matter], but in kind [form]."</note>, as Methodius in *Aglaophon* and Epiphanius in *Heresy 64* write of him. This Origenist error has been "encrusted" and renewed in our time by Taurellus <note>Nicolaus Taurellus (1547–1606), a German philosopher and theologian.</note> in *Of Eternal Things* (*de Rer. Ætern.*), page 620, who writes that it is not our deceased bodies that shall rise on the Last Day, but rather that God will create other bodies with which the souls shall then be united. But what does the Scripture say of this? "He guards all his bones, so that not one of them is broken" (Psalm 34). Likewise: "Your dead shall live, and rise with the corpse" (Isaiah 26). Job also comforts himself with this and says: "In my flesh I shall see God; him I shall see for myself, and my eyes shall behold him and no stranger" (Job 19).

Eutychius, the Patriarch of Constantinople, imagined and dreamed something regarding "spiritual bodies" and said *corpora hominum in resurrectione aërea & impalpabilia futura* <note>Latin: "that the bodies of men in the resurrection would be airy and intangible."</note>, as recorded by Gregory the Great in *Morals*, book 14, chapter 29, and Bede in book 6 on Luke, chapter 24. Now, the Apostle Paul certainly intends (1 Corinthians 15) that the glorified bodies shall have great power and glory, but that they should be spiritual—that is, bodies transformed into spirits which are of the same nature and essence as the air—that is entirely [contrary to] God's Word...

<meta>This page continues the author's argument for the physical resurrection of the body. He is countering the "Photinian" or Socinian view that bodies become pure spirits. He draws on the authority of early Church Fathers like Augustine, Gregory the Great, and Ambrose to prove that the resurrected body, while "spiritual" in power, remains physical in nature.</meta>

...[incorruptible]; they will indeed have spiritual properties about them, but not a spiritual essence, as Augustine teaches: <note>original: "Corpora glorificata erunt spiritualia, non quod in spiritus immutabuntur, sed quia spiritu vivificante subsistunt, non tamen corpora esse desistunt"</note> "Glorified bodies will be spiritual, not because they will be changed into spirits, but because they subsist by the quickening spirit, yet they do not cease to be bodies," from Book 13 of *The City of God*, chapter 22. And in the *Enchiridion* <note>A handbook on Christian faith, hope, and love written by Augustine.</note>, chapter 19. 

Similarly, Gregory the Great <note>Pope Gregory I (c. 540–604), a prolific writer and influential theologian.</note> also writes in Book 11 of his *Moralia* <note>A massive commentary on the Book of Job.</note>, chapter 31: <note>original: "In illa regni coelestis gloria erit corpus nostrum subtile quidem, per effectum spiritualis potentiæ, sed palpabile per unitatem naturæ, caro secundum naturam erit, sed secundum passionum desideria non erit, quia devicto mortis aculeo, in æterna incorruptione regnabit."</note> "In that glory of the heavenly kingdom, our body will indeed be subtle through the effect of spiritual power, but palpable [touchable] through the unity of nature; it will be flesh according to its nature, but it will not be according to the desires of passions, because once the sting of death is conquered, it shall reign in eternal incorruption." 

Moreover, the Apostle teaches that God will make our mortal bodies alive, Romans 8:11, just as Job says in chapter 19: "I will be encompassed with this, my skin." Likewise, 1 Corinthians 15: this corruptible—<note>original: "Hoc corruptibile"</note> this perishable [body]—must put on the incorruptible, and this mortal must put on immortality. For whatever kind of seed is scattered and sown in the earth, the same grows and shoots forth again, 1 Corinthians 15. So it is also with the deceased bodies; <note>original: "quod cecidit resurget, quod mortuum fuit reviviscet"</note> "that which fell shall rise again, that which was dead shall live again," as Ambrose <note>The 4th-century Bishop of Milan.</note> writes in *On the Faith of the Resurrection*, chapter 19. 

Therefore, John also prophesies in Revelation 20 that the earth and the sea shall give up the dead that are within them; and Ezekiel, chapter 37, shows us the same with the dry bones in the field, which are grown over with veins and flesh and covered with skin. Otherwise, other absurdities would arise if the flesh, which was a <note>original: "socia crucis"</note> partner of the cross, should not also be a <note>original: "socia gloriæ"</note> partner of glory—a matter I have not undertaken to discuss more extensively at this time.

The Manichaeans <note>Followers of Mani who believed the physical world was inherently evil and thus denied a physical resurrection.</note> in the time of Augustine, and the Libertines <note>A radical group often accused of moral indifference and denying traditional doctrines like the afterlife.</note>—to say nothing of the Epicureans <note>Followers of the Greek philosopher Epicurus; in this context, the term is used for those who believe there is no life after death.</note>, whom even the learned heathens opposed, as Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopulus, a Byzantine church historian.</note> testifies in Book 10, chapter 26—have mockingly laughed at everything recorded in Scripture concerning the Last Day for our comfort and salvation, considering it a fable or a little fairy tale. Likewise, the Anabaptists <note>literally "Re-baptizers," a radical wing of the Reformation often viewed as heretical by the mainstream.</note> teach nothing consistent regarding this article of faith; <note>original: "modo ajunt, modo negant"</note> "now they say yes, now they say no," as the Frankenthal Colloquy <note>A famous theological debate in 1571 between Reformed divines and Anabaptists in the Palatinate.</note> in Article 28 demonstrates.

The anointed church fathers in Rome <note>A sarcastic reference to the Pope and his cardinals.</note> have also mostly believed very little about the Last Day, as Luther indicates in Volume 6 of the Jena edition, folio 219; specifically Leo X <note>Pope Leo X (reigned 1513–1521), famously the target of Martin Luther’s early protests.</note>, who in greed, lust, and...

<meta>This page continues the author's polemical argument. Having established the theological necessity of the physical resurrection on the previous page, the author now pivots to critique the Roman Catholic Popes, arguing that their legendary corruption and personal atheism led them to mock the very idea of an afterlife or a resurrection.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<meta>catchword: as</meta>

...luxury, more than befits a Pope, he was steeped in, as Platina <note>Bartolomeo Platina (1421–1481), a Vatican librarian and author of a famous history of the lives of the Popes.</note> and Onuphrius <note>Onofrio Panvinio (1529–1568), an Augustinian historian who continued Platina’s work.</note> write of him in *The Lives of the Popes*, folio page 337. For although Bembo <note>Pietro Bembo (1470–1547), an influential Italian scholar and Cardinal.</note> wished to prove the truth to him from the articles of faith, he [the Pope] only practiced his Italian and rebellious mockery with it and answered: <note>original: "Quid mihi, narras fabulam"</note> "What is that to me? You are telling me a fable." <note>This refers to a famous anti-papal anecdote where a Pope—often identified as Leo X—allegedly called the Gospel a "profitable fable."</note> So also has Paul III—who kept around 45,000 whores in Rome, from whom he took an annual tribute, according to Bale <note>John Bale (1495–1563), an English historian and controversialist.</note>, Book 7, page 45—said in his final hour of death that he would now find out what he had always doubted: whether there is a God, a Devil, and a Hell, and a resurrection of the dead. What do you think—did these holy fathers not fare well? But they have a law that even if they drag a thousand souls with them into Hell, no one may say, "Dear Father, what are you doing?" <note>A reference to the canon law decree *Si Papa* (Distinction 40, Canon 6), which argued that the Pope is beyond human judgment unless he deviates from the faith.</note> 

What a horrible, unheard-of life the Popes otherwise led, according to the Epicurean <note>Referring to the philosophy of Epicurus, which was often misinterpreted in this era as a pursuit of base sensory pleasures.</note> verse: <note>original: "Lude, bibas, comedas post mortem nulla voluptas"</note> "Play, drink, eat; after death there is no pleasure." This same fact is well-known from history. For many among them were necromancers and sorcerers: Sylvester II, Benedict VIII, and Gregory VII—who, within thirteen years, cunningly executed six of his predecessor Popes with poison so that he might be raised to the Papal eminence and highness. John VIII was a whorish woman <note>A reference to the legend of "Pope Joan," a woman who supposedly reigned as Pope in the 9th century; modern historians generally consider this a myth.</note>, which is demonstrated and proven by credible witnesses in Book 10 of the *Catalog of Witnesses to the Truth*, pages 187–188. Julius II and Urbanus II tyrannized quite inhumanly and had many thousands of Christians strangled and miserably slaughtered, as Abbas Urspergensis <note>Burchard of Ursberg (d. 1231), a German chronicler.</note> and Guillaume Budé write. Gregory IX and Innocent III were so drowned in greed that even Frederick II had to redeem and buy absolution from Gregory for one hundred thousand ounces of gold, and Alexander, the King in Scotland, for a very large sum of money (see the *Continuator of the Sacred War*, Book 3, Chapter 10; and John Major, *On the Deeds of the Scots*, Book 4, Chapter 7). Clement IV desired the tithe from all of Europe (Aventinus, Book 7 of the *Annals of the Bavarians*). Alexander VI violated his own daughter. Constantine I had the Emperor kiss his stinking feet—as Josephus Stephanus Valentinus writes more extensively regarding such practices in *On the Kissing of the Feet of the Roman Pontiff*, Chapter 9; also Francisco Turriano against Sadeel, Chapter 14; and Antoninus, Part 3, Title 22, Chapter 5, Section 4. I will pass over in silence...

How will they stand? As the tree falls, so shall it lie; therefore, well is it for all who wait upon the LORD, who shall then hear, *Come, you blessed* <note>original: "Venite benedicti"</note>, "come here, you blessed of my Father, inherit the kingdom prepared for you from the beginning of the world." <margin>Matthew 25.</margin>

# The Second Chapter.
## When and at what time the Last Day will come; and whether it will happen according to the calculations of the Rosicrucians and Nagel, in the year 1623.

Since the decaying nature of this world has now moved very near to its end, and the Lord Christ will soon come again to hold judgment over the living and the dead, I consider...

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the uncertainty of the hour of judgment. The author transitions from warning against procrastination to examining the "signs of the times" that suggest the end of the world is imminent, while simultaneously cautioning against the hubris of those who try to calculate the exact date of the apocalypse.</meta>

...I do not need to demonstrate this at great length, as it is indeed universally known and recognized—and should be—that the writings of the Prophets and Apostles are fulfilled. The signs of which Christ speaks in <margin>Luke 21</margin> have preceded us, and more of them occur daily. We hear of wars and rumors of wars; we experience heavy, expensive times <note>a "tewre zeit" refers to periods of inflation, crop failure, or famine</note>; we see the great pride and complacency of men. All kinds of sin and vice are in full swing. Thus, we have nothing more to expect in Holy Scripture that should happen before the Last Day. Everything is finished and fulfilled. The Roman Empire <note>referring to the Holy Roman Empire, seen as the last of the four world empires from the Book of Daniel</note> is at its end. The "little horn"—namely the Turk <term>The Ottoman Empire, which many Reformation-era writers identified as the "little horn" of the beast in Daniel 7</term>—of which Daniel prophesies in <margin>Chapter 7</margin> has reached its peak. The splendor of the Papacy, of which Paul speaks in <margin>2 Thessalonians 2</margin>, has fallen, and the world lies in its final throes. It creaks and crackles at every corner, as if it were about to break right now. Therefore, we must firmly believe that the Last Day is near, right before the door; it is near and hastens greatly. <margin>Zephaniah 1.</margin> 

But when and at what time, in which year and day this shall happen, is known to no person on earth. Christ also does not wish for us to search and brood over it, <note>original Latin: "sed potius jubet punctum penè quodvis exspectare non hujus aut illius, sed cujusvis diei & horæ"</note> but rather commands us to expect almost any point in time, not of this or that day, but of every day and hour. <note>original Latin: "Siquidem ideò latet extremus dies ut exspectentur singuli"</note> For the last day remains hidden so that every single day may be awaited. We should be awake and ready at all times, so that when he comes, we may have joy and not be put to shame before him at his coming. <margin>1 John 2.</margin>

Curious people have often emboldened themselves to calculate the year and day of Christ's coming using the arts of calculation and reckoning. However, what they achieved thereby, and how they were publicly put to shame, is clear and manifest. Elias—not the prophet who lived in the time of King Ahab roughly 923 years before Christ's birth, but one of the Jewish Rabbis who lived about 200 years before Christ, as Gerson reports in the *Jewish Talmud* <margin>book 2, chapter 7; see also Galatinus book 4, chapter 20 and Paulus Weidner in *Locis Fidei* book 3, under the heading "the voice from the beginning"</margin>—prophesied that the world would stand for six thousand years, as we read in the book *Sanhedrin*, in the chapter "All Israel," and in the book *Abadazara*, chapter 1: <note>original Latin: "Sex millia annorum durabit mundus, postea conflagrabit: duo millia inane: duo millia lex: duo millia Messias, & propter peccata nostra dies"</note> The world shall endure for six thousand years, and afterward it will burn: two thousand years void; two thousand years of the Law; two thousand years of the Messiah; and because of our sins the days

<meta>This page continues the argument that while the Last Day is certainly approaching, the specific date remains a divine secret. The author transitions from general signs of the end times to a critique of historical attempts to calculate the exact year of Christ's return, citing both "unwritten" prophecies and the errors of early Church Fathers.</meta>

<sig>C ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

...the days and years shall be shortened. <note>original Latin: "dies & anni decurtabuntur"</note> Based on such a prophecy of Elijah, many have looked for—and it is still held today—the idea that the world should remain for only six thousand years, and then, or even before those years are fulfilled, the Judgment would begin. But because this is an <term>unwritten prophecy <note>original Greek: ἄγραφον (agraphon); a tradition not recorded in the canonical Bible</note></term>, a prediction that has no basis in God’s Word, we rightly let it go and remain only with what is proclaimed and shown to us in the Scriptures.

Afterward, in the time of the Apostles, such <term>false Christs <note>original Latin: PseudoChristi</note></term> stepped forward and taught that the Last Day would come at a time which they had determined. Against this, Paul writes in <margin>2 Thessalonians 2</margin> and says: Concerning the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ and our gathering to him, we ask you, dear brothers, that you do not let yourselves be quickly moved from your mindset nor terrified, neither by spirit, nor by word, nor by letters as if sent from us, claiming that the Day of Christ is at hand. Let no one deceive you in any way, for it will not come unless the falling away <note>the great apostasy or desertion of faith</note> comes first, and the Man of Sin is revealed, the Son of Perdition.

After the time of the Apostles, many such "calculation masters" arose, who calculated and set a specific number of years from the first coming of Christ until His second majestic return—some 365, some 400, some 500, or some 1,000 years. Regarding all of them, Augustine writes in <margin>The City of God, book 18, chapter 53</margin>: **"They rely on human conjectures, for they can produce nothing certain based on the authority of the canonical Scriptures. Indeed, He who said: 'It is not for you to know the times, which the Father has put in His own power,' loosens the fingers of all those who calculate such things, and commands them to be still."** <note>Augustine poetically suggests that God’s command "un-clenches" the busy fingers of those trying to count out the date of the apocalypse on their hands.</note>

So too have several of the ancient Church teachers stumbled dangerously in this matter and determined a specific time for how long the world should stand, such as Irenaeus in <margin>Book 5, final chapter</margin>, Lactantius in <margin>Book 7 of "On the Divine Reward," chapter 25, folio 328</margin>, and Gregory the Great in <margin>Volume 2, on 1 Kings chapter 9, column 1415</margin>. However, when one calculates the time and compares one date against another, it is found to have been only a vain dream and human ordinance. For Lactantius—to say nothing of the others—lived around the year of Christ 336. Were his <term>art of calculation <note>original German: Supputirkunst</note></term> correct, the world would have had to perish in the year of Christ 536.

<meta>This page continues the critique of "calculation masters" who attempt to determine the exact date of the apocalypse. It moves from historical examples—including chronograms and the predictions of Renaissance scholars—to a sharp condemnation of contemporary 17th-century groups like the Rosicrucians and Paul Nagel.</meta>

We read of similar examples in Volume 1 of the *Letters of Obscure Men* <note>original Latin: "Epistolae obscurorum virorum," a famous 16th-century satirical work</note>, letter 50, and in Philipp Camerarius's *Historical Meditations* <note>original Latin: "Succisivorum," referring to the "Opera Horarum Subcisivarum," a collection of historical anecdotes</note>, book 1, chapter 40: that some have tried to determine the time of the **Last Day** from these words: **"They shall look on Him whom they have pierced."** <note>original Latin: "VIDebVnt In qVeM pVpVgerVnt" (John 19:37). Some interpreters used this sentence as a chronogram, where specific letters are read as Roman numerals to calculate a date.</note> Some calculate 33 **Jubilee Years** <note>a period of 50 years in religious tradition</note> after Christ's resurrection—based on the 33 years He lived on Earth—and arrive at the year 1684. Others take three and a half Jubilees following the revelation of the **Antichrist** <note>a figure of evil expected before the end times; Protestants often identified the Pope as this figure</note> and count 175 years from the year 1520 (when the Pope in Rome was powerfully revealed as such); this leads from the birth of Christ to the year 1695 for the Last Judgment. **Michael Stifel** <note>a monk and mathematician famous for his failed prediction of the world's end in 1533</note> prophesied that the Last Day would come in the year of Christ 1533, on the feast day of Saint Lucy. Funccius, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Regiomontanus, Pico della Mirandola, Adelbertus Thermopedius, and several other historians and theologians also had their specific calculations for the Last Day: some set it for 1533, some 1588, some 1688, and others for the year 1670. One group maintains that Christ shall return by day, while others say He shall come by night, specifically around Easter, which they attempt to prove by referencing the Exodus from Egypt.

Now once again, ignorant prophets have arisen who call themselves **Rosicrucians** <note>a secret society of mystics that emerged in Germany in the early 1600s</note>. They imagine and convince themselves of a great wisdom above all other people, yet they have never fulfilled any of it in deed, and most of it is impossible to fulfill anyway. Furthermore, Holy Scripture teaches the opposite in many things—especially regarding the prophecy of the time of the Last Day—stating that Christ, according to their **calculation** <note>original Latin: "computation"</note> and reckoning, shall return in the year 1623 and begin His kingdom on Earth. This year and time reckoning is also favored by the Rosicrucian spokesman **Nagelius** <note>Paul Nagel, an astronomer and mystic known for his apocalyptic predictions</note> in his *Cabalistic Astrological Forecast* <note>original Latin: "Prognostico Astrologo Cabalistico"</note>, section D. iii. Yet he is so poorly grounded in such **the art of reckoning** <note>original German: "Supputirkunst"</note> that he even contradicts himself, stating that the "winter revolution" <note>a turning point in a cycle of time or planetary movement</note> will extend into the year 1624, section B. He also writes in section D. iii: "Let the year 1625 come and enter, then answer me regarding the great change and **Reformation** <note>here referring to a predicted spiritual or worldly renewal</note>." Now, if Christ is to begin His kingdom in the year 1623, how then shall the "winter"—that is, the hour of temptation—first come over the whole world in the year 1624? We may here rightly say against the Rosicrucians and Nagelius: **most irrationally.** <note>original Latin: "irrationabiliter"</note>

<meta>This page continues the critique of apocalyptic calculations, specifically targeting the astronomer Paul Nagel (Nägelius) and the Rosicrucian movement. The author uses a quote from the Church Father Irenaeus to argue that even Christ did not claim to know the hour of the Last Judgment, thus framing contemporary attempts at calculation as arrogant and misguided.</meta>

Puffed up with pride, you say you know the unspeakable mysteries of God. Since even the Lord himself, the Son of God, acknowledged that only the Father knows the day and hour of judgment, saying clearly: "But concerning that day and hour no one knows, not even the Son, but the Father alone." If, therefore, the Son did not blush to defer the knowledge of that day to the Father, but spoke the truth, then we should not blush to leave those questions which are too great for us to God; for no one is above the master. <note>original Latin: "inflati, audaciter inenarrabilia Dei mysteria scire vos dicitis... Iren. Martyr. lib. 2. advers. hæres. c. 48." This is a citation from Irenaeus of Lyons, a 2nd-century theologian who argued against the Gnostics' claims of secret divine knowledge.</note>

But lest anyone suspect that Nagel's **art of calculation**—just like that of his predecessors—is false and incorrect, he justifies and proves it with these words: Regarding the timing of the **Last Day**, which Stifel and many others **shamefully** <note>original Latin/German: "turpiter"</note> attempted to calculate, it must be known that Stifel and all others who ever sought to calculate the Last Day were not without reason in error and missed the truth. This is because, at that time, the moment to calculate and reflect upon such great things had not yet arrived; and even more so because they did not understand the **Holy Scriptures**, knew nothing of the **three ages** <note>A theological concept, often divided into the Age of Nature, the Age of the Law, and the Age of Grace</note>, and could not calculate or reflect upon any prophetic or apocalyptic numbers, especially the **Number of the Beast** <note>The number 666 from the Book of Revelation, often used by reformers to identify the Antichrist</note>. But because the time is now at hand for these great and mighty things to be revealed, and because the three ages are now understood and the secret numbers—especially the Number of the Beast—have been weighed, just as the "three days" have been perfectly considered, these things can no longer remain hidden. Indeed, we have over 36 **demonstrations** <note>formal proofs</note> of these matters and intend to publish them very soon.

When we thoroughly weigh and consider these words, they are nothing but **nullities**, **conjectures**, and **begging the question** <note>original Latin: "petitiones principij," a logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premise</note>. They offer not the slightest demonstration as to why the **Rosicrucians** <note>a secretive and controversial group of thinkers in the early 17th century who claimed to possess hidden wisdom</note> and Nagel so impudently venture such a prophecy about the **Last Day**. For the very arguments brought forward here were also claimed and used by the previous "prophets of the year"; most of them understood the prophetic and apocalyptic numbers far better and could calculate the **Number of the Beast** more reliably than any Nagelian Rosicrucian ever could—and yet they still **erred** <note>original Latin: "allucinirt," meaning to be deluded or to wander from the truth</note> quite grossly.

<meta>This page continues the critique of apocalyptic calculations, specifically targeting the astronomer Paul Nagel and others who attempted to predict the end of the world. It builds on the argument that divine timing is beyond human reach, citing both the Bible and Martin Luther to emphasize that while the "signs of the times" are visible, the exact date is a secret known only to God.</meta>

...ended, and nothing resulted from their prophecy. For anyone who wishes to speak or present something certain regarding future events—like the prophets of the Old Testament—must speak from the mouth of the Lord. Otherwise, it comes to nothing. A human being does not know what is to happen or occur in the future; rather, the Lord our God alone knows all things and sees at what time each thing shall happen. He announces what is to come and reveals what is hidden; he understands every secret, and no matter is hidden from him, as stated in **Sirach 42**. <note>original: "Syr. 42." referring to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus), which discusses God's omniscience.</note> Just as He himself says in **Isaiah 46**: "I am God and there is no other; a God like whom there is none elsewhere, I who declare beforehand what shall come afterward, and from the beginning things that are not yet done, and say: my counsel stands, and I do all that pleases me." <note>original: "Es. 46."</note>

Following this, there is a great difference and a notable distinction **between confused and distinct knowledge**: <note>original Latin: "inter confusam & distinctam scientiam." This is a scholastic term distinguishing between a general, vague awareness (confused) and a specific, detailed understanding (distinct).</note> Even if one has an infallible calculation from the writings of the Prophets and Apostles and from the secret numbers showing that the **Last Day** is near at the door, and that the Lord will soon return for Judgment—even before a person might think—nevertheless, the time, the year, the day, and the hour of our Lord’s **Advent** <term>Advent: The "coming" or second arrival of Christ for the final judgment.</term> remains hidden and uncertain. As **Luther** also writes concerning the 12th chapter of **Daniel**: "Although this interpretation seems," he says, "as if one should be certain of the Last Day—which day or year He shall come—yet Christ forbids us from knowing this in **Acts 1:7**." <note>original: "Act. 1. 7." where Jesus tells the apostles, "It is not for you to know times or seasons that the Father has fixed by his own authority."</note>

And in the Gospel, it falls far short: First, even when the sacrifice of the Gospel is publicly abolished, <note>This likely refers to the "abomination of desolation" mentioned in Daniel, interpreted here as the suppression of true Christian worship.</note> no one will be able to note the year or day so precisely when it begins, since it cannot cease in all places on a single day. Secondly, even if one knew when it was to begin, there are still the **1,290** and the **1,335 days** <note>These are symbolic numbers from Daniel 12:11-12, often used by historical chronologers to calculate the end of the world.</note> set down, which no one in all the world would notice. And in short, I think that these 1,335 days will not be publicly understood by the common people until they are fulfilled on the **Last Day**. This would only be otherwise if God were perhaps to raise up a **Noah** who could calculate those same days and hit the mark with certainty. 

But as for me, I am satisfied that the **Last Day** must be at the door; for the signs which Christ announced, and the apostles Peter and Paul, have now almost all occurred. And "the trees are budding, the Scripture greens and blooms." <note>A metaphor for the "signs of the times" suggesting that just as budding trees signal summer, current events signal the end.</note> Whether we...

<summary>The author argues that while biblical signs indicate the Last Day is near, the exact date remains a divine mystery. Citing Martin Luther and the Book of Daniel, he cautions that human calculations of the apocalyptic '1290' and '1335' days are unreliable without direct divine revelation.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the argument from the previous page, which asserted that while God alone knows the future, humans often pridefully attempt to calculate the exact timing of the Apocalypse. It transitions into a specific critique of the Rosicrucians and the astronomer Paul Nagel, who predicted that Christ’s kingdom would begin in 1623.</meta>

...not being able to know the day so precisely—it does not matter; let another do it better; certainly, everything is at its end.

The **Rosicrucian prophets**—who dream and imagine for themselves a complete recognition and knowledge of many hidden and unequal things beyond all other men—should take good note of this. In addition, they should learn that we currently wander in a **land of forgetfulness and imperfection**, <note>original Latin: "in terrâ oblivionis & imperfectionis."</note> where we can hardly hit upon what is before our eyes and only with difficulty discover what is at hand (**Wisdom 9**). <note>original: "Sap. 9." referring to the Book of Wisdom, a deuterocanonical book often cited in early modern theology to emphasize human limitation.</note> Why would a person wantonly brood over and investigate what is beyond his understanding and ability, and which belongs to God alone? What Paul says in **1 Corinthians 13** remains true: **Our knowledge is patchwork, and our prophesying is patchwork; but when that which is perfect comes, then that which is patchwork shall cease.** <note>original: "1. Cor. 13." A foundational scripture for the author’s argument that human theology is inherently incomplete until the afterlife.</note>

Furthermore, the Lord Christ did not wish to reveal this to his disciples when they asked him when he would restore the kingdom of Israel; instead, he said to them: **It is not for you to know the times or the hour which the Father has reserved for his own power** (**Acts 1:7**). <note>original: "Act. 1. 7."</note> Since God does not want us to know the time of Christ’s arrival and return, it is not fitting for us to search for it. Those who inquisitively undertake this act against God and his Word. Therefore, it is better to wait for Christ every day with a repentant heart than to ask improperly about the Day of Christ; **for curiosity is not useful, but sobriety is most useful**, <note>original Latin: "non enim utilis est curiositas, sed utilissima est sobrietas." In this context, "sobriety" refers to intellectual modesty and spiritual self-control.</note> as **Cyril** also teaches in Book 11 of his commentary on John, Chapter 3: <note>original: "Cyrill. lehret lib. 11. in Joan. c. 3." Referring to Cyril of Alexandria, a 5th-century Church Father.</note> **He did not indicate for how much time he would remain there, or when he would descend again; therefore it is not for us to know the times or the moments which the Father has placed in his own power.** <note>original Latin translation: "Non significavit quamto tempore ibi manebit..."</note>

Christ explains this even more clearly in **Matthew 24** and **Mark 13**: **Heaven and earth shall pass away, but my words shall not pass away. But of that day and hour no one knows, not even the angels in heaven, nor the Son, but only the Father.** Since no human—not even the angels in heaven—can know when Christ will return, or when all this shall happen (that heaven and earth pass away), it is certainly an unwise and un-theological claim that Christ shall begin his kingdom on earth in the **year 1623**. **Nagel** <note>Referring to Paul Nagel (died 1624), a mathematician and astronomer who used "astrology-theology" to predict that the millennium would begin in 1623. His work was highly controversial among orthodox Lutherans.</note> might well keep in mind...
