<meta>This page continues the argument that the exact date of the end of the world is intentionally hidden by God. It specifically addresses the theological difficulty of Christ's statement in Mark 13:32 and Matthew 24:36, where He claims even the "Son" does not know the hour, using this to refute contemporary sectarian views.</meta>

...be; what Paul says: <unclear><note>original Greek: "μὴ ὑψηλοφρονεῖν ἀλλὰ φοβεῖσθαι διὰ τὸ συμφέρον"</note> not to be high-minded, but to fear for one's own benefit.</unclear> Therefore, Hilary also writes in his *Commentary on Matthew*, chapter 26: **Christ, the Lord of all times, has removed the burden of our anxiety by saying that that day is known to no one; it remains unknown not only to the Angels, but even to himself.** <note>Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD) was a prominent bishop and theologian who defended the doctrine of the Trinity.</note>

However, when Christ says that even the Son knows nothing of it, this cannot be interpreted as if He were not—along with the Father and the Holy Spirit—the true and only God who knows, sees, hears, and understands all things. Such a view would be the blasphemy of the **Arians** or **Photinians**. <note>The Arians and Photinians were early Christian groups who were declared heretics for denying that Jesus was fully and eternally God in the same way as the Father.</note> Nor should it be interpreted as if omniscience was never communicated to Christ according to His human nature because of the bond of personal union, as the **Calvinists** would have it. <note>The author is referencing a 17th-century theological dispute: Lutherans believed Christ's human nature shared in divine attributes like omniscience, while Calvinists argued these attributes belonged only to His divine nature.</note> Rather, it happened **not out of the weakness of ignorance, but as a deliberate dispensation of silence,** as Hilary says in Book 10 of *On the Trinity*. Because it was not useful or healthy for us humans to know such things, He did not wish to reveal it, as Basil writes in Book 4 *Against Eunomius*: **Since, therefore, it was not beneficial for men to hear the time of judgment, He withheld it; for a perpetual state of waiting makes people more fervent in their piety. Truly, the knowledge that a long time would intervene would have made them more negligent in their piety, as we would hope to be saved by a later repentance. For how could He, who knew everything concerning that hour by experience (for He spoke of those things), be ignorant of that hour? Otherwise, even the Apostle would have spoken in vain: "in whom are hidden all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge."** <note>The author quotes Basil the Great to argue that Christ's "ignorance" was a teaching tool to keep the faithful vigilant.</note> Therefore, this is not to be attributed to Christ's ignorance, but to His wisdom, as Ambrose teaches in Book 5 of *On the Faith*, chapter 8: **It was not ignorance, but wisdom.** See also Chrysostom’s 78th homily on Matthew 24; Hilary’s *On the Trinity*, book 9, page 189; Augustine’s letters 78 and 79; and his *On Genesis Against the Manichaeans*, book 1, chapter 22.

Furthermore, this is indicated through the parables which Christ and the Apostles use, such as in **Luke 21**: "Take heed to yourselves, lest your hearts be weighed down with carousing, drunkenness, and cares of this life, and that Day come upon you unexpectedly. For it will come as a snare on all those who dwell on the face of the whole earth. Watch therefore, and pray always that you may be counted worthy to escape all these things that will come to pass, and to stand before the Son of Man."

<summary>The author argues that Christ's apparent ignorance of the Day of Judgment was a deliberate "silence" intended to keep believers spiritually vigilant, rather than a lack of divine knowledge. He cites several Church Fathers to refute Arian and Calvinist interpretations of Christ's nature.</summary>

**Matthew 24.** Therefore watch, for you do not know what hour your Lord will come: but you should know this, that if a householder knew what hour the thief intended to come, he would certainly watch and not allow his house to be broken into: therefore, be you also ready, for the Son of Man will come at an hour when you do not expect Him.

**1 Thessalonians 5.** But concerning the times and the hours, dear brothers, there is no need to write to you, for you yourselves know certainly that the Day of the Lord will come like a thief in the night: for when they say, "there is peace, there is no danger," then destruction will suddenly overtake them, just as labor pains come upon a pregnant woman, and they shall not escape.

**2 Peter 3.** The Day of the Lord will come as a thief in the night, in which the heavens will pass away with a great crash, but the elements will melt from the heat, and the earth and the works that are within it will be burned up.

**Revelation 3.** If you do not watch, I will come upon you like a thief, and you will not know what hour I will come upon you.

From these cited and mentioned parables, it is clear and manifest that the **Last Day** <term>Jüngster Tag: literally "The Youngest Day," a traditional German term for the Day of Judgment at the end of time.</term> will not only come quickly and swiftly, but also at an uncertain and unexpected time and hour, before any human thinks of it. For just as a poor little bird flies along in total security, creeping around on the bird-trap <note>The original "Vogelherd" refers to a patch of ground specifically prepared by a hunter to lure birds into nets.</note> seeking its food, and does not think that it is about to be trapped and ensnared at that moment; and just as a householder rests and sleeps securely, and does not know that a thief will break in during the night—otherwise he would watch and not allow it (Luke 12)—so also will be the coming of the Son of Man. 

When people are living most securely and saying with the wicked servant, "O, my Lord will not be coming for a long time yet" (Luke 12, Matthew 24), then destruction will quickly overtake them, like lightning flashes from heaven above and shines over everything under the sky; so will the Son of Man be in His day (Luke 17). **Cyprian** <note>St. Cyprian of Carthage (c. 210–258 AD) was an early Christian bishop whose writings were highly influential in the development of Western theology.</note> also teaches regarding this in Book 3 of *Testimonies to Quirinus*, Section 89, and says: **The end of the world comes suddenly, says the Apostle: the day of the Lord is like a thief in the night.** <note>original Latin: "Subitô venire finem mundi, inquit Apostolus: dies Domini sicut fur in nocte"</note>

But such complacency <term>securitet: from the Latin 'securitas,' meaning a false sense of security or spiritual apathy.</term> and certainty is now far too common among men; it is just as it was before the Great Flood <term>Sündfluth: literally "the sin-flood," the traditional German name for the biblical Deluge.</term>, of which Christ speaks in **Matthew 24**: "As it was in the days of Noah, so shall also the coming of the Son of Man be. All kinds of wickedness, all kinds of sin and vice, war and rumors of war, oppression of the poor, contempt for God’s Word and His preachers, times of scarcity, distress, and hardship are in full swing. For we know that He will come soon, but at what hour it shall happen is hidden from us (**Matthew 24**). Therefore, "let your loins be girded and your lights burning, and be like men who wait for their Lord when he will return from the wedding, so that when he comes and knocks, they may open to him immediately. Blessed are those servants whom the Lord, when he comes, finds watching. Truly I say to you, he will gird himself and have them sit at the table, and will go about and serve them. And if he comes in the second or third watch and finds it so, blessed are those servants." (**Luke 12**).

I prepare myself for this, so that whether I sleep, wake, study, eat, or drink, whenever the **Last Day** comes, it cannot come too suddenly for me.

However, why God does not wish for us to know the day and hour of His coming is well known and established from God's Word: For if a man knew the exact time of the **Last Day**, and when he was to die, he would live his life in all kinds of complacency <term>sicherheit: from the Latin 'securitas,' meaning a false sense of spiritual safety or apathy</term>. As **Basil** speaks of this in his fourth book against Eunomius, on page 50: he would wallow most horribly in the filth and mire of every kind of vice—which already happens more than enough—and postpone and save his repentance <term>Busse: the act of penance or turning back to God</term> until the hour of death. To prevent this from happening, the Last Day is hidden from us and the hour of death is uncertain, so that we might await the Lord at all times and be ready when He comes. As **Augustine** says in Epistle 80: "He warns not so that the good servant may know the end of times, but so that he remains vigilant in good work at all times, because he does not know the end of times." <note>original Latin: "Monet non ut bonus servus cognoscat temporum finem, sed ut omni tempore in opere bono vigilet, quia nescit temporum finem."</note> No one should think that they still have a long time to live, or that the Lord will not come so soon; rather, one should regard every single day as the Last Day, so that when He is revealed, one may have joy and not be put to shame (**1 John 2**). Like the **Five Wise Virgins**, we should always have oil in our lamps (**Matthew 25**). And with **Paul**, we should carry out a good warfare, keeping faith and a good conscience, so that on that day the crown of righteousness may be laid aside for us—and not for us alone, but for all those who love His appearing (**2 Timothy 4**). **Hilary** also speaks very finely on this in his commentary on Matthew, chapter 26: "Therefore, the day is placed beyond any fixed date, so that while He grants us a generous time for repentance, the uncertain boundary might keep us always watchful; and by speaking of it to no one, He limits His will of granting this day by no definition of His word, because, like the time of the flood, that great day will arrive in the very course of our life while we are all busy and repenting." <note>original Latin: "Ideò extra definitionem dies posita est, ut largum licet socius pænitentiæ nobis tempus indulgens, incerti tamen meta semper nos sollicitos detineret, & ipse nulli loquendo, voluntatem dandi hujus diei nulla dicti sui definitione cohiberet, quià secundum diluvij tempus in ipso vitæ nostræ cursu omnia agentibus ac pænitentibus magnus ille dies aderit."</note>

Furthermore, it is neither necessary nor useful for our salvation <term>seeligkeits: spiritual blessedness or the state of being saved</term> to know the time of Christ’s coming. If it were, God would have revealed it, just as the Lord has not kept anything else secret, but has revealed everything in the Scriptures abundantly and in detail that is necessary and proper for our faith and salvation. ...that those who go through the valley of sorrow <note>German: *Jammerthal*; often referring to the "Valley of Weeping" or "Valley of Baca" from Psalm 84</note> shall be adorned with much blessing, **Psalm 84**. If we did not know that after this life another and better life follows, and that we shall rise again on the Last Day <term>Jüngster tag: literally "the newest day," meaning the Day of Judgment</term> and enter into eternal glory—where it shall go eternally well for the godly, but eternally ill for the wicked—where the righteous shall have eternal joy and bliss, and the damned eternal torment and plague: for this reason, some of the ancient teachers argue and conclude that the Last Day must certainly come, so that the justice of God may be executed and fulfilled by the most just and most equitable distribution of rewards and punishments <note>original Latin: *justissima ac æquissima præmiorum ac pœnarum distributione*</note>. In this regard, the words of Zeno of Verona are worth noting: **"Whoever denies the resurrection condemns his own life. Cur enim mereatur felicitatem futuri temporis cernere, quem videas sacrilega impietate Dei potentiæ derogare?*</note> See Ambrose on the *Faith of the Resurrection*; Tertullian on the *Resurrection of the Flesh*, chapter 13; Chrysologus, sermon 118.

But now we know that the Lord Christ will soon come and take us to Himself, so that we may be where He is, where eternal joy hovers over our heads, and we shall have a purely lovely and laudable existence: eternal health and healthy eternity, secure tranquility and tranquil security, joyful happiness and happy joyfulness <note>original Latin: *æternam sanitatem & sanam æternitatem, securam tranquillitatem, & tranquillam securitatem, jocundam felicitatem, & felicem jocunditatem*</note>. Prosper of Aquitaine, *On the Contemplative Life*. **"Where youth does not grow old, life knows no end, beauty never fades, love never cools, health never withers, joy never decreases; where pain is never felt, groaning is not heard, nothing sad is seen, gladness is always maintained, evil is never feared, because there the highest good is possessed,"** <note>original Latin: *Ubi juventus non senescit, vita terminum nescit, decor nunquam palescit, amor numqum tepescit, sanitas numquam marcescit, gaudium numquam decrescit, ubi dolor numquam sentitur, gemitus non auditur, triste nil videtur, lætitia semper habetur, malum numquam timetur, quoniam ibi summum bonum possidetur*</note> Augustine on Psalm 148 and in his *Soliloquies*, chapter 35.

Therefore, we long with yearning—just as the prophets did for the first, so we for the last <term>Advent: from the Latin *adventus*, meaning "arrival" or "coming"</term> and appearing of Christ—and say with Isaiah, Chapter 64: "Oh, that You would rend the heavens and come down, that the mountains might melt before You, as hot water boils away from a fierce fire." Just as the simple and mute <note>German: *Dummen vnd Stummen*; refers to animals and the natural world as "irrational" creatures</note> creatures shine before us with their little sighs; they were subjected to vanity against their will, for they shall also be set free from the service of perishable existence into the glorious freedom of the children of God. Therefore they yearn with us, and are ever anxious...

<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the signs of the end times, moving from the general necessity of a final judgment to the specific biblical prophecies concerning the current era's spiritual turmoil and the proper role of astronomy in understanding these signs.</meta>

...therefrom; **Romans 8**. And indeed, we may rightly pray in these last days for a blessed end and for the beloved Last Day <term>Jüngster Tag: literally "the newest day," referring to the Day of Judgment</term>, because it is precisely the time in which we now live, of which John prophesied in **Revelation 20**: That Satan is loosed from his prison and goes out to deceive the heathens in the four corners of the earth, Gog and Magog, to gather them for a battle, whose number is as the sand of the sea. He is loosed and causes a mighty uproar, such that John also laments and says in **Revelation 12**: "Woe to those who dwell on the earth and on the sea, for the devil has come down to you and has a great wrath, and knows that he has little time." The closer the end comes, the more he rages and storms, as Isidore also writes in the first book of *The Supreme Good*, chapter 28: "The shorter the time the devil sees remaining for himself until he is condemned, the more he is moved with the great wrath of persecution—God’s justice permitting it—so that the chosen ones may be glorified, the wicked may be defiled, and a harsher sentence of damnation may burn against the devil." <note>original Latin: *Quanto breve tempus videt sibi restare diabolus ut damnetur, tantò in magna persecutionis ira movetur, divina justitia permittente, ut glorificentur electi, sordidentur iniqui, & ut diabolo durior exescat damnationis sententia.*</note>

But that such a troubled time would exist in these last days—as is already, alas, before our eyes—cannot be studied from the astronomical art alone, nor learned from annual prognostications <term>Prognostica: yearly almanacs or astrological predictions</term>. Rather, the Word of God prognosticates and prophesies all these things. Nevertheless, astronomy must not be rejected for this reason, provided that one does not force a sense of "necessity" from it, as did the Stoics and the Priscillianists <note>The Stoics believed in a fixed fate, while Priscillianists were a 4th-century sect often accused of combining Christian belief with fatalistic astrology</note>. Hence, Philastrius (in *Heresy 72*) reckons the "mathematicians" <note>In the medieval and early modern period, "mathematician" often referred to astrologers who calculated horoscopes</note> among the heretics; Lactantius (in book 2, chapter 17) calls such astrology "an invention of demons"; and Augustine says in *Questions on the Old and New Testaments*, question 115: "Nothing is so contrary to Christians as if they should apply care to the art of mathematics [astrology], for this is recognized as an enemy to the law of God." <note>original Latin: *Nihil tam contra Christianos quam si arti Mathesios adhibeant curam, hæc enim inimica dignoscitur legi Dei*</note> Scripture also speaks of this, saying that one should not be afraid of the signs of the heavens: **Jeremiah 10:2, Deuteronomy 4:19, Leviticus 19:31, Isaiah 44:25, and 47:13**.

However, if an astronomer merely indicates what the stars "incline" toward, outside the web of necessity <note>original Latin: *extra necessitatis telam*</note>—for as Ptolemy himself says in the *Centiloquium*: "A wise man rules the stars" <note>original Latin: *sapiens enim homo, ait ipse Ptolomæus in centiloquio, dominatur astris*</note>—and thereafter seeks further counsel from the Holy Scripture, holding the two great books—the upper and the lower, the Book of Nature and the Book of Scripture—faithfully against one another, then he can often prognosticate great things. This is especially true in these last days, which occur so shortly before the Last Day, and in such cases, astrology is not reprehensible, which...

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed Paul Nagel’s apocalyptic predictions regarding the "third day" and the 1260-day prophecy, arguing that human attempts to calculate the exact timing of the Last Day are mere conjecture. This page continues that critique by comparing various theological and historical interpretations of these prophetic numbers.</meta>

...various meanings, according to the opinion of many **theologians** and **historians**. For every month among the Greeks has 30 days, according to Joseph Scaliger in his work *On the Emendation of Time* <note>original Latin: "secundum Joseph. Scaliger. de emendat. Tempor." Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609) was a famous scholar who laid the foundations for modern scientific chronology.</note>. Now, if "a time" signifies 12 months, "two times" signifies 24 months, and "half a time" signifies 6 months, then there are precisely 42 months, which by this calculation certainly make 1260 days. These days, however, are explained and interpreted in different and conflicting ways. The Papists <note>A common Reformation-era term for Roman Catholics.</note> understand them as ordinary months and days, and from this "one time, two times, and half a time," they make only three and a half years; concerning this, they fabricate a story that the **Anti-Christ** shall tyrannize in Jerusalem for such a time and then immediately be killed. See Dionysius the Carthusian in his *Commentary on Daniel*, page 412 <note>original Latin: "Dionys. Carthusian. Comm. in Daniel. fol. 412."</note>; Bellarmine in *On the Roman Pontiff*, book 3, chapter 3 and following <note>original Latin: "Bellarm. lib. 3. de Rom. Pontif. c. 3. & seqq." Robert Bellarmine was a cardinal and a major figure in the Counter-Reformation.</note>; Gregory de Valentia, Ruard Tapperus, Pistorius, and other such Papists. 

But because St. Paul clearly writes in 2 Thessalonians 2 that the wickedness of the Anti-Christ was already stirring in his own time, though secretly, and would remain until Christ brings it to an end through the appearance of His coming, we understand the 1260 days as <term>Angelic</term> <note>original: "Englischen"; in this context, it refers to "prophetic" or "heavenly" years where a day represents a year.</note> or heavenly years—each day for a year—just as the Spirit of God Himself gives us such an interpretation in Ezekiel 4:6. Thus, "a time, two times, and half a time," as well as the 42 months and 1260 days, are nothing other than 1260 years.

However, as to when and at what time those 1260 years are supposed to have begun, we find differing opinions among theologians and historians. Ambrose, in Volume 5, page 215 <note>original: "Ambrosius Tom. 5. fol. 215." Referring to St. Ambrose, the 4th-century Bishop of Milan.</note>, understands it as the entire time from Christ’s passion until the Last Day. Some begin the count from the year of Christ 412, when the Goths conquered the mighty city of Rome and terribly destroyed and ravaged the ancient, laudable state of the Roman Empire. By this I mean that one should indeed research them, for they grant us great understanding, and believe with certainty that the **Last Day** will soon come; but when they expire, and at what time and hour the Day of the Lord will appear, that should be left to God. ...renewed such a damned error: So too Zwingli is almost of the same opinion, as he himself writes in *Letters to Myconius*, Volume 1, page 23: "The substance of the fugitive <note>original Latin: *refugorum*, referring here to the fallen angels</note> (angels) is simple and, as far as their essence is concerned, good; yet their conduct is wicked, though not equally for all, since they did not all sin with the same degree of affection, but some more vehemently and others more lightly; thus Origen <note>An early Christian theologian (c. 185–253 AD) whose views on the eventual salvation of all beings, including the devil, were later condemned as heretical.</note> seems to think not without reason that some of the angels will be restored to grace at the future judgment."

It is, however, unnecessary to refute this monstrous teaching and paradox at great length <note>original Latin: *prolixè*</note>, as Holy Scripture sufficiently proves and shows that Christ died for us humans alone, and not for the angels, neither good nor bad. For the good angels were not in need of such a Mediator—though the Calvinists write of this affirmatively <note>In this context, meaning they argue in favor of Christ being a mediator for good angels to ensure their stability.</note> in a most absurd manner, as seen in Szegedi <note>Stephanus Szegedi Kis, a Hungarian Reformed theologian.</note>, *Commonplaces*, page 82; Lambert Daneau's commentary on Augustine's *Enchiridion*, chapter 28; Franciscus Junius, *Theological Works*, Volume 2; and others, including Ruard Tapper, Volume 1, page 49, who is of the opinion "that it was also necessary for the angels to be born again" <note>original Latin: *quod etiam angelis necessarium fuerit renasci*</note>. This is false because they remained steadfast and did not fall away from GOD their Creator. 

The evil angels, however—or the devils—who did not stand in the truth (John 8) but turned away from God and abandoned their principality (Jude 6), were cast out of heaven (Revelation 12) and are kept for the judgment of the great day in eternal chains in darkness (2 Peter 2, Jude 6). Furthermore, Christ did not take upon Himself the "seed of angels" <note>Though the text cites Hebrews 1, the reference is to Hebrews 2:16: "For surely it is not angels he helps, but Abraham’s descendants."</note>, but rather took our name and seed, our human nature; therefore, He did not wish to, nor was He meant to, suffer for the angels, nor to do penance and pay for the devil's sin. As the Anhaltinians say in their argument against the *Formula of Concord*: "For that which He did not assume, He did not heal" <note>original Latin: *quod enim non adsumpsit non sanavit*. This is a famous theological principle: if Christ did not take on a specific nature, that nature cannot be saved by His sacrifice.</note>. 

Whosoever’s nature and sin the Lord Christ did not take upon Himself, and for whom He did not pay the blood-red <term>ransom</term> <note>original: *Lösegroschen*, literally a "redemption penny." In this period, it was a common way to describe the price Christ paid to "buy back" humanity from sin.</note>, they can never become children of light and co-heirs with Christ. James says in Chapter 2, "the devil also believes and trembles." This should be understood intellectually <note>original Latin: *historicè*; referring to 'historical faith' or mere mental assent to facts without trust in God's grace</note>, for he knows that God is our gracious Father and Christ our faithful Savior and Redeemer; yet he can take no comfort in this whatsoever, but must remain eternally damned and banished.

Finally, all the Prophetic and Apostolic writings testify that Christ, through His actions and His suffering <note>original Latin: *faciendo & patiendo*</note>, suffered and made atonement for all people, even for the ungodly (1 Corinthians 8:11, 2 Peter 2:1, 1 John 2). This is despite the fact that the Sacramentarians <note>A term used by Lutherans to describe Reformed or Calvinist theologians who held differing views on the nature of the Lord's Supper and predestination.</note> once again teach the opposite (Spindler in his *Report*, page 263; Zanchius, Volume 2, *Miscellaneous Works*, page 117 and 656; Rennecherus, *Golden Salt Catechism*, chapter 22; Piscator, Volume 2, *Theological Disputations*, pages 129–130; Conrad Vorstius, *Tessaradecas*, part 2, page 18; and the Heidelberg *Golden Ladder*, page 45). Because, however, not everyone believes in Christ—instead, the majority trample underfoot such a precious merit of the LORD—the Judge of all the world will, on the final day of reward and crowning, make known and manifest before all creatures the blessed and the cursed, the believers and the unbelievers, through His "Depart" and "Come" <note>original Latin: *ite* and *venite*, referring to the words of judgment in Matthew 25: "Come, you blessed" and "Depart from me, you cursed."</note>.

He will reject the ungodly and disobedient from Himself and cast them into the abyss of Hell, *where there will be death without dying, an end without ending, and failure without failing; for death lives on, the end is always beginning, and failure does not know how to fail, according to Rabanus Maurus <note>A 9th-century Benedictine monk and influential theologian.</note> in "On the Manner of Penance."* But after the "stinking goats" <note>A biblical metaphor for the wicked who are separated from the "sheep" (the righteous) at the Last Judgment.</note> have been damned by Christ the Judge and sentenced to the fire, there will be no more redemption; instead, they will bear their eternal shame and brand, and be as hideous and ghastly as the evil spirits themselves, so that "all flesh" will have more than enough to look upon (Isaiah 66). For it is an eternal fire (Matthew 25, Luke 3); the smoke of their torment rises from eternity to eternity, and they have no rest day or night (Revelation 14), just as Prosper of Aquitaine <note>A 5th-century Christian writer and defender of St. Augustine's teachings.</note> teaches in Book 3 of *The Contemplative Life*: "It twists but does not wrench away; it punishes but does not end."

Therefore, we not unjustly condemn and banish such a monstrous heresy <note>Specifically the doctrine of universalism, which suggests that hell is temporary.</note>, and say with the Fifth Council of Constantinople, Article 9: "If anyone says or believes that the torments of demons and ungodly men are temporary, and that they will have an end at some time, or that there will be a restoration of demons or ungodly men, let him be accursed <note>original Latin: *anathema sit*; a formal ecclesiastical condemnation.</note>."

Afterward <note>original German catchword: *Darnach*</note>

<summary>This page argues that while Christ's sacrifice was sufficient for all, the damned and the devils are excluded from salvation due to their unbelief. It emphasizes the eternity of Hell's torments, citing both scripture and historical theologians to condemn the idea that such punishment could ever end.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the refutation of theological errors regarding the end of the world. The previous page concluded with a discussion of Christ's final judgment, where He will separate the believers from the unbelievers with the words "Depart" and "Come." This page transitions to refuting "Chiliasm" or Millenarianism—the belief in a literal 1,000-year earthly kingdom.</meta>

->F iij<-

Afterwards, certain heretics appeared who dreamed that Christ, in His majestic return, would not so quickly and immediately bring this old and cold world to its final end. Instead, they believed He would first rule for a thousand years with the righteous and the chosen in this world, maintaining them in great power and splendor. They imagined they would be filled—in a truly Epicurean fashion <note>The author uses "Epicurean" as a pejorative to describe a focus on physical pleasure and luxury, such as fine dining, rather than spiritual joys.</note>—with exquisite dishes, sweet drinks, and hearty food, and be showered and delighted with every kind of lust and worldly joy, as Philastrius describes it. Such dreamers and heretics are called **Chiliasts** <note>original Latin: *Chiliastæ*; from the Greek word *chilia*, meaning one thousand.</note>, as Augustine teaches in *The City of God*, Book 20, Chapter 7. The name comes from *chilia*, which signifies a thousand; in Latin, they are called **Millenarians** <note>original Latin: *Millenarij*</note>, "Those of the Thousand," or "Soldiers of the Thousand" <note>original Latin: *Milliarij vel Miliciastæ*</note>, according to Isidore’s *Etymologies*, Book 8, Chapter 5, page 38.

As for the origin and beginning of this heresy, we find thorough accounts in the writings of Eusebius, Augustine, and Dionysius. For soon after the time of the Apostles, **Cerinthus**—who was a contemporary of the Blessed John the Evangelist, according to Irenaeus in *Against Heresies*, Book 3, Chapter 3—absorbed and spread such error, as Nicephorus writes of him in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 3, Chapter 14. Following him were **Papias** (who was a student of the Blessed John and a leader of the church at Hierapolis), **Nepos**, and **Coracion** (Eusebius, Book 7, Chapter 24; Nicephorus, Book 6, Chapter 21). 

Afterward, **Apollinarius**, **Victorinus of Pettau**, and even several of the Church Fathers immersed themselves in this error: **Justinus Martyr**, **Irenaeus**, **Tertullian**, and **Lactantius**. They were of the firm opinion that Christ should rule and govern in this world for 1,000 years, about which Lactantius writes in Book 7, Chapter 24:

> "Then those who are living in their bodies shall not die; but during those same 1,000 years they shall produce an infinite multitude, and their offspring shall be holy and dear to God. Around that same time, the prince of demons, who is the architect of all evils, shall be bound with chains and shall be in custody for the thousand years of the heavenly empire, during which justice shall reign in the world; so that he may not plot any evil against the people of God. However, when the thousand years of the golden age have passed, the devil shall be released, and the nations shall besiege the holy city. But God shall finally defeat the ungodly and hand them over to eternal punishments, and shall transfer the chosen to the glory of the Angels after the resurrection." <note>original Latin: *tunc qui erunt in corporibus vivi non morientur; sed per eofdem 1000. annos infinitam multitudinem generabunt...*</note>

Although such heresy was opposed early on by Dionysius,

The author identifies its origins with Cerinthus and notes that even respected Church Fathers like Irenaeus and Lactantius were influenced by this "error."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the refutation of Chiliasm (Millenarianism), specifically naming Philastrius, Jerome, and Augustine as historical opponents of the idea that Christ will establish a physical, 1,000-year earthly kingdom of sensory pleasure.</meta>

Philastrius, Jerome, and Augustine, and refuted and thwarted them with irrefutable arguments. Nevertheless, the Hellish master of a thousand arts <note>A traditional epithet for the Devil, suggesting his many deceptions.</note> has renewed this same fable through the **Anabaptists** <term>Anabaptists: A radical Reformation-era movement that often held apocalyptic and communalist views</term> in these latter days, and has even now incited such a **Chiliast**, namely Master **Paul Nagel** <note>Paul Nagel (d. 1624) was a German mathematician and astronomer who predicted a "Blessed Third Age" starting in 1624.</note>. He likewise dreams up for himself such a fleshly kingdom of heaven on earth and claims that the Scripture is "sealed" to all those who only admit the first and second ages and reject the "Third Blessed Age" <note>original: *seculum Benedictum*</note> (*Prognosticon*, letter D. iv.).

Because, however, this Chiliastic fantasy runs diametrically opposed and contrary to Holy Scripture, and is more Turkish <note>At this time, "Turkish" was often used as a synonym for Islamic beliefs, here referencing sensory descriptions of paradise.</note> than Christian, as it says in the treatise *Aquicel.*: *In paradise there will be rivers of wine, and they will be delightful to those who drink, and rivers of strained honey, and we shall abound there in all fruits.* Likewise, in the treatise *Murtileth*: *Those who fear God will be in shadows and by springs, and they will have the fruits they desire, and it will be said to them: eat and drink without disturbance, according to what you have done.*

And although such things are quite comfortable and pleasing to "world-tasting hearts" <note>i.e., those who crave worldly or material pleasures.</note>, I consider it necessary to briefly report that no such worldly kingdom is to be hoped for at the return of Christ, just as the Lord Himself teaches in John 18 and says: "My kingdom is not of this world; if my kingdom were of this world, my servants would fight for it, so that I would not be handed over to the Jews." Just as Christ's kingdom is "not of this world" <note>original Latin: *de hoc mundo*</note>, so also the believers, who are "in the world" <note>original Latin: *qui sunt in mundo*</note>, should not be "of the world" <note>original Latin: *de mundo*</note>, nor should they gawk and mimic after such a kingdom "which will be in this world" <note>original Latin: *quod erit in hoc mundo*</note>, as Augustine writes of it in Tractate 115 on John: *Come to the kingdom which is not of this world; come by believing, and do not be fierce by fearing.*

Indeed, if all this were not reported in detail to simple people, they could very easily be taken in, because what Nagel and other Chiliasts write with ecstatic words goes down quite "sweetly." In his *Prognosticon* (letter C. ii.), he says: "In the third blessed age <note>original Latin: *tertio benedicto seculo*</note>, we will be filled with all kinds of divine, angelic, and heavenly wisdom, and see and recognize the secrets of the entire Holy Scripture and of Nature with open eyes, when on Mount Zion the veil or covering shall be taken away from our faces: For the Lamb..."

<meta>This page continues the argument against the physical kingdom of the Chiliasts, moving from the refutation of "Turkish" (sensual) paradises to a scriptural definition of Christ’s kingdom as spiritual and eternal, specifically targeting the views of John Calvin and the Chiliastic "Three Ages" theory.</meta>

Christ, at this wedding of his and great **Supper**, will heartily and excellently treat his called and invited guests—those clothed in the proper wedding garment—satisfying and filling them with his wine of joy and heavenly hidden manna. Indeed, he will heartily adorn his Bride, and the journey home will soon follow. Here, still in this world, the wedding and the **Supper** will be held; after that, the wedding will move into the eternal Fatherland.

However, such a Chiliastic tale and fiction is rejected in detail, not only by Christ in John 18, but also in the entire Holy Scripture of the Old and New Testaments. Conversely, it is taught by all prophets and apostles that Christ is no such king from whom one should seek and find physical food and pasture, or the filling and stuffing of the belly here on earth upon his return. Instead, he is the Prince of Life and the Duke of Palestine, whom the Heavenly Father himself has enthroned, and to whom he has entrusted all majesty and dominion in **Zion** (Psalm 2); that he shall lead his rule in this world with the straight **scepter** of his Word and with the sword of his mouth (Psalm 45), in the midst of his enemies (Psalm 110). 

At his return, however, he will hand over the kingdom to God and the Father (1 Corinthians 15). This does not mean he will cease to rule—as **Calvin** quite blasphemously writes in book 2 of the *Institutes*, chapter 14, section 3—for he has an eternal kingdom and an eternal power that shall not be taken from him (Daniel 7). Rather, it means he will bring home and present his fellow citizens of the kingdom, or his people, to the Father, and that the <term>method of administration <note>original: *modus administrationis*</note></term> will no longer be or happen <term>through the spoken and visible word <note>original: *per verbum vocale & visibile*</note></term>—that is, through the Word and **Sacrament**—nor in the midst of enemies. Instead, he will then rule <term>without a medium <note>original: *absque medio*; meaning without the need for earthly tools like preaching or rituals</note></term> from eternity to eternity, and will himself be "all in all" (1 Corinthians 15).

Now, the Chiliasts indeed wish to gloss over such undeniable scriptural passages regarding the spiritual Kingdom of Christ with a made-up distinction, as if they were only to be understood as concerning the first and second times, or the first and second <term>ages <note>original: *seculo*</note></term>. They claim that in the "third time" and "third age," something quite different will happen and occur. However, this is a <term>mere begging of the question <note>original: *mera petitio principij*; a logical fallacy where the premise of an argument assumes the truth of the conclusion</note></term>, for it must first be proven whether such a Chilias- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing with the word "Chiliasmus" (Chiliasm) on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument against Chiliasm (Millenarianism), asserting that there is no scriptural basis for a literal thousand-year earthly kingdom. Regarding the <term>Chiliastic age <note>Referring to Millenarianism, the belief in a literal 1,000-year reign of Christ on earth before the final judgment.</note></term> and time that is said to come / there is a <note>original: "altum silentium"</note> deep silence in the Scripture, and it is not mentioned or touched upon by even the smallest letter by either the Prophets or the Apostles / therefore we rightly consider it a human poem and a fantastical work of fables.

Furthermore and secondly / God's Word teaches us / that Christ will not return <note>original: "visibiliter"</note> visibly before the Last Day / but as soon as we see him coming in the clouds of heaven with great power and glory / then the Last Judgment will be held / and all the world will be judged / Matthew 25. Therefore it is also called the "Youngest" and Last Day / because after it, no other human day is to be expected: just as the <term>Church Fathers <note>The influential early Christian theologians of the first few centuries.</note></term> themselves acknowledge / even those who held such Chiliastic opinions / such as <term>Justin Martyr <note>An early Christian apologist (c. 100–165 AD) who did indeed hold millennial views, though the author here enlists him to support the idea of only two Advents.</note></term> in his *Second Apology for Christians*, page 68, and <term>Irenaeus <note>A Greek bishop (c. 130–202 AD) known for his role in guiding and expanding Christian communities.</note></term> in *Against Heresies*, book 4, chapter 56 <note>original: "advers. hæres. lib. 4. c. 56."</note> / that the visible <note>original: "Advent Christi"</note> Coming of Christ shall only happen twice / once when he came in great humility and lowliness to redeem the human race / and the second time / when he will come in great glory to judge the human race.

Thirdly / not only does Daniel prophesy of the little horn / that is, of the <term>Turks <note>In the 16th and 17th centuries, many Protestant reformers interpreted the "Little Horn" of Daniel and the Antichrist as the Ottoman Empire (the "Turks").</note></term> / that it shall remain / until the Last Day / but all other Prophets and Apostles / yes, Christ himself teaches / that the sorrowful state of right-believing Christians in this world will not cease / but remain until the Last Day / as clearly stands in Daniel 7: "While I was looking at the horns / behold / there broke forth between them another little horn / before which three of the first horns were plucked out / and behold this same horn had eyes / like human eyes / and a mouth that speaks great things / this I saw until the thrones were set / and the <term>Ancient of Days <note>A title for God the Father in the Book of Daniel.</note></term> took his seat / whose clothing was snow white / and the hair on his head / like pure wool / his throne was like fiery flames / and its wheels burned with fire / and from the same went out a long fiery stream / thousand times thousands served him / and ten hundred times thousand stood before him / the Judgment was held / and the books were opened." Thus we also read / Daniel 12: "At that same time the great Prince Michael / who stands for your people / <note>The sentence breaks off here as the page ends; the text continues onto the next page.</note>

...to arise; for there will be such a time of trouble as has not been since there were people until that same time. At that time your people will be delivered, everyone who is found written in the book, and many of those who sleep under the earth shall awake, some to everlasting life, and some to everlasting shame and contempt. Similarly, Christ proclaims in Luke 21: people will faint from fear and from anticipation of the things that are coming upon the earth; for even the powers of the heavens will be shaken, and then they will see the Son of Man coming in the clouds with great power and glory. But when these things begin to happen, look up and lift up your heads, because your redemption draws near. 

Since this state of sorrow for pious Christians shall not be ended or turned away before the <term>Last Day <note>The Day of Judgment; the author argues that the "Youngest Day" is the final event of history, leaving no room for a later 1,000-year kingdom.</note></term>, we who belong to Christ must all sigh and coo like a poor, wretched turtledove, and we carry our lives like sheep among wolves, and like a rose among thorns, we must allow ourselves to be stung and broken by the world and hell (Song of Solomon 2, Matthew 10). 

So it is certainly a dreamt-up, unscriptural poem that <term>Nagelius <note>Paul Nagel (d. 1624), a German mathematician and astronomer who predicted a "golden age" or millennium starting in 1624, which the author of this text strongly opposes.</note></term> writes concerning the 1,000 years, in which the elect are said to live in joy and pleasure and rule over the world with Christ. Let no one imagine a constant joy as long as this visible world stands, for the rule for all of us is: whoever wants to live a godly life in Christ Jesus must suffer persecution (2 Timothy 3). As <term>Sirach <note>Referring to the Book of Sirach, also known as Ecclesiasticus, a book of wisdom found in the Apocrypha.</note></term> also teaches in chapter 2: My child, if you wish to be a servant of God, prepare yourself for temptation. Hold fast and endure, and do not waver when you are enticed away. Cling to God and do not depart, so that you may grow ever stronger. Whatever happens to you, endure it and be patient in all kinds of tribulation. For just as gold is tested by fire, so those who please God are proven through the fire of tribulation. 

We are all called to this: if we wish to rule and inherit with Christ, we must also suffer and die with Him here, as <term>Tertullian <note>Quintus Septimius Florens Tertullianus (c. 155–220 AD), an early and prolific Christian author from Roman Carthage.</note></term> writes in his *Book to the Martyrs*: <note>original Latin: "vocati sumus ad militiam Dei vivi, jam tunc cum in Sacramenti verba respondimus"</note> "We were called to the military service of the living God at the very moment when we responded to the words of the sacramental oath." Regarding this, <term>Gregory <note>Pope Gregory I, known as Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD), a doctor of the Church.</note></term> also writes very laudably and well in book 31 on Job, chapter 12: <note>original Latin: "Nec boni sine malis, nec mali sine bonis vivere possunt, utraq; pars sibi necessario"</note> "Neither can the good live without the evil, nor the evil without the good; each side is necessarily [a benefit] to the other..."

<meta>This page continues the refutation of Paul Nagel’s millenarian theories. ...it is fitting that the good be purified through the tempering of evils, and the evil be changed by the examples of the good. <note>original Latin: "congruat, ut boni per temperamenta malorum purgentur, & mali per exempla bonorum mutentur."</note>

**Fourthly**, the Apostle Paul rejects the opinion of all those who wish to live a <term>voluptuous <note>Referring to a life of physical pleasure and luxury, which the author argues is contrary to the spiritual nature of Christ's kingdom.</note></term> life with Christ in His kingdom, and who would treat and overfill themselves with grand meals; for the Kingdom of God, he says in Romans 14, is not eating and drinking, but righteousness and peace, and joy in the Holy Spirit. Whoever serves Christ in this way is pleasing to God and worthy to men. On this point, **Ambrose** <note>St. Ambrose (c. 339–397), a foundational theologian and Bishop of Milan.</note> writes in Volume 3, page 100: *It is not food and drink that is promised to us as a reward and honor, but the participation in heavenly grace and life.* <note>original Latin: "Non potus & victus nobis præmij loco spondetur & honoris, sed communicatio gratiæ cælestis & vitæ."</note>

**Fifthly**, **John** also rejects such a fanciful and <term>Chiliastic <note>From the Greek 'chilioi' (thousand); the belief that Christ will reign on earth for a literal 1,000 years before the final judgment.</note></term> fable in Revelation 20, for he speaks there of the souls of those who were beheaded, and says that they live and reign with CHRIST for 1,000 years. However, because souls are spiritual, they cannot enjoy such fleshly lusts, nor make use of an external government of grand eating and drinking; *for everything spiritual rejects what is fleshly.* <note>original Latin: "omne enim spirituale respuit carnale."</note> This is also why our glorified bodies in that life—because they will possess spiritual qualities—will have no need for eating and drinking.

What the Chiliasts and <term>Enthusiasts <note>German: "Phantasten." A derogatory term used for those who claimed direct personal revelation or "fanciful" interpretations of scripture.</note></term> introduce to the contrary, particularly from the 20th chapter of Revelation, is easily decided and answered. For while the "Ancient of Days" in Daniel 7 gives the Son of Man power and authority, honor and a kingdom, so that all nations, people, and tongues should serve Him; and while it is also prophesied in Matthew 24 that the Gospel of Christ’s kingdom shall be preached in the whole world as a testimony to all nations—how can <term>Nagelius <note>Paul Nagel (d. 1624), a mathematician and astronomer whose millenarian predictions are being attacked here.</note></term> prove a Chiliastic kingdom from this? Indeed, not a single letter of it is mentioned there. Rather, the Spirit of God indicates through the Prophet how all power in heaven and on earth has been communicated to Christ even according to His assumed human nature—as the LORD Himself explains in Matthew 28. This is denied by no orthodox person, but is constantly affirmed by us all. However, what the <term>Sacramentarians <note>A term often used by Lutherans for those (like followers of Zwingli or Calvin) who held different views on the Real Presence in the Eucharist; here, the author attacks their view on Christ's human nature.</note></term> teach to the contrary: *that omnipotence was not given to Christ as a man, but only a vicarious [power]...* <note>original Latin: "quod Christo homini non sit data omnipotentia, sed vicaria tantum..." This sentence ends mid-thought at the bottom of the page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the theological rebuttal against millenarianism (the belief in a literal 1,000-year earthly kingdom). While the previous page ended by discussing the spiritual nature of souls, this page opens by citing several Reformed theologians to argue that Christ’s power and presence are not limited by human constraints, before moving on to explain why the "1,000 years" in the Book of Revelation should not be taken as a future event.</meta>

...either a circumscribed or finite power; <note>original Latin: "tum aut finita potestas." This refers to a theological debate regarding whether Christ’s human nature is limited to one place or can be omnipresent.</note> as seen in the works of **Lambert Daneau**, page 1449; **Theodore Beza** in the *Colloquy of Montbéliard*, pages 264 and 279; **Antoine de la Roche Sadeel** on the *Truth of Human Nature*, page 144; **Lucas Trelcatius’s** *Institutes of Theology*, book 2, page 110; and **Girolamo Zanchi** on the *Nature of God*, book 2, chapter 1, question 3, thesis 3. This very idea is entirely contrary to this prophetic prophecy and all Apostolic writings, as has been richly explained elsewhere and extensively proven by all our theologians.

Furthermore, and secondly, Christ’s prophecy regarding the general expansion and spreading of the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelij"</note> shall not first be fulfilled in the <term>Chiliastic Kingdom <note>A literal 1,000-year earthly reign of Christ. The author argues this is a misconception.</note></term>; rather, it has already been fulfilled not long ago, as St. Paul teaches in Romans 10: *Their sound has gone out into all lands, and their words into all the world.* As Psalm 19 says: *There is no language nor speech where their voice has not been heard.* Therefore, those who do not believe the Gospel will receive a heavy judgment, as **Augustine** teaches on Psalm 18: *Therefore the sound went out into all the earth so that you might enter into heaven: O pestilential, litigious, most evil one, still wishing to err, O proud son, hear the testament of your father.* <note>original Latin: "Ideò sonus in omnem terram exijt ut tu in cœlam intres: ô pestilentiose, litigiose, pessime, & adhuc errare volens, ô superbe fili, audi testamentum patris tui."</note>

Finally and at last, even though Revelation 20 mentions the 1,000 years—which led some to believe that **Cerinthus** <note>An early heretic who was often accused by his opponents of being the "true" author of Revelation because of his supposedly materialistic views on the afterlife.</note> rather than John wrote the Revelation, as **Eusebius** reports in Book 3, Chapter 28 and Book 7, Chapter 24—the words do not mean that Christ shall reign here on Earth with His fellow citizens for such 1,000 years, nor that **Satan** shall only then be released to gather the Gentiles from the four corners of the Earth, namely **Gog and Magog**, against the mountains of Israel. 

Instead, the Spirit of God prophesies how an angel from heaven—who is the Archangel **Michael** (Revelation 12)—has bound Satan for 1,000 years so that he should no longer deceive the nations. This is not to be interpreted as if Satan would cause no harm or mischief at all within those 1,000 years; rather, this prophecy and vision deal with the great **persecutions of the Church** and open wars which, through God's special <term>providence <note>The protective care of God as a spiritual power.</note></term> and foresight, were calmed and mitigated. This happened when God chopped down the wicked "ruling trees," rooted out the tyrants, and put a ring in the nose of the persecutors, just as He did to **Sennacherib** in Isaiah 37 and 2 Chronicles 32. <note>Sennacherib was an Assyrian king whose attempt to destroy Jerusalem was miraculously thwarted.</note>

However, concerning when these 1,000 years are supposed to have begun, we find differing opinions among both ancient and modern church teachers:

<meta>This page continues the argument against a literal future millennium, specifically addressing the interpretation of the "1,000 years" mentioned in Revelation 20. **Augustine** in book 20 of *The City of God*, chapters 7, 8, and 9, explains this in two ways: 1. <note>original Latin: "Quod in altero millenario res sit sic peragenda..."</note> That the matter is to be carried out in the other millennium, that is, on the sixth millennial day, whose later spans are now unfolding. 2. <note>original Latin: "Synechdochicè, ut à parte totum intelligatur..."</note> Synecdochically <note>A figure of speech where a part represents the whole.</note>, so that the whole is understood from the part; that is, that a thousand years are taken for the entire span of the world's duration. **Ambrose** also agrees with this latter opinion in Volume 5, page 231, and understands the 1,000 years to mean the entire time from Christ's Ascension until the end of the world. 

Some calculate these years from the birth of Christ until the time of **Otto III** <note>Holy Roman Emperor, reigned 983–1002 AD.</note>. For after that time, Satan was loosed again and tyrannized fiercely against the Christian Church; it was then that the Papal traditions came into full swing, the Bible was hidden "under the bench," and Gog and Magog—the Turk and the Pope—caused great bloodshed and much offense, just as Ezekiel prophesied in chapters 38 and 39. 

Others set the beginning at the year of CHRIST 520, when the Pope in Rome, **Hormisdas**, through his legates, petitioned Emperor **Justin** concerning the protection of the Catholic faith and the uprooting of all heretics. 

Most of our theologians, however, begin these 1,000 years with **Constantine the Great** around the year of Christ 300; in this way, they expire around the year 1300. It was around this time that **Osman** <note>The founder of the Ottoman Empire.</note> violently increased and expanded the Turkish Empire, just as John prophesies: that after Satan is loosed, he shall stir up Gog and Magog—that is, the Turks and Tartars—against Christendom.

Although such differing opinions are found among the Church teachers regarding the beginning and end of these 1,000 years, they all nevertheless agree in this: that these years cannot be explained or understood as a <term>Chiliastic age <note>The belief in a literal, future 1,000-year earthly kingdom of Christ, which the author considers a "fable."</note></term>. Therefore, we rightly let such fables and scripture-less poems go, and we rejoice in this: that even though Satan is now loose and rumbles and rages so fiercely, it will only last for a little time and will soon come to an end. Then Satan, with all his henchmen and helpers, will be cast into the lake of fire and sulfur, and they will be tormented day and night, from eternity to eternity (Revelation 20).

# The Fifth Chapter.

## Regarding the signs that shall precede and occur before the Last Day.

Although we humans cannot know nor investigate the time and hour when the final rest of the <term>Last Day<gloss>Jüngster Tag; the Christian concept of the Day of Judgment at the end of the world</gloss></term> will break in; nonetheless, because the faithful <term>Lord of Amen<gloss>Amens-HERR; a title for Christ derived from Revelation 3:14, meaning the "faithful and true witness."</gloss></term>, Christ, gives all sorts of instructions and prophesies of different signs which shall precede and happen near the end of the world, we should diligently investigate them. We should let them be to us as a loud trumpet against sleep, laziness, and delay, so that we do not postpone turning back to the Lord, as noted in Sirach 5. <note>original Latin: "Quæ enim Deus occulta esse voluit, non sunt scrutanda, quæ autem manifesta fecit, non sunt negligenda, ne in illis illicitè curiosi & in istis damnabiliter inveniamur ingrati."</note> For what God wished to remain hidden must not be searched out, and what He has made manifest must not be neglected, lest in the former we be found unlawfully curious and in the latter culpably ungrateful, as Ambrose writes in *On the Calling of the Gentiles*, Book 1, Chapter 7. 

Which signs these are of the approaching day of the last trumpets, Christ teaches and informs us in Matthew 24, saying: "Immediately after the tribulation of those days, the sun will be darkened, and the moon will not give its light; the stars will fall from heaven, and the powers of the heavens will be shaken. Then the sign of the Son of Man will appear in heaven, and then all the tribes of the earth will mourn, and they will see the Son of Man coming on the clouds of heaven with power and great glory. And he will send his angels with a loud trumpet call, and they will gather his elect from the four winds, from one end of heaven to the other. Now learn a lesson from the fig tree: as soon as its twigs get tender and its leaves come out, you know that summer is near. Even so, when you see all these things, know that it is near, right at the door. Truly I tell you, this generation will not pass away until all these things have happened. Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will never pass away."

We also read this in Luke 21: "There will be signs in the sun, moon and stars. On the earth, nations will be in

# On the Pious Reflection of These Signs

It is often **thought** <note>original: "gedacht," which in this context can mean both "thought" or "mentioned/considered"</note> among the God-fearing that these terrifying changes in the nature of the world are not merely for destruction, but serve as a final <term>warning-bell<gloss>Warnungsglocke; a metaphorical call to spiritual alertness</gloss></term>. 

<margin>A necessary meditation</margin>
We should let this **thought** <gloss>Gedancke; a focused meditation or internal reflection</gloss> dwell within us: that as the physical sun withholds its light, the spiritual light of Christ begins to shine more brightly for those who wait for Him. <note>The author here contrasts the "darkening of the sun" mentioned in Matthew with the traditional Christian metaphor of Christ as the "Sun of Righteousness."</note>

### How We Should Hold These Signs in Our Minds

| The Sign | The Worldly Fear | The Christian **Thought** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Darkening Moon | Loss of direction | The end of the world's night |
| Falling Stars | Dread of catastrophe | The arrival of the heavenly host |
| Shaking Earth | Fear of death | The birth-pangs of a new creation |

->For God did not give us a spirit of fear, but of power.<-

It is rightly **thought** that whoever prepares his heart for the <term>Last Day<gloss>Jüngster Tag; the Day of Judgment</gloss></term> will not be surprised by these wonders, as one who is already awake does not fear the rising sun.

Especially, however, such persistence is perceived among the false teachers, who refuse to become sober again from their sins and escape the snares of the Devil, by whom they are held captive to do his will (2 Timothy 2:26). As Chrysostom writes in his sermon on heretics: <note>original Latin: "mendacium abscondunt & veritati etiam victi haut cedunt, os habent quod porta carens non clauditur"</note> "they hide their lies and, even when defeated, do not yield to the truth; they have a mouth that, lacking a gate, is never closed." One may read further on this in Irenaeus on the ancient heretics, Athanasius's first disputation against the Arians (page 113), Hilary's second book on the Trinity (page 17), and Augustine's seventh book on Genesis according to the letter (chapter 9). 

We have an example of this in the apostate and world-renowned <term>Master Heretic<gloss>Käßermeister; a derogatory term for a leader of a heretical sect, here referring to Arius</gloss></term> Arius. Although he was sufficiently defeated regarding his <term>error<gloss>Irrthumb; a theological mistake or false doctrine</gloss></term> and urged to renounce it, he remained hardened in the stiffness of his heart until he finally received the reward of unrighteousness. And, according to the little verse from Virgil: <note>original Latin: "-vitaq; cum gemitu fugit indignata sub umbras"</note> "and with a groan, the soul fled indignantly down to the shadows," he was suddenly rooted out. For when he had, through cunning and deceit, maneuvered back into episcopal dignity and high office, and was on his way with his entourage and <term>Anti-Trinitarian<gloss>Antitrinitarisch; those who deny the doctrine of the Holy Trinity, specifically the divinity of Christ</gloss></term> following to be enthroned and installed in the Church, God acted wondrously. Arius became quite anxious and fearful and hastily desired to "cover his feet" <note>A biblical euphemism for using the latrine, found in Judges 3:24.</note> in a certain place. As soon as he was led there, his intestines spilled out of his body, and he gave up his spirit (Epiphanius book 2, volume 2; Rufinus book 1, chapters 18–19; Sozomen book 4, chapter 10; Tripartite History book 7, chapter 16; Nicephorus book 8, chapters 4–6).

We read the same concerning Berengarius, the <term>Abbot of the Calvinists<gloss>Calvinistarum Abbate; the author is linking Berengarius of Tours (11th century) to the later Reformed/Calvinist tradition because of their similar views on the symbolic nature of the Eucharist</gloss></term>, who was convicted in various councils and condemned because of his <term>heresy<gloss>Käßerey; heretical teaching</gloss></term> (distinction 2 on consecration, chapter "I, Berengarius"; Volume 3 of the Councils, folio 601; Alfonso de Castro, book 6 against heretics, section on the Eucharist; Lindanus in his Dialogues of the Doubter, number 2). For although he promised and vowed to renounce his error, he never truly recognized his sin from the heart, nor did he properly repent the shipwreck of his faith. Instead, like a dog returning to its vomit (Proverbs 26:11) and like a sow that wallows again in the mire after being washed (2 Peter 2:22), Berengarius always became entangled once more in his previous errors. Therefore, he was also given over to a reprobate mind (2 Thessalonians 2) and condemned as one who has judged himself (Titus 3).

But how he was to depart this world and die, he uttered these most lamentable words: <note>original Latin: "Deus meus hodiè mihi adparebit aut ad meam salutem, ut spero, aut ad duram meam damnationem, sicut timeo, propter eos quos perversâ meâ doctrinâ decepi"</note> **"Today my God will appear to me, either for my salvation, as I hope, or for my harsh damnation, as I fear, because of those whom I have deceived with my perverse doctrine,"** that is, Today my God will appear to me either for my salvation—as I hope—or for my damnation—as I fear—because I have seduced and deceived many people with my <term>error<gloss>Irrthumb; specifically, a theological deviation or false teaching</gloss></term>. (Vincent of Beauvais, *Historical Mirror*, book 25, chapter 30; William of Malmesbury, book 3, chapters 58 and 59, *On the Deeds of the English*; Gerson, book against *The Romance of the Rose* and Sermon 1 *On the Lord's Supper*).

Although God the LORD finds an abomination and horror in those who turn aside to their crooked ways (Psalm 125), yet people have more desire for lies than for the truth. They heap up teachers for themselves according to their own pleasures; they depart from the faith—as Paul prophesied would happen in the last days (1 Timothy 4)—and cling to seductive spirits and the doctrines of devils, through those who are hypocritical liars and have their consciences seared as with a branding iron. Therefore, God also sends them strong delusions, so that they believe the lie, in order that all who did not believe the truth but had pleasure in unrighteousness might be judged (2 Thessalonians 2). We experience this especially in this time, when all kinds of errors are in full swing, and there is no <term>heresy<gloss>Kätzerey; a belief or opinion contrary to orthodox religious doctrine</gloss></term> so monstrous that it cannot find its <term>defenders<gloss>Patrocinatores; patrons or advocates who support and protect a cause</gloss></term>, just as the wise pagan Cicero writes in his *Paradoxa* 1: **"Nothing is so incredible that it cannot be made probable by speaking; nothing so horrid or unrefined that it does not shine through oratory and become polished."**

In these last days, we see three unclean spirits like frogs coming out of the mouth of the dragon and the mouth of the false prophet (Revelation 16). These are the babblers, as <term>Luther<gloss>Martin Luther; the German priest who initiated the Protestant Reformation</gloss></term> glosses in the margin, who now flatter princes and mock the Gospel. So also power is given to the locusts—which <term>Philipp Nicolai<gloss>A prominent Lutheran theologian and poet (1556–1608)</gloss></term> (in *The Kingdom of Christ*, book 2, chapter 5) interprets as the <term>Sacramentarians<gloss>Sacramentirer; a polemical term used by Lutherans to describe those who denied the real presence of Christ's body and blood in the Eucharist, such as Zwinglians or Calvinists</gloss></term>—as scorpions have power on earth to torment and plague mankind, and their torment is like the torment of a scorpion when it stings a man (Revelation 9:5, 6).

Therefore, Dr. Mylius <note>Georg Mylius (1541–1607), a prominent Lutheran theologian.</note> writes in his *Sermons on the Pope* that the Antichrist in Rome has, at the very least, taken twenty-three million and eight million five hundred thousand souls upon his notch-stick <note>A "Kerff" or tally stick used for debts or accounting; here, it refers to the Pope’s "account" of lost souls.</note>. That may well be a "Holy Father" and the "Vicar of Christ"—though I mean the Vicar of Satan, whose work he does. Instead of love, he threatens with hatred; instead of life, with death. And he wishes to remain unchecked; even if he drags several thousand souls with him into Hell, no one is supposed to say, "Dear Father, what are you doing?" <note>original Latin: "dist. 40. c. si Papa." This refers to a controversial decree in Canon Law suggesting the Pope cannot be judged unless he deviates from the faith.</note> Wherefore also Eberhard, Archbishop of Salzburg, as recorded in Aventinus, book 7, complains vehemently about such tyranny and arrogance of the Pope:

<note>original: "Romani Pontifices, inquit, arma in omnes Christianos habent, audendo, fallendo & bella ex bellis serendo, magni facti sunt, oves trucidant, occidunt, pacem concordiamq; terris depellunt, intestina bella & domesticas seditiones ab inferis eliciunt, indies magis ac magis omnium vires debilitant, ut omnium capitibus insultent, ut omnes devorent, ut universos in servitutem redigant."</note>
"The Roman Pontiffs," he says, "carry weapons against all Christians; by daring, by deceiving, and by sowing war upon war, they have become great. They slaughter the sheep, they kill them, they drive peace and harmony from the earth. They summon internal wars and domestic seditions from the underworld. Day by day, they weaken the strength of all more and more, so that they may trample upon the heads of everyone, devour everyone, and reduce the whole world to servitude."

Therefore, it is no wonder what one reads about **Pope Benedict IX**: that in the year 1056 he was strangled by the Devil in the forest, and was afterwards seen by a hermit with a body entirely hairy like a bear and with the head of a donkey. When he was asked where such a beastly transformation came from, he answered: "As I lived, so I am now; I was more like wild beasts than humans, therefore I rightly have such a form." (John Bale, book 5, *On the Roman Pontiffs*; Nauclerus, Volume 2, Generation 35; Platina, *Lives of the Popes*, page 166).

But so that the Papists might not be regarded and denounced as tyrants and Antichristian bloodhounds, they pretend to have reasons for their persecution—firstly, that one [cannot tolerate] any Evangelical...

<meta>This page continues the author’s polemical defense of the Lutheran faith against Catholic accusations. The previous page ended with Archbishop Eberhard of Salzburg lamenting the "tyranny and arrogance" of the Pope. This page argues that Lutherans are not heretics by legal definition and that the papacy has overstepped its spiritual bounds by seizing secular power.</meta>

<sig>H iij</sig>

[...obligated to] hold to the Catholic faith, according to which heretics should be rooted out. But as the deed is damnable, so the reasons are reprehensible; for it remains unproven that we **Lutherans**—as we carry such a name from that dear instrument **Luther**—are burdened with any heresy or false teaching. Nevertheless, they stalk us like heretics and thirst for our blood, solely because we will not honor their **God Mauzim** <note>original: "Mausen." A reference to the "god of fortresses" in Daniel 11:38, which Reformers often interpreted as the Catholic Mass or the Pope himself.</note> and will not consent to or approve of their traditions and statutes. 

For a **heretic** <term>hæreticus: a person holding an opinion at odds with what is generally accepted, specifically in a religious context</term> is defined as: <note>original Latin: "ut habetur c. 24. q. 3. c. hæresis, qui aliter scripturam intelligit, quàm sensus Spiritus Sancti flagitat, quô scripta est; & qui contumaciter defendit, quod pravè sapit"</note> *one who understands Scripture otherwise than the sense of the Holy Spirit requires, by whom it was written; and who stubbornly defends what he perversely understands.* 

We, however, remain with the **Scripture**, and everything we teach is founded and grounded in God’s Word; <note>original Latin: "& quidem simpliciter inhæremus scripturæ, nec repugnamus Deo, licet sensui & cogitationi nostræ absurdum esse videatur, secundum istud Chrysostomi hom. 83. in c. 26. Matth"</note> *and indeed we simply adhere to the Scripture, nor do we resist God, even if it seems absurd to our own senses and thoughts, according to that saying of Chrysostom in his 83rd Homily on Matthew 26.* 

Therefore, it is not we, but the **Papists** <term>Bäpstler: a derogatory term for followers of the Pope</term> who are the heretics, who do not leave the Scripture in the sense in which it was written, but instead saddle it with all sorts of church statutes; one twists the word of the other and they seduce the people with their lies and empty talk, as **Jeremiah** speaks in Chapter 23. And such things they carry out with force in the land, condemning us without a hearing—against which **Pope Evaristus** <note>An early Bishop of Rome (c. 99–107 AD); the author cites him to show that even early Popes would disagree with modern Catholic legal practices.</note> himself writes: <note>original Latin: "30. q. 5. c. nullum, quod ante veram justamq; probationem, nemo sit judicandus vel condemnandus"</note> *that before a true and just trial, no one is to be judged or condemned.*

Furthermore and secondly, the anointed **Church Fathers** are not commanded to rule in the manner of worldly lords (Luke 22) or to lord it over the people (1 Peter 5). The **Sword of Peter** <note>A symbol of the Pope's claimed authority to punish or use force.</note> was not given into their hands to persecute the Church with it, but rather the "woe" and the "gentle staff" (Zechariah 11) to pasture the sheep of Christ (John 21). And even if it were the Holy Pope himself, it does not belong to him by right, as a Pope, to wield **two kinds of swords** <term>The "Two Swords" doctrine (duo gladii) claimed the Pope held both spiritual and secular power over the world.</term>—*the secular and the spiritual*—as the Papists and the Canon Law write of him <note>The author here lists various Catholic legal sources and authors like Conrad Wimpina and Alexius Carerius who defended the Pope's supreme power.</note>. For these are **distinct powers** <note>original Latin: "distinctæ potestates"</note>...

<summary>This page argues that Lutherans are not heretics because they adhere strictly to Scripture, whereas the Papacy has illegitimately claimed worldly "secular" power (the second sword) that contradicts early church teachings and biblical mandates.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that the Pope and his followers (the "Papists") have exceeded their spiritual mandate by claiming secular authority and using violence against those they label as heretics. It emphasizes the distinction between spiritual and worldly power.</meta>

As Christ teaches in Luke 22: the earthly lords rule over others, and those in power are called "gracious lords," but you are not to be so. Therefore, it seems that ecclesiastical persons—as Tholosanus <note>Pierre Grégoire (1540–1597), a French jurist also known as Tholosanus.</note> says in *The Republic*, book 13, chapter 1, section 29—depart from their own duty by mixing themselves in secular affairs. <note>original Latin: "videntur igitur personæ Ecclesiasticæ... secllire à proprio munere, secularibus se immiscendo."</note> Peter Lombard also writes in book 4, distinction 37, that the Holy Church of God has nothing but the spiritual sword. <note>original Latin: "Sancta Dei Ecclesia non habet nisi gladium spiritualem."</note> Sebastian Heiss certainly dares, in his declaration of the Jesuit Aphorisms, aphorism 1, page 22, to dismiss and cloak this with a fictitious distinction: that the Pope has supreme spiritual power directly, but temporal power indirectly. <note>original Latin: "quod Papa habeat supremam potestatem spiritualem directè, temporalem indirectè."</note> But the words of Christ are far too bright and clear for any cloaking or covering to help. Thus Thomas Aquinas also writes in opuscule 15, chapter 3, that in one and the same office there should not be a different profession. <note>original Latin: "quod in uno eodemq; officio non debet esse dispar professio."</note> And Jean Gerson <note>A prominent French scholar and reformer at the University of Paris (1363–1429).</note> will not grant the Pope such two-fold power, in *On Church Power*, consideration 12, volume 1 of his works, folio 54.

Therefore, the **Papists** act against God's Word, against their office and duty, against Christian love, and against good conscience and faith when they persecute us without cause, solely for the sake of religion. For such was also ordained in the **Peace of Passau** <note>The Peace of Passau (1552) was a treaty that guaranteed religious freedoms to Lutherans within the Holy Roman Empire.</note> and agreed upon by the Imperial Estates: that no Estate should disturb another or attack them because of their teaching. I speak primarily of the spiritual fathers; the secular authorities may answer for themselves, for of the clergy it is said: they are shepherds and not strikers. <note>original Latin: "pastores sunt & non percussores," from the Decretum Gratiani, dist. 45.</note> Likewise, he who desires to preside and not to profit should not be a bishop. <note>original Latin: "qui præesse & non prodesse desiderat, episcopari non debet," citing Augustine's *City of God*, book 19, chapter 19.</note> Would to God that the **Papists** might hear and follow the salutary counsel of God; then it would go well for them. For he who hears and hears well, says Augustine in his 34th sermon *On Time*, fails and profits: he fails in iniquity and profits in truth; he fails the world and profits for God. <note>original Latin: "qui enim audit & benè audit... deficit iniquitati, proficit veritati, deficit seculo, proficit Deo."</note>

Thirdly, nowhere in Holy Scripture do we read that heretics should be killed. On the contrary, <term>τὸ προσέχειν</term> <note>Greek: *tò proséchein*, meaning "to take heed" or "to be on guard."</note> is commanded of us when Christ says in Matthew 7: *Beware of false prophets who come to you in sheep’s clothing, but inwardly are ravenous wolves.* And St. Paul says in Titus 3: *Avoid a heretical person after he has been warned once or twice,* and know that such a person is perverted and sins, being self-condem- <note>The text ends mid-word: "verur-" (verurteilt), meaning "condemned."</note>

<summary>The author argues that the papacy's claim to secular power and the violent persecution of Lutherans contradict both Christ's teachings and legal treaties like the Peace of Passau. He cites various theological authorities to prove that the Church's only "sword" is spiritual, not physical.</summary>
