<meta>This page continues the author's argument that spiritual leaders should not use violence or capital punishment against heretics. Having established on the previous page that the "two swords" (spiritual and secular) are distinct and that clergy should only wield the spiritual sword of the Word, the author now addresses the proper response to theological error, citing the Parable of the Tares and ancient Church authorities to advocate for tolerance over execution.</meta>

...has condemned: From which we see and understand that one should indeed not maintain spiritual friendship and fellowship with heretics, as **John** also teaches in *2 John, verse 10*. But for that reason one should not immediately draw the "strangling sword" over them, as **Felicianus Capitaneus** writes in *Catholic Explanations 45, part 3, page 1136*: "Let heretics be cut off either by iron or by fire, just as a branch from a tree." <note>original Latin: "Abscindantur hæretici vel ferro vel igne, sicut ramus ab arbore."</note> Likewise **Friedrich Staphylus** in *Defense against the Building of the Babylonian Tower of Martin Luther*, page 48; and *The Fortress of Faith*, Book 2, Consideration 11, folio 74. <note>Friedrich Staphylus (1512–1564) was a Lutheran scholar who converted to Catholicism and became a sharp critic of the Reformation. The *Fortalitium Fidei* was a 15th-century work against various perceived enemies of the faith.</note>

For when the disciples of **CHRIST** were also inclined to root out the weeds, the **LORD** answered them: "Let both grow together until the harvest, and at the time of harvest I will say to the reapers: gather the weeds first and bind them in bundles to be burned, but gather the wheat into my barns" (**Matthew 13**). This is also taught by <term>Canon Law</term> <note>original: "Geistliche Recht," referring to the laws governing the Church.</note>, Case 23, Question 4, Chapter "Tres personas": "The wicked are to be tolerated, and punished not with bodily but with spiritual vengeance. Whence, when the disciples were not received by the Samaritans and wanted to bring down fire from heaven upon them, saying to the Master: 'Do you want us to command fire to come down from heaven and consume them?' they heard: 'You do not know of what spirit you are.'" <note>original Latin: "Mali sunt tolerandi, nec corporali sed spirituali vindicta puniendi... nescitis, cujus spiritus estis." This quotes the "Decretum Gratiani," the foundation of medieval church law, which uses the story of Jesus and the Samaritans from Luke 9:54–55 to argue against violent retribution.</note>

For it is enough that one punishes sin and error with the two-edged sword of God’s Word, according to that saying of **Athanasius** in his *Second Oration against the Arians*: "Heretics are to be stoned with the arguments of Scripture." <note>original Latin: "lapidandi sunt hæretici scripturæ argumentis." Athanasius (c. 296–373) was the leading defender of the doctrine of the Trinity against the Arian heresy.</note> This is the example set before us by all the prophets and apostles: that one leaves the "weeding" to the <term>authorities</term> <note>original German: "Obrigkeit," the secular governing body or prince.</note>, and lets them use the sword entrusted to them only when the <term>heretics</term> are simultaneously <term>wicked men</term> <note>original Latin: "homines scelerati," referring to those who commit secular crimes such as theft or violence, rather than merely theological errors.</note>. Of this **Luther** speaks in Volume 8 of the Jena edition, folio 412: "otherwise an error of the mind is not of the class of capital crimes." <note>original Latin: "error animi non est ex genere capitalium." This draws on the legal principle that mental error or private belief should not be treated as a death-penalty offense.</note>

We must always hope, as long as a person lives, that he can improve and be converted, as **Prosper** says in **Augustine's** *Sentences*, 2: "As long as men live, some hope of conversion remains." <note>original Latin: "quamdiu homines vivunt, spes aliqua de conversione superest." Prosper of Aquitaine (c. 390–455) was a theologian known for distilling Augustine’s complex works into short "sentences" or aphorisms.</note> But if they do not convert, they will have punishment enough in the eternal fire of Hell, and it is not necessary that they be tortured in this temporal life, as **Luther** writes in Volume 4, folio 480. Concerning this, we find more extensive reports among the ancient Church Fathers—**Tertullian** (*To Scapula*), **Hilary** (*Against Auxentius*), **Ignatius** (*Letter 6 to the Philadelphians*, page 31), **Augustine** (*Book 2 against the Letters of Petilian*, chapter 24, and *Letters 158 and 159*), and **Lactantius** (*Divine Institutes*, Book 5, chapter 20, page 228)—all of whom wanted heretics to be punished, and yet kept alive.

<summary>The author uses the Parable of the Tares and citations from early Church Fathers and Canon Law to argue that heretics should be countered with scripture rather than execution, maintaining that secular punishment is only for civil crimes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument against the use of force in matters of faith. While the previous page focused on the "two swords" and the Parable of the Tares to show that clergy should not use violence, this page shifts focus to secular rulers. It argues that even though the state has the power of the "sword" for civil matters, it has no right to use that power to coerce religious belief or "plant" faith by force.</meta>

Fourthly: although the <term>secular estate of government</term> <note>original German: "Weltlicher Regierstand," referring to civil authorities like princes or magistrates.</note> has been commanded to hold the sword (**Romans 13**), it is nevertheless not fitting for them to violently expand religion, or to plant their faith with the sword, or to force their subjects to follow their example and accept their teaching. This is the opinion not only of the <term>Papists</term> <note>original German: "Bäpstler," a derogatory Reformation-era term for Roman Catholics.</note>, who put this into practice, but also the <term>Sacramentarians</term> <note>original German: "Sacramentirer," a term used (often by Lutherans) for Reformed/Calvinist theologians who held a symbolic view of the Lord's Supper.</note>: **Danaeus**, book 2 of *Christian Ethics*, chapter 13, page 185; **Zanchius**, volume 2 of *Miscellanies*, page 282; **Lampadius** in *Censures*, page 52; **Lipsius**, book 4 of *Politics*, chapter 3; and the author of **Herborn** in the notes to the *Exposition of the Catechism*, page 147.

For it is an entirely un-Christian undertaking, in which God has no pleasure, because He desires voluntary and unforced service from mankind, as **Sirach** teaches in **Chapter 15**: He created man from the beginning and gave him the choice: if you will, then keep the commandments and do what pleases Him in right trust. He has set before you fire and water; reach for whichever you will. Man has before him life and death; whichever he wants shall be given to him. Furthermore, God alone rules over the conscience, and not the <term>authorities</term> <note>original German: "Obrigkeit."</note>, as **Keckermann** also confesses in book 1 of his *Political System*, page 523. 

If someone now wishes to poison and burden his conscience—which is often a restless <term>lute-player</term> <note>original German: "Lautenſchläger," a metaphor for a nagging or noisy conscience that "strikes a chord" of guilt or anxiety within a person.</note>—with foreign and false teaching, it will be hard enough for him to "kick against the pricks" (**Acts 9**) when he must give an account of his faith before GOD. Therefore, **Lactantius** rightly writes in book 5, chapter 20: 

> Religion is to be defended not by killing, but by warning; not by cruelty, but by patience; not by crime, but by faith. For the former belong to the wicked, the latter to the good. <note>original Latin: "Defendenda est religio non occidendo, sed monendo, non ſævitiâ sed patientia, non ſcelere, sed fide..."</note>

Likewise **Tertullian** in *To Scapula*, at the end:

> It is not the property of religion to compel religion, which ought to be taken up spontaneously, not by force. <note>original Latin: "Non eſt religionis cogere religionem, quæ ſponte ſuſcipi debet non vi."</note>

And several of the **Sacramentarians** also agree with this opinion—regardless of the fact that their actions often prove the opposite—such as **Goclenius** in *Philosophical-Theological Disputation 20*, thesis 47; and **Antonius Benbellona** in *Pragmatic Sanctions on the Religious Peace*, part 2, chapter 7, page 54. Likewise, it was decreed and decided in the **Fourth Council of Toledo**, canon 56, in the chapter "Concerning the Jews," distinction 45:

> that no one shall be forced into believing. <note>original Latin: "quod nemini ad credendum vis inferatur."</note>

And **Tholosanus** writes in *On the Republic*, book 12, chapter 4, section 11:

> Just as an unwilling will cannot be forced, so too should religion be spontaneous. <note>original Latin: "Quemadmodum cogi non poteſt invita voluntas, ſic nec quæ debet eſſe ſpontanea religio."</note>

But this is little regarded in the Papacy; the decree of **Innocentius Albanus** carries much more weight, from whom... <note>The page ends mid-sentence; the catchword "Dresser" indicates the next page begins with a reference to the historian Matthäus Dresser.</note>

<summary>The author argues that secular rulers have no divine authority to coerce religious belief, citing both biblical texts (Sirach) and early Christian fathers (Lactantius, Tertullian) to assert that faith must be voluntary.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Roman Catholic ("Papist") position on religious coercion. The previous page ended with a mention of Pope Innocent I (referred to as Innocentius Albanus). <sig>J</sig>

**Dresser** writes in his *Millennium*, volume 5, page 182, that <note>Innocent I</note> was the first who, around the year of Christ 402, decided to bring the unbelievers to the faith by force.

Fifthly: it is a <term>Papist truth</term> <note>A sarcastic reference to the polemical accusation that the Catholic Church taught that "faith need not be kept with heretics."</note> that one should not keep one's word to an <term>Evangelical</term> <note>original German: "Evangelischen," referring here to Protestants.</note>. Such lying and deceiving, such hypocrisy and secret malice, originates from the Devil, who is a father of lies (**John 8**). This is explicitly <term>rejected</term> <note>original Latin/German: "rejicirt."</note> and condemned in the Scriptures of the Old and New Testaments; instead, it is commanded that our speech should be "yea, yea; nay, nay" (**Matthew 5**), and indeed **without deception**, according to **Irenaeus**, book 3, chapter 15. <note>original Latin: "sine fuco."</note> Anything beyond or against this is false and untruthful, which God finds an abomination and a horror (**Proverbs 12**). For the LORD hates these six things, and the seventh is an abomination to Him: proud eyes, a lying tongue, hands that shed innocent blood, a heart that devises wicked schemes, feet that are quick to do harm, a false witness who speaks bold lies, and one who stirs up discord among brothers.

Likewise, the honorable <term>Heathens</term> <note>Refers to the classical Greek and Roman philosophers.</note> had a particular displeasure for untruth and hypocrisy when one does not fulfill or keep their promise in deed, as **Cicero** writes in book 3 of *On Duties*: 

> Agreements and promises must always be kept, provided they were not made by force or by evil deceit; those are not to be kept which are not useful to those to whom they were promised. <note>original Latin: "Pacta & promissa semper sunt servanda, quæ nec vi, nec dolo malo facta sunt: illa servanda non sunt, quæ non sunt iis ipsis utilia, quibus illa promisit."</note>

And **Seneca** says: 

> I would rather lack success than loyalty; he is just even to an enemy who keeps loyalty in his counsel; he who loses loyalty can lose nothing more. <note>original Latin: "Malo mihi successum deesse, quàm fidem: etiam hosti est æquus, qui habet in consilio fidem, fidem qui perdit, nil potest ultra perdere."</note> 

For faith <note>original Latin: "fides," meaning both religious faith and "keeping one's word" or "good faith."</note> is the **foundation of justice**, the ground and floor upon which justice is built (**Cicero**, book 1 of *On Duties*; **Lipsius**, book 2 of *Politics*, chapter 14). Where faith fails, justice is weakened and all human **society** is abolished, according to **Livy**, book 9, decade 1. Therefore, the **Imperial Laws** also demand that **a promise given must be kept** (**arg. l. ult. ff. de legat.**, alongside others cited by **Hotman**, **Hakelman**, **Duaren**, **Bodin**, and **Tholosanus**). <note>The author lists several prominent 16th-century legal scholars and political theorists to support the idea that legal contracts and oaths are the bedrock of civilization.</note>

Since it is therefore incumbent upon everyone to keep faith eternally and to perform in deed what he has promised—for **Augustine** says in *Sermon 22* that "faith" [*fides*] is named from that which "is done" [*fit*]: 

> The word 'faith' consists of two syllables; the first syllable comes from 'deed' [*facto*], the second from 'God' [*Deo*]. <note>original Latin: "fides enim adpellata est ab eo, quod fit... duæ syllabæ sonant, cum dicitur fides, prima syllaba est à facto, secunda à Deo." Augustine is using a creative etymology to argue that faith is only real if it results in action.</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that promises must be kept regardless of religious differences. The previous page established that "good faith" is the foundation of justice and society, using citations from Cicero, Seneca, and Augustine. Here, the author addresses the counter-argument that Catholics (referred to as "Papists") are unjustly accused of breaking faith with heretics, specifically by citing the execution of Jan Hus despite a promise of safe passage.</meta>

*I ask you therefore, do you believe? You say, "I believe": then do what you say, and that is faith:* <note>original Latin: "Interrogo te ergò, utrum credas? dicis credo: fac quod dicis, & fides est." This continues the quote from Augustine from the previous page.</note> Regarding this, we could introduce lovely and laudable examples of those who held their faith firmly and inviolably even toward their enemies; however, it is not my intent or purpose in this treatise to discuss such questions in detail, which is why I only wished to touch upon them briefly.

To be sure, <term>Becanus</term> <note>Martinus Becanus (1563–1624), a prominent Jesuit theologian known for his polemics against Protestants.</note> (Volume 2, work 1) wants to excuse the Papists, as if it were falsely attributed to them that they should not keep their word, since all Catholics on the contrary teach that *faith must be kept with heretics* <note>original Latin: "fidem esse servandam hæreticis."</note>. But it is only a <term>bald excuse</term> <note>original Latin: "calva excusatio," meaning a thin or transparently false excuse.</note>, for **Jacobus Simancha**, Bishop of Badajoz, in *Catholic Institutions* (chapter 46, number 5), writes explicitly:

> It also pertains to the punishment and hatred of heretics that the faith given to them need not be kept, notwithstanding an oath. <note>original Latin: "Ad hæreticorum pœnam & odium etiam pertinet, quod fides illis data servanda non sit non obstante juramento." Simancha was an influential Spanish canon lawyer and bishop whose writings on the Inquisition were widely cited.</note>

Thus also the **First Lateran Council** under Gregory VII, the **Second Lateran Council** under Innocent III, and the **Council of Constance** decreed and published that one should keep no faith with any heretic; and furthermore, by Apostolic authority, they absolve those from their oath who are bound by loyalty or an oath to excommunicated persons. This is what **Jan Hus** experienced with great pain, having to lose his life and be sacrificed in the fire because of it. For although he was given a **safe conduct** <note>original Latin: "Salvus conductus." This was a legal document guaranteeing a traveler protection within a ruler's territory.</note> to the Council by **Emperor Sigismund**—which indeed should rightfully have been kept, for *a safe conduct given by a prince must be observed* (**law 1, Code: concerning those who... alongside others**) not only in arriving but also in departing, for the security of coming includes the security of leaving (**law 1, Code: concerning shipowners; Gail, book 1, observation 52, number 5**)—nevertheless, the "anointed fathers" at **Constance** <note>original German: "Coßnitz," the German name for the city where the Council of Constance (1414–1418) was held.</note> committed such an oath and conduct to the fire <note>original Latin: "Vulcano," referring to the Roman god of fire.</note> and roasted the pious Hus in the fire for an unheard-of and unmerited cause. They were at once the accusers, judges, and <term>executioners</term> <note>original Latin: "carnifices."</note>, so that it may well be said, as **Gail** says (book 1, observation 52, number 6): *A safe conduct is a fragile security.* <note>original Latin: "Salvus conductus fragilis cautio est."</note> And despite this, they still want to be excused, as if they had done nothing evil nor acted unjustly, just as **Becanus** writes (Volume 2, page 206): *To punish heretics is nothing evil.* <note>original Latin: "hæreticos punire nihil mali est."</note>

<meta>
catchword: is

...eck, whether anything is being falsely attributed to the <term>Papists</term> <note>A common early modern term used by Protestants to refer to Roman Catholics.</note>—regarding that, we let all the world judge and pass sentence.

Then the elect will stand at the right hand of Christ and say: *this is true justice, that the Pope, who sits in your seat, and performs the deeds of Lucifer, should lose with contempt the seat in which he presumed to sit, and be a partaker of the punishment of Lucifer* <note>original Latin: "hæc est vera justitia, ut Papa, qui sedet in sede tua, & opera habet Luciferi amittat cum contemtu sedem, in qua præsumsit sedere, & particeps sit pœnæ Luciferi."</note>, **Author of the Burden of the Church** <note>original Latin: "Autor oneris Eccles." # Whether the Pope is the Antichrist, or whether another specific person from the tribe and lineage of Dan shall arise before the Last Day, who will possess both the name and the deeds of the Antichrist; of this it is asked once again:

<image-desc>A large decorative drop-cap letter "D" begins the first paragraph, featuring intricate scrolling foliage and floral patterns within a square border.</image-desc>

That the <term>Antichrist</term> <note>In this context, a prophetic figure of ultimate evil. Protestants identified this with the office of the Papacy, while Catholics viewed it as a specific individual yet to come.</note> shall come and be revealed before the <term>Last Day</term> <note>The Day of Judgment at the end of the world.</note>, of this we should not doubt in the least; for Paul and John give us infallible instruction regarding this, and set the coming and revelation of the Antichrist as a clear sign of the coming of Christ (*2 Thessalonians 2; 1 John 2*). However, whether the Antichrist has already been revealed, or whether he is yet to come, regarding this we find differing and contrary opinions. For the <term>Papists</term> <note>A term used by reformers to refer to Roman Catholics who follow the Pope.</note> do not want to admit that Rome is prefigured by **Babylon** of which John speaks (*Revelation 14*), and that their Holy Father the Pope is the Antichrist. Instead, they claim that another specific person, before the Day of the Lord breaks, will arise from the lineage of **Dan** from among the Jews, and will tyrannize and rule in Jerusalem for <term>three and a half years</term> <note>original German: "vierdhalb Jahr." An archaic way of saying "the fourth half," or three and a half.</note> (*Bellarmin, On the Roman Pontiff, book 3, chapters 2, 12, and 13*). To this opinion <term>Zanchius</term> <note>Girolamo Zanchi (1516–1590), a Protestant theologian who held some views on the end times that differed from the Lutheran mainstream.</note> also agrees in his treatise on the end, in *Miscellanea, chapter 2*, stating <note>original Latin: "quod ante finem seculi singularis persona & tyrannus veniat"</note> "that before the end of the age a single person and tyrant shall come," for which he has been not unfairly criticized and rebuked by his fellow believers, as witnessed by <term>Whitaker</term> <note>William Whitaker (1548–1595), an English Protestant academic and theologian.</note> in *On the Roman Pontiff, controversy 4, question 5, chapter 1, page 568.*

We, however, understand the Antichrist not as a single person alone, but as the entire Antichristian kingdom, which the Popes have administered one after another; just as <term>Irenaeus</term> <note>An early Church Father (c. 130–202 AD).</note> (*book 5, chapter 25*) and <term>Origen</term> <note>An early Christian scholar (c. 184–253 AD).</note> (*Homily 30 on Matthew*) are of such opinion and indicate <note>original Latin: "quod Antichristus non sit unus homo, sed corpus & multitudo hominum"</note> "that the Antichrist is not one man, but a body and multitude of men." This way of speaking is not unreasonable or unusual in the Scripture; for when Daniel prophesies of the <term>four monarchies</term> <note>The traditional interpretation of Daniel 7, identifying four world empires (Babylon, Persia, Greece, and Rome).</note> (*Chapter 7*), he understands by the **four beasts** not just one person, but the empires of the Assyrians, Persians, Greeks, and Romans, and thus all monarchs or persons who would exist and live within such a kingdom. Thus, the *singularity of the person* <note>original Latin: "singularitas personæ"</note> in the Antichristian kingdom is also [noted/treated] by the Apostle Paul tru-

## Will Enoch and Elijah then not return before the Last Day and preach the Gospel?

Regarding this, we once again find unequal and contrary opinions, for the <term>Papists</term> <note>A term used by Reformers to refer to those loyal to the Pope.</note> hold it as credible that these two holy men of God, Enoch and Elijah, are still alive, specifically for the purpose that they should return and oppose the **Antichrist**, preserve the elect in the faith of Christ, and convert the Jews; *Robert Bellarmine, book 3 on the Roman Pontiff, chapter 6, page 1124; Martin Becan, volume 1, page 608 & volume 2, page 506; Fernando Vellosillo, Remarks on Ambrose, question 2, folio 2.* <note>The author cites prominent Catholic Counter-Reformation theologians to show the opposing view.</note> 

We, however, teach the contrary: that although Enoch and Elijah live, they do not live in an earthly paradise, <note>original Latin: "ut Pontificij volunt" (as the Papists wish)</note> but rather in **God’s** hand and no torment touches them, *Wisdom 3*. <note>A reference to the Book of Wisdom 3:1, which states that the souls of the righteous are in God's care.</note> For the former [Enoch] God took away and he was seen no more, *Genesis 5*; the latter [Elijah] ascended alive to heaven in a fiery chariot, *2 Kings 2*, so that we might have examples of how our souls never die but live and behold God face to face, and that our mortal bodies shall also rise again on the Last Day and live eternally with them. But as for the idea that Enoch and Elijah should return and oppose the **Antichrist**, by whom they would also be killed...

<meta>catchword: as</meta>

<summary>The author rejects the Catholic belief that a future Antichrist will emerge from the tribe of Dan, arguing instead that the Pope is the already-revealed Antichrist. He also disputes the idea that Enoch and Elijah will return to earth to fight the Antichrist, viewing them instead as examples of the soul's immortality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the biblical prophecies regarding the end times are being fulfilled in the present day through the Reformation. ...as Vellosillo and Becanus <note>Fernando Vellosillo and Martin Becan were prominent Catholic theologians who argued for the literal return of Enoch and Elijah.</note> write; we consider that to be a fable and a Papist lie. For Elijah has already come—if only the **Papists** <note>A term used by Reformers to refer to those loyal to the Pope.</note> would only believe and accept it—namely, Luther. Just as the **Lord** Christ speaks of John the Baptist in **Matthew 11**, who went forth in the spirit and power of Elijah and has, through **God’s** grace, mightily opposed and resisted the Papist **Antichrist**. Thus, we need to wait for no other Elijah, just as we know of no other **Antichrist**, nor believe anything of one, especially since Holy Scripture knows nothing of such things: <term>neither concerning the return of Enoch and Elijah, nor the coming of a future Antichrist</term> <note>original Latin: "neq; de reditu Enoch & Eliæ, neq; de Adventu AntiChristi futuri."</note>.

Furthermore, and secondly, **God** the **Lord** will not awaken anyone from the dead, nor send anyone from heaven to us to preach the Gospel and oppose the heretics; we have "Moses and the Prophets, whom we should hear," **Luke 16** <note>A reference to the Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, implying that Scripture alone is sufficient for salvation and no further miracles or messengers from the dead are needed.</note>. We have a "firm prophetic word," and do well to pay attention to it "as to a light that shines in a dark place, until the morning dawn breaks," **Isaiah 8, 2 Peter 1**. Therefore, He also provides a great host of evangelists, **Psalm 68**, and equips them with His Spirit—some more than others—that they should "lift up their voice like a trumpet," **Isaiah 58, Hosea 8**, and warn their listeners against the **Antichrist** and other heretics and fanatics <note>The original German "Schwermern" refers to "Enthusiasts" or radical reformers whom the author views as dangerous or deluded.</note>, so that they are not deceived nor suffer shipwreck in their faith and salvation.

And these are also the witnesses of whom John speaks in **Revelation 11**, who shall prophesy for one thousand two hundred and sixty days, clothed in sackcloth. This is not only what the text of the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation.</note> demands, but what all fine and reliable theologians testify. For if Enoch and Elijah were to be these two witnesses alone in their return and at the arrival of the **Antichrist**—as Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a key Catholic defender against the Reformation.</note> wishes—then many absurdities would follow. Hoë <note>Matthias Hoë von Hoënegg (1580–1645), a prominent Lutheran court preacher and polemicist.</note> writes of these more extensively in his commentary on **Revelation chapter 11, page 465**: [according to Bellarmine's logic] they would be killed by the **Antichrist** *after* the death of the **Antichrist**, and rise again two and a half days *after* the Last Day. These are quite un-theological absurdities; therefore, we rightly hold no stock in, nor believe, such **Antichristian** fables about the return of Enoch and Elijah.

What is argued to the contrary by Bellarmine and other **Papists**...

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument against the literal return of Enoch and Elijah. The previous page established that Martin Luther is the "Elijah" who was promised, and the author now addresses specific scriptural proofs used by Catholic theologians to argue otherwise.</meta>

...is objected—specifically from **Sirach chapter 44, verse 16** <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus, a book found in the Catholic Bible but considered apocryphal and non-authoritative by most Protestants.</note>—but this is very easily settled. For it is no <term>canonical book</term> <note>A book officially accepted as part of the Holy Scripture from which articles of faith can be derived.</note> from which an article of faith can be taken, explained, or proven. Furthermore, it does not say in the Greek text <note>original: "quod sit translatus in paradisum, ut det gentibus pœnitentiam, sed simpliciter, μετετέθη, ὑπόδειγμα μετανοίας ταῖς γενεαῖς, translatus est, exemplum pœnitentiæ generationibus"</note> that "he was translated into paradise to give repentance to the nations," but simply that **"he was translated, an example of repentance to the generations."**

Thus, Enoch and Elijah are presented to us as a model and mirror of **piety** and godliness, not so that we should foolishly gape and wait for their return. That return was fulfilled in John the Baptist, according to the prophecy in **Malachi 4**, as explained not only by the angelic messenger Gabriel in **Luke 1**, but by Christ Himself in **Matthew 17**. The ancient teachers of the Church are also of this opinion, including Jerome <note>Early church father and translator of the Latin Vulgate.</note> in his commentary on Malachi 4, Hilary in his commentary on Matthew, canon 17, and Origen in his 3rd homily on Matthew. Whatever Augustine writes to the contrary in his 4th tract on John is not according to the norm and form of Holy Scripture, but follows the **conjecture** and opinion of the Jewish Rabbis—as even Jansenius <note>Cornelius Jansen (1510–1576), a Catholic Bishop known for his scholarly biblical commentaries.</note> testifies in his *Ecclesiastical Explanations*, chapter 44, verse 15.

Furthermore, these holy men of God are not presented to us so that we—alongside other canonized saints—should worship them with divine honors as the **Papists** do. Regarding this, it is rightly said, as Augustine teaches in *On True Religion, chapter 55*:

> Let our religion not consist in the worship of dead men; for if they lived piously, they do not seek such honors from us. Instead, they desire that we worship Him through whose light they rejoice that we are their fellow-servants. Therefore, they should be honored for the sake of imitation, but not adored as an act of religion.

Rather, Enoch, Elijah, and other Christian teachers are set before us as examples so that we should observe their God-fearing conduct and follow their deeds, as it says in **Hebrews 13**: "Remember your teachers, who have spoken the word of God to you; consider the outcome of their way of life and imitate their faith."

## Will all Jews also be brought to the Christian faith and converted before the Last Day?

**A**lthough several of the ancient Church teachers—Jerome, Augustine, and Chrysostom—held the opinion that there would be a general conversion of the **Jews**...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the potential conversion of the Jewish people before the Last Judgment. The author transitions from the theological possibility of their conversion to a harsh polemic, citing historical and legal reasons why such a mass conversion is unlikely to occur.</meta>

...[the] Jews unanimously and consistently believe and affirm, and also proclaim and announce as a sign of the forthcoming Last Judgment. However, because the curse upon them is too heavy—not only the **common and legal curse** <note>original: "maledictio communis & legalis," referring to the biblical curses for breaking the Law of Moses.</note> of which Moses speaks in Deuteronomy 27, but also a **personal and natural one** <note>original: "propria & naturalis"</note> which they inherited from their parents. These parents, at the time of Christ’s capital trial <note>original: "peinlichen Halsgerichts," a legal term for a trial involving corporal or capital punishment.</note>, shouted and cried at the top of their lungs: "His blood be upon us and upon our children" (Matthew 27).

For this reason, God has poured out His displeasure upon them, and the fierce wrath of the Lord has seized them; their habitation has become a wasteland, and there is no one left to dwell in their tents, just as the Spirit of God prophesied in Psalm 69. Thus, such a general conversion of the Jews is more to be wished for than to be hoped for, as Luther indicates in Volume 8 of the Jena edition, folio 56 and following. 

Now, while such a curse could indeed be taken from them, and the blessing and rain of the Lord poured out over them—inasmuch as God reaches out and stretches His arms of grace all day long to a disobedient people who walk according to their own thoughts on a path that is not good (Isaiah 65), and He desires that all men be helped and come to the knowledge of the truth (1 Timothy 2)—as Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335), a Greek historian of the late Byzantine Church.</note> also writes in Book 17, chapter 3: **"God always sharpens the sword of justice with the oil of mercy"** <note>original: "semper Deus gladium justitiæ acuit oleo misericordiæ"</note>.

But they heap up for themselves—according to their stubborn and unrepentant hearts—wrath for the Day of Wrath and the revelation of the righteous judgment of God (Romans 2). They desire the curse, so it has come upon them; they do not want the blessing, therefore it must remain far from them (Psalm 109). For just as their **ancestors** <note>original: "majores"</note> and forefathers invented and poured out all sorts of shameful lies and blasphemies against Christ, so the Jews of today remain persistent in such malice, blaspheming and reviling the Lord Christ and all who are on His side. As Epiphanius <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 315–403), a Church Father known for his "Panarion," a catalog of heresies.</note> writes in Book 1, Volume 2, Heresy 29: **"that they curse Christians three times every single day"** <note>original: "quod singulis diebus ter maledicant Christianis"</note>. 

From this, Ambrose <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397), the Bishop of Milan and one of the four original Doctors of the Church.</note> speaks not wrongly in his commentary on Psalm 61, Volume 2, folio 399: **"The Jews are deservedly estranged from Christ, because they rejected the price of His passion"** <note>original: "Judæi meritò peregrinantur à Christo, quia precium passionis ejus repudiarunt"</note>. And they do not stop at such blasphemy, but also seek after the lives and bodies, the property and blood of Christians. For the author of the <term>Fortress of the Faith</term> <note>original: "Fortalitium Fidei," an influential 15th-century anti-Jewish and anti-Islamic polemic written by the Spanish Franciscan Alfonso de Espina.</note> writes in Book 3, Consideration 7, folio 144, that in the year of Christ 1345, they poisoned all the waters and wells in **Germany** <note>original: "Almania"</note>...

<summary>The author argues that a mass conversion of the Jews is unlikely due to a self-imposed curse and ongoing hostility toward Christianity. He supports this with citations from Church Fathers and historical accusations of well-poisoning.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the alleged crimes of the Jewish people and the theological debate over their conversion, moving from the accusation of well-poisoning to the blood libel and eventually to Martin Luther's interpretation of biblical prophecy concerning their future.</meta>

<sig>K ij</sig>

...[with] the hope that all Christians in that province should die from it. They have often miserably executed and killed the innocent children of Christians, as mentioned by Thomas, Patriarch of Barbary <note>likely referring to Thomas of Cantimpré (c. 1201–1272), a Dominican monk; his work *Bonum universale de apibus* (The Universal Good of Bees) contains some of the earliest written accounts of the "blood libel" accusation.</note> in his *Book of Bees*, chapter 30. For this reason, not only did Philip, the King of France, [expel them] from his kingdom in the year of Christ 1183—as Vincentius Magnus <note>Vincent of Beauvais (c. 1184–1264), a Dominican friar and author of the *Speculum Maius*, one of the most influential encyclopedias of the Middle Ages.</note> writes in his *Mirror of History*, book 30, chapter 25—but many other lords and rulers have also driven them out of their lands and cities. Thus, God the Lord may justly complain of them and say:
> Israel, you bring yourself into misfortune; your salvation lies only with me (Hosea 13).

Luther also writes about this in the <term>Church Postil</term> <note>original: "Kirchen Postill," a collection of sermons by Martin Luther intended for use by clergy and laypeople.</note>, in the Gospel for the second Sunday in Advent, folio 31b. He calls this generation "the Jews," and here this passage clearly proves that the common saying—that all Jews shall become Christians—is not true. They cite the passage from John 10 for this purpose: "There shall be one shepherd and one sheepfold." However, this was fulfilled not when the Jews came to the Gentiles, but when the Gentiles joined the Jews and became Christians in the time of the Apostles. This is how Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430), a pivotal Church Father whose views on the Jews—that they should be allowed to survive but in a state of humiliation—shaped medieval policy.</note> also explains it many times, and as the words of Christ Himself indicate when He says in John 10:
> I have other sheep that are not of this sheepfold; I must bring them also, and they will hear my voice, and there will be one shepherd and one sheepfold.

Here you see clearly that He speaks of the Gentiles who have come to the Jewish sheepfold; therefore, the passage was fulfilled long ago. But here He says: "This generation shall not pass away until the end," meaning that the Jews, who crucified Christ, must remain as a sign; and even if many of them are converted, yet the race and the nature of them must remain.

But because the Prophets and Apostles have clearly prophesied concerning the conversion of the Jews—that the remnant in Israel, who are scattered here and there, shall be gathered from everywhere in the last days when the <term>fullness of the Gentiles</term> <note>A reference to Romans 11:25, a key text in Christian eschatology regarding the timing of the salvation of Israel.</note> has entered, and be brought to the flock of Christ—not so that their kingdom and priesthood should be established again, which they have often vainly attempted (Eusebius, book 4, chapter 6; Philip’s Chronicle <note>original: "Phil. Chron.," referring to the *Chronicon Carionis*, a history of the world updated and edited by Philip Melanchthon.</note>, book 3, folio 70), but so that they shall be dedicated to the Christian faith and taught and converted to the Kingdom of Heaven, we shall fully hope that those prophetic and apostolic...

<meta>This page continues the theological discussion on the potential conversion of the Jewish people before the Last Judgment, transitioning from the interpretation of the "one sheepfold" to the specific prophecies of the Apostle Paul regarding the salvation of "all Israel."</meta>

...prophetic prophecies shall not remain unfulfilled, and that some of the Jews may yet be enlightened before the <term>Last Day</term> <note>The Day of Judgment or the end of the world in Christian theology.</note>. For as Ambrose <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 339–397), the Bishop of Milan and a highly influential figure in the early Church.</note> writes in Volume 3, folio 119: <note>original Latin: "non enim sic illos Deus excidit ait Ambros. Tom. 3. fol. 119. ut non possit iterum inserere si convertantur, qui per Prophetam dixit, revertentes ad me replantabo"</note> "For God has not cut them off in such a way that He cannot graft them in again if they are converted, He who said through the Prophet: I will replant those who return to me." For so writes the Apostle Paul in Romans 11: "I would not have you ignorant, dear brothers, of this mystery, lest you should be proud: blindness in part has happened to Israel, until the <term>fullness of the Gentiles</term> has come in, and so all Israel (<note>original: "Juden und Heyden per Synecdochen"</note> both Jews and Gentiles, by way of <term>synecdoche</term> <note>A figure of speech where a part is used to represent the whole; here, "Israel" is interpreted to mean the entire body of believers, both Jewish and non-Jewish.</note>) shall be saved, as it is written: There shall come out of Zion the Deliverer, and shall turn away ungodliness from Jacob; and this is my covenant with them, when I shall take away their sins." At what time and hour this shall begin and happen, however, is not for us to investigate; it is a mystery, says Paul, which God has reserved for Himself alone to know and to perform. Haymo <note>Likely Haymo of Halberstadt (d. 853), a Carolingian author and bishop known for his biblical commentaries.</note> also writes very Christianly regarding this in his *Commentary on the Epistles of Paul*, folio 32: <note>original Latin: "In his dictis Apostoli quod mysterium sit in lapsu judicium discimus, sed ex qua profunditate consilium veniat, cum ipso Paulo ignoramus, & si ipse sciebat, non tamen manifestavit."</note> "In these words of the Apostle, we learn that there is a mystery in the judgment of their fall, but from what depth the counsel comes, we, along with Paul himself, are ignorant; and even if he himself knew it, he nevertheless did not reveal it."

Therefore, the Jews should be tolerated wherever they live now, both because of such Christian hope—that they shall yet be converted before the Last Day—and also because we have in them a <term>mirror of wrath</term> <note>original: "Zornspiegel," a common Reformation-era concept where a group's suffering was viewed as a visible reflection of God's justice and anger against sin, serving as a warning to others.</note> of the righteousness of God. This is the opinion of Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo.</note> in *The City of God*, book 18, chapter 46, and many other old and new teachers of the Church. However, they must be tolerated only on the condition that they live modestly and peaceably, and do not employ their cursed <term>usury</term> <note>The practice of lending money at interest, which was often restricted or prohibited for Christians but permitted for Jews under specific legal frameworks, frequently leading to social tension.</note> against Christians, as has been decreed in the <term>Imperial Circles</term> and <term>Diets</term> <note>Refers to the administrative and legislative assemblies of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>, such as the Recess of the Imperial Deputation of '77, and as Petrus Denaisius <note>A 16th-century legal scholar who wrote on the procedures of the Imperial Chamber Court (*Reichskammergericht*).</note> writes in his work on the law of the Imperial Chamber. Also, their synagogue gatherings should not be permitted to them, as the <term>Papists</term> <note>A derogatory term for followers of the Roman Catholic Church.</note> sometimes allowed for the sake of the <term>holy penny</term> <note>original: "sancti denarij," likely referring to taxes or fees paid by Jewish communities to the Church in exchange for protection or the right to worship.</note>, according to the Decretals of Gregory, book 5, title 6, concerning the Jews, chapter 7.

<margin>They must be brought to the sermons.</margin>

Rather, they should be compelled as much as possible to hear God’s Word and to attend our beautiful church services, for these are the means that serve and are beneficial to them for faith.

<summary>The author argues that Jews should be tolerated in Christian society both as a "mirror" of God's judgment and in the hope of their eventual conversion, as prophesied by St. Paul. However, he insists this tolerance be strictly regulated by imperial law, forbidding usury and encouraging forced attendance at Christian sermons.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the eschatological discussion, moving from the potential conversion of the Jews to the fate of the Ottoman Empire (referred to as "the Turk") and its role in the events leading to the Last Judgment.</meta>

# Shall the Turk not be uprooted and destroyed 
## before the Last Day?

Regarding this, we find various opinions. Even the Turkish <term>Foqui</term> <note>Likely a corruption of the Arabic term *faqīh*, referring to Islamic jurists, scholars, or preachers.</note> or preachers themselves are said to suspect that their empire will soon fall. Indeed, they calculate 1,000 years from the beginning of <term>Mahomet</term> <note>An archaic Western transliteration of the name Muhammad, the prophet of Islam.</note> until its end and departure. They derive this from a legend that Muhammad, from whom they have their origin, supposedly held up his ten fingers in his final hour of death. <term>Dr. Herlicius</term> <note>David Herlicius (1557–1636), a German physician and astronomer known for his prognostications and "Theologico-Astronomical-Historical" treatises.</note>, in his treatise on theological, astronomical, and historical matters, set the downfall of the Turkish Empire around the year 1600. Others believe it shall reach its end in the year 1670. 

Now, although everyone among us heartily wishes that this sworn enemy of all Christendom were already destroyed, and that we no longer had to fear his tyranny—since he is rightly called a "Turk," which means a devastator and destroyer (though some derive this little word from the river <term>Tirek</term> <note>The Terek River in the North Caucasus.</note>)—because he lays waste to land and people, destroys churches and schools, blasphemes the Most High, and presumes to change times and laws: nevertheless, it is known and recognized from the prophecy of Daniel that he shall remain until the <term>Last Day</term>. For thus speaks the Prophet in Chapter 7:

> But as I looked at the horns, behold, there came up among them another little horn, before whom three of the first horns were plucked up by the roots; and behold, this horn had eyes like the eyes of a man, and a mouth that spoke great things. I watched until the thrones were set in place, and the <term>Ancient of Days</term> <note>A title for God used in the Book of Daniel.</note> did sit, whose garment was white as snow, and the hair of his head like pure wool; his throne was like fiery flames, and its wheels like burning fire. A long fiery stream issued and came forth from before him; thousand thousands ministered unto him, and ten thousand times ten thousand stood before him. The judgment was set, and the books were opened. I watched then because of the voice of the great words which the horn spoke; I watched even until the beast was slain, and its body destroyed, and given to the burning flame.

This little horn is Muhammad or the Turk, as <term>Luther</term> <note>Martin Luther, the primary figure of the Protestant Reformation, who frequently interpreted the Ottoman threat through a biblical, apocalyptic lens.</note> and other theologians explain, before whom three horns from the first ten horns were plucked out: Egypt, Asia, and <term>Græcia</term> <note>Greece.</note>.

<summary>The author discusses various prophecies regarding the end of the Ottoman Empire, noting that while some predicted its fall in 1600 or 1670, biblical prophecy in Daniel suggests "the Turk" will persist as a scourge until the final Judgment Day.</summary>

Moravia, Bohemia, as well as Silesia, will be greatly devastated; but God will powerfully assist the Emperor, so that he will strike down the Turk in the **valleys of the Danube** <note>original: "Vallibus Danubij"</note>, and thereafter, through a Duke of Württemberg, he shall capture Constantinople. ## Will the Gospel be preached in all the world before the Last Day?

Pontif. c. 4."</note> will not concede that the universal expansion and spreading of the Gospel has already occurred in the whole world, but instead still "apes and gapes" for it—just as he does for the **total desolation of the Roman Empire** <note>original: "omnimodam Romani imperij desolationem"; from book 3, chapter 5 of the same work.</note>—seeking thereby to confirm his dream and Papal tradition of the still-impending arrival of the Antichrist; nevertheless, it is known and certain from God’s Word that the Apostles, following their Lord’s command in *Matthew 28*, went out into all the world and taught and preached the Gospel to all creatures. This is clearly shown by St. Paul in *Colossians 1:4, 23*, where he says: "since we heard of your faith in Christ Jesus and of the love for all the saints, because of the hope laid up for you in heaven, of which you have heard before in the word..."

<meta>This page continues the scriptural argument refuting Cardinal Bellarmine's claim that the Gospel has not yet reached the entire world. ...of the truth in the **Gospel** <term>Gospel</term> <note>original: "Evangelio"</note> that has come to you, as it has also into all the world, and is fruitful, as it is in you from the day that you heard it and recognized the grace of God in truth. Thus he also speaks in *Romans 10*: **Their sound has gone out into all lands, and their words into all the world**, which **Ambrose** <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397), Bishop of Milan and one of the most influential ecclesiastical figures of the 4th century.</note> glosses very finely in *Volume 3, page 147*: **He testifies that the preaching of God was heard by the Jews to such an extent, that he even pronounces the world to be filled with the divine announcement.** <note>original Latin: "Usq; adeò, ait, prædicationem Dei à Judäis auditam esse testatur, ut etiam impletum orbem divina adnuntiatione pronuntiet."</note>

For St. **Paul** confesses and testifies of his own person and office that from Jerusalem and roundabout unto **Illyricum** <note>A Roman province encompassing the eastern Adriatic coast; modern-day Balkans.</note> he has fulfilled everything with the Gospel of Christ (*Romans 15*). Now, if this one Apostle preached the Word of God in so many various places, what might the other Apostles have accomplished? Therefore **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339), often called the "Father of Church History."</note> also writes in *Book 3, chapter 8* that in the time of the Apostles, **the sound of the holy Apostles and their words reached the ends of the earth** <note>original Latin: "sonus sanctorum Apostolorum & in fines terræ verba illorum."</note> throughout all the world.

This was also prefigured by the Prophets in **Ezekiel 1** and **Zechariah 6**, where the horses and chariots go out into all four corners of the world; for by this nothing else is signified than that God's Word should be carried about in the wide and broad world. For this reason, the ancients also call the Evangelical ministry of preaching the **vehicle of the Holy Spirit** <note>original: "vehiculum Spiritus Sancti"</note>—a triumphal chariot upon which the Holy Spirit enters into us and performs his work in and upon us. Likewise, they conversely call the Holy Spirit the **soul of the word** <note>original: "animam verbi"</note>, or the soul of the Word and the preaching office, because without his enlightenment and effect, the Bible is a sealed book to us (*Isaiah 29*). For the natural man understands nothing of what is of the Spirit of God; it is foolishness to us, and we cannot reach nor grasp it (*1 Corinthians 2, 2 Corinthians 3*). But the Holy Spirit is the **master-builder** <term>master-builder</term> <note>original: "Werckmeister"; referring to the Spirit as the primary craftsman or architect of a believer's faith.</note> of faith in us, and gives great emphasis to the word and the **outward signs of grace** <term>signs of grace</term> <note>original: "gnadenzeichen"; usually referring to the sacraments or the effective power of the Word.</note> of the word, so that it does not pass without fruit; otherwise all planting and watering would be in vain if God did not give the growth (*1 Corinthians 3*).

Even though today the Word of God is not taught and preached in all places in the world, the greatest part of those with whom it has been have willfully cast it away from themselves, as then...

## Since Christ says in Matthew 24 that before the Last Day, the sign of the Son of Man shall appear in the heavens, it is asked: what sort of sign might this be?

Just as various opinions are found among both ancient and modern Church teachers regarding the **prognostics** <term>prognostics</term> <note>original: "Prognosticis"; signs or omens that predict the future.</note> and signs of the **Last Day** <term>Last Day</term> <note>original: "Jüngsten Tages"; refers to the Day of Judgment at the end of the world.</note>—for some explain them **allegorically** <note>original Latin: "allegoricè." This means interpreting the signs as symbols or metaphors rather than literal physical events.</note>, such as **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420), known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note> in *Book 4 on Matthew*, **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430), a foundational theologian of Western Christianity.</note> in *Volume 4, Questions on Both Testaments, chapter 105*, and **Theophylact** <note>Theophylact of Ohrid (c. 1055–1107), a Byzantine archbishop and influential Bible commentator.</note> on *Matthew chapter 24*. 

Others explain them **naturally, yet in such a way that they signify something supernatural** <note>original Latin: "naturaliter, ita tamen ut supernaturale quid significent."</note>, which is also in accordance with Scripture, regardless of what is spread and circulated against it in the *Book of True Lies*, page 245. <note>This likely refers to a polemical or satirical work of the era criticizing literal interpretations of prophecy.</note> 

Some claim that the firmament of heaven is moving ever closer to us, and the sun no longer stands as high above us as before. This **sentence** <term>sentence</term> <note>In this context, "sentence" means a formal opinion or judgment.</note> and opinion is refuted in detail by **Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a French religious scholar known for his work on chronology and history.</note> in *Exercise 90, folio 2*. 

Likewise, there are various explanations of the "sign of the Son of Man." For **Jerome, Chrysostom, Hilary, Augustine, Theophylact,** and **Euthymius** <note>Euthymius Zigabenus (died c. 1118), a Byzantine monk and theologian.</note> understand it to be the **Cross of Christ**, which shall appear in the air as an indication that the crucified Christ will soon arrive. 

The **Author of the Imperfect Work** <note>original Latin: "Autor imperfecti oper."; refers to the *Opus Imperfectum in Matthaeum*, an ancient commentary on Matthew formerly attributed to Chrysostom.</note> in *Homily 49* believes such a sign will be Christ himself, **bearing the evidence of His passion, or His body marked with wounds, which He will show during the judgment.** <note>original Latin: "habens testimonia passionis, sive corpus ejus vulneribus signatum, quæ in judicio ostensurus."</note> 

**Origen** <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253), an early and influential Christian scholar.</note> in *Tract 30 on Matthew* writes that **the very majesty and glory of Christ returning shall serve in the place of the sign.** <note>original Latin: "ipsa Christi redeuntis majestas ac gloria signi loco futura."</note> 

Some are of the opinion it will be a special victory sign, or an image and shield of a triumph appearing in heaven. Some interpret it as a special miraculous star, and wish to prove it thus: because a special star appeared to the Wise Men in the East at the first coming of Christ, so shall it also be at the final **Advent** <term>Advent</term> <note>original: "Advent"; the "coming" or "arrival" of Christ.</note> of the **LORD**...

## Why then is the Last Day delayed and postponed for so long?

—pleading for sins, says <term>Jerome</term> in Volume 8 of his commentary on Psalm 31. Here [in this life], the forgiveness of sins happens through mercy; there [after death], the retribution according to justice begins. "And he who does not receive the remission of sins here, will not be there; he will not be there, however, because he could not reach eternal life, for eternal life is the remission of sins," says <term>Ambrose</term> in his book *On the Benefit of Death*, chapter 2. <note>original Latin: "Et qui hîc non accipit remissionem peccatorum, illic non erit... quia vita æterna est remissio peccatorum"</note> Thus also writes <term>Cyprian</term> in his first treatise against Demetrian:

> When one has departed from here, there is no longer any place for <term>penance</term>, no effect of satisfaction; here life is either lost or held; here eternal salvation is provided for by the worship of God and the fruit of faith, lest anyone be delayed by sins or by years from coming to attain salvation. For the person still remaining in this world, no penance is too late; the entrance to understanding lies open, and for those who seek and understand, access is easy. <note>original Latin: "Quando istinc excessum fuerit, nullus jam locus pœnitentiæ est... hîc vita aut amittitur, aut tenetur..."</note>

Next, and secondly: although it seems a long time to our eyes before the <term>Last Day</term> comes, with God there is no delay nor a long time. For "one day is before the Lord," says Peter in his second Epistle, chapter 3, "as a thousand years, and a thousand years as one day." Or as Moses speaks in Psalm 90: "A thousand years are before you like the day that passed yesterday, and like a <term>night watch</term>." <note>The "night watch" refers to a traditional division of the night used by ancient soldiers and guards.</note> Now, a night watch is a short time, perhaps three hours, as <term>Augustine</term> says regarding Psalm 90.

Likewise, such a delay—and the lifespan of all people—is slight and small before God; "He will come neither sooner nor later than He promised," <note>original Latin: "nec citius nec tardius quàm promisit venturus est"</note> like a drop of water compared to the sea, and like a grain of sand compared to the sand of the sea, <term>Sirach 18</term>. This is how such a postponement is regarded before God. But just as with God there is no change or variation of time, neither long nor short, so we should also note this for our comfort: that in eternal life it will be the same for the <term>elect</term> <note>original: "Außerwelten"; those chosen by God for salvation.</note> because of the unspeakably great joy that will be there, <term>Isaiah 65</term>, <term>Revelation 21</term>.

Here on earth, indeed, a day of mourning or a year of anguish is very burdensome to many a troubled heart, and lasts a very long time for them, so that they often cry out with <term>David’s little deer</term> for fresh water, Psalm 42, and wish with Paul to be "dissolved" and be with Christ, Philippians 1. But there, a thousand years will be to us like a single day, for in eternal life there is actually no time, Isaiah 66, Revelation 21. Rather, it is <term>eternity</term>, <note>original Latin: "ævum"</note> which is—

<summary>This page quotes Church Fathers like Jerome, Ambrose, and Cyprian to emphasize that repentance must happen in this life, as no mercy is available after death. The author also explains that while the Last Day may seem delayed to humans, it is a mere moment in God's eternal perspective.</summary>

<meta>catchword: bailiff/</meta>

—above time and above motion, according to <term>Scaliger</term> in exercise 259, folio 5. For time and motion suggest imperfection, as Scaliger notes in exercises 18 and 68, and in Book 1 of *On Plants*, folio 253. <term>Pererius</term> also writes of this in Book 13 of his *Physics*, chapters 3 and 4, noting that such imperfection is not to be attributed to glorified bodies. 

Furthermore, the <term>elect</term> <note>original: "Außerwelten"; those chosen for salvation.</note> shall forever shout for joy with a cheerful spirit, "and that joy shall never decrease," says <term>Augustine</term> in chapter 35 of his *Soliloquies*. Because of such immense joy, a thousand years will seem like a single day to the elect, just as a day is to us here. > This shall be the entire reward: that once a person has attained the <term>contemplative life</term>, they may insatiably gaze upon the author of their <term>blessedness</term>, <note>original Latin: "beatitudo"</note> rejoice in Him, obtain from Him what they hoped for, and remain without end in that state which they reached by living holily. <note>original Latin: "Hoc totum erit præmium, ut vitæ contemplativæ semel compos effectus homo..."</note>

By contrast, however, a single day in the fires of Hell will feel longer than a thousand years because of the great agony and eternal torture which the damned suffer; "for just as there is no end to the joy of the good, so there will be no end to the torment of the wicked," as <term>Gregory</term> says in Book 4 of his *Dialogues*, chapter 49. <note>original Latin: "sicut enim non est finis gaudio bonorum, ita non erit finis tormento malorum."</note>

# The Sixth Chapter.

### From whence it comes that these last times before the <term>Last Day</term> are so very difficult, afflicted, and sad, and in what we should find comfort.

Oh, how much talk and lamentation is heard among people regarding war and rumors of war, regarding great inflation and unbearable burdens! Yet there is almost no one who will take it to heart that we daily deserve this through our great sins and earn it well with our transgressions. For our sins cover and overspread the entire earth like a thick black cloud; they rise up like a stinking smoke and soot and come before God's righteous judgment seat, so that He must descend again, as He did at <term>Sodom and Gomorrah</term> in Genesis 18, and pour out from His <term>bowls of wrath</term> <note>A reference to the "vials" or "bowls" of God's judgment mentioned in the Book of Revelation.</note> all kinds of punishments and plagues upon us. For He is no <term>bailiff of sin</term>— <note>original: "Sündenvogt"; an official who oversees or tolerates misconduct. The author is arguing that God does not ignore or preside over sin without punishing it.</note>

<summary>The author describes eternity as a state of timeless joy for the saved and eternal agony for the damned. He then opens Chapter 6 by arguing that contemporary social and military hardships are divine punishments for human sin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument that God is not the cause of human sin, refuting the views of several Reformed theologians. bailiff / who drives and pushes people toward everything they do, whether it be good or evil, as Spindler claims in his report on page 189. Nor does He misuse the thoughts of men, or drag and pull them according to His secret power—as if by a hidden rope—to the end that they always intend, as Rennecher writes in *The Golden Catechism of Solomon*, page 32, and Peter Martyr in *Common Places*, class 2, chapter 2, folio 153. He is a righteous and jealous God, who is not pleased by godless behavior; whoever is evil cannot remain before Him (Psalm 5). His eyes are pure, so that they may not look upon evil (Habakkuk 1). He is a true judge and a GOD who daily threatens; if one will not repent, He has whetted His sword, bent His bow, and aimed it, and has laid deadly arrows upon it; He has prepared His arrows for destruction (Psalm 7).

Since we ourselves deserve such punishments, and daily pile them higher and increase them with our sins, why do we complain and grumble so much? Let each man grumble against his own sin. Let us search and seek out our nature and turn ourselves to the Lord; let us lift up our hearts along with our hands to God in Heaven, saying: "We have sinned and been disobedient; therefore You have rightly not spared us" (Lamentations 3). **For it is not so much the mourning of the sinners that makes God indignant and angry as it is the sinning itself,** says <term>Chrysostom</term> <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Early Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople known for his preaching.</note> in homily 46 on Genesis. <note>original Latin: "Peccantes enim non dolore magis Deum indignari facit & irasci quàm peccare"</note> But if we recognize and confess our sin, He is faithful and just to forgive us the sin and cleanse us from all unrighteousness (1 John 1). **If we confess, God spares; if we acknowledge, He forgives, because the condemnation of sin by us,** as <term>Augustine</term> says on Psalm 110, **is its remission by God.** <note>original Latin: "Si nos confitemur, Deus parcit si nos agnoscimus, ille ignoscit, quia damnatio peccati à nobis... est remissio à Deo."</note>

When we consider how people now behave in these last times toward the noble, dear, and worthy treasure of the Divine Word, we must confess that it is not only the heretics and <term>fanatics</term> <note>original German: "Schwemer" (Schwärmer); a derogatory term for radical reformers who emphasized inner "spirit" over written scripture.</note> who deal with it so falsely and treacherously,

Some make out of Sundays and <term>holidays</term> <note>original German: "Feyrtagen" (Feiertagen).</note> a nocturnal <term>Epicurean life of pleasure</term> <note>original German: "Epicurisches wolleben"; referencing the Greek philosopher Epicurus, whose name was often used in this era to describe those who lived only for physical indulgence and gluttony.</note>, and they imagine that the days of rest were ordained for the very purpose that they should gorge, feast, carouse, and live in all kinds of sin and shame. As **Nazianzenus** <note>St. Gregory of Nazianzus, a 4th-century theologian and Church Father.</note> says: *The feasts of the common people are the gateway to sins* <note>original Latin: "Festa vulgi sunt janua peccatorum".</note>. For certainly on no other day are more drinking-societies and <term>gluttonous banquets</term> <note>original Latin/German hybrid: "Freßconvivia".</note> held than on the feast days themselves. Such <term>beer-heroes</term> <note>original German: "Bierhelden"; a sarcastic term for those who boast of their ability to drink large quantities of alcohol.</note> commit a double sin: first, that they turn the holiday into a day of sin, and second, that they stain and defile themselves with the <term>vice of drunkenness</term> <note>original German: "Saufflaster"; literally the "drinking-vice."</note>, which **GOD** has so earnestly forbidden upon the loss of salvation (Luke 21; Ephesians 5; 1 Corinthians 6; Galatians 5). Moreover, nothing good results from gluttony, as **Solomon** points out in Proverbs 23:

> Where is woe? Where is sorrow? Where is strife? Where is complaining? Where are wounds without cause? Where are red eyes? Namely, where one lingers over wine and comes to drink up what is poured out. Do not look at the wine when it is so red and stands so beautifully in the glass; it goes down smoothly, but afterward it bites like a serpent and stings like an adder. Then your eyes will look for other women, and your heart will speak perverse things, and you will be like one who sleeps in the middle of the sea, and like a sleeper on top of a mast.

<meta>This page continues the critique of social and religious failings, specifically concluding the definition of drunkenness (begun on the previous page) and transitioning into a condemnation of those who break the Sabbath by working or doing business on Sundays. The author cites both biblical law (Exodus, Numbers) and secular law (Constantine, Justinian, and Saxon law) to justify the strict observance of the day of rest.</meta>

<meta>Catchword: and (German: vnd)</meta>

For it <note>This list completes the definition of "Drunkenness" from the previous page.</note> is the mother of all outrages and the source of faults, the root of crimes and the origin of vices; it is a disturbance of the head, a subversion of the senses, a tempest of the tongue, and a storm of the body. It is the shipwreck of chastity, the loss of time, a voluntary insanity, a shameful weakness, a baseness of character, the disgrace of life, the infamy of one’s reputation, and the corruption of the soul. <note>original Latin: "enim est flagitiorum omnium mater... animæ corruptela." This was a common rhetorical list used by medieval and Reformation-era preachers to describe the vice of "Ebrietas."</note>

Some lie over their account books on **Sundays**, waiting for work, and will not allow themselves enough leisure to hear **God’s** word. Such people are an abomination and a horror to the **LORD**, and they cause us all to have to pay the penalty of His wrath along with them. For **GOD** has earnestly commanded that the **Sabbath** be kept holy, as He says in **Exodus 23**:

> Six days you shall do your work, but on the seventh day you shall rest, so that your ox and your donkey may have rest, and the son of your female servant and the stranger may be refreshed.

And for this reason, the word <term>Sabbath</term> <note>original Latin/Hebrew: "Sabbathum" from "שבת".</note> has its origin in the Hebrew word *Shabat*, which means <term>he rested or ceased</term> <note>original Latin: "quievit, cessavit".</note>. *Shabat* or *Shabbaton* means <term>Sabbath, rest, repose, or cessation</term>, signifying that one should rest and keep the day holy from all sins and an evil life, and also from weekly labor—*for that which lacks alternating rest is not lasting.* <note>original Latin: "quod enim caret alternâ requie durabile non est." A quote from Ovid emphasizing that rest is necessary for endurance.</note>

Similar edicts were also promulgated by **Constantine the Great** and **Justinian** <note>Roman Emperors famous for establishing legal codes; Constantine first decreed Sunday as a day of rest in 321 AD.</note> in the <term>Code concerning Holidays</term> <note>original Latin: "l. fin. C. de Ferijs." Referring to the "Codex Justinianus."</note>. Likewise, we read in the <term>Saxon Constitutions</term> <note>original Latin/German: "Constit. Saxon. art. 17. in art. general." A body of laws for the Electorate of Saxony.</note> that those who desecrate Sunday without cause and refuse to keep it holy shall be punished with a fine. What punishment was set for Sabbath-breakers in the Old Testament we read in **Numbers 15**: for when the poor little man gathered wood out of disobedience, he was stoned to death for it.

However, unavoidable necessity is exempted from this, as such things are ordained in the written laws <note>The author cites various sections of the "Digest" (ff.), part of the Body of Civil Law (Corpus Juris Civilis).</note>. Therefore, the **LORD Christ** excuses his disciples who, out of the necessity of hunger, plucked ears of grain and ate them on the Sabbath (**Matthew 12**).

But since we do not regard or even despise **God’s** word, it is indeed no wonder that the **LORD** feeds us with <term>the bread of tears</term> <note>original German: "Thränenbrodt"; a biblical metaphor (Psalm 80:5) for great sorrow and divine punishment.</note>—

<meta>This page continues the author’s polemic against the Calvinists (Sacramentarians). <meta>This page continues the author’s theological argument regarding the "personal union" of Christ's two natures. we say that the flesh of the Lord has been made <term>composite</term> <note>original Greek: σύνθετος (synthetos). This term refers to the "composition" of the divine and human natures into the single person of Christ.</note> with the Godhead because of the <term>personal union</term>. <note>The theological doctrine (unio personalis) that the divine Word and human nature are joined in one individual Person, Jesus Christ.</note> Therefore, the whole Christ is also worshiped according to both natures in a single act of adoration, just as Augustine, Chrysostom, John of Damascus, and other ancient teachers of the Church say:

> I adore the Lord’s flesh—indeed, His perfect humanity—for this reason: that it has been taken up by the Divinity and united with the Godhead, so that I may confess not one and then another, but one and the same Son of God, both God and man.

Furthermore, there are people to be found among us who very rarely practice the *sursum corda* <note>Latin: "lift up your hearts." A traditional call to prayer in the Christian liturgy.</note> and lift up their hearts to **GOD** in heaven through prayer. And even when they do pray occasionally, it happens only out of habit; it is merely an old, cold "mouth-babble" that clings and hovers on the lips, and does not flow from a fire-zeal of faith. The prayer of such people is an abomination to the **LORD**, *Proverbs 28*; it is vain cursing and sin, *Psalm 109*; and it is not heard, *John 9*. For whatever happens without faith does not please God, *Romans 14, Hebrews 11*, as Augustine also says on *Psalm 118*:

> Furthermore, one cries out with the whole heart only when one thinks of nothing else; such prayers are rare for many, but frequent for a few.

And the **LORD** Himself complains about this in *Psalm 50*: "Why do you declare my statutes and take my covenant into your mouth, seeing you hate instruction and cast my words behind you?" And in *Isaiah 29* He says: "Because this people draws near to me with their mouth and honors me with their lips, but their heart is far from me, and their fear of me is a commandment of men which they have been taught; therefore, I will proceed to do a marvelous work with this people, a marvelous work and a wonder, so that the wisdom of their wise men shall perish and the understanding of their prudent men shall be blinded." Cyprian also writes very laudably and well regarding this in his *6th Sermon*:

> What sluggishness it is to be led away and captured by foolish and profane thoughts when you pray to the Lord, as if there were anything else you should be thinking about more than that you are speaking with God? How can you ask to be heard when you do not even hear yourself? Do you want God to be mindful of you when you ask, while you yourself are not mindful of yourself? This is to fail entirely to guard against the enemy; this is to offend the majesty of God through negligence in prayer when you pray to the Lord—

<meta>This page continues the author's moral exhortation regarding prayer. The previous page ended with a quote from St. Cyprian warning against negligence and "sluggishness" in prayer, where one's mind wanders while speaking to God. This page completes that thought and moves to address specific groups who neglect prayer or pray with unforgiving hearts.</meta>

<sig>N ij</sig>
<meta>Catchword: Some</meta>

—verence, that is, to be awake with the eyes but to sleep with the heart, whereas a Christian ought to be awake in the heart even when he sleeps with his eyes. <note>The author concludes the quote from Cyprian, emphasizing that true prayer requires the "watchfulness" of the soul, not just the physical posture of being awake.</note>

Some people follow in the footsteps of the <term>Prodicians</term> <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic sect who believed they were "children of the King" and thus not bound by common religious laws or the necessity of outward prayer.</note> and <term>Adamites</term> <note>A radical sect that sought to return to the "innocence of Adam" before the Fall, often rejecting traditional religious practices.</note>, and do not consider it necessary to pray constantly because God Himself knows, sees, and understands what we need better than we do. But what does the Scripture say about this?

> Ask, and it will be given to you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you. *Matthew 7*.

For even though our distress and concerns are not unknown to the **LORD**, He nevertheless wants us to call upon Him, so that we may be reminded of from whom we receive and obtain so much good without ceasing, as Augustine also writes in chapter 7 of *On the Gift of Perseverance*:

> *God could have given to us without our praying, but He willed to remind us through our prayer from whom we receive these benefits.*

However, we do not question the **LORD** about our distress as if He did not know it, nor do we reveal our lack to Him as if He did not understand it; rather, we only ask the profoundly godly, merciful **GOD** and Father that He would graciously have mercy on us and change and lessen our state of need. Therefore Jerome says on *Matthew 6*:

> *We are not narrators, but petitioners; it is one thing to narrate to one who is ignorant, and another to request from one who knows. In the former, we inform faithfully; in the latter, we entreat piteously.* <note>original Latin: "Non sumus narratores, sed rogatores." Jerome distinguishes between telling God "news" (which is impossible) and asking God for "mercy" (which is necessary).</note>

Some people pray while meanwhile living in hatred, envy, and enmity with their neighbor; such people pray against themselves. For how can they receive forgiveness of sins from God if they will not forgive their neighbor? How should good come to us from above if we refuse good to our neighbor? Therefore, Christians should live peaceably and quietly with one another, and gladly forgive their offenders quickly while they are still on the way, as the **Lord** Christ also admonishes us in *Mark 11*:

> When you stand and pray, forgive if you have anything against anyone, so that your Father in heaven may also forgive you your failings.

So also writes Tertullian in his book *On Prayer*, Volume 2:

> *Let us not ascend to God’s altar before we resolve any discord or offense we have contracted with our brothers. For what is it to approach the peace of God without peace? To approach the remission of debts while retaining them? How shall he who is angry with his brother appease the Father, when all anger has been forbidden to us from the beginning?*

<summary>The author refutes the idea that prayer is unnecessary by citing Augustine and Jerome, arguing that prayer reminds us of our dependence on God. He then warns that prayer is invalid if the petitioner harbors malice or refuses to forgive their neighbor.</summary>

Some are much too small in faith / consider themselves far too lowly and unworthy / that God should hear their prayer / *and their prayer, as Bernard says in Sermon 4 for Lent, is seriously hindered by faint-heartedness of spirit and immoderate fear.* <note>original Latin: "pusillanimitate spiritus, & timore immoderato."
