A clever wordplay meaning "His character/customs and His postponements."</note> For God does not always hear us according to our will / as we also often do not know what we ask / *Matthew 20.* but according to what he knows / is useful and good for us: His help is of two kinds / just as our distress and concerns are / spiritual and physical: Spiritual help is always with the godly / even though physical temptation still burdens them / and external need still presses us / and if God knows / that it is useful and healthy / to rescue from physical needs / then he has the power and the ability to change such things / for he is the mighty **LORD** of the strong mountain / who alone is powerful / *Psalm 65.* and abundantly more

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of patience in prayer. It transitions from the reasons for God’s delays to the spiritual comfort found in suffering, describing the Christian life as a "road of misery" that leads to eternal joy.</meta>

<sig>N iij</sig>

can do / more than we humans can understand and comprehend / *Ephesians 3.* Therefore we should not become weary and tired in prayer / but always persevere / *for he does not want to give quickly so that you may learn to desire more ardently*, <note>original Latin: "ideo enim non vult citò dare ut discas ardentius desiderare." This sentiment, common in the writings of the Church Fathers, suggests that God’s silence is a tool to strengthen the believer's spiritual hunger.</note> as also Macarius writes in *Homily 9: He who does not quickly receive the desired good things is, through this delay and the long-suffering of God, more inflamed and aroused, so that he expects the heavenly goods more ardently.* <note>St. Macarius (c. 300–391) was an Egyptian monk and hermit; his homilies were highly regarded in the Reformation era for their focus on the internal work of the Holy Spirit.</note> And when God defers and postpones his help / it certainly happens for our best / *it is not out of adversity; but for our benefit, because he does not hear many according to their will, so that he may hear them for their salvation*, Isidore, *Sententiae, book 3, chapter 7;* Augustine, *Tractate 6 on the Epistle of John.* <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636) was a scholar-bishop; the author quotes him to show that "unanswered" prayers are often a mercy, preventing us from receiving things that would harm our souls.</note> From which we should also hope / that it will certainly happen one day / and be richly compensated / if not in this <term>state of mourning</term> <note>original: "Trawrstande." This refers to the earthly life, viewed as a temporary period of exile and sorrow compared to the joy of heaven.</note> and in this life / then certainly in that heavenly life of joy / *for that which God is about to grant, if he delays it, he does not take it away; he who promised does not act with levity, nor is he easily moved from his purpose; what he promised is fixed, he cannot deceive; he has the means by which to do it,* Augustine on *Psalm 65.*

If, however, the necessity of divine providence is signified, Scripture itself teaches that God's decrees are immutable; from which it follows that the fall of Adam, since God decreed to permit it, [occurred] necessarily..."

...happened <note>original: "accidisse." This completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the necessity of the Fall of Adam.</note>. We read similar things in many other <term>Calvinists</term> <note>Followers of John Calvin; here the term is used polemically by a Lutheran author to suggest they deviate from "correct" doctrine.</note>, whose testimonies cannot all be introduced here: Calvin, *Institutes* book 3, chapter 23, sections 1, 7, and 8; Beza in the *Colloquy of Montbéliard on Predestination*, page 519; Zanchius, book 5, *On the Nature of God*, chapter 1, question 4, thesis 2, page 588; Rennecher, *Golden Salt of the Catechism*, chapter 6, page 28; Bucanus, place 14, pages 133, 136, 137; Polanus, *Catholic Symphony*, chapter 2, thesis 2, page 113; Piscator, volume 1, *Theological Disputations*, place 6, pages 136, 137; the Heidelberg [theologians] in the Appendix, page 173 and following.

Now, although this monstrous teaching is not new, nor was it first devised and spun by the Calvinists, but was recently hatched from the <term>Stoics</term> <note>Ancient Greek philosophers who believed that all events are determined by fate.</note> and the <term>Manichaeans</term> <note>An early religious movement often accused by Christians of believing that human nature is inherently evil and lacks free will.</note>—as Cicero writes about this in his book *On Divination*, and Gellius in book 6, chapter 2—it is nonetheless a truly abominable blasphemy which brings all sorts of absurdities with it. For if everything should and must happen by necessity, then the <term>free will</term> of man in civil and worldly matters is abolished, so that he may not do what he wishes, as <term>St. Bernard</term> <note>Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential French abbot.</note> indicates in his book *On Grace*:

->But God allows to each of us the freedom of life and a sense [of will], not fixing necessity upon one side or the other.<- <note>original Latin: "Atqui Deus permittit unicuique nostrum libertatem vitae, sensumq; non necessitatem in alterutrum affigentem."</note>

Also <term>Gregory of Nyssa</term> <note>A 4th-century church father known for his philosophical approach to theology.</note> in book 7 of *Philosophy*, chapter 1.

It is in vain that one establishes anything praiseworthy, gives good laws and order, exhorts toward the good, and warns against the evil, or preaches and hears God's Word. It is for nothing that Christ died for us, and that we believe in Christ. It is unjust for God, or the government, to punish evil, because it truly had to happen by necessity—as even the servant of <term>Zeno</term> <note>Zeno of Citium (c. 334–262 BC), the founder of the Stoic school of philosophy.</note> (who was the Patriarch of the Stoics, according to Laertius in book 7, *Life of Zeno*) said when he was being led to torture:

->that he was being punished unjustly, because he had been forced by fate to sin.<- <note>original Latin: "se injustè plecti, quia fato coactus esset peccare."</note>

It is unjust that GOD subjects people to temporal and eternal punishment because of their sins, if not only our first parents were created for the purpose that they had to sin, but also because we ourselves sin by necessity—as the Calvinists publicly defend and represent such monstrous paradoxes. For thus writes <term>John Calvin</term> clearly in book 3 of the *Institutes*, chapter 23, section 7:

->Whence did it happen that the fall of Adam wrapped so many nations, along with their infant children, in eternal death without remedy, if not because it so seemed fit to God? Here tongues, otherwise so talkative, must be silent. I confess, indeed, that the decree is horrible; yet no one can deny that God foreknew [it].<- <note>original Latin: "Unde factum est, ut tot gentes unà cum liberis eorum infantibus, æternæ morti involveret lapsus Adæ absq; remedio, nisi quia Deo ita visum est? Hic obmutescere oportet tam dicaces alioqui linguas. Decretum quidem horribile fateor, inficiari tamen nemo poterit, quin præsciverit Deus." Because **God** now wills that men should and must sin, and He steers, incites, and drives them toward it—much like someone leads a dog or a pig on a rope, and either lets it go forward or pulls it back, and steers it where he wishes—how shamelessly do the **Heidelberg [theologians]** <note>Refers to the faculty at the University of Heidelberg, the intellectual center of German Reformed (Calvinist) theology.</note> write in the *Report*, chapter 7, page 313. <meta>This page continues the argument against the Calvinist view of necessity. While the previous page discussed how Reformed theologians allegedly make God the author of sin by comparing humans to arrows or animals on a leash, this page shifts to the distinction between God's foreknowledge and His active decree. The author argues that God's knowledge of future sins does not cause them to happen, using the analogy of an astronomer predicting an eclipse.</meta>

...furthermore [He] forbade such things to our first parents / Genesis 2. But since **God** knows, sees, and hears how men, against His will and command, willfully wallow in the filth and mire of sin, He sends us all kinds of punishments and plagues, so that we should turn back, improve our lives, and become more pious.

And this is also the reason why He has allowed prophecies in the Scripture long ago concerning war and rumors of war, and other heavy punishments for sin, which are to precede the **Last Day**; not that such things shall and must happen of necessity, but because He, as an all-knowing **God**, knows well that in the last days, abominable, <term>Sodomitic sins</term> <note>A common early modern term for grievous or unnatural sins, often referring generally to extreme moral decay or specifically to sexual immorality, used here to describe the state of the world before the apocalypse.</note> will be in full swing—as is now evident before our eyes. For because we humans sin, **God** lets such punishments come, but not because it was previously proclaimed. 

For foreknowledge is not the cause of things, as **Calvin** <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), the primary figure of the Reformed tradition. The author here disputes Calvin’s assertion in the *Institutes* regarding the link between God's foreknowledge and His will.</note> would have it in *Institutes*, Book 3, Chapter 23, Section 6, page 316, and **Zanchi** <note>Hieronymus Zanchi (1516–1590), an Italian Protestant Reformer whose works on the nature of God were foundational for Reformed scholasticism.</note> in *On the Nature of God*, Book 5, Chapter 1, Question 4, Thesis 2, page 588. Instead, it is knowledge determined by <term>secondary causes</term>. <note>In theology, God is the "First Cause," while the choices of humans or the laws of nature are "secondary causes." The author argues God foresees what humans choose to do, rather than causing them to do it.</note> <note>original Latin: "Præscientia enim non est caussa rerum, ut vult Calvin... Sed notitia ad caussas secundas determinata."</note> 

As **Timpler** <note>Clemens Timpler (1563–1624), a German philosopher and physicist whose work bridged the gap between Aristotelianism and Protestant theology.</note> also writes in *Metaphysics*, Book 4, Chapter 2, Question 25: 
> The foreknowledge of **God** is not the <term>efficient cause</term> <note>The actual agent or force that brings something into being. The author is saying that God's knowing about the future doesn't make the future happen, just as knowing it will rain doesn't cause the rain.</note> of the things foreknown; for a thing is not going to happen because **God** foreknows it, but rather because it is going to happen, **God** foreknows it. <note>original Latin: "Præscientia Dei non est caussa efficiens præscitorum, quia non ideo aliquid futurum est, quia id præscit Deus, sed quia futurum est, ideo Deus præscit."</note>

Just as an **astronomer** proclaims beforehand that an eclipse of the sun or moon will occur, and yet such an eclipse does not happen because the astronomer predicted it, but because of the alignment of the two great heavenly bodies: So also has **God** proclaimed in His Word that in the last days there will be a sorrowful time; yet this does not happen out of necessity or because it was previously indicated, but because we deserve it with our sins. If we were now to do true penance and cry out to **God** for relief and rescue with heart-felt devotion, then the **Lord** would turn from His wrath and leave a blessing behind Him; for with the holy He is holy, and with the upright He is upright, Psalm 18. Therefore, change your wickedness through repentance, and **God** will change His judgment. <note>original Latin: "Muta igitur malitiam per pœnitentiam, & Deus mutabit sententiam."</note>

We have an example of this in the **Ninevites**, to whom **God** had announced their final destruction and the downfall of their city through the prophet **Jonah**; but when they believed in **God** and turned to the **Lord** with heartfelt regret and penance...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the biblical Ninevites from the previous page. The author uses their story to prove that God’s decrees of punishment are conditional—meaning they can be changed if people repent—rather than absolute or "necessary" as the Calvinists claim. This leads into a new chapter regarding the Second Coming of Christ.</meta>

<sig>D ij</sig>

...[then] **God** relented of the disaster that He had said He would do to them, and He did not do it, **Jonah 3**. <note>original German: "ha rewet dem HERREN des vbels / daß er geredt hatte / vnd thets nicht"</note> Likewise, we should also turn to **God** from the bottom of our hearts and earnestly pray, *deliver us from evil* <note>original Latin: "libera nos à malo"</note>, and then He will spare us and do what is best and dearest for us. For the legalistic <term>sermons of punishment</term> <note>original German: "Straffpredigten." These were sermons specifically focused on God's judgment and the consequences of sin, intended to provoke fear and repentance.</note> occur **specifically** <note>original Latin: "determinatè"</note> and not **absolutely**, [but] with the **condition**: what if men do not improve and become more pious? As **Augustine** <note>Aurelius Augustinus (354–430), Bishop of Hippo and one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers.</note> writes in his *Second Exposition on Psalm 31*: **If God has covered sins, He did not wish to notice them; if He did not wish to notice them, He did not wish to take heed of them; and if He did not wish to take heed of them, He did not wish to punish; He did not wish to acknowledge them, He preferred to forgive.** <note>original Latin: "Si texit peccata Deus, noluit advertere, si noluit advertere, noluit animadvertere, & si noluit animadvertere, noluit punire, noluit agnoscere, maluit ignoscere." This is a play on Latin legal and sensory terms; if God "covers" a sin, He chooses not to "see" it for the purpose of legal judgment.</note>

It is therefore abominable to hear that everything must happen out of necessity; by this, not only is **God** proclaimed as the author and sustainer of all evil, but His **providence** <note>The protective care of God or of nature as a spiritual power.</note> and foresight are also abolished. As **Taurellus** <note>Nicolaus Taurellus (1547–1606), a German philosopher and physician who argued for the independence of philosophy from theology, often clashing with orthodox views.</note> clearly writes, it would be unnecessary for God to sustain the world, *On Eternal Things*, Book 1, page 159, and *On the World*, Book 2, Chapter 5, page 240—or [the world] is made subject to the laws of Nature. In short, everything that is good is annihilated in this <term>un-theological Paradox</term> <note>In the 17th century, a "paradox" often referred to a statement or doctrine that was contrary to received opinion or common sense, often used pejoratively here to mean "absurdity."</note>, and conversely, all evil is established. We may rightly say: **defenders of fate are fools**, **Augustine**, *Tractate 31 on John*; **and if your heart were not foolish, it would not believe in fate**, **Augustine**, *Tractate 37 on John*. <note>original Latin: "defensores fati sunt fatui... & si cor tuum non esset fatuum, non crederet fatum." Augustine often argued against the Stoic or pagan idea of "fatum" (blind fate), insisting instead on divine providence and human will.</note>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the necessity of a Final Judgment. Since the world is currently "upside down"—with the wicked prospering and the righteous suffering—the author argues that divine justice requires a future day of reckoning to balance the scales. He uses vivid metaphors of roses among thorns and foxes in a vineyard to describe the current state of the Church.</meta>

...always just, punishing evil, but also merciful, rewarding good. Now, however, in this old and misshapen world, there is such a distressed state and confusion that our **LORD God’s** little roses must allow themselves to be severely pricked and broken by the wild thorn-hedges; they wander on the road of misery from one accident to another. Conversely, however, the thistles and thorns bloom and flourish, and it goes well for the godless. They are in no danger of death but stand firm like a palace; they are not in misfortune like other people and are not plagued like other men. Therefore, their defiance must be a precious thing, and their outrage must be called "well done." Their person struts like a fat belly; they do whatever they only think of. They annihilate everything and speak evil of it; they speak and blaspheme from on high. Whatever they speak must have been spoken down from heaven; whatever they say must be valid on earth, **Psalm 73**.

Because the evil fare well in this world and the pious fare poorly, a general **Day of Judgment** <term>Day of Judgment</term> <note>original German: "Gerichtstag." In Christian theology, the final and eternal judgment by God of every nation.</note> must certainly be at hand, on which **God** will judge the world with righteousness, **Psalm 9**, punishing the evil according to their merit and rewarding the pious, so that each one receives according to how he has acted, **2 Corinthians 5**. 

Furthermore, the **old serpent** <note>A traditional biblical reference to the Devil or Satan, originating from the Book of Genesis and Revelation.</note> along with her hellish swarm and vermin still crawls and creeps forevermore within the enclosure of the **LORD Christ’s** Church, and the stinking goats are found right in the middle of the sheepfold of the Christian Church. These become extremely vexing and burdensome to the little flock of our highly-deserving **Immanuel** <note>A Hebrew name appearing in the Book of Isaiah meaning "God with us," used here as a title for Jesus Christ.</note>. Likewise, the **heretical foxes** <note>original German: "Kezerischen Füchse." A common metaphor in the Reformation era for false teachers or heretics who "spoil the vines" of the Church.</note> do great damage in Christ’s vineyard and dig thirstily after the imperishable seed of the **Gospel** <note>original Latin/German: "Evangelij."</note>. By this, the great **King of Glory** and Judge of all the world is moved to soon set out and, with right earnestness and zeal, sweep and cleanse His dearly-earned community from such blasphemers and criminals. He shall cast the old hellish serpent with all her henchmen into the eternal fire and repay all the stinking goats for their wickedness and lust upon their heads and into their bosoms, **Isaiah 66**. 

Therefore, we should not, along with the <term>Sadducean sow-teachers</term> <note>original German: "Sadduceischen Sewlehrern." This is a derogatory term for those who, like the ancient Jewish sect of Sadducees, denied the resurrection of the dead. The "sow" imagery was a common 17th-century polemic suggesting their teachings led to a filthy, immoral life.</note>, regard the sermons of the **Last Day** as a human poem or fable and then sin bravely without repentance or fear. Instead, we must certainly respect it; the **LORD** will soon...

<meta>catchword: come-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that a Final Judgment is necessary because, in this life, the wicked often prosper while the righteous suffer. He uses agricultural and animal metaphors to describe the corruption within the Church and warns against those who deny the coming resurrection.</summary>

...come with many thousand saints to hold judgment over all, and to punish all the godless for all the works of their godless conduct by which they have been godless, and for all the harsh things that godless sinners have spoken against him, **Epistle of Jude, verse 15**. He has already prepared his throne for judgment, **Psalm 9**, and has set a day on which he will judge the circle of the earth with righteousness through a man in whom he has decreed it, and he offers faith to everyone after he has raised him from the dead, **Acts 17**.

## But who shall be the judge in this Final Judgment? <term>Final Judgment</term> <note>original German: "Jüngsten Gerichte," literally "the Youngest Judgment." This is the standard German term for the Last Judgment or the end of the world.</note>

Concerning this, we find infallible instruction in the Scripture: that it shall be the **LORD Christ**, who has ascended into heaven, sits at the right hand of God, his Almighty Father, from whence he shall come to judge the living and the dead. For the Father judges no one, but has given all judgment to the Son, so that they all may honor the Son just as they honor the Father, **John 5**. So we also read in **Matthew 25**: when the Son of Man shall come in his glory, and all the holy angels with him, then he shall sit upon the throne of his glory, and all nations shall be gathered before him. And St. Peter says in **Acts 10**: Jesus of Nazareth is ordained by God to be a judge of the living and the dead. Again, Paul speaks in **Acts 17**: God will judge the world through a man in whom he has decreed it. This man is Christ, as the Apostle explains in **2 Timothy 4**: "So I testify before God and the Lord Jesus Christ, who is to come to judge the living and the dead, at his appearing and with his kingdom." So also teaches the Evangelist and Apostle John in **Revelation 1** <note>original Latin: "Apoc. 1," short for Apocalypsis.</note>: "Behold, he comes with the clouds, and every eye shall see him, even those who pierced him, and all tribes of the earth shall wail. Yes, Amen." However, this is not to be interpreted as if the power of judgment did not also belong to the Father and the Holy Spirit; for just as there is only one Divine being and one and the same essential attributes among all three Persons...

<summary>The author cites various New Testament passages (Jude, Acts, John, Matthew, Timothy, and Revelation) to prove that Jesus Christ is the divinely appointed judge for the Last Day, while noting that this authority is shared within the Trinity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Final Judgment, transitioning from Christ’s appointment as judge to an exploration of the Trinity’s shared authority and the specific role of Christ’s two natures—divine and human—in the judgment process. It concludes by beginning a scriptural justification for the role of his humanity.</meta>

<meta>catchword: man</meta>

of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; so also is the **authority of judgment** <note>original Greek: ἐξουσία κρίσεως</note>, the power to hold court, common to all three Divine Persons, as far as it concerns the consensus and the authority, or the majestic power and glory. For just as the Father created and made the world and everything in it through the Son (**John 1, Psalm 33**), so He will also judge the world through the Son (**Acts 17**). But as far as the proclamation and the execution of the judgment is concerned, that the **LORD Christ** alone will perform. Therefore, Augustine <note>St. Augustine (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian of the Western Church.</note> writes in his 21st treatise on John: "This was said because in the judgment, no one but the Son will appear to men: the Father will be hidden, the Son manifest. In what way will the Son be manifest? In the form in which He ascended: for in the form of God He is hidden with the Father; in the form of a servant He is manifest to men. Therefore, the Father judges no one, but has given all judgment to the Son in a manifest way. For this was said because in the judgment no one but the Son will appear to men, and that openly; in which manifest judgment the Son will judge, because He Himself will appear to those who are to be judged." <note>original Latin: "Hoc dictum est... adparebit."</note>

## Will Christ judge the world according to both natures? <term>Naturen</term> <note>In Christian theology, this refers to the "Two Natures" of Christ—the belief that He is simultaneously fully God (divine nature) and fully man (human nature).</note>

Although the <term>Sacramentarians</term> <note>A term used by Lutherans to describe Reformers (like followers of Zwingli or Calvin) who disagreed with the physical presence of Christ’s body in the bread and wine of the Lord's Supper.</note> cannot deny that the LORD Christ will come again in the clouds on the Last Day, even according to His assumed human nature; nevertheless, they will not allow that the Person of Christ shall judge the world according to **both** natures. "The judgment," they say, "will truly be of divine power, but the ministry will be of the assumed nature" <note>original Latin: "judicium, ajunt, verè erit potentiæ divinæ, sed ministerium assumtæ naturæ."</note> (**Admonition of Neustadt against the Book of Concord, page 23; Zanchi, Book 2 on the Nature of God, chapter 1, question 3, thesis 3, folio 76; Sadeel on the Truth of the Human Nature, folio 191; Anhalt in the defense against Johann Matthäus, page 239 and following; Lorenz Ludwig in the doctrine of the Melanchthon school, page 175**). But because we wish to clarify such a question according to the form and norm of God's Holy Scripture, it is clear and manifest from it that the whole Christ, according to both natures, shall be the judge of all people. For the Father has given power to the Son to hold judgment, **because He is the Son of Man**... <note>original Greek: ὅτι ὑιὸς ἀνθρώπου</note>

<summary>The author argues that while the authority to judge is shared by the Trinity, the visible execution of judgment belongs to Christ alone. He specifically disputes with 'Sacramentarians' to assert that Christ judges using both His divine and human natures.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Two Natures of Christ, St. Augustine, Sacramentarians, Son of Man, authority of judgment, divine power, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that Christ judges the world according to both His divine and human natures. It provides scriptural and patristic (writings of early Church Fathers) evidence to counter the view that His humanity is merely a passive instrument in the judgment.</meta>

...He has authority, because He is the Son of Man <note>original Greek: ἐξουσίαν ἔχει (*exousian echei*)</note> / John 5. If the **LORD Christ** has now received might and power from the Father to hold court as the Son of Man—as Gualterus <note>Rudolf Gwalther (1519–1586), a Swiss Reformed pastor and influential theologian.</note> also testifies in his *Commentary on John*, leaf 79—how then can His humanity be excluded from this? Is it not rather the case that whoever holds the office of judge <note>German: *Richterampt*</note> is the judge? Christ holds the office of judge according to both natures: "as God He possesses innate sovereignty, as man He receives it by decree," <note>original Latin: *ut Deus innatum habet imperium, ut homo decreto accipit*</note> according to Theodoret <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–457 AD), a bishop and influential commentator on the Psalms.</note> on Psalm 2. According to His divine nature, He possesses it naturally, because He is the only-begotten Son and the essential radiance of the Father; according to His human nature, He possesses it personally through grace <note>original Latin: *ex gratia personaliter*</note>, because both natures are personally united with one another. Therefore, Christ is the judge according to both natures. This is also explained very finely by the ancient teachers of the Church, such as Cyril <note>Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444 AD), a patriarch known for his defense of the union of Christ's natures.</note> in Book 2 on John, chapter 144:

> "For this reason He adds, 'because He is the Son of Man,' so that we might understand that all things were given to Him as a man, who, being a creature, has nothing of Himself; but the only-begotten Son is not a participant in life, but is life by nature, just as the Father is." <note>original Latin: *Ideò subjicit... sicut pater est.*</note>

And Augustine in his 22nd treatise on John:

> "According to this, He received the power of judging because He is the Son of Man; for according to His being the Son of God, He always has this power. He who was raised received it; He who was born received it; He who was crucified received it." <note>original Latin: *Secundum hoc... crucifixus est.*</note>

Secondly, everything that is given to Christ in time is to be understood according to His human nature; for according to His divinity <term>Gottheit</term> He has and retains all things unchangeably, without humiliation or exaltation, from eternity to eternity. Now, however, the **LORD Christ** has been given power, honor, and a kingdom over all nations in time—the might and power to hold judgment—therefore, this is to be understood according to His human nature. The second point of this concluding argument is to be proven not only from the little verse cited and referenced above, but the prophet Daniel also witnesses to it in Chapter 7:

> "I saw in this vision by night, and behold, one came in the clouds of heaven like a Son of Man, even to the Ancient of Days, and was brought before Him; He gave Him power, honor, and a kingdom, so that all peoples, nations, and tongues should serve Him." <note>A reference to Daniel 7:13-14, a key prophecy of the Messiah.</note>

The **LORD** Himself also explains it in Matthew 26:

> "It shall surely come to pass," He says, "that the Son of Man will come in the glory of His Father, with His angels, and then He will reward every man according to his works." <note>Though cited as Matthew 26, this text mirrors Matthew 16:27, where Christ discusses His role as judge.</note>

Thus also writes Augustine in Book 3 on the Creed, chapter 7.

<margin>Through- / these.</margin>

<summary>The author uses citations from the Church Fathers (Cyril, Augustine, Theodoret) and the Bible (Daniel, Matthew) to argue that Christ’s authority to judge is not just a divine attribute, but a power given specifically to His human nature as the 'Son of Man.'</summary>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological argument that Christ performs His role as Judge through both His divine and human natures. The catchword at the bottom of the previous page was "Was," transitioning into this discussion of the "Son sitting at the right hand."</meta>

The fact that the Son is said to sit at the right hand of the Father demonstrates that the very man whom Christ took up has received the power of judging.

Third, this is also required by the personal union of the two natures in Christ. Because this union is inseparable and indissoluble—so that both natures constitute only one person—both natures also work and judge, not *separately* <note>original Greek and Latin: διηρημένως (*diē rēmenōs*) / *disjunctim*</note>, but *together* <note>original Greek and Latin: ἡνωμένως (*hēnōmenōs*) / *conjunctim*</note>. Thus, the divine *activity* <note>original Greek: ἐνέργεια (*energeia*)</note> is not *deprived* <note>original Greek: ἄμοιros (*amoiros*)</note> of the human, nor the human of the divine, according to the rule of Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian of the early Church.</note>, as cited by Damascene <note>John of Damascus (c. 675–749 AD), whose work "On the Orthodox Faith" codified much of the doctrine on Christ's natures.</note> in Book 3, chapter 19. And Christ performs *human things divinely and divine things humanly.* <note>original Greek: τὰ ἀνθρώπινα θεϊκῶς καὶ τὰ θεῖα ἀνθρωπίνως (*ta anthrōpina theikōs kai ta theia anthrōpinōs*)</note>

Fourth, Christ will return in a visible manner, just as He was taken up into heaven (Acts 1). Now, the **LORD Christ** cannot ascend and descend *visibly* <note>original Latin: *visibiliter*</note> according to His divine nature, but only according to His human nature. Therefore, this visible appearance and judgment will also be according to His human nature, as Tertullian <note>Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD), an early Christian author from Carthage known for defending the reality of Christ's physical body.</note> in his book *On the Flesh of Christ*, chapter 24, and Alcuin <note>Alcuin of York (c. 735–804 AD), a key scholar of the Carolingian Renaissance.</note> in Book 3 *On the Faith* have written very laudably and well:

> "The judge will appear in such a form as can be seen, both by those He is about to crown and by those He is about to damn." <note>original Latin: *Talis adparebit judex, qualis videri possit, & ab ijs quos coronaturus, & ab ijs quos damnaturus.*</note>

Fifth, Christ will hold the final judgment according to the nature in which He was judged, so that the ungodly might see him whom they have pierced (Zechariah 12, Revelation 1). But Christ was judged according to His human nature; *therefore*, He will also hold the final judgment according to that same nature. This is also explained by Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), the most influential theologian in Western Christianity.</note> in Sermon 64 *On the Words of the Lord*:

> "That form which was judged here shall judge; He who stood before a judge shall sit as judge; He who was made a false criminal shall condemn the true criminals." <note>original Latin: *Forma illa judicabit, quæ hic judicata est, sedebit judex, qui stetit sub judice, damnabit veros reos qui factus est falsus reus.*</note>

And in Treatise 21 on John:

> "For He will come in human form; this the ungodly shall see, and those placed at the right hand shall see it, and those separated to the left shall see it, just as it is written: 'They shall look on him whom they pierced.' If they are to see him whom they pierced, they will see the body itself, which they struck with a spear. The Word <note>The *Logos* or divine essence of the Son.</note> is not struck by a spear; for in this [the body] the ungodly were able to see what they were also able to wound. They will not see the God hidden within the body; after the judgment He will be seen by those who will be at the right hand." <note>original Latin: *Sic enim veniet in forma humana... post judicium videbitur ab his qui ad dexteram erunt.*</note>

# What assistants and assessors will Christ the Judge have?

Of this the **LORD Christ** himself gives us report and says: Truly I say to you / that you / who have followed me / in the regeneration <note>The "regeneration" or "renewal of all things" refers to the restoration of the world at the Second Coming.</note> / when the Son of Man shall sit / upon the throne of his glory / shall also sit / and judge the twelve tribes of Israel / Matthew 19. And again he says / I will appoint the Kingdom to you / as my Father has appointed it to me / that you shall eat and drink at my table / and sit on thrones / and judge the twelve tribes of Israel / Luke 22. Thus also says Paul / do you not know / that the saints shall judge the world / 1 Corinthians 6. From these words we see and understand / how the Apostles and other saints / will be the assessors <term>Assessors: Assistant judges who sit beside the primary judge to offer counsel or signify agreement.</term> and assistants of Christ the Judge on the Last Day: Such judging by the Apostles will happen / first, through consensus and approval <note>original Latin: *consensu & adprobatione*</note>, in that they will be highly pleased by the final judgment of Christ / which he will place and pass over the ungodly / heartily loving and praising it / and with one accord singing together the Song of Moses the servant of God / and the Song of the Lamb / they will call out with a joyful voice / Great and wonderful are your works / LORD Almighty God / just and true are your ways / you King of Saints: Who shall not fear you / and praise your name? For you alone are holy / all heathens shall come and worship before you / for your judgments have been made manifest / Revelation 15.

After that, they will judge through the preaching of the Word <note>original Latin: *verbi prædicatione*</note>, through the Word / which they have preached / heard and believed / for God will judge the secrets of men / through JESUS Christ / according to the Gospel, Romans 2. As the LORD himself also explains / John 12. Whoever despises me / and does not receive my words / has already the one who judges him / the Word which I have spoken / that will judge him on the Last Day. For no new judgment will be conceived and formulated in the Last Judgment / but what is taught here / that will then be executed and fulfilled / whoever believes shall be saved / but whoever does not believe / shall be damned / John 3.

judge the world / **by the judgment of examples** <note>original Latin: *Exemplorum dijudicatione*</note>, because they have gone before the ungodly with good examples / by which the ungodly were more often offended than improved / therefore they shall step forward / and condemn and banish the ungodly / because they would not improve themselves through either their teaching / nor through their examples / and convert to **GOD** with true repentance / as **Christ** indicates / *Matthew 12.* The people of Nineveh <note>The capital of the ancient Assyrian empire; its people famously repented after Jonah's preaching.</note> will rise up with this generation / (with the blinded Jewish people) / and will condemn it, for they did penance after the preaching of **Jonah** / and behold, here is more than **Jonah**: The Queen of the South <note>Traditionally identified as the Queen of Sheba.</note> will rise up at the Last Judgment with this generation / and will condemn it / for she came from the ends of the earth / to hear the wisdom of **Solomon** / and behold, here is more than **Solomon**.

## Will Christ the Judge descend from heaven in a physical, spatial way when He returns to hold judgment?

<image-desc>Large ornamental initial N at the beginning of the first paragraph.</image-desc>

**N**ow, because the whole of **Calvinism** <term>A branch of the Protestant Reformation; here the author criticizes their more symbolic or "reasonable" interpretations of scripture</term> is built upon the sandy foundation of human reason / neither can the **Sacramentarians** <term>A polemical term for those who denied the real, physical presence of Christ's body in the bread and wine of the Lord's Supper</term> judge or conclude anything regarding Christ’s ascending and descending / regarding Christ’s presence everywhere / regarding heaven and eternal life / regarding the most venerable Holy Supper / and other articles of faith / except what is reasonable, natural, and spatial: For **Dame Hulda** <note>original: "Fraw Hulda." A figure from German folklore, here used to personify human Reason as a meddling or limited mistress who leads people astray.</note>, Reason, knows not how to predict anything else / just as the natural man understands nothing else of the things of the Spirit of God / for it is foolishness to him / he cannot grasp or reach it / *1 Corinthians 2, 2 Corinthians 3.*

> "He does not grasp," says **Ambrose** <note>original Latin: *Non assequitur...*</note> <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397), an influential Bishop of Milan and a "Doctor of the Church."</note> in Volume 3, folio 165, "anything except what he sees, nor does he think it possible for things to happen except in the way he already knows; therefore, whatever he hears that is different from what he knows, he judges to be foolish."

As long as the "white and black night-spirit" / which **Zwingli** <note>Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), a leader of the Swiss Reformation. The author is mocking Zwingli's claim that a spirit—whether "black or white"—appeared to him in a dream to suggest a specific interpretation of the Eucharist.</note> himself mentions in Volume 2, folio 249 / keeps the Calvinists imprisoned under the rule of reason / we have nothing better to expect from them / but if they should and would / (which is more to be wished for than hoped) / compel their reason into the obedience of faith / as **Paul** desires in *2 Corinthians 10*: Then they could become one with us in the foundation / of which the **churches of the Palatinate** <note>A territory in the Holy Roman Empire that famously toggled between Lutheran and Calvinist doctrines during this era.</note> otherwise boast without cause in the *Heartfelt Admonition*, page 25. And we would gladly receive them into our spiritual brotherhood / which they have indeed always sought, but never obtained / as **Luther** indicates in the *Jena Edition, Volume 3, folios 339, 341, and 533.*

> "For there can be no union in the church," says **Martyr** <note>original Latin: *nulla enim in ecclesia...*</note> <note>Likely referring to Peter Martyr Vermigli (1499–1562), a Reformed theologian.</note> himself in *Common Places*, class 4, chapter 6, folio 789, "among those who do not maintain the same faith."

But what should we wish for / what should we hope for / the Sacramentarians have involved themselves so deeply in obedience to reason / that they believe nothing else and let nothing else be valid / except what is uniform and measurable to reason / as **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430), whose writings were used as authorities by both Lutherans and Calvinists.</note> rightly writes of such people / in the book *On the Usefulness of Believing*, chapter 1.

> "Men who have progressed and fallen too far into these bodily things, think that nothing else exists, except that which..." <meta>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</meta>

...perceive through these five best-known messengers of the body <note>The five senses: sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch.</note>, and the impressions and images they have received from these, they wish to take with them even when they attempt to withdraw from the senses. From their deadly and most deceptive rules, they think they can most correctly measure the ineffable depths of truth.

For, to come to the discussion of the proposed question, they speak altogether carnally of such a high mystery, since they dream up for themselves such a heaven where Christ has gone, which is a created, spatial, and circumscribed <term>A theological term meaning limited by physical boundaries or "fenced in" to a specific location</term> place.

They believe and teach that Christ ascended in a spatial manner, from place to place <note>original Latin: *de loco in locum*</note>.

<meta>This page continues the argument that heaven is not a physical, geographic location but a spiritual state. It uses biblical descriptions of the end of the world to contrast the "created heavens" (the sky/atmosphere) with the "heaven of the blessed" (the eternal presence of God).</meta>

-defiled and unfading inheritance (1 Peter 1). For the created heavens will pass away in fire on the **Last Day** <note>Judgment Day or the end of the world.</note> with a great crash; the elements will melt from heat, and the earth and the works within it will be burned up, as Peter clearly speaks (2 Peter 3). The heaven of the blessed, however, will not pass away, but will stand eternally. The righteous shall rejoice and be glad within it without ceasing. Therefore, the heaven of the blessed is neither created nor made: **it is not created, but prepared** <note>original Latin: *non est creatum sed præparatum*</note> (Isaiah 64, John 14, Matthew 25).

Furthermore and primarily, heaven is not a natural, circumscribed place where the **elect** <note>Those chosen by God for salvation.</note> have their certain cells and private spaces, like monks and nuns in a cloister, as the **Scholastics** <term>Medieval theologians who used formal logic and philosophy to explain Christian doctrine; the author here critiques their overly literal or "physical" descriptions of the afterlife.</term> write of it: **Bonaventure** <note>Saint Bonaventure (1221–1274), a prominent medieval Italian theologian.</note> (*Sentences*, book 1, distinction 2, page 36); and **Bullinger** <note>Heinrich Bullinger (1504–1575), a Swiss Reformer whose views on the presence of Christ were often at odds with the author's Lutheran perspective.</note> in the book on the words of John 14:2: **"In my Father's house are many mansions"** <note>original Latin: *In domo Patris mei mansiones multæ sunt*</note>.

For even the philosophers say **that outside of heaven there is neither place, nor vacuum, nor time** <note>original Latin: *quod extra cœlum nec locus sit, nec vacuum nec tempus*</note> (**Aristotle**, *On the Heavens*, book 1, chapter 9; **Commentary of Coimbra**, *Physics*, book 8, chapter 10); meaning that beyond the visible heavens, there is neither place nor time. These things belong only to this life, just like eating and drinking, and other such things we require here.

3. Likewise, the holy angels are in heaven, yet they are not in a certain place **by circumscription, but in a "where" by definition** <note>A technical theological distinction: "circumscription" means being physically contained by boundaries (like water in a glass); "definition" means being present in a location without being physically limited by its dimensions.</note>.

4. So too the glorified bodies are not circumscribed in a certain place, for they are like the angels (Matthew 22)—not in essence, but in their properties and qualities. The glorified bodies are **definitively in a certain "how"** <note>original Greek: *pō*—implying they exist in a specific state or manner rather than at a physical coordinate.</note>.

5. If heaven were a certain place, it would also have to be a certain **body** <note>original Latin: *corpus*</note>; and in that case, heaven would in turn have to be within another place, **and this in turn in another, and so on; but this is absurd and un-theological** <note>original Latin/Greek: *hoc vicissim in alio, & sic consequenter, sed hoc atopon est atheologon*. The author argues that if you define heaven as a physical box, you need a bigger box to hold it, leading to infinite nonsense.</note>.

Since heaven is not a created, natural, or circumscribed place, it follows undeniably that the Lord Christ did not ascend, nor will He return, **in a spatial manner** <note>Moving through physical space from point A to point B.</note> from one place to another. <meta>This page continues the theological argument that Christ is not physically restricted to a distant "heavenly location." The previous page established that heaven is a spiritual state of God's presence rather than a geographic place with physical dimensions. This page applies that logic to the nature of Christ, arguing that because he is at the "Right Hand of God," he is omnipresent—even in his human form—rather than locked away until the end of time.</meta>

On the contrary, Scripture reports that Christ is in all places and at all ends, **"where two or three are gathered in my name"** (Matthew 18), and that he is with us until the end of the world (Matthew 28). This is not according to his divinity alone, or according to his spirit—as Sohn <note>Georg Sohn (1551–1589), a Reformed professor at Heidelberg.</note> (*Works*, volume 3, page 64), the Anhaltini <note>Theologians from the Principality of Anhalt who adopted Reformed/Calvinist views.</note> in the *Apologia* (page 17), Beza <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), the successor to John Calvin in Geneva.</note>, Calvin, Junius <note>Franciscus Junius (1545–1602), a prominent Reformed theologian.</note>, and all other **Calvinists** <note>Followers of John Calvin; here used as a polemical label for those who argued that Christ’s physical body must be in one specific location in heaven.</note> would have it—but also according to his assumed human nature, and thus according to his entire person and both natures.

**"For the Word is nowhere without the flesh, nor the flesh without the Word"** <note>original Latin: *verbum enim nusquam est sine carne, neque caro sine verbo*</note> as **Cyril** <note>Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444), an early Church Father central to defining the union of Christ’s divine and human natures.</note> also says (book 12 on John, chapter 32): **"He is indivisibly and entirely in all things, since he who is one everywhere is in no way divided"** <note>original Latin: *Impartibiliter atque integrè in omnibus est, cum unus ubique sit nullo modo divisus*</note>.

Hence he did not wish to speak of himself as being separate, says **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine (354–430), the influential North African bishop and theologian.</note> on Psalm 56, **"because he did not wish to be separate, saying: 'Behold, I am with you even unto the end of the age'"** <note>original Latin: *quia noluit esse separatus, dicens, Ecce ego vobiscum sum usque ad consummationem seculi*</note>.

Furthermore, Christ ascended above all heavens so that he might fill all things (Ephesians 4) and is exalted to the **Right Hand of God**, from whence he shall come to judge the living and the dead. Now, however, the Right Hand of God is no specific or spatial place in heaven, as **Bullinger** <note>Heinrich Bullinger (1504–1575), a Swiss Reformer who argued for a more literal, localized heaven.</note> writes (in his treatise on the words of the Supper and in the book on celestial mansions against **Brenz** <note>Johannes Brenz (1499–1570), a Lutheran leader who defended the doctrine of Christ’s omnipresence (Ubiquity).</note>, pages 43, 52, 60). For no created place—whether within, outside, above, or below all heavens—can be so blessed, so glorious, and so holy that it can or may be called the "Right Hand of the Lord." For the same is nothing other than the infinite majesty and glory, the great power and omnipotence of God, which is **circumscribed** <term>Limited or enclosed within physical boundaries or coordinates.</term> by no place, enclosed in no place, and excluded from nowhere. Rather, it is present everywhere and fills all in all, creates all, governs and sustains all, as David witnesses (Psalm 139):

> **"If I take the wings of the morning and dwell in the uttermost parts of the sea, even there your hand would lead me, and your right hand would hold me"** <note>Psalm 139:9-10.</note>.

It follows therefore undeniably that Christ, in his final **Advent** <note>The Second Coming of Christ at the end of the world.</note> and return for judgment, will not descend from heaven in a spatial manner—moving from one place to another and thereby leaving the Right Hand of God. Instead, during his visible future coming and appearance, he will remain sitting invisibly at that same Right Hand. One will perceive through his works that this almighty and omniscient Judge is truly exalted, as a man, into the majesty of the Right Hand of God, as the Lord

<meta>This page continues the argument against the spatial limitation of Christ's body. Having established that the "Right Hand of God" is not a physical throne but God's omnipresent power, the author now addresses Christ's return (the Second Coming). He argues that Christ "descending" from heaven is an act of revelation rather than physical movement through space, and disputes the Reformed view that Christ’s reign at the Right Hand will eventually end.</meta>

himself indicates (Matthew 26): "From now on you will see the Son of Man sitting at the right hand of power and coming in the clouds of heaven."

For just as John the Baptist saw the Spirit of God descending in the form of a dove and coming over Christ (Matthew 3)—and yet such a <term>descending</term> <note>original Latin: *descendere*</note> or coming down is and means nothing other than a <term>manifesting</term> <note>original Latin: *manifestare*</note> or revealing—so also will the **LORD** Christ return for judgment while nevertheless remaining in heaven at the Right Hand of God. And such a "descending" will be nothing other than a visible appearance and revelation of the flesh of Christ before all the world.

## Will Christ’s sitting at the Right Hand of God not then cease after his return?

It is true that **Calvin** <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), the French theologian whose followers argued Christ’s body remains locally "enclosed" in heaven until the end of time.</note> (Book 2 of the *Institutes*, chapter 14, section 3) maintains that **Christ** shall sit at the Right Hand of God, dominating and reigning until the end of the world, and then he shall cease and hand over the rule <note>original German: *Regiment*</note> to the Father. But this is a completely unscriptural and blasphemous opinion. For an eternal power has been given to the **Lord** Christ, which cannot be taken from him (Daniel 7). He has received an eternal rule that does not cease but lasts eternally, just as **Pierius** <note>Urbanus Pierius (1546–1616), a theologian involved in the "Crypto-Calvinist" controversies in Saxony.</note> also confesses in the colloquy <note>A formal theological debate or conference.</note> held with **Hunnius** <note>Aegidius Hunnius (1550–1603), a leading Lutheran theologian who defended the physical omnipresence of Christ's body.</note> and other visitors in the year [15]92 on the 21st of July, page 25. Therefore Paul says that we too shall reign with him (2 Timothy 2). This is certainly true, he says: if we die with him, we shall live with him; if we endure, we shall reign with him; if we deny him, he will also deny us; if we do not believe, he remains faithful, for he cannot deny himself. Similarly, we read in Hebrews 2 that although everything is put under Christ's feet, we do not yet see that everything is subject to him; we will only truly experience this in that life to come.

Furthermore, this is also provable from the personal union <note>The theological doctrine that Christ's divine and human natures are perfectly joined in one person.</note>, which remains indissoluble for eternity; for Christ sits at the Right Hand of God according to his assumed human nature, and

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>The author explains that Christ's "descent" at the end of time is a visible revelation of his already-present body, not a physical journey. He also refutes Calvin’s idea that Christ’s reign will end, asserting instead that his rule and the union of his two natures are eternal.</summary>

<keywords>Second Coming, Descent of Christ, Revelation, John Calvin, Urbanus Pierius, Aegidius Hunnius, Personal Union, Right Hand of God, Eternal Reign, Lutheranism</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the eternal reign of Christ. The previous page established that Christ's "descent" for judgment is a revelation of his presence rather than a physical movement. Here, the author uses the doctrine of the Personal Union to prove that Christ’s rule at the Right Hand of God is eternal, refuting John Calvin’s interpretation of 1 Corinthians 15.</meta>

and has received all power in heaven and on earth according to the same <note>The human nature of Christ.</note> because of the <term>Personal Union</term> <note>The theological doctrine that Christ's divine and human natures are perfectly joined in one person.</note>. Since the union of the natures lasts eternally, it follows that such a sitting and such infinite power must also last eternally and not cease. For if the truth of our salvation, says **Fulgentius** <note>Fulgentius of Ruspe (c. 467–533 AD), a North African bishop known for his defense of orthodox Christianity against Arianism.</note> (Book 1 to Thrasimund, page 166), is believed to be in the Savior, it follows that in one and the same mediator of God and men, the man Jesus Christ, the undivided and unconfused perfection of the divine and human substance must not be denied. For the Son of God, begotten of the Father's substance, neither lost the eternity of his divine nature while taking up that which was temporal for the eternal salvation of temporal beings; nor did he lack the lordship of his natural and true divinity—he who elevated the truth of the "servant’s form" <note>A reference to Philippians 2:7, describing Christ taking on human nature.</note> he had accepted with an endless and un-losable lordship. <note>original Latin: "Si enim veritas nostræ salutis... in Salvatore creditur, consequenter in uno eodemq; quasi mediatore Dei & hominum, homine Jesu Christo divinæ humanæq; substantiæ indivisa & inconfusa perfectio non negetur, quoniam Dei Filius de patris substantia genitus, nec sempiternitatem divinæ naturæ perdidit, dum hoc quod erat temporale pro temporalium æternâ salute suscipit; nec dominio naturalis & veræ divinitatis caruit, qui veritatem formæ servilis acceptam interminabili & inamissibili dominio sublimavit."</note>

What **Calvin** objects against this—namely, how Christ will hand over the kingdom to God the Father, as Paul says in 1 Corinthians 15—is easy to answer. For one must distinguish the administration and rule from the style and manner of the same. For Christ is not going to hand over the kingdom to the Father, but rather the manner of ruling <note>original Latin: "non enim regnum traditurus est Christus patri sed modum regiminis?"</note>. Now the **LORD** rules in the midst of his enemies through the ministry and the office of preaching; but then such a manner of rule will cease, and Christ will reign in the <term>triumphant church</term> <note>original Latin: "ecclesia triumphante"; the community of believers in heaven who have achieved final victory over sin and death.</note> without the assistance of the ministry and the assault of enemies. Therefore, the handing over of the kingdom is not a loss [of it], as **Hilary** <note>Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD), a prominent Latin theologian.</note> says (Book 11 *On the Trinity*), which is apparent from Christ's own words; for he says, "All things have been handed over to me by my Father; all power is given to me." Therefore, if to have given is to have lacked, the Father also lacked those things which he gave. But the Father did not lack them by handing them over; therefore, the Son also cannot be understood as being about to lack those things which he hands over to the Father. <note>original Latin: "Traditio igitur regni non est amissio... ait enim omnia mihi tradita sunt à patre, data est mihi omnis potestas: Ergô si dedisse caruisse est, pater his quoque; caruit, quæ dedit: Sed pater tradendo non caruit, igitur nec Filius intelligi potest cariturus his quæ patri tradit."</note>

## In what form, however, will Christ return? In humility and lowliness, or in power and glory?

...to enthrone and confirm us as great princes of heaven through faith; however, in the second and final return, he will not appear in such a form, but magnificent and powerful, in great majesty and glory.

> For he will come, says **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers.</note> (Book 3 *On the Creed*, chapter 8), who first came in secret, now manifest in power; he who was judged will come to judge; he who stood before man is to judge every man. God will come manifestly. What does it mean that God will come manifestly? Not as a humble man before, but as the God-man, sublime in majesty, he will come and judge. <note>original Latin: "veniet enim ille, ait Augustinus lib. 3. de Symb. c. 8. qui prius venit occultus, in potestate manifestus, ille qui judicatus est, veniet judicaturus ille qui stetit ante hominem, judicaturus est omnem hominem: Deus manifestè veniet: Quid est Deus manifestè veniet? non sicut prius homo humilis, sed sicut Deus homo majestate sublimis veniet & judicabit."</note>

But in what form such a majestic return shall proceed and happen, the prophetic and apostolic writings report to us in detail. First, the **Lord** Christ will come in a visible manner with a "shout of command" <note>original Greek: *ἐν κελεύσματι* (en keleusmati)</note>, with a great battle cry (1 Thessalonians 4), which goes forth with power before the arrival of Christ and, as it were, admonishes all people to open doors and gates, for the **Lord** of Glory shall appear. 

Secondly, the voice of the **Archangel** will be heard (1 Thessalonians 4). For just as the first coming of Christ was announced by an **Archangel** (Luke 1), so also the second and final <term>Advent</term> <note>From the Latin *adventus*, meaning "arrival" or "coming."</note> of Christ will be proclaimed and cried out by an **Archangel**. But what kind of voice it will actually be, we cannot know now, because the Scripture indicates nothing of it; "it is the mark of a learned theologian to be ignorant of many things." <note>original Latin: *eruditi Theologi est multa ignorare*</note> **Theophylact** <note>Theophylact of Ohrid (c. 1055–1107), a prominent Byzantine commentator on the Bible.</note> is of the opinion that it will be such a voice: "prepare yourselves all, for the judge is at hand" <note>original Latin: *apparate vos omnes, judex enim præsto est*</note>: O you children of men, prepare and ready yourselves; behold, the Judge of all the world is near. 

Thirdly, the **Lord** will come down from heaven with the trumpet of God (1 Thessalonians 4). And he will let this powerful, thundering voice be heard: "Arise, you dead" <note>original Latin: *surgite mortui*</note>, stand up you dead and come before the judgment, as Christ himself explains (John 5): "Truly, truly I say to you, the hour is coming, and is now here, when the dead will hear the voice of the Son of God, and those who hear it will live." For it is the voice of Him who is the resurrection and the life (John 11). Therefore, it penetrates and rings out into all the graves of the dead, so that the deceased can no longer rest and sleep therein, but must arise and appear before Christ's judgment seat. And regarding this, **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note>

# How should we now dispose and prepare ourselves for such an arrival
## of Christ?

Because the **Last Judgment** <note>German: *leßte Gericht*</note> shall proceed so very earnestly and strictly, it is wholesome, useful, and necessary that we prepare ourselves worthily for it; for then everything will be revealed and brought to light which is secret and hidden (**Matthew 10, 1 Corinthians 4**). Mankind must give an account for every idle word they have spoken (**Matthew 12**), as it also stands in the <term>Book of Wisdom</term> <note>The Wisdom of Solomon, anেজ deuterocanonical book often cited in early modern theology.</note>, Chapter 1: he who speaks unrighteousness cannot remain hidden, and Justice, which shall punish him, will not fail; for the plots of the godless must come before the court, and his speech must come before the **Lord**, so that his vice may be punished. Thus also **Daniel** prophesies how the books shall be opened on this great day of judgment (**Daniel 7**): the Book of Moses and of the Lamb, the Book of Life and of Death, of the Law and the Gospel; and from these the *final sentence* <note>original Latin: *sententia definitiva*</note> shall be *solemnly proclaimed* <note>original: *solemniter promulgirt*</note> and effectively carried out. As also the Evangelical **John the Seer** <note>original Latin/German: *Vident Joannes*</note> prophesies of this in **Revelation 20**:

> I saw a great white throne, and him who sat upon it, from whose face the earth and the heaven fled away; and no place was found for them: And I saw the dead, both great and small, stand before God, and the books were opened, and another book was opened, which is the book of life; and the dead were judged according to the writing in the books, according to their works: And the sea gave up the dead which were in it; and death and hell gave up the dead which were in them; and they were judged, every man according to his works: And death and hell were cast into the fiery lake; this is the second death: And if anyone [was] not

There will also be no more reconciliation or intercession there; *for now is the time of forgiveness for those who repent, but after death will be the time of vengeance for those who neglected to confess their crimes* <note>original Latin: "nunc enim est tempus remissionis pænitentibus, sed tempus erit post mortem vindicationis negligentibus confiteri scelera sua"</note>, Augustine, Sermon 66 *On Time*. Such preparation and readiness, however, happens with true <term>penitence</term> <note>The author uses both the Latinate "pænitetz" and German "busse" to describe a deep, transformative regret for sin.</note> and repentance, *which consists not in leaves or branches, as Gregory says in 1 Kings 15 <note>In older biblical traditions, 1 Samuel was often titled 1 Kings.</note>, but in fruit* <note>original Latin: "quæ non in folijs aut ramis... sed in fructu consistit"</note>; that we have heartfelt regret and dread over committed sins, and with faithful devotion sigh and cry out that God would not look upon our great sins, but pronounce us free of them out of grace for Christ's sake. For in such a manner our names are recorded and written in the **Book of Life**, when we recognize and confess our sins, and hold ourselves to the only Atone-maker and Savior **Jesus Christ**, who cleanses us from all unrighteousness and rescues us from the coming wrath (**1 Thessalonians 1**). If we say we have not sinned, we make him a liar, and his word is not in us.* Therefore Bernard also writes in Sermon 2 *On the Day of the Lord's Nativity*, page 39: *Whoever does not ardently desire penance seems to say by his works that he does not need penance* <note>original Latin: "Quisquis non ardenter desiderat pænitentiam, videtur operibus dicere, non indigere se pænitentia"</note>,

**A**lthough the Christian Church here in this <term>vale of tears</term> <note>German: "Jammer-thal." <meta>Continuity: This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the mortality of the human body. It transitions from acknowledging physical decay to the theological promise of the resurrection, specifically addressing the skepticism of reason versus the assurance of Scripture.</meta>

<sig>A</sig>

...and decay, yet He will awaken us anew and bring us out of the graves. Because this far surpasses all human reason, keenness of mind, and understanding, it is necessary to briefly explain and prove this highly important <term>article of faith</term> <note>German: "Glaubensartickel." A fundamental doctrine that a Christian must believe.</note> regarding the resurrection of the dead from the foundation and ground of Divine Holy Scripture. For otherwise, no one would believe it if it were not grounded in the Scripture and could be clarified from it, since it seems altogether unbelievable and impossible to reason. Flesh and blood cannot grasp how a person who has died and been turned to ashes—who not long ago became <term>maggots and rot</term> <note>German: "Mot vnd Kot." A visceral rhyming phrase emphasizing the physical reality of decomposition.</note>, dust and earth—should come forth alive again from the dust and be clothed with eternal immortality. 

For this reason, the <term>Sadducean sow-teachers</term> <note>German: "Sadducæischen Sewlehrer." A harsh polemical term. The Sadducees were a Jewish sect that denied the resurrection; "sow-teacher" was a common Reformation-era insult for those perceived as teaching "filthy" or beastly errors.</note> among the Jewish people (*Matthew 22*), as well as *Hymenaeus* and *Philetus* (*2 Timothy 2*), and other mockers and despisers today, are so strongly and powerfully bewitched by reason that they deeply despise and laugh at this great mystery and openly deny the <term>resurrection of the flesh</term> <note>German: "Aufferstehung des Fleisches."</note>. Therefore, for our comfort and the constant, true refreshment of our hearts in this difficult <term>pilgrimage</term> <note>German: "Pilgrimsschrafft." The theological concept that life on earth is a temporary journey toward heaven.</note>, we wish to firmly ground, strengthen, and prove this article of faith from both the Old and the New Testament.

## Will the resurrection of the dead certainly happen and begin on the Last Day? <note>German: "Jüngsten Tage," literally "the youngest day," referring to Judgment Day.</note>

**A**lthough a natural person, when following his naked reason, can understand and grasp nothing of such a high mystery—because it is an article of faith and not of sight—nevertheless, the Holy Spirit often presents to us the <term>book of nature</term> <note>original Latin: "librum naturæ."</note> alongside the <term>book of Scripture</term> <note>original Latin: "libro scripturæ."</note>. He wants us to look at the <term>sermon of the fields</term> <note>German: "Feldpostille." A "postil" is a book of sermons; here, the author suggests nature itself acts as a preacher of God's truths.</note>: the little seeds and the blades of grass. For just as the seed first dies in the earth and then shoots forth and grows again in the beautiful springtime, so it is with our decayed little bones, or with our bodies. They are placed into their little chambers of sleep so that they may rest from all their labor (*Isaiah 56, Revelation 14*). They are sown in <term>God’s Acre</term> <note>German: "Gottesacker." A traditional German name for a cemetery, viewing the graveyard as a field where bodies are planted as seeds for the resurrection.</note>, so that they

<summary>This page argues that while the resurrection of the body seems impossible to human reason, it is a foundational biblical truth. The author compares the burial of the dead to the planting of seeds in a field, which "die" only to rise again in the spring.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page follows the discussion of biblical precedents for the resurrection (such as Lazarus and the daughter of Jairus) and begins to argue that the Resurrection of the Dead is a logical and theological necessity based on the core Articles of Faith in the Christian Creed.</meta>

also hereafter be further fulfilled according to the Word of the LORD.

Finally, this work and mystery of the Resurrection of the Dead is also established and proven in and through other Articles of Faith:

I. From the Article of the Incarnation of Christ <note>The doctrine that God the Son became human in the person of Jesus.</note>; for because he has taken on our flesh and blood, and we are bone of his bones and flesh of his flesh (Ephesians 5), we are hereby—through this personal assumption of our nature and the close relationship of his body with our bodies—as if born again to a living hope of the Resurrection of the Dead and of an imperishable nature, which Jesus Christ has brought to light, and conversely, has stripped death of its power (2 Timothy 1).

II. From the Article of the Suffering and Death of Christ; for because Christ has redeemed the whole human being, not the soul alone but also the body, it follows that the body is also freed from sin, death, and the devil; although it must indeed die because of the sin that clings to it, it shall nevertheless rise again by the power of the suffering and death of Christ.

III. From the Article of the Resurrection of Christ; for if Christ is risen, then we shall also not remain in the grave, as Paul explains in detail in 1 Corinthians 15. Since Christ destroys the "mother of death," which is sin, and overcomes its "father," which is the devil, and furthermore swallows up death itself eternally (Isaiah 25), so that this <term>strangler</term> <note>original: "Würgehals," a graphic 16th-century term for a murderer or personification of death.</note> may no longer rule over us. **He holds no real power, but only the mask of power** <note>original: "non imperium, sed larvam habet imperij"</note>, and though we must hold our *rest* <note>original: "requiem"</note> in the earth for a short time, he will yet wake us up again, and as he lives, so shall we also live (John 14).

IV. From the Article of Holy Baptism; for the whole human being is baptized and born again, not the soul alone—as the <term>Sacramentarians</term> <note>A polemical term used here to describe theologians like John Calvin who held different views on the physical nature of the sacraments.</note> claim (Calvin, *Institutes* Book 4, Chapter 15, Section 14; Heidelberg Report, Chapter 2, page 49)—but also the body. *For just as the generation* <note>In this context, "generation" refers to physical birth.</note> *is of the whole human, so also is the regeneration* <note>original: "sicut enim generatio totius hominis est, ita & regeneratio"</note>, as witnessed also by Daneau in his *Commentary on Augustine’s Enchiridion* (Chapter 25, page 80). Since our bodies have now become a Temple of God in Baptism (1 Corinthians 3 and 6), and our members are members of Christ (1 Corinthians 6), they must also be kept blameless for the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ (1 Thessalonians 5).

V. From

<summary>This page argues that the resurrection of the body is a necessary consequence of major Christian doctrines, including the Incarnation, the Atonement, and Baptism. It emphasizes that Christ redeemed the whole person—body and soul—rather than the soul in isolation.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the theological argument regarding the physical resurrection of the dead. Finally, this work and mystery of the Resurrection of the Dead is also established and proven in and through other Articles of Faith. 

**I.** From the Article of the Incarnation <term>Incarnation: The Christian doctrine that God took on human form in Jesus Christ.</term> of *Christ*; for because he took upon himself our flesh and blood, and we are bone of his bone and flesh of his flesh (*Ephesians 5*), we are hereby through this personal assumption of our nature and the close kinship of his body with our bodies, as if reborn to the living hope of the Resurrection of the Dead and of an imperishable nature, which *Jesus Christ* has brought to light, and conversely has stripped death of its power (*2 Timothy 1*). 

**II.** From the Article of the Suffering and Death of *CHRIST*; for because *Christ* has redeemed the whole human being—not the soul alone but also the body—it follows that the body is also freed from sin, death, and the devil; although it must indeed die because of the sin that clings to it, it shall nevertheless rise again by the power of the suffering and death of *CHRIST*. 

**III.** From the Article of the Resurrection of *CHRIST*; for if *CHRIST* is risen, then we shall also not remain in the grave, as *Paul* explains in great detail (*1 Corinthians 15*). For *CHRIST* destroys death’s mother, which is sin, and overcomes its father, which is the devil, and furthermore swallows up death itself eternally (*Isaiah 25*); so that this cutthroat <note>original: "Würgehals" — literally "strangle-neck," a vivid Early Modern German term for a killer or executioner.</note> may no longer rule over us; <note>original: "non imperium, sed larvam habet imperij"</note> **he holds not a true empire, but only the mask of an empire**, and even if we must keep our rest <note>original: "requiem"</note> in the earth for a short time, he will yet wake us again, and as he lives, so shall we also live (*John 14*). 

**IV.** From the Article of Holy Baptism; for the whole person is baptized and reborn, not the soul alone, as the *Sacramentarians* <term>Sacramentarians: A term used by some reformers (often Lutherans) to describe those who held a more symbolic view of the sacraments, such as Calvin or Zwingli.</term> would have it (*Calvin, Institutes Book 4, Chapter 15, section 14; also the Heidelberg Report, Chapter 2, page 49*), but also the body; <note>original: "sicut enim generatio totius hominis est, ita- & regeneratio"</note> **for just as the generation of the whole man exists, so also does the regeneration**, as witnessed also by Danaeus <note>Lambert Daneau (c. 1530–1595), a prominent French Reformed theologian.</note> in his *Commentary on Augustine’s Enchiridion, Chapter 25, page 80.* Because our bodies have now become a temple of God in baptism (*1 Corinthians 3 & 6*) and our members are members of *Christ* (*1 Corinthians 6*), they must also be kept blameless until the coming of our **LORD JESUS CHRIST** (*1 Thessalonians 5*).
