--- Page 151 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the list of Articles of Faith that prove the Resurrection, moving from the four previously discussed (Incarnation, Death of Christ, Resurrection of Christ, and Baptism) to the fifth: the Holy Supper.</meta>

the Article of the Holy Supper <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>; for there the whole human being is fed and given to drink with the Body and Blood of Christ—not for this temporal life, because it is no earthly food for the belly, but for eternal life. In the Supper, the soul is not fed alone—as the <term>Sacramentarians</term> <note>A term used by Lutherans for those, like Zwingli or Calvin, who denied the real physical presence of Christ's body in the bread and wine.</note> would have it again (Pezelius on the Lord's Supper, page 328; the Emden defense against Hunnius, page 269)—but also the body. From this, Irenaeus (Book 4, Chapter 34), Cyril, and other ancient Church Fathers also argue for the Resurrection of the Dead.

And Oecolampadius <note>Johannes Oecolampadius (1482–1531), a leader of the Reformation in Basel.</note> himself says (Book 3, Letters, folio 133; also Goclenius, Disputation 5, *Philosophical-Theological Collection*, thesis 23): ***"The Body of Christ eaten in the Supper is the cause of our resurrection"*** <note>original Latin: "Corpus Christi in cœna manducatum nostræ resurrectionis caussa est"</note>. Since our bodies are now fed with the life-giving flesh of Christ and given to drink for eternal life with the soul-saving blood of Christ, they shall certainly rise again on that Day. Therefore, with Paul, we also consider dying to be our gain (Philippians 1), because Christ is our life, and we endure all pain in the hope of the glory to come.

## Shall the whole human being then rise from the dead?

**J**ust as the body alone dies, and not the soul at the same time, so also shall the body—and not the soul—rise again. This is contrary to what the <term>Arabici</term> <note>A 3rd-century sect in Arabia who believed the soul died and decayed with the body but would be revived by God on Judgment Day.</note> taught in former times (Nicephorus, Book 5, Chapter 23; Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 6, Chapter 26; and the legal decree *Distinction 24, Question 3, Chapter "Quidam"*).

When the withered and dead bones of the deceased hear the voice of the Son of God in their graves and rise again to life and come forth, then body and soul shall be reunited anew. Thus, the whole human being—according to whether they have done good—enters into eternal life, or—according to whether they have done evil—is cast into the abyss of Hell. For it is not the same with the human soul as it is with the souls of irrational animals—as the <term>Albiani</term> <note>Likely a reference to a heterodox group; in this context, they are accused of "fanatical dreaming" or "wild claims" regarding the soul.</note> wildly claimed—which must die and perish, and therefore have no part or salvation in eternal life. After all, they [the animals] were created for this earthly life solely for the sake of man; but man was made for eternal life (Wisdom 2), not for death,

<summary>This page concludes the argument that the physical body must be resurrected because it partakes in the Holy Supper, and clarifies that while the body dies and the soul lives on, both will be reunited at the final judgment.</summary>

<keywords>Holy Supper, Eucharist, Irenaeus, Cyril of Alexandria, Oecolampadius, Arabici, Albiani, immortality of the soul, Resurrection of the Body, 16th-century theology</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the discussion on the nature of the soul—specifically its immortality in contrast to the mortality of the body—and begins to address the location of the soul after death, critiquing Catholic doctrines like Limbo.</meta>

...as the <term>Photinians</term> <note>Followers of Photinus of Sirmium, a 4th-century bishop whose views were seen as a precursor to later denials of the Trinity and the soul's natural immortality.</note> claim (see <term>Ostorod</term> <note>Christoph Ostorodt (d. 1611), a prominent Socinian theologian.</note> in his *Instruction*, chapter 33, and <term>Faustus Socinus</term> <note>An Italian theologian whose ideas led to Socinianism, which often challenged traditional views on the soul and the Trinity.</note> in his *Theological Lectures*, chapter 1), but rather it is immortal. However, through sin, death and all misery entered the world (Romans 5 & 6); this does not mean the soul became mortal in the same way the body did, but rather that because of sin, the human being is subjected to the second and eternal death. "**For this is the death of the soul: when it is forsaken by God and lacks the blessed life**" <note>original Latin: "hæc enim est mors animæ, quando à Deo deseritur, & vita beata caret," from Augustine, *On the Trinity*, Book 14, Chapter 4</note>. 

Indeed, the <term>Epicureans</term> <note>Followers of Epicurus, often stereotyped as denying the afterlife.</note>, <term>Sadducees</term> <note>A Jewish sect at the time of Jesus known for denying the resurrection.</note>, <term>Anabaptists</term>, and even Pope Paul III <note>A provocative claim common in Protestant polemics of the era, suggesting that even high-ranking Catholic officials were secretly skeptical of core Christian tenets.</note> doubted the immortality of the soul quite a bit; but Scripture says clearly that the body alone, and not the soul, is subject to death (Matthew 10:28; Wisdom 3:1; Mark 12:26). 

Therefore, the holy men of God commended their souls to the Lord in their final hour of death, as Stephen did in Acts 7. "O Lord Jesus," he cried out fervently as the stones flew around his head, "receive my spirit!" David did the same in Psalm 31: "Into your hands I commend my spirit; you have redeemed me, O Lord, you faithful God." And Christ himself says in Luke 23: "Into your hands I commend my spirit." 

We should follow these examples and also commend our little souls—when we must walk the way of all flesh—into the Lord's hands, considering that they are then very well cared for. They receive a **safe conduct** <note>original Latin: "salvum conductum"</note>—a free, secure pass and escort on the road to eternal life—and for their <term>safe-keeping</term> <note>original German: "gewarsamb"</note> the Heavenly <term>Guard</term> <note>original German: "Gwardy"</note> is appointed, who wait for them and carry them into Abraham's bosom (Luke 16).

## Where is the soul in the meantime, once it has departed from the body?

**H**ere we find among the Papists many foolish dreams and self-invented human statutes and interpretations. For they cannot be satisfied with Hell and Heaven or eternal life, but instead dream up—contrary to God's clear and revealed Word—a special place they call the **limbo of the fathers** <note>original Latin: "limbus patrum," a concept in medieval theology of a place where the souls of the Old Testament righteous waited until Christ's resurrection.</note>, where the souls of the fathers who died in the Old Testament are...

<summary>This page argues for the immortality of the human soul against various "heretical" and Catholic views, emphasizing that while the body dies, the soul is escorted by angels to God. It begins a critique of the concept of Limbo.</summary>

<keywords>immortality of the soul, Photinians, Socinianism, Augustine, Pope Paul III, safe conduct, angels, Abraham's bosom, Limbo of the Fathers, Protestant polemic</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the critique of the Catholic doctrine regarding the soul's state after death, specifically identifying and refuting the various "places" assigned to souls by the Roman Church before turning to the Protestant view of suffering in this life.</meta>

...[where the souls of the fathers] have gone. After that is the **limbo of infants** <note>original Latin: "limbum infantum," the traditional Catholic concept of a place for the souls of unbaptized infants who, while spared the pains of hell, are denied the beatific vision of God.</note>, the first place where the souls of little unbaptized children are kept. Thirdly, there is **purgatory** <note>original Latin: "purgatorium"</note>, that is, the "Cleansing Fire," where the souls of people who did not do enough penance here on earth must suffer pain and be tormented until they have fully atoned and made satisfaction there, or are **liberated** <note>original Latin: "liberirt"</note> and rescued from it through vigils and <term>soul-masses</term> <note>original German: "Seelmessen," specific Catholic masses offered for the benefit of souls in purgatory.</note>. 

This is not only found in the *Fortress of Faith* <note>original Latin: "Fortalit. Fidei," a 15th-century work by Alfonso de Espina.</note> (Book 2, Consideration 6, Article 13, page 64), but other **Papists** also write of it: **Vincent of Beauvais** (*Mirror of Nature*, Book 30, Chapter 168), **Konrad Wimpina** (*Summary of Heresies*, page 5), **Stapleton** (*Evangelical Antidote* on Matthew Chapter 5, page 56), **Raymond** (*Summary*, pages 10 and 11), **Coster** in his *Handbook of Controversies* (page 490), **Thomas [Aquinas]** (Volume 1, page 56, and in the *Supplement*, Question 69, Article 7), **Bellarmine** (Book 2 on Purgatory, Chapter 11), and **Becanus** (Volume 1, Opus 15, and Volume 3, Opus 10, Chapter 3). 

For this reason, they engage in so much pleading and praying for the souls of the deceased, just as various formulas are set down in the *Manual for Curates* (Book 2, Consideration 8) for how they should pray so that the souls may soon be redeemed from the strict and bitter pain of Purgatory. Indeed, they believe that the more intercessory prayer occurs, and the more vigils and soul-masses are held, the sooner the soul is released from that place. As **Albertus Magnus** writes in *On the Office of the Mass* (Chapter 16, Treatise 3): "**So many suffrages can be performed for one man that he is liberated in an instant.**" <note>original Latin: "Tot possunt fieri pro uno homine suffragia, ut statim in momento liberetur."</note>

Now, although **Förner** <note>Friedrich Förner (1568–1630), a Catholic bishop and fierce opponent of the Reformation.</note> argues in his *Report on Indulgences and the Jubilee Year* (page 203) that purgatory is an **article of faith** <note>original Latin: "articulus fidei"</note>, we are nevertheless not bound to believe it. This is because **Tholosanus** <note>Pierre Grégoire, a French jurist and professor at Toulouse.</note> himself admits (*Syntaxis*, Book 32, Chapter 14), as does **Bellarmine** (*On Purgatory*, Book 1, Chapter 11), that the idea is taken from **Virgil, Cicero,** and other pagan writers. 

For the Scripture knows nothing of it; it mentions only Heaven and Hell. "**We are completely ignorant of a third place, nor do we believe in it, because we do not find it in the Scriptures,**" says **Augustine** (Volume 7, Book 5 of *Against the Pelagians*, page 957). Pious Christians have enough "purgatory" in this world so that they need not desire any other; indeed, it is prophesied that on the road of lamentation, they must enter the Kingdom of God through the cross and tribulation (Acts 14). Furthermore...

<summary>This page critiques the Catholic doctrines of the Limbo of Infants and Purgatory, citing numerous Catholic authorities to demonstrate the Roman position before using Augustine and the Bible to argue that such "third places" are unscriptural and pagan in origin.</summary>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument against the Catholic doctrine of Purgatory by asserting that Christ's sacrifice is the only true "cleansing fire" and that repentance is impossible after death.</meta>

We have here a **purgatory** <note>original Latin: "purgatorium," a place of cleansing</note> against our misdeeds, for the **blood of Jesus Christ, the Son of God, cleanses us from all sins** (1 John 1). Such a purgatory and washing away of sins is so powerful that our vices are completely washed away and purified through it; there remain no "reservations" or unpaid sins left behind which a person must personally suffer for and atone for, neither in this life nor in the next. As **John Duns Scotus** <note>a late-medieval Franciscan theologian and philosopher known as the "Subtle Doctor"</note> writes (*On the Sentences*, Book 4, Distinction 15, Question 1): "**God did not arrange to give grace to a sinner without the passion of Christ, without which grace there can be no <term>satisfaction</term> <note>theological payment or atonement for the debt of sin</note> at all, because it would not be equivalent in any way, nor simply, nor in divine acceptance.**" <note>original Latin: "neq; etiam disposuit Deus dare gratiam peccatori, sine passione Christi, sine qua gratia, nulla potest esse satisfactio omninò, quia nec æquivalens aliquomodo, nec simpliciter, nec indivina acceptatione"</note> 

Thus no man can stand righteous before God and be found innocent, for as **Leo [the Great]** says in his 81st Letter: "**Only in Christ does man find himself innocent.**" <note>original Latin: "in solo Christo... homo se invenit innocentem"</note> Likewise, **Cassander** <note>George Cassander (1513–1566), a theologian who tried to find common ground between Catholics and Protestants</note> writes in his *Consultation* (Article 12): "**Only the passion and death of the only-begotten Son of God is the satisfaction and propitiation for our sins.**" <note>original Latin: "sola passio & mors unigeniti Filij Dei... est satisfactio & propitiatio pro peccatis nostris"</note>

However, such washing away of sins occurs only in this life and not in the life to come, "**for then mercy ceases and justice takes its place,**" <note>original Latin: "tunc enim cessat misericordia & locum habet justitia"</note> or as **Basil** <note>Basil of Caesarea, a 4th-century Church Father</note> writes in his *Morals* (Summary 1, Chapter 2): "**Now is the time of repentance and the remission of sins; in the world to come, however, there will be the righteous judgment of retribution.**" <note>original Latin: "Nunc est tempus pœnitentiæ & remissionis peccatorum, in futuro verò seculo erit justum judicium retributionis"</note> For while we are still in this earthly life, the door of grace stands open to eternal life; after this, however, it is bolted and sealed tightly enough against the damned—as even the Papists themselves admit: "**that a man cannot repent after death.**" <note>original Latin: "quod homo post mortem non possit resipiscere"</note> (See **Radulfus Ardens** <note>a famous 12th-century preacher</note> in his first homily for the fourth Sunday of Advent, and **Thomas [Aquinas]**, Part 1, Question 64, Article 2).

Therefore, it is said: "**as the tree falls, so shall it lie**" (Ecclesiastes 11). "**In whatever state the Lord finds you when He calls, in that state He likewise judges you,**" says **Cyprian** <note>bishop of Carthage and 3rd-century martyr</note> in his book *On Mortality* (Section 12). <note>original Latin: "qualem te invenit Dominus cùm vocat, talem pariter & judicat"</note> Or as **Augustine** says in his 80th Letter: "**In whatever state one's own final day finds him, in that state the final day of the world will seize him: since in whatever condition a person dies on that day, in such a condition will he be judged on that day.**" <note>original Latin: "In quo quemq; invenerit suus novissimus dies, in hoc eum comprehendet mundi novissimus dies : quoniam qualis in die isto quisq; moritur, talis in die illo judicabitur"</note> 

If someone now dies in unbelief and departs from here with a wounded conscience, he will not find in Purgatory "**remedies for his pains,**" <note>original Latin: "dolorum remedia"</note> as **Jean Benedicti** <note>a 16th-century Franciscan author</note> writes in his *Treatise on the Burial of the Dead* (Letter E, 1). Rather, he will suffer eternal pain in Hell; for as **Thomas [Aquinas]** himself says in the *Compendium of Theology* (or Opuscule 3, Chapter 174), the soul remains perpetually... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "net" indicates the next page begins with "manet" (remains).</note>

<summary>This page argues that Christ's sacrifice is the only sufficient "purgatory" for sin, refuting the need for a post-death cleansing fire. It cites various Church Fathers and even medieval Catholic scholastics to support the claim that repentance is only possible during one's earthly life.</summary>

<keywords>purgatory, atonement, satisfaction, Duns Scotus, Thomas Aquinas, Augustine, Cyprian, repentance, salvation, Christ's blood, final judgment</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues from the previous discussion of Thomas Aquinas’s view on the soul’s state after death, specifically the idea that the soul remains fixed in the disposition it held at the moment of passing.</meta>

...remains in that end which it established for itself as final in the state of death. <note>original Latin: "net in eo fine, quem sibi ultimum in statu mortis præstituit"</note>

But if someone dies in faith, his soul is carried into **Abraham’s bosom** (Luke 16) and passes from the realm of faith into the realm of sight, where it is in God’s hand and no torment touches it (Wisdom 3).

Moreover, the soul is spiritual, *but the fire of purgatory is physical, as the Papists* <note>a historical term for Roman Catholics</note> *claim* (*Compendium of True Theology*, Book 7, Chapter 1; Raymond's *Summa*, page 10; Bellarmine, *On Purgatory*, Book 2, Chapter 11). <note>original Latin: "ignis autem purgatorius est corporalis, ut Pontificij volunt"</note> Now, if the soul were to suffer in the imagined purgatory, it would have to be constituted and proportioned in such a way that it could be affected by and sensitive to physical fire; *for between the acting agent and the suffering subject, a just* <term>symmetry</term> <note>original Greek: συμμετρία (symmetria), meaning a fitting proportion or balance</note> *and proportion are required. But between the physical and the spiritual, there is no proportion. Therefore, the soul does not suffer physically.* <note>original Latin: "inter agens enim & patiens justa requiritur... & proportio. Atqui inter corporale & spirituale nulla est proportio. Ergo nec anima patitur corporaliter."</note> Many more such arguments could be introduced if it were necessary; therefore, it remains irrefutably true that the soul of the righteous person who has fallen asleep blessedly in faith in Christ is freed from all pain after this world. The soul of the unrighteous, however, is in the place of torment from which there is no redemption. Hence even **Lombard** <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), author of the "Sentences," the standard medieval theology textbook</note> himself writes (Book 3, Distinction 19, Letter A): "*If we look correctly with the gaze of faith upon Him who hung upon the wood for us, we are loosened from the bonds of the devil—that is, from sins—and thus we are freed from the Devil so that even after this life he finds nothing in us to punish.*" <note>original Latin: "si rectè fidei intuitu in illum respicimus, qui pro nobis pependit in ligno, à vinculis diaboli solvimur, id est à peccatis, & ita à Diabolo liberamur, ut nec post hanc vitam in nobis inveniat, quod puniat."</note>

The Spirit of God explains this here and there in the Scripture, in both the Old and New Testaments, and warns us that we must certainly not depart from here with unatoned sins, lest we suffer shipwreck regarding our salvation. As even the pagan **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, a Roman Stoic philosopher</note> says (Letter 27): "*Provide this for yourself regarding the day of death: let your vices die before you do.*" <note>original Latin: "Hoc tibi circa diem mortis præsta, moriantur ante te vitia"</note> For now is the acceptable time; now is the day of salvation (2 Corinthians 6), in which we are heard when we cry out to God and seek forgiveness for sins. But there [in the afterlife], all howling and sighing, all speaking and lamenting is in vain and useless. Therefore **Sirach** <note>also known as Ecclesiasticus, a book of the Biblical Apocrypha</note> says (Chapter 17): "So turn now to the Lord, and leave your sinful life; pray to the Lord and cease from evil; hold yourself to the Most High and turn away from injustice, and hate idolatry with earnestness. Who will praise the Most High in the..." <note>The text ends mid-sentence as a catchword for the following page.</note>

<summary>The author argues that the soul is spiritual and cannot be harmed by physical fire, thus debunking the physical "cleansing fire" of Purgatory. Citing both Scripture and historical figures like Peter Lombard and Seneca, the text emphasizes that salvation must be secured through faith before death.</summary>

<keywords>soul, physical fire, Purgatory, Peter Lombard, Seneca, Sirach, spiritual vs. physical, salvation, repentance, Robert Bellarmine</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the citation from the Book of Sirach and continues the argument against Purgatory, emphasizing the immediate transition of the soul to either eternal joy or the abyss upon death, supported by various biblical passages and early Church fathers.</meta>

Hell? <note>original German: "Helle?" – this completes the sentence from the previous page: "Who will praise the Most High in Hell?"</note> For only the living can praise; the dead—as those who are no more—cannot live; therefore praise the **LORD** while you are alive and healthy. So says Isaiah Chapter 38: Hell does not praise you, nor does death glorify you, and those who go down into the pit do not wait for your truth, but only those who live praise you, as I do now; the father will make your truth known to the children.

We see and understand this in the story of the Rich Man and poor Lazarus (Luke 16). For as soon and immediately as the former dies, he travels into the abyss of hell, regardless of his wealth and all <term>vigils</term> <note>prayers traditionally held on the eve of a funeral</note> and <term>requiem masses</term> <note>original German: "Seelmessen" – masses offered for the repose of a soul</note>; but the latter is not sent to purgatory because he is poor, but when he dies, his soul is carried by the angels into **Abraham’s bosom**. What happened to them still happens to all people; for whoever believes, their soul is transferred and moved into the eternal life of joy immediately after death, without "pilgrimaging" <note>original German: "pilgrimeren" – likely a derisive reference to the soul's supposed wandering through the levels of purgatory</note> and without any delay or postponement. There it is in **God's** hand, and no torment touches it (Wisdom 3). It is bound in the bundle of the living with the **LORD** (1 Samuel 25). And therefore it can neither be dead nor sleep, for it returns to the **LORD** who gave it (Ecclesiastes 12), and walks before him in the land of the living (Psalms 27 and 116), and has fullness of joy and a lovely being at **God's** right hand forever (Psalm 16). As St. Paul also says (2 Corinthians 5), we know that if our earthly house of this tent is broken, we have a building built by **God**, a house not made with hands that is eternal in heaven. Likewise, we read in Hebrews 9 and 10 that Christ entered into the Holy Place through his own blood and found an eternal redemption; from which it follows that **CHRIST** has given power to all his believers through his entrance to walk that same way, as the **Lord** himself says (Luke 23) to the thief on the cross: "Truly I say to you, today you will be with me in Paradise." Justin Martyr also writes of this in his book *Questions and Answers*, question 75:

> After the departure of souls from the body, the righteous are immediately separated from the unrighteous; for they are led away by angels to places worthy of them. And the souls of the righteous go indeed into paradise, where they enjoy the company and sight... <note>original Latin: "Post exitum animarum è corpore, illicò justi ab injustis segregantur abdúcuntur enim ab angelis ad condigna sibi loca. Ac justorum quidem animæ in paradisum, ubi consuetudine & aspectu fruuntur"</note>

...of angels and Archangels, but also through the vision of the Savior Christ himself, according to what was said: we are away from the body, and present with the Lord. <note>original Latin: "angelorum & Archangelorum, per visionem autem & ipsius servatoris Christi, pro eo atq; dictum est; peregrè absumus à corpore, & præsentes adsumus apud Dominum." This concludes the quote from Justin Martyr on the previous page.</note>

Therefore we should rejoice over the righteous, as **Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential Early Church Father known for his preaching</note> says in Homily 3 on the Epistle to the Philippians, page 1224: not only because they live here, but also when they have passed away. For they are not dead, but live with God, and have all good things in abundance. They have the true, living, and longed-for life of joy, where there is pure youth without old age, pure light without darkness, gladness without sadness, peace and unity without discord and strife. They are so full of joy and blessedness that in this life one cannot grasp it with thoughts, nor speak of it sufficiently as is necessary:

> *Who therefore would not strive with all their might to reach such great glory, so that they might become a friend of God? That they might rejoice with Christ immediately? That after earthly torments and punishments, they might receive divine rewards?* <note>original Latin: "Quis ergô non omnibus viribus elaboret ad tantam claritatem pervenire, ut amicus Dei fiat? ut cum Christo statim gaudeat? ut post tormenta & supplicia terrena, præmia divina percipiat?"</note>

**Cyprian** <note>Thascius Cæcilius Cyprianus (c. 210–258), Bishop of Carthage and a notable early Christian writer</note> in *On the Exhortation to Martyrdom*, chapter 12. And indeed, if that were not so, how could Simeon say (Luke 2), "**LORD**, now let your servant depart in peace"? Or how could Paul have such a great desire to depart and be with **Christ** (Philippians 1)? But because they know that a dwelling is prepared for them in heaven (John 14), they have a desire to travel there with peace and joy, so that they may enjoy and obtain that blessedness.

However, it is to be diligently noted that if our souls are to appear before God and behold his gracious countenance with joy without ceasing, we must also commend them into the **LORD's** hands in our final hour of death (Psalm 31), and adorn them with true repentance. Then, if we fall asleep in **Christ**, we travel the path to life, and it is said of us: "The death of the saints is held in high esteem before the **LORD**" (Psalm 116). Otherwise, if we depart from here in unrepentance and outside of **Christ**, we can never reach or arrive there, even if we had the "Russian passport." For **Alexander Guagninus** <note>Alessandro Guagnini (1538–1614), an Italian-born soldier and writer known for his chronicles of Eastern Europe</note> writes in his *Description of Sarmatian Europe* <note>original Latin: "descriptio Europæ Sarmatiæ"</note> that the Muscovite and Russian priests give the dying special <term>diplomas</term> or <term>letters of safe conduct</term> <note>original German: "Gleitsbrieffe" — letters intended to ensure a soul's passage into heaven</note> addressed to St. Peter for a great deal of money, so that when they appear with them, they might all the sooner be ad- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "gelaf-", likely part of "eingelassen," meaning "admitted."</note>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the "Russian passports"—superstitious letters of safe conduct sold by priests—and provides a translated example of such a document. It then pivots to the grim fate of the unrepentant after death, contrasting the false security of these "passports" with the reality of divine judgment.</meta>

...admitted. And because one of these little letters was translated into our German language in the year 1542 by **Dr. Conrad Lagus** <note>Conrad Lagus (died 1546), a prominent legal scholar and diplomat who traveled to Russia</note> and brought back by him during his legation, I consider it not unserviceable to include it here, so that everyone may see how greatly the people in the world are blinded:

## The Russian Passport into Eternal Life.

**M**akary, <note>Makary II, who served as Metropolitan of Moscow and all Russia in the mid-16th century</note> by the grace of God, Archbishop of Kiev and Halych, and in all the land of Rus; to our Lord and friend **St. Peter**, a gatekeeper of God the Almighty: We command you and announce to you that at this time, a servant of God, a Prince Fyodor Solodomirsky, <note>Sedor Solodormijzki in the original text; likely a contemporary Russian nobleman</note> has departed; that you let him in straight away, without any hindrance or delay, into the Kingdom of God. We have <term>absolved</term> <note>released from the legal and spiritual debt of sin</note> him of all his sins and given him the blessing; and so that you do not transgress anything in the end, and so that nothing else happens to him, we have given him this our letter of absolution: Given in our highest monastery in Kiev in the year 1541, on the 30th of July.

However, regarding the souls of the unbelieving and unrepentant after a person’s death, the situation is such that they, like the rich man (Luke 16), come immediately and without delay to the place of torment, into the fiery pool which burns with fire and sulfur (Revelation 21). For whoever does not believe in the name of the only-begotten Son of God is already judged (John 3), and after temporal death, the <term>execution</term> <note>original Latin: "Execution" — the carrying out of a judicial sentence</note> begins, so that the souls suffer pain and eternal destruction from the face of the **LORD** and from his glorious power (2 Thessalonians 1). Their worm does not die, and their fire is not quenched (Isaiah 66). They lie in hell like sheep, and death gnaws on them (Psalm 49). They drink of the wine of God’s wrath, which is poured out and pure in the cup of his wrath, and are tormented with fire and sulfur before the holy angels and before the Lamb, so that the smoke of their torment rises from eternity to eternity, and they have no rest day or night (Revelation 14), as **Bernard** <note>Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic</note> writes extensively on this in the book *On the Soul*, chapter 38. What <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword indicates the next word is "think."</note>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the quote from Bernard of Clairvaux began on the previous page, describing the psychological and physical horrors of hell, before transitioning to a theological inquiry regarding the timing of the soul's entry into the afterlife.</meta>

"What do you think," he says, "what heart-sorrow, distress, and mourning, lamentation, and misery will befall the godless when they are separated from the company of all the saints and from God's presence, and delivered into the power of the devils? When they go into the eternal hellish fire, where they shall mourn, howl, sigh, and wail forever and ever, without end or ceasing, without measure or limit, and never again come to any joy? For they will be eternally tormented; the tormentor does not grow weary, and the tormented does not die. The hellish fire burns and yet does not consume the one being burned; there is an unbearable pain and exceedingly great fear; the second death gnaws and plagues them forever, and yet the damned remain forever alive; they die so that they live, and live so that they die forever and ever." No human can sufficiently express what a great martyrdom this is, nor can we reach it with our thoughts; therefore we should listen to Moses and the Prophets, and believe in **CHRIST**, and also prove such faith through works, and persevere steadfastly until the end, so that we do not come to the place of torment, as **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers</note> also admonishes in his 14th treatise on John: *Believe in Christ, who was made mortal for your sake, so that you might grasp Him as immortal; for when His immortality begins, you too shall not be mortal. He lived while you were dying; He died so that you may live; He brought the grace of God, and took away the wrath of God: God drank death, so that death would not drink man.* <note>original Latin: "Crede in Christum, factum propter te mortalem, ut illum capias immortalem... Deus bibit mortem, ne mors biberet hominem."</note>

### In what manner does this occur, and by what means are souls brought so quickly and nimbly to their place?

**S**ince we have God's clear and manifest Word that such a swift change occurs with the souls of the deceased, we should be satisfied with that and not brood or ask further how such a thing might happen; for God knows the measure and manner, means and ways for such a passage <note>the original term "tractu" suggests a drawing or pulling along a path</note>, as the Scripture also indicates that by God's hand and omnipotence the souls of the righteous are received. Therefore David also asks and prays: "do not leave me, and do not take your hand from me, God my salvation; for my father and my mother leave me, but the Lord receives me," **Psalms 27.** Thus also the Lord Christ promises that he will take his own to himself, as he says in **John 10:** "My sheep hear my voice, and I know them, and they follow me; and I give them eternal life, and they shall never perish, and no one shall snatch them out of my hand." Beyond this, God's Word teaches us that the holy angels wait for our little souls and carry them into <term>Abraham’s Bosom</term> <note>A traditional biblical metaphor for the place of comfort where the righteous rest in the afterlife, specifically mentioned in the Parable of Lazarus and the Rich Man</note>, **Luke 16.** Not one angel alone, who would be strong enough for the task, but many angels; for they all have joy in human salvation and rejoice greatly that they are to perform this service for us, furthering our souls into their blessed company toward eternal joy.

Furthermore, we have the Word and the Faith, which are beneficial to our salvation, of which David says: "Lord, send your light and your truth, that they may lead me and bring me to your holy mountain, and to your dwelling, that I may go in to the altar of God, to the God who is my joy and delight," **Psalms 43.** Thus we also read in **Hebrews 6:** "we hold fast to the offered hope, which we have as a sure and firm anchor of our souls, which also enters into the inner part behind the curtain, where the <term>Forerunner</term> <note>Original German: "Vorleuffer"; a title for Christ representing one who goes ahead to secure entry for those following behind</note> has entered for us: Jesus, having become a High Priest forever according to the order of Melchizedek."

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion on the transition of the soul. ...how it may otherwise happen further; concerning this we should not and will not inquire improperly, but rather entrust it to **God**. He knows well how it can be; what He does, stands; what He wills, occurs; and He can lead everything to a glorious and laudable conclusion. As the **Preacher Solomon** <note>The book of Ecclesiastes</note> says in **Chapter 3**: "I perceive that everything **God** does, that endures forever; one can add nothing to it nor take anything from it; and **God** does this so that men should fear before Him." What **God** does, stands there; and what He wills to do, must come to pass. **For He pursues and chases after it.** <note>The author uses a forceful German idiom "tracht vnd jagt jhm nach" to emphasize God's active fulfillment of His will</note> Thus also says the **Lord** Himself in **Zechariah 8**: "If it seems impossible in the eyes of the remnant of this people in this time, should it therefore also be impossible in my eyes?"

But just as the souls of the righteous are promoted and accepted into salvation by **God's** Hand of Grace through the Word and Faith, so also are the souls of the damned taken up into damnation by **God's** Hand of Wrath because of their unbelief. Such a manner of speaking is not only used by **Brenz** <note>Johannes Brenz (1499–1570), a prominent Lutheran Reformer</note> in his *Recognition against Bullinger, page 219* <note>original: "Recognit. cont. Bullinger"</note>—for which he was rebuked by **Beza** <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), a major Reformed theologian and successor to Calvin</note> in *Volume 1, page 597*—but the Royal <term>Seer</term> <note>original Latin: "Videns"; referring to David’s prophetic insight</note> **David** also teaches us this in **Psalm 21**: "Your hand will find all your enemies; your right hand will find those who hate you; you will make them like a fiery oven when you look upon them; the **Lord** will swallow them up in his wrath, and fire will consume them." Furthermore, just as the souls of the blessed and the elect are brought to their place and accompanied through the service of the holy angels, so too are the souls of the unblessed and the damned snatched away by **God’s** command and power. Through the service of evil spirits, they are brought to the place of torment, where they suffer anguish and pain forever and ever.

Thus they also have a blessed and peaceful life there, where they no longer know of terror and anxiety, of pain and torment, of weeping and wailing; for there is pure peace and security, indeed the highest joy and salvation. They behold **God** from face to face and cannot rejoice enough in this, as is written in the **Book of Wisdom** in **Chapter 4**: "The souls of the holy are in grace and mercy after this life, and **God** has a watch over them." ...their fragrance. Arise, my beloved, and come here; my beautiful one, come here.

From this we see and understand that the **souls of the righteous** <note>original: "Seelen der Gerechten"</note> are in inexpressibly great joy, and with all the holy angels, they laud and praise **God**. They perform such work without ceasing with heartfelt joy and delight, and they do not become faint or weary from it; for **God** is the sole, highest, and permanent good. And because they possess **God**, they delight themselves forever in such great goodness and cannot behold Him enough. All their doing is to love and praise **God** without end, to praise and love without effort, so that we may well say with **David**: "As the deer pants for fresh water, so my soul cries out to you, **LORD** my **GOD**; my soul thirsts for **GOD**, for the living God; when shall I come there, that I may behold **God's** face?" **Psalm 42**.

O heartfelt life, which **God** has prepared for those who love Him! O holy and quiet life, full of all adornment and glory! O blessed and peaceful life, full of all comfort and salvation! When shall I depart from this miserable <term>valley of sorrows</term> <note>original: "Jammerthal"; a common historical metaphor for earthly life as a place of suffering and exile</note> and enter into the eternal <term>hall of joy</term> <note>original: "Frewdensahl"</note>, so that I may behold the holiness of the **LORD** and refresh my thirsty soul with the comforting water of His mercy? Ah, **LORD**, how long must I dwell here? Come then, and let me see the day of eternal delight, the day of joy and salvation which you, **LORD**, have made, in which we shall rejoice and be glad.

**LORD of Hosts** / Isaiah 6: Revelation 4. and thank **God** for all His works / for all His wonders and benefits / and delight themselves in His power and might / in His dominion and glory / in His goodness and truth.

Furthermore, the **souls of the righteous** <note>original: "Gerechten Seelen"</note> also have a heartfelt longing for the **Last Day**, and cry out to **God** for vengeance against those who killed them and persecuted the **Christian Church**, as it is written in **Revelation 6**: 

> I saw under the altar the souls of those who had been slain for the sake of the **Word of God** and for the sake of the testimony they held: And they cried with a loud voice and said, **LORD**, you Holy and True, how long until you judge and avenge our blood on those who dwell upon the earth? And to each of them was given a white robe, and it was said to them that they should rest yet a little while, until the number of their fellow servants and brothers, who were also to be killed just as they were, should be completed. 

This happens, however, not as if they doubted **God's** justice—for they know that **God** is just and leaves no evil unpunished—nor does it happen out of impatience, for they are in perfect bliss. Much less does it happen out of hatred toward their persecutors, for in **Eternal Life** there is pure peace and no enmity, pure love and no anger, pure delight and no displeasure. Instead, it happens <term>out of love and zeal for justice</term> <note>original Latin: "ex amore & zelo justitiæ"</note>, out of the zeal for justice and love for the glory of **God**, according to the **LORD'S** will. Just so, the saints also pray out of such zeal in **Psalm 79**: "Let the vengeance for the blood of your servants that has been shed be known among the nations before our eyes," about which **Augustine** writes more extensively in *Sermon 11 on the Saints*.

<margin>☞</margin> <note>The scribe has included a "manicule" or pointing hand here to highlight the following theological distinction regarding the intercession of saints.</note>
Finally, it may well happen that the righteous and holy souls offer up their longing and pleading collective prayers and pray for the whole of **Christendom** on earth, for the propagation and preservation of the **Word** and of true **Divine Service**. From this, however, it does not follow that the deceased saints should be called upon by us, as happens in <term>Popery</term> <note>original German: "Papsthumb"; a common Reformation-era term for the Roman Catholic Church and its hierarchy</note>, where they hold more to the <term>Hail Mary</term> <note>Latin: "Ave Maria"</note> than to the <term>Lord's Prayer</term> <note>Latin: "Pater noster"</note>. In this same way, they also write <term>"that Mary was with the Lord in the same labor and work of redemption,"</term> <note>original Latin: "quod Maria fuerit cum Domino in eodem redemptionis labore & opere"</note> *The Mariale* <note>Likely a reference to the "Mariale," a medieval collection of writings or sermons regarding the Virgin Mary.</note> book 1, chapter 3; <term>"that she is the mediatress of men, the wash-"</term> <note>original Latin: "quod sit mediatrix hominum, ablu-"; the text breaks off here at the start of the word "ablutrix," meaning a female cleanser or washer of sins.</note>

<meta>catchword: trix</meta>

<summary>The author explains that the souls in heaven desire the Day of Judgment not out of spite, but out of a holy zeal for God's justice. The text transitions into a Protestant critique of Catholic Mariology, arguing that while saints may pray for the living, the living should not pray to them.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the Protestant critique of Roman Catholic Mariology and the veneration of saints. It specifically completes the title "cleanser of sins" (ablutrix criminum) from the previous page's catchword and begins to argue that saints in heaven, while blessed, do not have knowledge of earthly affairs.</meta>

...cleanser of crimes, consoler of all, gate of heaven, way of salvation, <note>original Latin: "etrix criminum, consolatrix omnium, porta cœli, via salutis"</note> as cited by Jan David in the *Pancarpium Mariano* <note>A famous Jesuit devotional book featuring emblems of the Virgin Mary, published in 1607.</note> page 194; and the *Paris Missal* <note>A liturgical book containing the instructions and texts for celebrating the Mass in Paris.</note> in the Prose of the Mass for the Annunciation. <margin>X</margin> For even though the saints in heaven wish us all good things and perhaps even pray for the <term>Church Militant</term> <note>The "fighting" church on earth, as opposed to the "triumphant" church in heaven.</note>, <margin>7</margin> they still cannot know the needs and concerns of people on earth, as <term>Costerus</term> <note>Frans Coster (1532–1619), a prominent Jesuit theologian known for his "Enchiridion" (Manual) defending Catholic doctrine.</note> claims in his *Manual on the Veneration of Saints*, page 454. "For they do not see," says **Augustine** in his book *On the Care to be Taken for the Dead*, chapter 13, "whatever is done or happens in this life of men." <note>original Latin: "non enim vident... quæcunq; aguntur, aut eveniunt in ista vita hominum"</note> For they are snatched away before misfortune, as written in **Isaiah 57** <note>The text cites Isaiah 56, but the reference to being "snatched away" from evil is Isaiah 57:1.</note>, so that they should no longer see the evil, just as **God** says to the pious King **Josiah** in **2 Chronicles 34**: "They are liberated from all evils, so that they no longer endure any, either by suffering or by feeling compassion." <note>original Latin: "Liberati sunt ab omnibus malis, ut nec ulla vel patiendo vel compatiendo amplius sustineant"</note> 

Regarding this, we also read in <term>Papal Law</term> <note>The *Corpus Juris Canonici* or Canon Law of the Catholic Church.</note> C. 13. q. 2. c. *qui divina* and c. 29, where it clearly stands: "that the dead do not know what is done here by the living," <note>original Latin: "quod mortui nesciant quid hic à vivis geratur"</note> and at the same time the saying of **Isaiah Chapter 63** <note>OCR says 64, but the text is 63:16.</note> is cited: "Yet you are our Father; for **Abraham** does not know of us, and **Israel** does not acknowledge us." Furthermore, the deceased saints cannot hear or perceive the prayers of the living, much less can people in this life apply the saints' <term>merit</term> by virtue of their intercession, as **Lombard** <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), whose "Sentences" was the standard textbook of medieval theology.</note> claims in Book 4 of the *Sentences*, distinction 45. 

So, nothing happens because of their intercession; rather, everything that **God** does <margin>[[</margin> happens out of grace for the sake of **Christ**, <margin>]]</margin> whom the holy souls in heaven also honor, laud, and praise in **Revelation 5**. Therefore, we should call upon **God** alone and not the deceased saints, just as **Christ** also commands in **Matthew 4**, for the **LORD** is "all in all" (**1 Corinthians 15**). He is the one who can and will hear our prayer and give us what is useful and salvific for us, both here in time and there eternally. "We indeed honor the saints, but we do not adore [worship] them; for whoever serves the creature rather than the Creator vanishes in his own thoughts." <note>original Latin: "honoramus quidem sanctos, sed non adoramus, qui enim creaturæ potius quam creatori servit, evanescit in suis cogitationibus"; a quote from Augustine's *On True Religion*, chapter 10.</note>

When we read through the Church histories with diligence, we must wonder to the highest degree that **Satan** so effectively... <note>The sentence is interrupted by the catchword.</note>

<sig>B</sig>

...is powerful in the children of unbelief; for there have always been people of deranged minds who taught ungodly things about the state of souls after this life. The **Dominicans** believed—as the **Anabaptists** <note>original German: "Widerteuffer"</note> are also said to believe—that the souls fall into a deep sleep and thus rest until the Final Day. The **Tertullianists** <note>A sect following the rigors of Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD), though here accused of specific errors regarding the soul's nature.</note> dreamed up the transformation of souls into evil or good spirits. Some were of the opinion that souls die at the same time as the bodies. Some believed that they pass again into other bodies <note>A reference to transmigration or reincarnation.</note>. The **Armenians** and **Fratricelli**, **otherwise called Beguines** <note>Spiritual movements often deemed heretical in the Middle Ages for their views on poverty and the afterlife.</note>, indeed regarded the souls as immortal; nonetheless, they taught that before the Last Day, they are not **admitted** to the *vision of God* <note>original Latin: "ad visionem Dei"</note>, meaning they cannot behold God face to face. This harmful and dangerous error has always had a great following and approval; for even some of the Church Fathers taught thus: **Justin** in Question 76; **Theophylact** on Chapter 11 of the Epistle to the Hebrews; **Aretas, Bishop of Caesarea**, on Chapter 6 of Revelation; and **Victorinus the Martyr** on the same passage; and **Bernard** in his 4th Sermon on the Saints. The Papists <note>A term used by reformers to refer to Roman Catholics.</note> are also of the same opinion; **See William of Ockham** in his *Work of 93 Days*; **Marsilius** in the 4th book of *Sentences*, Question 13, article 3; and **Jean Gerson**, Volume 2, folio 42. For this reason, **John XXII** <note>Pope John XXII (r. 1316–1334), who famously caused a controversy by preaching that the saved do not see God until the General Resurrection.</note> strictly commanded the theologians at Paris that they should **promote** no one to the rank of **Doctor** if he were not of the opinion that souls could not behold God’s face before the Last Day. Likewise, **Calvin** writes in Book 3 of the *Institutes*, Chapter 25, Section 6, that the souls of the righteous indeed come to blessed rest, but before the Last Day they are not in bliss *but expect the fruition of promised glory*. <note>original Latin: "sed expectant fruitionem promissæ gloriæ"</note>

On the contrary, however, God’s Word reports to us that the blessed souls come to God immediately once the person dies and behold Him there—not in the Word through a dark mirror <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 13:12: "For now we see through a glass, darkly."</note>, as happens here, but face to face (**1 Corinthians 13, 1 John 3**). For how could they otherwise be blessed *without the vision of God*? <note>original Latin: "sine Dei visione"</note> Or how could they be in God's hand if they did not behold God? (**Wisdom 3**). Secondly, if the blessed souls were not to behold <gloss>behold</gloss> God before the Last Day, then their state in that life would be far more wretched than in this life; for now we see the LORD...

...our God in the Word; but there, by no means [is it so]. *Therefore, the state of souls is more wretched if there is absolutely no vision and glory there.* <note>original Latin: "miserior igitur est status animarum, si illic planè nulla sit visio & gloria"</note> Thirdly, Stephen, when he was about to and wished to die, saw the holiness of God and Jesus standing at the right hand of God (**Acts 7**). If he was able to see such things in his final hour of death, how much more will his soul—now that he has passed away—behold God’s glory and look upon Christ face to face. Fourthly, Paul desires to be released and to be with Christ (**Philippians 1**). How could the Apostle be with Christ if he were not to behold Christ? Likewise, the twenty-four elders fall down before the Lamb who sat upon the throne (**Revelation 5**); which, once again, cannot be or happen without beholding Him. Fifthly, the souls of the righteous will be where the holy angels are and possess the same kind of blessedness with them (**Hebrews 12**). But the holy angels always see the face of the Father in heaven (**Matthew 18**). *Therefore, the souls of the righteous will also see God face to face.* <note>original Latin: "Ergò etiam justorum animæ Deum videbunt de facie ad faciem"</note> Sixthly, the souls of the blessed and the righteous are entirely free from all pain in that life. However, to be deprived <note>The original uses the term "privirt," from the Latin "privatio," meaning the lack of something that ought to be present.</note> and robbed of the sight of God is the highest and greatest torment and pain; therefore, they shall not be alienated from the sight of God nor robbed of His glory. Seventhly, not a single reason can be shown or given that should prevent the blessed souls from beholding the **LORD**; for even if they are outside the body, the soul does not need the body for such a blessed beholding. This is because it does not happen *through mental images, or through the mind’s reflection on mental images* <term>phantasmata: the sensory images or "phantasms" that medieval and early modern scholars, following Aristotle, believed the mind required to think while joined to a physical body.</term>, but in a special way and manner that we humans in this mortality can neither know nor experience; we must save it until we too are brought to such a beholding of **God** and are blissfully promoted to eternal life.

However, although the souls of the deceased may think down and have a longing for their own to soon come to such rest, all this happens according to God's will, without any pain or fleshly passions. <term>non enim præposterum est desiderium...: "for the desire is not distorted, but tempered to the will of God, and therefore also free from pain."</term>

However, if we judge this according to God’s Word, it is neither proper nor Christian; for once a person has died, he comes...

# Can the souls not, however, appear again here on earth?

**T**he Papists indeed claim that the souls of the deceased should appear here again—about which <term>Jacobus de caussa Carthusianus</term> <note>Also known as James of Jüterbog (c. 1381–1465), a monk who wrote a famous treatise on the appearance of spirits.</note> wrote a large book—in order to support and maintain their invented <term>Purgatory</term> <note>original German: "Fegfewr," literally "cleansing fire."</note>, their vigils, and masses for the dead. From this, they have also often dreamed up many *apparitions* and appearances, as <term>Ludovic. Lavater.</term> <note>Ludwig Lavater (1527–1586), a Swiss Reformed theologian who wrote a widely read work on ghosts and spirits from a Protestant perspective.</note> writes in *Concerning Specters*, part 1, chapter 7. Indeed, they even cunningly staged these things themselves, as <term>Erasmus</term> <note>Desiderius Erasmus (c. 1466–1536), a famous humanist scholar who often used wit to expose church corruption.</note> teaches in his 22nd book of *Letters*. For instance, they once let many live crayfish with small burning candles attached to them crawl around the churchyard in the evening, and thereafter declared that these were the souls from Purgatory who wanted to be redeemed through masses for the dead. This fraud was finally revealed when several such dead crayfish were found, which the mass-priest had not gathered up again. Many such and similar deceptive appearances occurred within Popery, through which they captivated and deceived the people. 

But if we judge this according to God’s Word, it is clearly known that no soul of any deceased person—whether he was good or evil—wanders or appears here on earth after death. Rather, they are nothing but devils and <term>Poltergeister</term> <note>A German term for "rumbling" or "noisy" spirits; the author suggests these are demonic deceptions rather than human souls.</note>, who strengthen people either in error or unbelief through such appearances...

<summary>The author argues that praying for the dead is either redundant or futile and dismisses claims of ghosts as Catholic deceptions or demonic tricks intended to uphold the doctrine of Purgatory.</summary>

...to strengthen [unbelief], or else to frighten and hunt people, yes, often bringing them into the peril of death. For the souls of the righteous are in God's hand, but the souls of the unrighteous are in hell, from which they shall never be redeemed. Thus it was also refused to the <term>Rich Glutton</term> <note>Refers to the parable of the Rich Man (Dives) and the beggar Lazarus in Luke 16.</note> in hell that his brothers should be warned by a deceased person; for we have Moses and the Prophets, whom we should hear, and we should not seek counsel from the dead (Deuteronomy 18, Isaiah 8).

Therefore, if a spirit comes before you, do not ask whether it is a good or evil spirit, but simply hold these words before it: "They have Moses and the Prophets." Then he will soon sense what you mean. If he is a good spirit, he will love you all the more because you carry your and his God’s Word so freely and joyfully. If he is an evil spirit—as are all those who thus rumble and torment—he will soon take <term>refuge</term> <note>original: "refugium," meaning an escape or flight.</note> and say, "Farewell, I can stay here no longer." <term>Chrysostom</term> <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential early Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople.</note> also writes about this in his 29th Homily on Matthew:

> "A soul separated from the body cannot wander in these regions; for the souls of the righteous are in the hand of God, but sinners are immediately led away after this departure, which is made clear by [the story of] Lazarus and the rich man." <note>original Latin: "Non potest anima à corpore separata in his regionibus errare, justorum enim animæ sunt in manu Dei, peccatores verò post hunc exitum continuò abducuntur, quod à Lazaro & divite planum efficitur."</note>

Furthermore, the soul is a spiritual being which cannot be seen with our mortal eyes. The body, however, must mold and rot in the earth; it must become earth again from which it was taken (Genesis 3, Sirach 41). How then can the body appear when it has rotted? Or how can we see the soul, since it is spiritual?

It is therefore an unscriptural untruth, what the <term>Papists</term> <note>A term used by reformers to refer to Roman Catholics loyal to the Pope.</note> zealously claim regarding the appearances of souls and spirits. For although Moses and Elijah allowed themselves to be seen and heard on the mountain at <term>Christ's Transfiguration</term> <note>The biblical event where Jesus's divine nature was revealed to his disciples (Matthew 17).</note> (Matthew 17), they did not come from <term>Purgatory</term> <note>original German: "Fegfewr."</note>—as the Papists invent regarding deceased souls who supposedly appear here again, according to <term>Bellarmin</term> <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a Jesuit Cardinal and one of the most important figures of the Catholic Counter-Reformation.</note> in *Concerning Purgatory*, book 1, chapter 11—but rather they came from Heaven. 

Moreover, God had special reasons why they should appear and hold conversation with Christ regarding His departure, which He was to fulfill in Jerusalem. Furthermore, it was not only the souls of Moses and Elijah that appeared, but these two prophets appeared in their glorified bodies, so that the Papists' fable about the wandering and appearance of souls cannot be confirmed by this. Nor can the <term>Witch of Tador</term> <note>Likely a reference to the "Witch of Endor" from 1 Samuel 28. The author argues that the spirit she summoned was not actually the soul of the prophet Samuel.</note>...

The trickery at <term>Endor</term> <note>A reference to the biblical account in 1 Samuel 28 where King Saul consults a medium.</note> <margin>H B</margin> contributes nothing to the matter, because through her incantations and **sorcery**, she did not bring forth the prophet **Samuel**, but rather a deceptive image that resembled him. For **Samuel's** soul was in eternal life, in the hand of **God**, from which it could be removed by no sorcery. The body had long since rotted in the earth and could not be awakened again by any **sorcery**, since **God** alone—and not the **Devil**, much less his <term>accomplices</term> <note>original: "Helffershelffer," literally "helper's helpers."</note>—can make the dead live through His infinite power. <margin>.</margin>

> **Tomorrow you and your sons shall be with me.**

For **Saul**, as a **godless king**, died in despair and killed himself, so that he did not come to the true prophet **Samuel**, but certainly to the one by whose help this bewitched mischief was founded and arranged.

## What is the condition of the souls of the damned, and what do they do in Hell?

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'G' featuring scrolling floral motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>
**J**ust as the souls of believers, in that very moment and twinkling of an eye when they depart from the body, are carried by the holy angels into <term>Abraham's Bosom</term> <note>A biblical term for the place of comfort for the righteous dead.</note> and brought to salvation, where they have nothing but peace and joy and a lovely existence at **God's right hand** forever and ever (**Psalm 16**): So too, the souls of **unbelievers** come immediately, in that very moment and twinkling of an eye when they depart from the body, into the place of torment—as was also touched upon and introduced above—where they suffer unspeakably great **martyrdom** and **pain**. Their <term>worm of an evil conscience</term> <note>A traditional metaphor for the internal gnawing of guilt and regret in the afterlife.</note> gnaws and plagues them without ceasing, as it also stands in <term>Wisdom 4</term> <note>original: "Sap. 4," referring to the Book of the Wisdom of Solomon.</note>: 
> **The godless shall be in great narrowness**

they shall be; they will come discouraged with the conscience of their sins, and their own sins will rebuke them to their faces. They feel God's wrath and disfavor, which is such a terrible pain and burden that no human can bear; they revile and blaspheme, and accuse one another; they die so that they may live, and live so that they may die, and this because of great fear and terror, so that they always await the Last Judgment, where they will be condemned and sentenced to eternal martyrdom with all the hellish tormentors and devils.

Concerning this, *Augustine* now writes in *The Threefold Habitation, chapter 1* <note>original: "Augustinus de Tripl. habitac. c. 1." This refers to "De Triplici Habitaculo," a medieval theological text formerly attributed to St. Augustine.</note> with thoughtful words as follows:
> The <term>Pool of Hell</term> <note>original: "Pfuel der Hellen," a term for the lake of fire.</note> is full of darkness, disunity, enmity, discord, envy and hatred, folly, misery, shame, full of anger, bitterness and pain, full of hunger and thirst, sadness, punishment and revenge, full of all misfortune, evil and misery, which one cannot fathom with thoughts, nor sufficiently express with words: For just as in the kingdom of heaven one has everything one desires, so they have nothing in hell that they wish for and desire; in the kingdom of heaven everything is present in which one has pleasure and joy; in hell is everything that torments and martyrs the damned.

Since we have now until this point provided a thorough account of the state of souls, we must further turn ourselves to the <term>Resurrection of the Dead</term> <note>The Christian doctrine that at the end of time, the physical bodies of all people will be raised and reunited with their souls.</note> and prove from the foundation of Holy Divine Scripture how the same will come to pass; for it is not enough that one knows for certain **that there will be a future resurrection** <note>original Latin: "quod sit futura resurrectio"</note>, that a resurrection of the dead will happen on the <term>Last Day</term>, but one must further know what kind of bodies will rise again, and how we shall be summoned before **CHRIST'S** <term>Judgment Seat</term> <note>original: "Rechenbäncklein," literally a "little accounting bench," a metaphor for a place where a merchant or official settles accounts.</note> at the **Last Judgment**.

and burned to ash, or for the most part have decayed and rotted in the earth; nevertheless, they shall all come forth again and rise with perfect bodies, for **GOD** preserves the bones of all people, so that not one of them is broken, *Psalm 34*. He has numbered all our little hairs upon our head, *Matthew 10*, so that not one of them must perish without His will. He is almighty, and with Him nothing is impossible, *Luke 1*. Therefore, through His divine power and omnipotence, He can also bring the bones of all people together again, as is to be seen in the <term>bones of Ezekiel's field</term> <note>A reference to the vision in Ezekiel 37, where a valley of dry bones is miraculously restored to life; a central image in Christian theology for the physical resurrection.</note>, *Ezekiel 37*. For as the prophet sees in the spirit that the bones come together again, becoming covered with sinews and flesh, and God breathes a living breath into them; so also on the <term>Last Day</term>, when the Judge of the living and the dead shall return, and the trumpet of God shall be blown, and the voice of the archangel shall be heard, the bones of all people shall come together again, and they shall go forth alive from their graves and rise again: some to eternal life, and some to eternal shame and disgrace, *Daniel 12*.

This is also shown to us by the mouth and foundation of truth, **CHRIST**, *John 5*: "The hour is coming," He says, "in which all who are in the graves shall hear His voice and shall go forth—those who have done good, to the resurrection of life; but those who have done evil, to the resurrection of judgment." Thus also says St. Paul, *Acts 24*: "I have hope in God, which they themselves also look for, namely, that there shall be a future resurrection of the dead, both of the righteous and the unrighteous," and *1 Corinthians 15*: "We shall all rise again." Likewise, John prophesies in the <term>Apocalypse</term> <note>The Book of Revelation.</note>, chapter 20: "I saw the dead, both great and small, standing before God, and the books were opened; and another book was opened, which is the Book of Life; and the dead were judged according to what was written in the books, according to their works. And the sea gave up the dead that were in it, and Death and Hell gave up the dead that were in them, and they were judged, every one according to their works."

Afterward, every human being carries an image and a reminder of their joyful resurrection in their own body; for our temporal <note>original: "zeitlicher," referring to our earthly or time-bound existence.</note>

## Shall all people, good and evil,
## by the power of the merit and Resurrection of Christ,
## rise from the dead?

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'D' featuring foliate scrolls and floral patterns.</image-desc> That all and every individual, whoever they are and however they have died, shall rise from the dead—no one should doubt this, as Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), an influential early Christian theologian.</note> also says in his *Handbook to Laurence* <note>original: "Enchirid. ad Laurent."</note>, chapter 84: "A Christian must in no way doubt that the flesh of all humans who have been born and will be born, and who have died and will die, shall rise again." <note>original: "Resurrecturam carnem omnium quicunq; nati sunt hominum atq; nascentur, & mortui sunt atq; morientur, nullo modo dubitare debet Christianus"</note> Likewise, there is no dispute or doubt that the believers, by the power of Christ's Resurrection, shall come forth again from their graves. However, regarding the unbelievers and the wicked, we find differing opinions. For the <term>Calvinists</term> <note>Followers of the Reformed tradition of John Calvin.</note> believe and teach that they will indeed rise, but not by the power of Christ's Resurrection, but rather "by the necessity and efficacy of that divine decree found in Genesis 2:17" <note>original: "sed divini illius decreti necessitate & efficacitate, quod habetur, Gen. 2. 17." referring to the warning given to Adam in Eden.</note> (as cited by Bucanus <note>Guillaume Bucanus, a Reformer and professor at Lausanne.</note> and Ursinus <note>Zacharias Ursinus, a leading theologian of the Reformed movement and primary author of the Heidelberg Catechism.</note>). Or, they suggest it is "on account of the righteous judgment of God, to which they will be awakened so that they may be punished eternally," as Polanus <note>Amandus Polanus von Polansdorf, a prominent Reformed theologian.</note> writes. On the contrary, God’s Word teaches us that all people—good and evil, believers and unbelievers, righteous and unrighteous—are in Christ, and

<meta>This page continues the theological argument that Christ's resurrection power extends to all humanity. <margin>X ij</margin>

by the power of His resurrection and merit, they shall rise; for Christ is indeed the <term>firstfruits</term> <note>The first part of a harvest; used here to mean Christ is the first of many who will be resurrected.</note> of those who sleep (1 Corinthians 15). Just as the firstfruits of the sheaves—which the Israelites, by God's command, brought annually into the house of the Lord—were blessed by the priest and dedicated as an offering to God, and this blessing extended over the entire supply of grain in the field (Exodus 23, Leviticus 23). So also has Christ become the firstfruits in the great harvest of the human race, so that through His blessing, merit, and resurrection, the entire harvest—that is, all and every individual person—would be blessed, and would have and obtain the resurrection in Him. This is what St. Paul also indicates with clear words in 1 Corinthians 15: "As in Adam they all die," he says, "so in Christ shall they all be made alive, but each in his own order: Christ the firstfruits, then those who belong to Christ when He comes." 

Secondly: those who are purchased and harvested by Christ through His precious blood shall also rise again by the power of such redemption. Now, however, it is not only the <term>elect</term> <note>In theology, those chosen or predestined by God for salvation.</note> who are redeemed by Christ—as Piscator <note>Johannes Piscator (1546–1625), a German Reformed theologian known for his views on the atonement.</note> claims (against Schaffman, thesis 110, and volume 2 of his *Theological Disputations*, pages 129–130), as well as Zanchius <note>Girolamo Zanchi (1516–1590), an influential Italian Protestant theologian.</note> (Volume 2 of his *Miscellanies*, page 117), Spindler <note>Josua Spindler, a Reformed polemicist.</note> in his *Report*, page 263, Beza <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), the successor to John Calvin in Geneva.</note>, Musculus <note>Wolfgang Musculus (1497–1563), a significant Reformed theologian.</note>, Kimedonchius <note>Jacobus Kimedoncius, a Dutch professor of theology.</note>, and other <term>Calvinists</term> <note>Followers of the Reformed tradition who often held to 'limited atonement'—the idea that Christ died only for the saved.</note>—but rather, Christ has redeemed all people on Earth. As it stands in John 1: "Behold, this is the Lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world," and John 3: "For God so loved the world that He gave His only begotten Son, so that all who believe in Him should not be lost, but have eternal life." Likewise, 1 John 2: "If anyone sins, we have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the righteous, and He is the atonement for our sins—and not only for ours, but also for those of the whole world." 

And such universal redemption occurred not only **sufficiently** <note>original Latin: "sufficienter"; the idea that Christ's death was valuable enough to save all, even if it didn't apply to all.</note>, but also **effectively** <note>original Latin: "efficienter"; here used to argue that Christ intended His death to be a real payment for every person's sins.</note>, such that Christ died even for the damned and fully made satisfaction for them, as Paul clearly speaks in Romans 14 and 15, and 1 Corinthians 8:11; likewise the Apostle Peter in 2 Peter 2:1. Therefore, it follows undeniably that all people, believers and unbelievers alike, shall rise by the power of Christ's resurrection—though to different ends: the former to eternal life, the latter to eternal disgrace and shame (Daniel 12, John 5).

Now, Adam's sin spreads through the entire width and breadth of the world and has reached us all, and through sin, death (Romans 5), which is the "wages of sin" (Romans 6). Therefore, Christ's life and resurrection also extend to each and every person, and <term>consequently</term> <note>original Latin: "per consequens"</note> they shall all rise in Christ according to the prophecy of Paul in 1 Corinthians 15. For "where 'all' is said, there no one is excepted, no one is excluded," says [Saint] Ambrose <note>A 4th-century bishop and influential Church Father.</note> in his work *On Repentance*, chapter 10. <note>original Latin: "ubi enim omnis dicitur, ibi nemo excipitur, nullus excluditur"</note>

For everything Christ has done—through His actions and omissions, through His suffering and death—hangs together inseparably. Whoever possesses or despises one benefit, possesses or despises them all. ## But will all people, by virtue of Christ's resurrection, rise to the same end?

**I**ndeed, *Francesco Pucci of Florence* <note>Francesco Pucci (1543–1597) was a Florentine thinker who traveled across Europe. He was controversial for his "unscriptural" belief that all humans are born in a state of grace and will ultimately be saved.</note> in his *Fifth Argument on the Efficacy of Christ as Savior*, has quite thoughtlessly spread this unscriptural opinion: that the punishment imposed on the human race because of sin should cease once the human body has turned to dust and earth, and that all people shall rise to the same end—namely, to salvation. In a similar way, *Zwingli* <note>Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), the primary figure of the Swiss Reformation. The author is likely critiquing Zwingli's view that virtuous heathens who never heard the Gospel might still be found in heaven.</note> in volume 2, page 559, praises the unbelieving pagans as blessed. 

But if we wish to judge this according to the norm and form of Divine Holy Scripture, the Prophet *Daniel, Chapter 12,* and Christ Himself in *John 5* inform us that the believers, who have done good, shall rise to life; but the unbelievers, who have deeply immersed themselves in all kinds of sins without repentance, shall rise to the resurrection of judgment, so that they may be condemned and receive according to what they have done during their bodily life (*Romans 14, 2 Corinthians 5*). For although the **LORD** Christ has redeemed all people and desires that they all should come to the knowledge of the truth and be saved (*1 Timothy 2*)—which He confirms with a solemn oath in *Ezekiel 18 & 33*:

->As I live, says the **LORD** God, I do not desire the death of the sinner, but rather that he should turn and live:<-

Nevertheless, because the majority tread Christ's merit underfoot and will not allow themselves to be converted and taught toward salvation, they bring misfortune upon themselves (*Hosea 13*). The judgment stands which **CHRIST** establishes and sets in *John 3*, where He says: he who does not believe shall be damned. And such judgment is not abolished by temporal death; rather, it is then that the <term>execution</term> <note>original: "exsecution" — referring to the carrying out of a legal sentence.</note> and fulfillment truly begin, so that the souls of the ungodly immediately enter the place of condemnation. Now, just as the souls suffer eternal pain because of unbelief and other sins which they committed here in the body, so also must the body, which at the same time sinned—

<summary>The author rejects the "unscriptural" idea of universal salvation proposed by Francesco Pucci and Zwingli, arguing instead that while resurrection is universal, its outcome is divided between eternal life for believers and eternal judgment for the unrepentant.</summary>
