sinned <note>The text continues from the previous page: "...so also must the body, which at the same time sinned..."</note> — shall rise on the Last Day and be afflicted with the same punishment and pain, as *Cyprian* <note>St. Cyprian of Carthage (c. 210–258 AD), an early Christian bishop and martyr whose writings were foundational for Western theology.</note> writes in his *Sermon on the Resurrection*:

> <note>original Latin: "Resurrectio communis est, & ante tribunal Christi..."</note> The resurrection is common to all, and it is necessary for both the just and the unjust to stand in the body before the tribunal of Christ; for God's justice dictates this, so that piety and impiety may be weighed with their due rewards. And those who refused to find an end to their evils shall be enclosed in infinite vengeance within their punishments; but those who gloried in the cross, reigning with the Crucified One, shall become partakers of a blessed eternity.

So also writes *Augustine* <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential "Doctors of the Church."</note> in the *Enchiridion*, chapter 92:

> <note>original Latin: "Resurgent boni & mali sed conditione inæquali..."</note> Both the good and the evil shall rise, but in an unequal condition. Christians shall rise blessed, so incorruptible in glory that they are even <term>impassible</term> <note>original: "impassibiles" — a theological term meaning incapable of feeling pain or suffering.</note>, for they shall be clothed in the glory of impassibility and rise into life and eternal joy. The ungodly shall rise miserable in the highest ignominy, capable of suffering, for they shall suffer eternal punishment through death. Their "living" is an eternal dying, where perpetual pain is not ended but afflicts them; it is like a perpetual corruption. For there is no true life except where one lives happily, nor is there true incorruption except where health is corrupted by no pain.

# Will all people then rise at the same time?

Although the Jews and Rabbis hold the opinion that those buried in the land of Canaan <note>Refers to a Jewish tradition that the resurrection would begin in the Holy Land, and those buried elsewhere would have to roll through underground tunnels to reach Israel before rising.</note> shall rise first, nevertheless—because the Last Day and the Last Judgment will not proceed slowly, but will happen quickly and nimbly—we certainly believe that the Resurrection of the Dead will not proceed <term>successively</term> <note>original: "successivè" — meaning one after another in a sequence.</note>, where one comes forth after another. Rather, all who are in the graves, when they hear the voice of the Son of God, shall rise at the same time, wherever they may be buried, near or far. For before God, everything is present, and what is far from us is nearest to Him. This is known and recognized not only from the Prophet *Daniel, Chapter 12*, and the Evangelist *John, Chapter 5*, and *St. Paul, 1 Thessalonians 4*, but we also see it in the "field of bones" of *Ezekiel* <note>Refers to the vision in Ezekiel 37 of the Valley of Dry Bones coming back to life.</note>, among which such a rattling and crackling arises that they all become living at once; it does not happen slowly, but quickly and nimb-

# What, then, will be the circumstance for those who live to see the Last Day? Will they not die?

<margin>Romans 5</margin>
<margin>John 14 & 15</margin>
<margin>Psalm 14</margin>
<margin>Romans 3</margin>
<margin>Hebrews 9</margin>
<margin>1 Corinthians 15</margin>
**S**ince death has spread to all people through sin, and we have all sinned, and iniquity flows in us like water, no sinful person is secured or freed from death—as it stands in Hebrews 9: "It is appointed for man once to die, and after that the judgment." However, this distinction will be made for those living on the Last Day: they will not die slowly, one after another, as happens now. Instead, it will happen nimbly, so that they are all at once, in a moment and a twinkling, both dead and alive, and suddenly transformed, as St. Paul teaches. Regarding these words, *Luther* <note>Martin Luther (1483–1546), the central figure of the Protestant Reformation.</note> comments and writes very finely: "The Last Day will come so suddenly," he says, "like a snare, before anyone expects it; when the world will be entirely secure, and in a moment everything transforms." By this, he does not mean to deny that we all must die, but rather he says: "That we shall not fall asleep"—meaning those who are caught in that same final hour—"they will not pass away like a person normally does on their deathbed, nor be scraped into a grave or under the earth." For here the Scripture calls "falling asleep" those who enter a grave or a coffin; but these will [be changed]

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the sudden transformation of the living on the Last Day. It picks up from the previous page's discussion of how those alive will not experience a traditional death and burial, but rather an instantaneous change of state.</meta>

[be changed] out of this life and enter into that one, so that they do not come under the earth <note>meaning they will not be buried in graves</note>, but shall simply be transformed. For the Greek word <note>original: *allassō* (ἀλλάσσω), used in 1 Corinthians 15:51</note> that stands here primarily means to "change" or "alter" in such a way that one is moved from one place to another—as if moving out of the water onto dry land, or from the earth into the air. 

Thus we shall also be found there in a moment, in another place and in another manner—those of us who just an hour before were here on earth, in the house or in the field, and suddenly snatched away from the table or bed, or from work, whether we are walking, standing, sitting, or lying down. So it is that in a moment we shall be dead and alive again, and changed in all things, and hover above in the clouds. He means this kind of change here, although he also includes the other change of <term>quality</term> <note>original Latin: *qualitatis* — referring to the nature or properties of the resurrected body</note> or form, of which he has already spoken above: that the body will put on a different garment—that is, it shall be <term>transfigured</term> <note>original: *verkleret* — historically used to mean glorified, radiant, or made spiritually "clear"</note> and become bright, far more magnificent and beautiful than the sun. 

But this will not happen while the body still walks in this lodging <note>referring to the world or the mortal state</note> and in this garment <note>mortal flesh</note>; instead, in that same moment, everything must first be stripped naked, burned to powder, and snatched away in that very instant. He explains this further himself in 1 Thessalonians 4, showing that it shall all happen at once in an instant: the dead shall be moved out of their graves, and we shall be snatched away with them, however and wherever we are found, and torn away from this mortal being and life, to be transfigured together at the same time. 

For it cannot and must not happen so slowly that one person always buries the next until we all die one after another; rather, we must all be gathered together at once and thus be moved through the transition together. Yet, this will not happen without death; nevertheless, **GOD** will demonstrate His almighty power and majesty, so that in a single moment everything that is on earth must be consumed, and the whole world shall lie in a heap and become something else. And we shall be eternally alongside and with **CHRIST**, but the others who have not believed shall be cast away into eternal <term>torment</term> <note>original: *Quall* (modern: Qual)</note>.

<summary>This page describes the instantaneous transformation of those living at the Last Day, explaining that they will experience a rapid death and resurrection that bypasses burial. It emphasizes the "transfiguration" of the body into a glorified, sun-like state and the sudden destruction and renewal of the world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Resurrection, specifically addressing the sequence of events during the Last Day. ## Will the living, however, not precede those with the Lord who have passed away and shall rise from the dead?

Although some people will still be living at the arrival of the Lord for Judgment, they will not precede those who "sleep" <note>a common biblical metaphor for those who have died in the faith</note> in the presence of the Lord. Instead, when the trumpet of the <term>Archangel</term> <note>original: *ErtzEngels*</note> is blown and the voice of the Son of God is heard, the dead shall rise first. After that, the living shall be snatched up and carried away together with them to meet the Lord in the air. All of this will happen in a "now" and in the twinkling of an eye—that the dead arise and the living are snatched away and transformed with them. 

As St. Augustine says <note>original Latin: *in ictu oculi ut Augustin. lib. 20. de C. D. c. 20.* — referring to "The City of God," Book 20, Chapter 20</note>, this occurs "in the twinkling of an eye." And indeed, it happens in the very act of being snatched away, as St. Ambrose says <note>original Latin: *Ambrof. Tom. 3. fol. 264.*</note>: "Death will come as if through sleep, so that the soul, having departed, is returned in a moment; for when they are taken up, they will die, so that as they reach the Lord, they may receive their souls in the Lord’s presence, because they cannot be dead while they are with the Lord." 

St. Paul clearly indicates this to us in 1 Thessalonians 4 and says: "This we say to you as a word of the Lord: that we who live and remain until the coming of the Lord will not precede those who sleep. For the Lord Himself will come down from heaven with a war-cry and the voice of the Archangel, and with the trumpet of God; and the dead in Christ shall rise first. Afterward, we who live and remain shall be caught up together with them in the clouds to meet the Lord in the air, and so we shall be with the Lord forever. Therefore, comfort one another with these words."

## Since heaven and earth shall melt in fire, how can our bodies be preserved in such a fiery glow?

Although on the <term>Last Day</term> <note>original: *Jüngsten tage*</note> there will be nothing but fire in all places and ends—so that even the elements shall melt from the heat and the heavens shall pass away with a great crash (as seen in 2 Peter 3)—nevertheless [shall]... <meta>catchword: shall</meta>

It also introduces the final destruction of the world by fire, questioning how human bodies can survive such heat.</summary>

# Will those who are living, however, not precede those who have died and shall rise from the dead before the Lord?

Although some people will still be living at the arrival of the **LORD** for Judgment, they shall not precede those who sleep <note>meaning those who have already died</note> before the **LORD**. Instead, when the trumpet of the <term>Archangel</term> <note>original: *ErtzEngel*</note> is blown, and the voice of the Son of God is heard, the dead shall rise first. Afterward, those who are living shall be <term>snatched up</term> <note>original: *hingezückt* — referring to the Rapture or being caught up</note> and carried away together with them, to meet the **LORD** in the air. 

All of this will happen in a trice and a moment, so that the dead arise and the living are snatched away and changed with them "in the twinkling of an eye" <note>original Latin: *in ictu oculi*, a reference to 1 Corinthians 15:52</note>, as Augustine [writes] in the 20th book of *The City of God*, chapter 20. And indeed, this happens in the very act of being snatched away, as Ambrose says in Volume 3, page 264: "death will occur as if through a sleep, so that the soul, having departed, is returned in a moment. For when they are taken up, they shall die, so that reaching the Lord, they may receive their souls in the presence of the Lord, because they cannot be dead while with the Lord." <note>This explains the paradox of how the living "die" and are "resurrected" instantly during their ascent to heaven.</note> 

St. Paul clearly shows this to us in 1 Thessalonians 4, saying: "This we say to you as a word of the **LORD**: that we who are alive and remain until the coming of the **LORD** will not precede those who are asleep. For the **LORD** himself will descend from heaven with a <term>shout</term> <note>original: *Feldgeschrey* — literally a "field cry" or battle cry used to signal an army</note>, with the voice of the Archangel, and with the trumpet of **GOD**; and the dead in Christ will rise first. Then we who are alive and remain shall be caught up together with them in the clouds to meet the **LORD** in the air, and so we shall always be with the **LORD**." Therefore, comfort one another with these words.

# But since heaven and earth shall melt in fire, how can our bodies be preserved in such fiery heat?

Although on the Last Day there will be pure fire in all places and ends, so that even the elements melt from the heat and the heavens shall pass away with a great crash (2 Peter 3), yet

...we should not worry about where we will be or remain at that time. For He who preserved the three men—Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego—in the glowing furnace, so that the fire did not harm their bodies nor singe a hair on their heads, and not even the smell of burning could be detected on them (Daniel 3), He can and will also most graciously preserve us in the final fire of this world. We will not even notice as heaven and earth turn to powder. Therefore, we should simply entrust this to the power and omnipotence of God and place ourselves in His hands, so we shall surely remain safe. 

Indeed, Christ Himself says in Matthew 24 that the angels will wait for the <term>Elect</term> <note>original: *Außerwelten* — those chosen by God</note> and gather them. Also: where there is a carcass, there the eagles gather; that is, where Christ is, there also are the Elect. And as He lives, so shall we also live (John 17). If the <term>Angelic Guard</term> <note>original: *Englische Quardy*</note> protects us and we are where Christ is, then we are well cared for; we need not fear any fire. The ungodly and condemned may fear the fiery wrath of God, as they shall and must be sentenced to eternal pain, but we have much more reason to rejoice, because our redemption draws near (Luke 21).

# Will the untimely little children who died in their mother's womb, as well as the <term>monstrous births</term> <note>original Latin: *partus prodigiosos* — a historical term for births with significant physical deformities or abnormalities</note>, also rise on the Last Day?

**O**lthough many little children die in the <term>womb</term> <note>original: *Mutterleibe*</note> before they are mature and fully formed—all of which stems from <term>original sin</term> <note>original: *angeerbten Sünde* — literally "inherited sin"</note>—nevertheless, we should doubt neither their salvation nor their resurrection. This applies also to "monstrous births," *provided they are truly human births* <note>original Latin: *modò sint veri partus humani*</note>. ## But will people rise again in their deformity, 
### as they were when they died?

In this world, it is indeed the case among the children of men that some are mute and simple-minded, crooked and lame, deaf and blind, or otherwise deformed; but in the resurrection it will not be so. "There nothing unseemly will exist, but whatever is to be, this will be fitting, because it would not be so if it were not fitting," Augustine in the *Enchiridion*, chapter 90. <note>original Latin: *ibi indecorum non erit, sed quicquid futurum est, hoc decebit...*</note> There all lack and infirmity shall cease, and a beautiful, magnificent, and well-formed body shall come forth "without any flaw, without any deformity, without any corruption and difficulty," Augustine in the *Enchiridion*, chapter 91. <note>original Latin: *sine ullo vitio, sine ulla deformitate, sine ulla corruption & difficultate*</note> As **St. Paul** indicates this in **1 Corinthians 15**: It is sown perishable, and it will rise imperishable; it is sown in dishonor, and it will rise in <term>glory</term> <note>original German: *herzligkeit* — splendor or magnificence</note>; it is sown in weakness, and it will rise in power; it is sown a natural body, and it will rise a spiritual body. "Not that they cease to be bodies, but because they exist by a life-giving spirit," Augustine, Book 13 of the *City of God*, chapter 22, and *Against Adimantus*, chapter 22. <note>original Latin: *non quod corpora esse desistant, sed quia spiritu vivificante subsistunt*</note> For all infirmity and weakness, all sick-

## How Then Shall the Dead Arise: Naked and Bare, or in Clothes?

There are, without doubt, many simple folk who do not know what they should hold and believe regarding this question; therefore, I do not deem it unnecessary to explain the same for the instruction of the simpler ones <note>original Latin: *ad erudiendos simpliciores*</note>. For if we follow human reason, we can know or understand nothing at all of this, even though Taurellus <note>Nicolaus Taurellus (1547–1606), a philosopher-physician who sought to reconcile reason with faith</note> emboldens himself to use his reason to investigate the resurrection and its manner <note>original Latin: *ejusdemque modum*</note> in the preface to *Concerning Eternal Things*, page 17 and following. We see the same tendency among the Turks <note>referring to Muslims</note>, the Photinians <note>a sect that denied the divinity of Christ and held unconventional views on the soul</note>, and similar heretics and chatterers, who have only vain, fleshly thoughts regarding this. But if we judge according to the Scriptures and set reason aside, we find that after this life we will no longer have need of clothes, or anything else of the sort that happens here in this world. For now our bodies are weak and needy; we must have for our care and maintenance food and drink, clothes and shoes, house and home; but in the resurrection we will no longer have such weak bodies, but we shall be

whom Christ, in His state of humiliation <note>original: *stande seiner Erniedrigung*. In Lutheran theology, this refers to the period of Christ's life on Earth where He set aside His divine majesty to live as a human.</note>, healed through His almighty, miraculous hand—how they were so quickly and nimbly able to speak, see, and walk rightly, even though they still wandered in mortal tabernacles <note>A biblical metaphor (from 2 Peter 1:13) comparing the human body to a temporary tent or "hut."</note>, had flesh and blood upon them, and were full of sin. How then could the Lord God not be able to create and act in the same way, so that in the resurrection the **glorified bodies** would no longer know any single weakness? That they would no longer have any single defect upon them? Since they are indeed without sin and have laid aside the mortal tabernacle.

Therefore, all sad and sorrowful little hearts <note>The author uses the diminutive *Herzklein*, a term of endearment and comfort for the suffering.</note>, who cry out with David’s little deer for fresh water (**Psalm 42**), and all sick and frail people, should take note of this for their special comfort and true refreshment of the heart: that they may overcome with patience what is laid upon them by God. For it lasts but a moment and will be completed with unspeakable glory, honor, and majesty, as Saint Paul indicates in **1 Corinthians 15**.

# How then will the dead rise? 
## Naked and bare, or in clothes?

<insert>T</insert>here are, without doubt, many simple people who do not know what they should hold and believe regarding this question; therefore, I do not consider it unnecessary <term>to educate the simpler ones: *ad erudiendos simpliciores*</term> to explain this. For if we follow human reason, we can know or understand nothing at all of this, regardless of how <term>Taurellus</term> <note>Nicolaus Taurellus (1547–1606) was a German physician and philosopher who attempted to use logic to explain theological mysteries.</note> dares to use his reason to investigate the resurrection <term>and the manner of the same: *ejusdemque modum*</term> in his *Preface on Eternal Things*, page 17 and following. We see the same thing among the <term>Turks</term> <note>A common historical term for Muslims.</note>, <term>Photinians</term> <note>A sect of Christians whose views on the nature of Christ and the resurrection were considered heretical by the mainstream church.</note>, and similar heretics and <term>chatterboxes: *Schwebzer*</term> <note>A derogatory term for those who talk idly or fanatically about religion without sound doctrine.</note>, who have only vain, fleshly thoughts about this. 

But when we judge according to the Scriptures and set reason aside, we find that after this life we will no longer have need of clothing, or anything else that occurs here in this world. For now our bodies are weak and needy; we must have food and drink, clothes and shoes, house and home for our care and maintenance. But in the resurrection, we will no longer have such weak bodies, but we will

rise in power (**1 Corinthians 15**) to eternal life, where God will be all in all. He will restore everything in a heavenly manner, so that we will have no need for clothing, for house or yard, nor for eating or drinking (**Romans 14, Revelation 7**). We will not be together in a fleshly way, as Christ indicates in **Matthew 22**, but are like the angels of God in Heaven. For clothes came into this world because of sin, as we read in **Genesis 3**, where previously our first parents were naked, and such nakedness was for them a special ornament and decoration. Once there is no more sin, we will also no longer need a <term>sin-cover: *Sündendeckel*</term> <note>A term used by the author to describe clothing as a physical reminder of the Fall of Man.</note> and clothes. 

Therefore, the proud and haughty might well consider this—those who chase after unstable fashions, who like the <term>Rich Purple Squire</term> <note>A reference to the "Rich Man" (often called Dives) from the parable in Luke 16:19, who was clothed in purple and fine linen.</note> dress themselves magnificently every day, even beyond their station and means, and parade around boasting in foreign, overseas patterns. For such splendor and pride serve no purpose, but rather do much harm to salvation, as **Isaiah** indicates in **Chapter 3**. Furthermore, such things soon reach their end with temporal death. What does it profit us to so expensively adorn and decorate this <term>stinking maggot-sack: *stinckenden Madensack*</term> <note>A vivid, common Reformation-era metaphor for the mortal body to remind readers of their humility and inevitable decay.</note> here? Let us much more strive for the eternal ornament of the soul, for the robe of righteousness and for the garments of salvation (**Isaiah 61**); then the Lord will have pleasure in such beauty (**Psalm 45**), and we may appear with them before God’s countenance, and shall then be and remain with the Lord for all time (**1 Thessalonians 4**).

### Will not the very same body, which is 
### buried in the earth, rise again?

Not only have the pagans mockingly denied this, but <term>Origen</term> <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 185–253 AD), an early Christian scholar whose views on the spiritual nature of the resurrection body were often contested by later theologians.</note> also taught <term>that the body would rise again not identical in number, but in species</term> <note>original: *corpus non idem numero sed specie resurrecturum*. In theological terms, "identical in number" means the exact same physical matter, while "identical in species" would mean a new body that just looks like the old one.</note>; that it would not be the same body, but another which was like and similar to it, <term>as reported by Methodius in Aglaophon and Epiphanius in Heresy 64</term>. And such error was not only renewed by <term>Durandus</term> <note>Durandus of Saint-Pourçain (c. 1275–1334), a philosopher who argued that the soul could be reunited with any matter, not necessarily the original atoms.</note>, who promoted this monstrous speech and blasphemy: <term>that it could not happen, even through the absolute power of God, that the same body in number should rise</term>; but in our time, Taurellus has also—

teaches concerning the *Eternal Resurrection*, page 620. Among the **Calvinists**, and **Matthaeus Cerrarius** among the **Anabaptists**, it is taught that our bodies will not come forth again, but that God will create other bodies which shall be united with our souls. So also writes **Schegpius** <note>Jakob Schegk (1511–1587), a physician and philosopher who argued for a more spiritualized view of the resurrection body.</note> in *Antisimonio section 9, page 420*: <note>original Latin: *quod alia erit substantia corporis gloriosi, quàm nunc est.*</note> **that the substance of the glorified body will be different from what it is now.** 

The Holy Scripture, however, teaches us to the contrary: that the very same body that is shoveled into the earth and buried shall also rise again. For God preserves all the bones of the saints, so that not one of them is broken (**Psalm 34**); likewise, not even a hair from their head shall perish (**Luke 21**). This is so that the very same body which here had to suffer and die shall rise again, and there also rule and inherit, as **St. Paul** says in **2 Corinthians 5**: we must all appear before the judgment seat of Christ, so that each one may receive according to what he has done during his bodily life, <term>through the body</term> <note>original Greek: *διὰ τοῦ σώματος* (dia tou somatos). This phrase emphasizes that judgment is tied to actions performed specifically in the physical body.</note>, <term>in the body</term> <note>original Latin: *in corpore*.</note>, whether it be good or evil. 

So we also read in **1 Corinthians 15**: this corruptible <note>original Latin: *hoc corruptibile*, namely the body that had to decay in the earth.</note> must put on the incorruptible, and this mortal must put on immortality. Likewise, in **John 5**: All who are in the graves will hear the voice of the Son of God and come forth. Now, since our bodies are in the graves after death and must decay, therefore those very same ones—and no others—will come forth and rise again. **Job** grasps this comfort in his suffering and says in **Chapter 19**: I know that my Redeemer lives, and he will hereafter awaken me out of the earth, and I shall thereafter be surrounded with this my skin, and in my flesh I shall see God; him I shall see for myself, and my eyes shall behold him and not another. So also says **Isaiah** in **Chapter 26**: Your dead shall live, and shall rise with the corpse.

We see this also in the little seeds, a metaphor **Paul** uses in **1 Corinthians 15**. For the very same thing that is sown also grows forth again, once it has died in the earth; so it is also with our bodies. The very same ones that are sown in the <term>God’s Acre: *Gottesacker*</term> <note>A traditional German term for a cemetery, viewing it as a field where bodies are "planted" like seeds awaiting the harvest of the resurrection.</note> will grow forth and rise again on the Last Day, as **Tertullian** says in the book *On the Resurrection*, chapter 52: <note>original Latin: *Videamus jam nunc quo corpore venturos mortuos disputet*.</note> **"Let us now see with what body he argues the dead will come."** And well—

<meta>This page continues the argument for the "numerical identity" of the resurrected body, using the metaphor of the seed introduced on the previous page to demonstrate that the body which dies is the same one that is restored.</meta>

"...that what has broken forth immediately shows," as if someone asks such a thing, he says: **"Fool, that which you sow is not made alive unless it has first died."** <note>original Latin: *stulte, inquit, tu quod seminas, non vivificatur nisi mortuum fuerit.*</note> Let this then be established by the example of the seed: no other flesh is made alive than the very same flesh that died.

Furthermore, God the Lord has consecrated and hallowed the bodies of believers as his temple (**1 Corinthians 3 & 6**). Christ has redeemed not only our souls but also the body—and thus the whole person—with his own precious blood. Likewise, we are fed and given drink with Christ’s body and blood in the <term>Holy Supper</term> <note>The Eucharist or Communion.</note>. All of this happens not primarily for this life, but for the eternal life; therefore, if our bodies are to enjoy such a goal and purpose, they must also rise again from death and come forth from the graves.

Moreover, if it were not our own bodies that were to rise again, but if God instead were to make *other* bodies to be united with our souls, then it would not be a <term>resurrection</term> <note>original Latin: *resurrectio*, literally a "rising again" of what previously fell down.</note> but a <term>creation</term> <note>original Latin: *creatio*.</note>. However, regarding such a "creation" or making of new bodies, the Scriptures maintain an eternal silence. Therefore, our own bodies shall rise again; **"for it is our body,"** as **Tertullian** says in the fifth book *Against Marcion*, chapter 9, **"which loses its soul, and by losing it, becomes dead; thus the term 'dead' applies to the body. Furthermore, if there is a resurrection of the dead, and the 'dead' is nothing other than the body, then it will be a resurrection of the body. Likewise, the very word 'resurrection' claims no other thing than that which has fallen."** And so it happens, as **Rufinus** <note>Tyrannius Rufinus (c. 345–410), a monk and historian best known for his commentary on the Apostles' Creed.</note> says in his *Explanation of the Apostles' Creed*, section 41: **"that for each soul, not a confused or foreign body, but the very one it once had is restored."**

Finally, we have an example of this in the Lord Christ; for just as he rose, so shall we also rise. Because he is our head and we are his members, our bodies shall also become like his glorified body (**Philippians 3**). Now, Christ rose in his very own body (**John 2**); therefore, we too shall rise in our own bodies, **"not in some other foreign bodies, but in those very ones by which we are now surrounded, and in which we are now buried,"** as **Jerome** says in his work *Against the Errors of John of Jerusalem*. **Ephrem** <note>Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373), a prolific Syriac deacon and hymnographer.</note> also writes of this in his *Sermon on the Resurrection*.

## Will the glorified bodies also have flesh and blood in the resurrection?

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial letter 'H' featuring stylized floral and foliate motifs.</image-desc>

Regarding this matter, we find entirely contrary opinions among our opponents. For the <term>Photinians</term> <note>Followers of Photinus of Sirmium, a 4th-century bishop whose views were often associated by later writers with those who denied the physical nature of the resurrection.</note> do not admit "that the elect are to have flesh and blood in eternal life," <note>original Latin: *quod electi carnem & sanguinem sint habituri in vita æterna*</note> as Christoph Ostorodt writes in his *Instruction*, chapter 14. So also writes Jakob Schegk in his *Against Simonius*, pages 420 and 440; likewise Rudolph Goclenius in his *Adversaria*, question 63, pages 278 and 279: "that glorified bodies are devoid of matter." <note>original Latin: *quod corpora gloriosa materiæ sint expertia*</note> 

However, Holy Scripture informs us to the contrary: that while the glorified bodies will certainly possess other qualities and angelic attributes, they will nonetheless not be "immaterial bodies" <note>original Greek: *αϋλα* (aüla), meaning without matter or substance.</note>. For if that were the case, they would not be the bodies we have now. Instead, they will be true bodies in terms of their matter, substance, and essence, in every respect as our bodies are now. "For God did not take away the substance to which He assigned an angelic quality," <note>original Latin: *non enim substantiam Deus abstulit, cui qualitatem angelicam attribuit*</note> says **Tertullian** in *On the Resurrection of the Flesh*. Even Goclenius himself must confess this in his eighth disputation *On the condition of the glorified body*, question 2. For that very same body which is now mortal and corruptible will put on the incorruptible and the immortal (**1 Corinthians 15**). And that same body which lies in the dust of the earth shall wake up again (**Daniel 12**), and we shall see God in our flesh with our own eyes (**Job 19**).

Now, although Saint Paul says that the glorified bodies will be "spiritual" (**1 Corinthians 15**), he does not intend by this to abolish the essence and substance of a true body. Just as he does not say that they will be "spirits" <note>original Greek: *πνευμα* (pneuma)</note>, but rather "spiritual bodies" <note>original Latin/Greek phrase: *corpora πνευματικά* (pneumatika)</note>. They will not be spirits, but they will be spiritual—that is, they will possess spiritual qualities—as **Anselm** writes in the *Proslogion*, chapter 25: "it will not be a spiritual body in its essential nature, but in its power." <note>original Latin: *non erit corpus spirituale quidditate sed potestate*</note> For a body cannot be two things at once: a spirit and a body; "no being becomes two opposites, through privation and contradiction." <note>original Latin: *nullum ens fit duo opposita, privatione & contradictione*</note>

## Will the glorified bodies also have flesh and blood in the resurrection?

On this point, we find entirely contrary opinions among our opponents. For the <term>Photinians</term> <note>Followers of Photinus of Sirmium; in this context, it refers to Socinian or non-traditional thinkers who denied the physical resurrection of the same flesh.</note> do not admit "that the elect shall have flesh and blood in eternal life" <note>original Latin: *quod electi carnem & sanguinem sint habituri in vita æterna*</note>, as **Ostorod** writes in his *Instruction*, chapter 14. Likewise, **Schegkkius** <note>Jakob Schegk (1511–1587), a physician and philosopher who often clashed with theologians on the nature of the body.</note> writes in *Antisimonio* (pages 420 and 440), and similarly **Goclenius** <note>Rudolf Goclenius (1547–1628), a German scholastic philosopher.</note> in *Adversaria* (question 63, pages 278–279), "that glorified bodies are devoid of matter" <note>original Latin: *quod corpora gloriosa materiæ sint expertia*</note>.

However, Holy Scripture informs us to the contrary: that although the glorified bodies will possess different qualities and <term>angelic</term> <note>German: *Englische*—here meaning "angel-like" or "heavenly," not related to the English nation.</note> characteristics, they will nonetheless not be **immaterial** bodies <note>original Greek: *ἄϋλα* (ayla)</note>. For in such a case, they would not be the same bodies that we have now. Rather, they will be true bodies according to their matter, substance, and essence, in every way as our bodies are now. "For God did not take away the substance to which He attributed an angelic quality," <note>original Latin: *non enim substantiam Deus abstulit, cui qualitatem angelicam attribuit*</note> as **Tertullian** says in *On the Resurrection of the Flesh*. Even Goclenius himself must confess this in his eighth disputation *On the Condition of the Glorified Body*, question 2. For that very same body which is now mortal and corruptible shall put on the incorruptible and the immortal (**1 Corinthians 15**); and that same body which lies in the dust of the earth shall wake up again (**Daniel 12**); and "in our flesh, with our eyes, we shall see God" (**Job 19**).

Now, although St. Paul says that the glorified bodies will be "spiritual" (**1 Corinthians 15**), he does not intend by this to abolish the nature and substance of a true body. Note that he does not say they will be **spirits** <note>original Greek: *πνεύματα* (pneumata)</note>, but **spiritual** bodies <note>original Latin/Greek: *corpora πνευματικά* (pneumatika)</note>. They will not be ghosts; rather, they will be spiritual in the sense that they possess spiritual qualities. As **Anselm** <note>Anselm of Canterbury (c. 1033–1109), an influential Benedictine monk and philosopher.</note> writes in his *Proslogion*, chapter 25: "it will not be a spiritual body in its essence, but in its power" <note>original Latin: *non erit corpus spirituale quidditate sed potestate*</note>. For a body cannot be two things at the same time—both a spirit and a body—"no entity becomes two opposites, by privation and contradic-" <note>The Latin phrase *nullum ens fit duo opposita, privatione & contradic-* ends mid-word, continuing with "tione" (contradiction) on the following page.</note>

<summary>The author argues that resurrected bodies retain their physical substance and "flesh and blood," refuting opponents who claim they become immaterial spirits. He clarifies that a "spiritual body" refers to its new heavenly qualities, not a change in its essential physical nature.</summary>

<keywords>glorified body, flesh and blood, Photinians, spiritual body, Tertullian, Anselm, 1 Corinthians 15, Job 19, materiality, substance</keywords>

"...to show immediately what has broken forth," as if someone were to ask such a thing foolishly; he says: "What you sow is not brought to life unless it has died." <note>original Latin: *quod erupit statim ostendere... tu quod seminas, non vivificatur nisi mortuum fuerit.* A reference to 1 Corinthians 15:36, where Paul compares the death and resurrection of the body to a seed planted in the ground.</note> Therefore, let it be established from the example of the seed that no other flesh is brought to life than that very flesh which has died.

Furthermore, God the **LORD** has consecrated and hallowed the bodies of believers as His temple (1 Corinthians 3 & 6). Christ, with His own precious blood, has redeemed not only our souls but also the body, and thus the entire person. Likewise, we are fed and given to drink with Christ's body and blood in the <term>Lord's Supper</term> <note>German: *Abendmahl*—The Eucharist or Holy Communion. The author argues that because the physical body receives the sacrament, it must be intended for eternal life.</note>. All of this, however, does not happen primarily for this life, but for eternal life. Therefore, if our bodies are to enjoy such a purpose and goal, they must also rise again from death and come forth from the graves.

Furthermore, if it were not our own bodies that were to rise again, but if God were to make other bodies to be united with our souls, that would not be a **resurrection** <note>original Latin: *resurrectio*</note>, but a **creation** <note>original Latin: *creatio*</note>. However, regarding such a creation and the forming of new bodies, there is an eternal silence in the Scriptures. Therefore, our bodies themselves shall rise again. "For it is our body," as **Tertullian** <note>Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD), an early Christian author from Carthage.</note> says in Book 5 of *Against Marcion*, chapter 9, "which loses its soul, and by losing it, becomes dead; thus the term 'dead' applies to the body. Furthermore, if the resurrection is of the dead, and the 'dead' is nothing other than the body, then the resurrection will be of the body. Thus, the very term 'resurrection' claims no other thing than that which has fallen." <note>original Latin: *corpus enim nostrum est... quod amittit animam... resurrectionis vocabulum non aliam rem vindicat, quàm quæ cecidit.* Tertullian's linguistic argument is that "resurrection" literally means "standing up again," which can only apply to something that has fallen down (the physical body).</note> And so it happens, as **Rufinus** <note>Rufinus of Aquileia (c. 344–411 AD), a monk and theologian.</note> says in his *Explanation of the Apostles' Creed*, section 41, "that for each soul, a body is restored that is not confused or foreign, but the very one it previously possessed."

Finally, we have an example of this in the **LORD** Christ. For just as He has risen, so shall we also rise; because He is our head and we are His members, our bodies shall also become like His glorified body (Philippians 3). Now, Christ rose in His very own body (John 2); therefore, we too shall rise in our own bodies—"not in some other foreign bodies, but in those very ones by which we are now surrounded and in which we are now buried," as **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), known for the Vulgate Bible translation.</note> says in *Against the Errors of John of Jerusalem*. **Ephrem** <note>Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD), a Syriac Christian deacon and hymnwriter.</note> also writes of this in his *Sermon on the Resurrection*.

<summary>The author argues that the resurrection must involve the same physical body that died, citing the analogy of a seed, the sanctity of the body as a temple, and the physical resurrection of Christ. He distinguishes "resurrection" (rising again) from "creation" (making something new), insisting that the original body is restored.</summary>

<keywords>resurrection, numerical identity, Tertullian, Rufinus, Jerome, 1 Corinthians 15, Lord's Supper, seed analogy, physical body, Christ's resurrection</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the "numerical identity" of the resurrected body—the belief that the exact same physical body that dies will be the one that rises. The previous page ended with a discussion of God’s power to reassemble the body; this page addresses whether these restored bodies will still consist of physical "flesh and blood" or if they become purely spiritual substances.</meta>

...right. "The earth shall restore that body of man which it received, whether it was torn apart by a beast, or eaten by a bird, or devoured by any living creature, and not even one hair of the man shall be missing in the sight of the Judge." <note>original Latin: *Restituat terra corpus hominis illud...* This concludes the quote from the previous page, emphasizing that God's restoration is so perfect that not even a single hair is lost, regardless of how the body was destroyed.</note>

### Will the glorified bodies in the resurrection also have flesh and blood?

Regarding this, we find entirely contrary opinions among our antagonists. The **Photinians** <note>Followers of Photinus of Sirmium (d. 376 AD), who held heterodox views on the nature of Christ and the resurrection.</note> do not admit "that the elect are to have flesh and blood in eternal life" <note>original Latin: *quod electi carnem & sanguinem sint habituri in vita æterna*</note>, as **Ostorod** <note>Christoph Ostorodt (d. 1611), a prominent Socinian (Unitarian) theologian who argued against the physical resurrection of the same body.</note> writes in *Instruction*, chapter 14. Similarly, **Schegkius** <note>Jakob Schegk (1511–1587), a Lutheran physician and philosopher known for his Aristotelian views.</note> writes in *Antisimonio*, pages 420 and 440, as does **Goclenius** <note>Rudolph Goclenius (1547–1628), a German philosopher known for his work in logic and ontology.</note> in *Adversaria*, question 63, pages 278–279: "that glorified bodies are devoid of matter." <note>original Latin: *quod corpora gloriosa materiæ sint expertia*</note>

However, the Holy Scripture informs us to the contrary: that although glorified bodies will take on different qualities and <term>angelic properties</term> <note>German: *Englische eigenschafften*. In this context, "English" is an archaic spelling of "Engelisch" (angelic). The author means the bodies will behave like angels, not that they are from England.</note>, they will nevertheless not be <term>immaterial bodies</term> <note>original Greek: *ἄϋλα* (ayla) — meaning without matter or substance.</note>. For in such a case, they would not be the same bodies that we have now. Instead, they will be true bodies according to their **matter, substance, and essence**, in every way as our bodies are now. "For God did not take away the substance to which He attributed an angelic quality," as **Tertullian** says in *On the Resurrection of the Flesh* <note>original Latin: *non enim substantiam Deus abstulit, cui qualitatem angelicam attribuit...*</note>, which even **Goclenius** himself must admit in his *Disputation 8 on the Condition of the Glorified Body*, question 2.

For this very body, which is now mortal and corruptible, shall "put on the incorruptible and the immortal" (1 Corinthians 15). And this very body, which lies in the dust of the earth, shall wake up again (Daniel 12). And we shall "see God in our flesh with our own eyes" (Job 19).

Now, although Saint Paul says that the glorified bodies will be **spiritual** (1 Corinthians 15), he does not intend by this to abolish the essence and substance of a true body. He does not say that they will be <term>spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *πνεύματα* (pneumata)</note>, but <term>spiritual bodies</term> <note>original Greek: *πνευματικά* (pneumatika)</note>. They will not be spirits; rather, they will be "spiritual," meaning they will possess spiritual qualities. As **Anselm** <note>Saint Anselm of Canterbury (1033–1109), a Benedictine monk and philosopher.</note> writes in *Prosologion*, chapter 25: "it will not be a spiritual body in its essence, but in its power." <note>original Latin: *non erit corpus spirituale quidditate sed potestate*</note> For a body cannot be two things at the same time—a spirit and a body: "no being becomes two opposites, by way of privation and contradiction." <note>original Latin: *nullum ens fit duo opposita, privatione & contradictione*</note>

<meta>catchword: resurrection,</meta>

<summary>The author argues against the Photinians and others who believe glorified bodies are purely immaterial. Citing Tertullian and Anselm, he asserts that while the resurrection body gains "angelic" qualities and is called "spiritual," it retains its physical matter and substance.</summary>

<keywords>glorified body, flesh and blood, Photinians, spiritual body, Tertullian, Anselm, Goclenius, 1 Corinthians 15, physical resurrection, matter vs. spirit</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "spiritual body" from 1 Corinthians 15. It clarifies that a body becoming "spiritual" does not mean it ceases to be physical matter, but rather that it is governed by the spirit rather than earthly needs. The text uses classical and medieval authorities to define the specific physical traits of the resurrected body.</meta>

...contradiction, or by alternating exchanges, because distinct types of being do not mix. <note>The text concludes a point from the previous page using logic from Joseph Justus Scaliger and Benedictus Pererius, arguing that a thing cannot be two opposite things (like purely spirit and purely flesh) at the same time.</note> In the same way, Christ also says in Matthew 22 that we will be <term>like angels</term> <note>original Greek: *ἰσάγγελοι* (isangeloi)</note> and not <term>angels</term> <note>original Greek: *ἄγγελοι* (angeloi)</note> themselves. "For just as it is now called a 'natural body,' yet is still a body and not a soul; so then it will be a 'spiritual body,' yet still a body and not a spirit," as **Augustine** writes in *Enchiridion*, chapter 91. <note>original Latin: *Sicut enim nunc corpus animale dicitur...* Augustine (354–430 AD) was an influential North African theologian; his *Enchiridion* is a handbook on Christian faith.</note>

However, nothing hinders the glorified bodies in their blessedness from having and retaining flesh and blood, for they are no longer mortal and corruptible, but have risen in power and glory (1 Corinthians 15). Death is swallowed up in victory (Hosea 13, Isaiah 25), and they have become immortal and have received <term>spiritual subtleties</term> <note>The term "subtlety" in medieval theology refers to the ability of the glorified body to pass through other matter, similar to how Christ passed through closed doors.</note>; "indeed, they are made so refined that they can be carried by a breeze," as **Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), Archbishop of Constantinople, known for his eloquent preaching.</note> says in his *41st Homily on the Epistle to the Corinthians*. "Yet they are not called 'subtle bodies' by reason of having an airy substance," as **Origen** <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD), an early Christian scholar whose views on the "spiritual" nature of the resurrection body were often controversial.</note> claims, "but through the effect of spiritual power," as **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory I (c. 540–604 AD), known as Gregory the Great.</note> rightly says in *Moralia in Job*, book 11, chapter 31.

Furthermore, these are <term>impassible bodies</term> <note>From the Latin *impassibilis*, meaning "incapable of suffering" or "beyond the reach of pain."</note>, which no longer feel any single weakness or pain, but instead possess everlasting joy without ceasing or end (Psalm 37, Revelation 21, Isaiah 65). "For the joy of eternal life shall never decrease, nor shall love grow cold; no groan shall be heard, nor pain felt; nothing sad shall be seen, and gladness shall always be held." <note>original Latin: *gaudium enim vitæ æternæ nunquam decrescet...*</note> Finally, these are perfect bodies, such that they lack nothing in the slightest. Therefore, we may rightly glory and say with **Bernard** <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153 AD), a French abbot and primary builder of the Cistercian order.</note> in his *Sermon on the Desertion of Goods*: "the body of immortality and impassibility shall sit endowed with a certain twin happiness, free from all necessity, and secure from all corruption, a stranger and immune to all lust, full of glory, and finally conformed to the brightness of Christ's body. The soul shall also sit, fearing nothing and desiring nothing more, being truly full of blessedness and secure in its fullness."

The <term>Scholastics</term> <note>Medieval university theologians who used Aristotelian logic to explain Christian doctrine.</note> commonly establish four qualities of glorified bodies: **impassibility** (freedom from pain), **agility** (speed and ease of movement), **clarity** (radiance or brightness), and **subtlety** (refined essence), as noted by **Peter Lombard** <note>Author of the *Sentences*, the standard medieval theology textbook.</note> in book 4, distinction 49, and **Bonaventure** <note>A prominent medieval Franciscan theologian and philosopher.</note> in book 4, distinction 49, article 2, question 1.

Otherwise, we find in the Scripture six kinds of qualities and properties which the glorified bodies possess:
1. **Immortalitas** (Immortality)
2. **Incorruptibilitas** (Incorruptibility)
3. **Spiritualitas** (Spirituality)
4. **Potentia** (Power)
5. **Perfectio** (Perfection)
6. **Decor & Claritas** (Beauty and Brightness)
(1 Corinthians 15, Daniel 12, Matthew 13, Wisdom 13). Because "it has not yet appeared what we shall be" (1 John 3).

...also all of this is far beyond our understanding, as the ancient teachers of the Church say: "How great is that blessedness, so hidden from our eyes; it escapes all speech, it exceeds every sense of the human mind, it surpasses desires and prayers; it can be acquired, but it cannot be estimated." <note>original Latin: *Quàm magna est beatitudo illa...* This quote is attributed by the author to **Augustine** in *The City of God*, book 22, chapter 21; **Bernard** in his *4th Sermon on All Saints*; and **Chrysostom** in *On the Repair of the Fall*.</note> Therefore, we must also hold ourselves in moderation regarding this and not speak or write more or further than what is grounded in Scripture; for what the Lord Himself says in His Word, that is truth, but whatever is beyond or against it is rightly set aside and rejected.

## But what will be the condition of the bodies of the damned? Will they also be glorified?

**I**ndeed, the bodies of the godless and the damned shall be immortal, but not <term>impassible</term> <note>original Latin: *immortalia erunt sed non impassibilia*. While the bodies of the saved are "impassible" (incapable of feeling pain), the damned are "passible"—they remain immortal so they can exist forever, but they are fully capable of feeling the agony of their punishment.</note>, for they must live eternally so that they may be eternally tormented, because with their excessive sins they have deserved nothing else. They also did not wish to recognize the time of visitation, nor convert to the Lord with true repentance. They shall not be glorified and magnificent bodies, but ghastly and hideous, as **Isaiah** says in chapter 66: "They shall go forth and look upon the corpses of the people who have transgressed against me: for their worm shall not die, and their fire shall not be quenched, and they shall be an abomination to all flesh." 

In short, the damned bodies shall be without any ornament or magnificence, full of pain and anguish, full of tribulation and misery, full of grief and sadness. The worm of their conscience shall gnaw at them, their sin shall plague them, and they shall have no rest day or night, as **Ephrem** <note>Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD), a prominent Syriac Christian deacon and hymnwriter often cited regarding the Last Judgment.</note> also says in his book *On the Last Judgment*, chapter 4: "That horrible face of their own sins shall meet them, which stands as more bitter and harder than any torment; and tortured by the lashes of their own conscience, they shall frequently utter those unhappy words and say: 'Woe, woe to us miserable ones, to whom it happened to see this day!' For wherever the sinner then turns his eyes, he will behold the images of his own sins."

He argues that the wicked are resurrected with immortal but "passible" bodies, meaning they are physically preserved only to endure eternal, hideous torment and the "gnawing worm" of a guilty conscience.</summary>

For just as the state of misery of pious Christians in this world will be turned into joy (**John 16**), so the earthly joy of the ungodly shall afterwards be transformed into great suffering and sadness. And just as everything regarding the bodies of the chosen is magnificent and elegant, so everything regarding the bodies of the damned is unrefined, an abomination, and a horror. For regarding the chosen, it is said—as **Augustine** says in *The City of God*, book 22, chapter 19:

> All deformity, all slowness, all infirmity, and all corruption shall be absent from our bodies. <note>original Latin: *Aberit à corporibus nostris omnis deformitas, omnis tarditas, omnis infirmitas, omnis corruptio.*</note>

Therefore they are before the throne of God and serve Him day and night in His temple. And He who sits on the throne will dwell over them; they shall hunger no more, nor thirst anymore; neither shall the sun nor any heat fall upon them. For the Lamb in the middle of the throne will feed them and lead them to living fountains of water, and God will wipe away all tears from their eyes." 

But with the damned, it is said that they shall have their part in the lake that burns with fire and sulfur (**Revelation 21**). And those whom they held here as a mockery and a taunt <note>literally "an example," referring to someone held up for ridicule</note> shall in turn find them an abomination (**Wisdom 5, Isaiah 66**). And indeed, the more they have sinned here, the more they will suffer anxiety and pain, as **Gregory** writes in his *Dialogues*, book 4, chapter 43:

> Indeed, the fire of hell <term>Gehenna</term> <note>Gehenna is a term derived from the Hebrew "Ge Hinnom" (Valley of Hinnom), used in the New Testament to describe the place of eternal punishment.</note> is one, but it does not torment all sinners in one way. For however much the fault of each person demands, so much punishment is felt there. For just as in this world many people stand under one sun, yet they do not feel the heat of that same sun equally—because one swelters more, another less—so there, in one fire, there is not one single degree of burning; for as there is a diversity of bodies here, so there is a diversity of sins there. <note>original Latin: *Unus quidem est gehennæ ignis...* This passage argues that while the "fire" is the same, the subjective experience of agony is proportioned to the gravity of one's sins.</note>

## In what stature and size of body will the chosen rise and come forth?

**R**egarding this, we find differing opinions; for **Anselm** <note>Anselm of Canterbury (c. 1033–1109), though the author here refers to the *Elucidarius*, a popular medieval theological manual often attributed to him but likely written by Honorius Augustodunensis.</note> writes in the *Elucidarius*, chapters 93 and 96, that the dead shall rise...

<meta>This page continues the inquiry into the physical state of the resurrected. It specifically addresses the debate over whether the resurrected will appear as they were at their time of death or in a "perfect" youthful state, drawing on the opinions of scholastic and patristic authorities before concluding with a call for humble uncertainty.</meta>

...at the age and in the stature and measure which they had when they died. Some have held—as cited by **Peter Lombard** <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160) was a central figure in medieval theology; his "Sentences" became the standard textbook for students of divinity.</note> in book 4, distinction 44—that the believers will rise **according to the measure of the stature of Christ** <note>original Latin: *secundum mensuram staturæ Christi*</note>, following the measure of the perfect age of Christ, as **Paul** speaks in **Ephesians 4**. **Augustine**, however, in *The City of God*, book 22, chapter 15, explains this as referring to the age and not to the physical height of the body. Thus it would be a **youthful age, not a decrepit or a childish one** <note>original Latin: *ætates juvenilis non decrepita aut puerilis*</note>. This means that the chosen will be like beautiful, noble, and well-formed youths and maidens, as **Bonaventure** <note>Saint Bonaventure (1221–1274) was a major Franciscan theologian and contemporary of Thomas Aquinas.</note> says in book 4, distinction 44: **"All shall rise in the body in such a quality and size as they were, or could have been, in the youthful age in which Christ himself rose."** <note>original Latin: *omnes & tanti & tales resurgent in corpore, quales & quanti fuerunt, vel esse potuerunt in ætate juvenili, in qua etiam resurrexit Christus.*</note>

**Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636) was a prolific writer whose works served as an encyclopedia for the Middle Ages.</note> writes similarly in *On the Highest Good*, book 1, chapter 29: **"The resurrection of the dead will happen 'unto a perfect man, according to the measure of the age of the fullness of Christ,' namely in the age of youth; this age requires no further growth and stands full and robust, without any leaning toward decay, being perfect in every respect."** <note>original Latin: *Resurrectio mortuorum in virum perfectum...*</note> See also **Gregory of Valentia** <note>A 16th-century Spanish Jesuit theologian.</note> and the **Coimbra College** <note>A group of Jesuit scholars at the University of Coimbra, famous for their extensive commentaries on Aristotle.</note> in their physics commentaries.

But since the Apostle **Paul** in **Ephesians 4** is not actually speaking of the resurrection of the dead or the stature and size in which the chosen will come forth, **but of the perfection of the acknowledgment and knowledge of the mysteries of God** <note>original Latin: *sed de perfectione agnitionis & scientiæ mysteriorum Dei*</note>, and since no other person can give a grounded report on this (because God’s Word provides no information), **for it has not yet come into experience** <note>original Latin: *nondum enim venit in experimentum*, as **Augustine** says in *The City of God*, book 22, chapter 21</note>, I consider it much more advisable and useful that we let the matter rest until the experience itself comes. To brood and search much into it now is futile, for we cannot reach an answer. **"For it is better,"** says **Augustine** again in *On the Literal Meaning of Genesis*, book 8, chapter 5, **"to doubt hidden things than to argue about uncertain ones."** <note>original Latin: *melius enim est... dubitare de occultis, quàm litigare de incertis.*</note>

When the dear **Judgment Day** <term>Jüngster Tag</term> <note>Literally "the Youngest Day," the traditional German term for the Last Judgment.</note> comes, we will experience it truly. For now, it has not yet appeared what we shall be; but we know that when it does appear, we shall be like Him, for we shall see Him as He is (**1 John 3**). **"Then we shall know perfectly, when we shall perfectly rest and perfectly see,"** as **Augustine** writes in *The City of God*, book 22, the final chapter. <note>original Latin: *tunc perfectè sciemus quando perfectè vacabimus & perfectè videbimus*</note>

# Can the glorified bodies also be seen by us?

After establishing that the **glorified bodies** <term>verklärte Leiber</term> <note>The "transformed" or "clarified" bodies of the redeemed after the resurrection, believed to possess new, supernatural qualities.</note> retain their true nature, essence, and being, the **Calvinists** <note>Followers of the theological traditions of John Calvin; here, the author is engaging in a common 17th-century denominational debate regarding the physical properties of the resurrected Christ and the saints.</note> wish to argue from this that they can indeed be seen by us. They cite **Calvin** in his *Harmony of the Evangelists* on Luke 24, **Tossanus** in his commentary on 1 John, **Danaeus** in his commentary on Augustine’s *Enchiridion*, **Beumler** against the Heilbrunn Synopsis, the **Heidelberg Report**, **Oecolampadius** on Job, and **Bucanus**.

If we, however, wish to judge and give an opinion on this according to the form and norm of the **Holy Divine Scriptures**, we must confess that while glorified bodies are always seen by other glorified bodies, they are not always seen by us who still wander about in this **mortal hut** <note>A common early modern metaphor for the earthly human body, emphasizing its temporary and fragile nature.</note>, nor can they be seen, **unless a special divine dispensation is added** <note>original Latin: *nisi singularis dispensatio divinitus accesserit*</note>. For although they are truly bodies, they are nonetheless glorified bodies, which shine like the sun in the firmament of heaven (**Daniel 12, Matthew 13**) and are like the **angels** (**Matthew 22**).

Now, we cannot see the angels with our mortal eyes, as we read regarding the **servant of Elijah** <note>The text names Elijah (Eliæ), though the biblical account of the servant's eyes being opened to see heavenly chariots in 2 Kings 6 features his successor, Elisha.</note>, who was standing next to his **preceptor** <term>Præceptor</term> <note>Teacher or master.</note> and master in the midst of the holy angels who were protecting them from the Syrians, and yet he could not see them until his eyes were opened (**2 Kings 6**). Likewise, we cannot properly look upon the **sun**, which is nonetheless subject to vanity <note>A reference to Romans 8, suggesting that even the sun is part of the "corruptible" physical world, yet it is still too bright for us.</note>; **for the solar light dulls the sight, and stings and blunts human eyes** <note>original Latin: *lux enim solaris hebetat visus, pungit & retundit humanos oculos*</note>, as **Zabarella** <note>Giacomo Zabarella (1533–1589), a leading Italian philosopher of the Renaissance.</note>, **Galen** <note>The 2nd-century Greek physician whose works dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note>, and the **Coimbra College** <note>The Jesuit commentators at the University of Coimbra.</note> write. Similarly, the **air** is not seen by us, although it is a **true natural body, because air is transparent, and what is transparent does not enter the eyes but passes through them** <note>original Latin: *verum corpus naturale, quia aër pellucidus est, pellucidum autem oculos non immeat sed transmeat*</note>, as **Scaliger** <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a prominent scholar of the Renaissance.</note> notes. 

Since neither the holy angels, nor the sun, nor the air can be seen by us [properly], we will certainly also not be able to see the glorified bodies; **for between the thing sensed and the sensing organ, a just symmetry and proportion is required** <note>original Latin: *nam inter sensibile & sensorium justa requiritur συμμετρία (symmetria) & proportio*</note>, as the **Coimbra College** and **Fonseca** <note>Pedro da Fonseca (1528–1599), a Portuguese Jesuit philosopher.</note> state; and where there is lack of proportion, where no [visibility occurs...]

<meta>catchword: perception</meta>

<summary>This page argues that glorified bodies are generally invisible to mortal eyes due to the lack of "proportion" between their radiant, celestial nature and our limited human senses, using analogies of the sun, air, and the invisibility of angels.</summary>
<keywords>glorified bodies, visibility, Calvinism, Elisha's servant, optics, Zabarella, Scaliger, Coimbra College, sensory proportion</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the invisibility of resurrected bodies, transitioning from the philosophical requirement of "proportion" between the eye and the object to specific biblical examples of divine radiance and Christ's post-resurrection appearances.</meta>

perception. <note>original Latin: *sensio*. This continues the thought from the previous page regarding the philosophical requirements for sight.</note> **Scaliger** in his *Exercitationes* <note>Referring to Julius Caesar Scaliger’s 'Exotericarum exercitationum', a massive work on natural philosophy.</note> notes that between our weak eyes and glorified bodies there is no proportion; therefore, glorified bodies cannot be seen by us.

Furthermore, we see and understand this from the **transfiguration** <term>Transfiguration</term> <note>The event described in the Gospels where Jesus' appearance was transformed to reveal his divine glory to three of his disciples.</note> and glorification of Christ (**Matthew 17**). This was merely like a foretaste of eternal glory, **or a dark image of future things, as Chrysostom says in his fifth letter to Theodorus the Fallen** <note>original Latin: *seu obscura imago futurorum, ut ait Chrys. Ep. 5. ad Theod. laps.* John Chrysostom was an influential 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople.</note>. For the Apostles could not endure such great glory and such heavenly radiance; they fell down on their faces and were terrified. Similarly, the children of Israel could not look upon the face of **Moses** because it shone so brightly (**Exodus 34, 2 Corinthians 3**). Since glorified bodies exist in perfect majesty and far exceed the glory and radiance of Moses' face, we will be much less able to see them with our eyes.

Beyond this, many bodies rose with Christ and came forth from their graves (**Matthew 27**); however, these were not seen by everyone, **but they appeared to many** <note>original Greek: *sed ἐνεφανίσθησαν πολλοῖς*</note>. Because they appeared not to all, but to some, it certainly follows that this did not happen **by the natural constitution of the body, but by a unique economy and divine dispensation** <note>original Latin/Greek: *ex naturali corporis constitutione, sed ex singulári οἰκονομίᾳ & divina dispensatione*. 'Economy' here refers to God's management or plan for human salvation.</note>. Those to whom God opened their eyes were able to see them, while to others they remained invisible.

Finally, we also have the example of Christ, who was often seen after the resurrection in his glorified body, **not because he had to be, but because he willed to be** <note>original Latin: *non quia debuit, sed quia voluit*</note>. For the radiance in Christ's body when he rose, **as Augustine says in Book 22 of the City of God, chapter 19, must be believed to have been hidden from the eyes of the disciples rather than absent; for a human and weak sight could not have endured it** <note>original Latin: *claritas enim... in Christi corpore cum resurrexit, ab oculis discipulorum potius abscondita fuisse, quam defuisse credenda est: non enim eam ferret humanus atq; infirmus aspectus*. St. Augustine was one of the most significant Latin Church Fathers.</note>. At times he also became invisible, as it says in **Luke 24**. He vanished before them: **he became invisible from them** <note>original Greek: *ἄφαντος ἐγένετο ἀπ’ αὐτῶν*</note>. For he did not depart from them so quickly [by physical movement], as the **Sacramentarians** <note>A derogatory term used by Lutherans for those (like Calvinists) who denied the physical presence of Christ's body in the bread and wine of the Lord's Supper.</note>—such as the **Palatines**, **Kednādon**, **Pezelius** <note>Christoph Pezel, a leading Reformed theologian.</note> in the report against **Selneccer’s** <note>Nikolaus Selnecker, a primary author of the Lutheran 'Formula of Concord'.</note> Testament, and **Thalman**—would have it. Otherwise, they would surely have seen him [leaving]; indeed, they would have followed on his heels. Rather, he became invisible and was suddenly gone from their sight:

<meta>catchword: born</meta>

<summary>The author argues that resurrected, glorified bodies are naturally invisible to mortal eyes due to their overwhelming radiance, citing the Transfiguration, the shining face of Moses, and Christ's ability to appear or vanish at will as proof that visibility is a divine gift rather than a physical necessity.</summary>
<keywords>Transfiguration, Moses, invisibility, Augustine, Chrysostom, Sacramentarians, Pezelius, Selneccer, divine dispensation, resurrected bodies</keywords>

...get away; just as **Pezelius** <note>Christoph Pezel (1539–1604), a prominent Reformed theologian.</note> in his work against Hunnius, page 147, and **Goclenius** <note>Rudolf Goclenius (1547–1628), a German philosopher and professor at Marburg.</note> in the first Disputation of the Philosophical-Theological College themselves confess: that it was a disappearance <note>original Latin: *disparitio*</note>.

# Will glorified and transfigured bodies also be so swift and nimble that they can be wherever they wish in an instant?

Although we cannot properly know what kind of swift movements glorified bodies will possess—**"therefore I do not dare to define rashly what I am not able to conceive," according to Augustine in Book 22 of the City of God, chapter 30**—it is nevertheless certain that they will not be so heavy and slow, nor will they need to go from one place to another gradually as we must do now. Instead, they will travel swiftly, and wherever they wish to be, they will be there immediately, just like the holy angels, to whom they will also be equal in their properties (**Matthew 22**). For their movement <note>original Latin/German: *motus vnd bewegung*</note> does not happen **in time from one point to another** <note>original Latin: *in tempore de uno termino ad alium*</note>, as **Peucer** <note>Caspar Peucer (1525–1602), a scholar and son-in-law of Philipp Melanchthon.</note> teaches in *On Divination*, page 148, but in a moment—quickly and nimbly—just as the sun’s radiance penetrates **without any delay of time** <note>original Latin: *sine ulla temporis mora*</note>, according to the **Coimbra Commentaries** <note>The *Collegium Conimbricense*, a group of Jesuit scholars at the University of Coimbra known for their influential commentaries on Aristotle.</note> on Aristotle's *On the Soul*, Book 2, chapter 7, page 214, and **Zabarella’s** first book *On Vision*, chapter 5 <note>Giacomo Zabarella (1533–1589), an Italian philosopher famed for his works on logic and natural philosophy.</note>. Thus, the angels and the glorified bodies travel like flames of fire (**Wisdom 3, Psalm 104**) **without any extension or delay of time** <note>original Latin: *sine tractu & mora temporis*</note>.

We also see this in the **Lord Christ**, who suddenly vanished from his disciples in his glorified body **without local departure** <term>Local departure</term> <note>original Latin: *sine locali discessu*. This means Christ did not have to physically walk away or travel through space to leave the room; he simply ceased to be present there.</note> (**Luke 24**) and suddenly appeared to them **without local approach** <term>Local approach</term> <note>original Latin: *sine locali accessu*. The theological claim that Christ appeared instantly rather than traveling from a distance to reach the disciples.</note> when he came through locked doors (**John 20**). For he did not climb in through the roof or window, as the **Sacramentarians** <note>A term used by Lutherans to describe those (like the followers of Calvin or Zwingli) who held a symbolic view of the Eucharist.</note> blasphemously claim—such as **Peter Martyr** <note>Peter Martyr Vermigli (1499–1562), an influential Reformed theologian.</note> in his *Dialogues*, folio 95, and **Conrad Quantius** in the *Jever Colloquy held in Friesland in the year [15]77*, signature E. 5. Likewise, no angel needed to roll away the gravestone—**as even Calvin testifies in Book 4 of the Institutes, chapter 17, section 29, and in his Final Admonition to Westphal, page 78**—or open the door, **as Gualter suggests in his book *On the Incarnation*, page 82** <note>Rudolf Gualther (1519–1586), a Swiss Reformed reformer.</note>. Nor did the doors open by themselves, as the **Heidelbergers** <note>Reformed theologians associated with the University of Heidelberg.</note> would have it in their Report, chapter 5, pages 130–131, and likewise **Thalman** <note>Benedikt Thalmann, a Reformed theologian who debated against the Lutheran view of the omnipresence of Christ's body.</note> in his *Assertions*, page 225.

Instead, Christ came to his disciples **while the doors were shut** <note>original Greek: *tōn thyrōn kekleismenōn*</note>, and this did not happen slowly or through physical space in the way we might walk to someone—**"in the way that even a fat magistrate dressed in red boots could have entered,"** as **Zwingli** <note>Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), the Swiss Reformer. The author is quoting Zwingli’s mocking critique of the Lutheran view of Christ's presence.</note> blasphemes with such shameless words in part 1 of his *Response to Luther’s Confession*, page 465. Rather, he came to them so quickly that he stood in their midst before they were even aware of him; therefore they were terrified and afraid, thinking they saw a ghost (<term>Luke 24</term>). 

Just as Christ was able to be in an instant wherever he wished with his glorified body, so too will our glorified bodies be able to do the same, for they will be like the transfigured body of Christ (<term>Philippians 3</term>). There, nothing will be able to stop them—neither stick nor stone, neither bolt nor seal, neither door nor hinge, neither wall nor partition. They will pass through quickly, and wherever they only wish to be, they will immediately be there, without any hindrance, without any burden or delay (**Augustine, Sermon 174 on Time, and Bernard, On the Feast of All Saints, Sermon 4**). 

They will move much faster and more nimbly than we move back and forth with our thoughts—**"for the agility of glorified bodies far exceeds the speed of our thoughts"** <note>original Latin: *agilitas enim corporum glorificatorum longè excedit velocitatem nostrarum cogitationum*</note>—because they are spiritual bodies endowed with great nimbleness and glory: 

> **"From which slowness is to be taken away, but not wholeness; necessity, but not will; so that without a late delay of time or the hindrance of weight, they are where they wish to be, and thus the now-spiritual body follows without any difficulty wherever the spirit, perfected in equality with angelic bliss, wishes to go"** <note>original Latin: *quibus auferenda est tarditas...*</note> (**Prosper, *On the Contemplative Life*, Book 1, chapter 11** <note>Prosper of Aquitaine (c. 390–455), a Christian writer and disciple of Augustine.</note>).

This is also shown by the Apostle Paul (<term>1 Corinthians 15</term>) when he says: "We shall not all sleep, but we shall all be changed," and this suddenly **in a moment** <note>The Greek *en atomō*, from which we get "atom," referring here to an indivisible unit of time.</note>, at the time of the last trumpet; and in <term>1 Thessalonians 4</term>: "we shall be caught up together with them in the clouds, to meet the Lord in the air, and so we shall be with the Lord always." Since glorified bodies are moved so quickly to meet the Lord **in an instant** <note>original Greek: *en atomō*</note>, they will certainly also possess such agility **that they can be wherever they wish without a successive and slow local movement** <note>original Latin: *ut sine successivo tardoq; motu locali esse possint, ubi velint*</note>. For the fact that we must now travel so slowly from one place to another...

traveling; this comes from **burdensome heaviness** <note>original Latin: *onerosa gravitate*</note>, because the weight of the Adamite flesh and corruptible body still clings to us, and **from the resistance of the medium** <note>original Latin: *ex medij resistentia*</note> <note>The author cites several Renaissance and Scholastic philosophers here: Benedictus Pererius, Duns Scotus, and Giacomo Zabarella, who discussed physics and the movement of heavy and light objects.</note>. But neither of these causes will exist in that world: **there will be no resisting medium** <note>original Latin: *ibi non erit medium resistens*</note>, for there is nothing there but God himself, the holy angels, and the chosen.
