Now, where there is no perishable time, there is also no natural **motion** <note>original Latin: *motus*</note> and movement, **for all motion happens in time** <note>original Latin: *omnis enim motus fit in tempore*</note> <note>Citing Isennach and Lintholf, academic authors of "Physical Briefs" or textbooks on natural philosophy.</note>, and **all time is the measure of motion according to before and after** <note>original Latin: *omne tempus est mensura motus...*</note> (**Aristotle, *Physics*, Book 4**; **Zabarella, *On the Constitution of Natural Science*, Book 1**).

Beyond this, no perishable time and natural movement can be attributed or measured to **glorified bodies**, **because time and motion smack of some imperfection** <note>original Latin: *quia tempus & motus redolent aliquam imperfectionem*</note>. But the chosen in eternal life are in the land of vision <note>The "Beatific Vision," where the saved see God face-to-face.</note>, where they lack nothing; rather, everything is perfectly complete. Whatever they only wish for and desire, they have it in abundance and superfluity, which likewise cannot be **unless they are immediately where they wish to be** <note>original Latin: *nisi statim sunt ubi esse volunt*</note>. Therefore, we conclude with the ancient Church Father **Augustine** and say: **"we shall have such bodies that we are where we wish, when we wish"** <note>original Latin: *nos talia corpora habituros, ut ubi velimus, quando voluerimus, simus*</note> (**Sermon 174 on Time**).

However, if we judge this according to the Scriptures—and **take off the coarse shoes of human reason** just as Moses and Joshua did <note>A reference to Exodus 3:5 and Joshua 5:15, where shoes are removed on holy ground; the author suggests that logic must give way to divine mystery.</note>—then we must confess **that glorified bodies are able to penetrate dimensions** <note>original Latin: *quod corpora glorificata possint penetrare dimensiones*; this is the technical term for one solid object passing through another.</note>. For this truly happened at the birth of Christ, **where he penetrated the womb of Mary** <note>original Latin: *ubi penetravit uterum Mariae*</note>, because he was born of a pure, intact virgin. The gate remained locked and yet the Lord passed through it (<term>Ezekiel 44</term>), as **Ludwig Lavater** <note>A Swiss Reformed theologian (1527–1586).</note> himself indicates in his commentary on Ezekiel, Chapter 44, page 198. **For the closed gate,** says **Augustine** (Sermon 2 on the Birth of the Lord), **is the seal of modesty, the integrity of immaculate flesh; she was not violated by the birth, having been more greatly sanctified by the conception** <note>original Latin: *Porta enim clausa... est signaculum pudoris...*</note>.

On the other hand, the **Calvinists** teach once again that the Virgin Mary gave birth to her little son, the Lord Jesus, **with an opened womb** <note>original Latin: *utero aperto*; implying a natural physical birth that broke the hymen or "opened" the mother's body.</note> and brought him into the world. This is found in **Sadeel** (*Theological Works*, folio 451), **Vorstius** (*Antapodixis* 3, page 292), **Martini** (*Summary of the Faith*, page 111, and *Against Mentzer*, page 182), and the **Heidelberg** appendix (pages 52, 53). **Sohn** (*Volume 2*, page 217). That is entirely a **scripture-less fable**, despite what **William Whitaker** <note>An influential English Puritan theologian (1548–1595).</note> says (*On the Marks of the Church*, Question 5, Chapter 18, page 497): **that it is a blemish to say Christ was born from the virgin with a closed womb** <note>original Latin: *quod sit naevus dicere Christum ex virgine clauso utero natum esse*</note>.

For the Prophet **Isaiah** proves this clearly in Chapter 7: "Behold," he says, "a virgin is pregnant and shall bear a son." <note>original Hebrew: הִנֵּה הָעַלְמָה הָרָה, *Hineh ha-almah harah*.</note> What could be said or spoken more clearly than that a virgin shall give birth? This is also evident from the primary language, for the word **Almah** <note>The Hebrew word for "young woman" or "virgin."</note> comes from the root **Alam**, which means **he hid or concealed** <note>original Latin: *occultavit, abscondit*</note>. This is to show that she is an **untouched and hidden virgin** <note>original Latin: *intacta & abscondita virgo*</note> **before the birth, during the birth, and after the birth; for just as Christ was conceived, so also was he born: he entered without suffering, and exited inexpressibly** <note>original Latin: *ante partum, in partu & post partum, sicut enim Christus fuit conceptus, ita & natus, impassibiliter introijt, ineffabiliter exijt.*</note>.

Now, the glorified bodies are also bright-shining bodies—**most bright bodies, indeed more brilliant than the rays of the sun** <note>original Latin: *corpora lucidissima, immò fulgidiora radijs solaribus*</note> (Matthew 13, 1 Corinthians 15). They are like the holy angels (Matthew 22). Therefore, they will similarly be able to penetrate and pass through all things; **for the body of the saints**, as **Augustine** says in the book *On the Cognition of the True Life*, Chapter 45, **shall rise spiritual, because in speed, lightness, and clarity it will be equal to spirits, and every solid object will be penetrable to it, just as the Lord’s body rose while the stone was shut and entered while the doors were closed** <note>original Latin: *resurget spirituale, quia velocitate, levitate, perspicuitate spiritibus erit æquale, & omne solidum erit ei penetrabile...*</note>.

### Will the glorified bodies not be in a certain place?

**A**ll that is outside of God has its finite nature and essence, and is somewhere in a place <term>circumscriptively or definitively</term> <note>Technical terms in scholastic theology: "circumscriptively" means a body whose dimensions are measured by the space it occupies; "definitively" means being present in a location without being bound by physical dimensions, like a soul in a body.</note>. **It is proper to God alone to fill all things by his power, not partially anywhere, but being entirely everywhere** <note>original Latin: *solius Dei proprium est complere omnia vi sua, non partiliter uspiam, sed ubiq; totum esse*</note>, as **Arnaldus** writes in Book 6. He alone is the one who fills Heaven and Earth (Jeremiah 23, 1 Kings 8, Psalm 139), not only **by energy** <note>original Greek: ἐνεργείᾳ (*energeia*); referring to God's external actions or power.</note> according to power and effect—as **Lambert Daneau** <note>A French Reformed theologian (1530–1595).</note> claims in *On the Refutation of Heretics*, page 119—but also **by essence** <note>original Greek: ὀυσίᾳ (*ousia*); referring to God's fundamental being or substance.</note> according to his infinite substance and Divine being, as **John of Damascus** writes in Book 1 of—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of God's omnipresence versus the spatial limitations of created beings. It draws on John of Damascus and Julius Caesar Scaliger to argue that while God is everywhere by His essence, glorified bodies, though no longer bound by "natural" physical constraints (circumscription), still exist in a specific manner distinct from God's infinity.</meta>

<sig>Aa iij</sig>

In *The Orthodox Faith*, Chapter 16, **John of Damascus** <note>A Syrian monk and influential theologian (c. 675–749 AD).</note> writes: **The Divine is indivisible; it is entirely present everywhere as a whole, both whole in all things and whole above all** <note>original Greek: Τὸ θεῖον ἀμερὲς, ὅλον ὁλικῶς ἐστὶ πανταχοῦ, καὶ ὅλον ἐν πᾶσι καὶ ὅλον ὑπὲρ τὸ πᾶν</note>. **God is all things, above all things, beyond all things, within all things, yet not enclosed; outside all things, yet not excluded**, as **Scaliger** <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a renowned Italian scholar and physician.</note> writes in Exercise 365, folio 2; compare also **Augustine’s** 57th Epistle. Since glorified and transfigured bodies also have their finiteness, it follows that they are not present in all places at once, but are in a certain <term>how</term> <note>original Greek: πῶς (*pōs*)</note> or "where." For **"whereness"** <note>original Latin: *Ubietas*</note> **is a characteristic of every being except God**, as **Scaliger** notes in Exercise 359. 

Whatever is in a place somewhere is there either in a natural way **circumscriptively** <note>Meaning its physical dimensions are contained and measured by the space it occupies.</note>, just as we are currently circumscribed in a certain place, or it is there **definitively** <note>Meaning being present in a location by "definition" of its existence there, without being physically bound by the dimensions of that space, similar to how a soul is in a body.</note> in its "where." Regarding the first and natural way—**circumscriptively**—glorified bodies are not in Heaven in that manner, despite what the Calvinists claim, such as **Peter Martyr Vermigli** <note>A major Italian-born Reformed theologian (1499–1562).</note> in his *Dialogue on the Body of Christ*, and in his *Common Places* (Article 15, page 1310); or **Keckermann** <note>Bartholomäus Keckermann (c. 1572–1609), a German Reformed theologian and philosopher.</note> in his *Appendix on Place and the Located* (page 146); **Sadeel** <note>Antoine de la Roche Chandieu (1534–1591), a French Reformed theologian.</note> in *On the Truth of Human Nature* (page 197); and **Zanchius** <note>Girolamo Zanchi (1516–1590), an Italian Protestant Reformer.</note> (Volume 8, *Response to Hold.*, page 954). 

For although they are truly bodies, it does not follow from this that they must be circumscribed, because they are no longer **soulish** <note>original Greek: ψυχικὰ (*psychika*); referring to the natural, animal-like life of the body.</note> but **spiritual** <note>original Greek: πνευματικὰ (*pneumatika*)</note> bodies—spiritual bodies which are like the angels of God (Matthew 22). The holy angels, however, **are not in a place by circumscription, nor do they move locally**, as **Timpler** <note>Clemens Timpler (1563–1624), a German philosopher.</note> argues in his *Metaphysics* (Book 4, chapter 4, question 30), **but are in a "where" by definition.** Therefore, glorified bodies will also not be circumscribed in a certain place in Heaven in the way we are here in this mortality. 

Secondly, the Heaven of the blessed, in which transfigured bodies have their heavenly being, is not a naturally created place (Revelation 20:11), as even the pagan **Aristotle** admits in *Physics* (Book 4, chapter 5) and in *On the Heavens*. **Therefore, those things that are there are not suited to being in a "place"** in the natural sense. 

Thirdly, **Aristotle** and other philosophers with him say (in *Physics*, Book 4) that "place" is the **limit** <note>original Greek: πέρας (*peras*)</note> **of a body that is perceptible, movable, and perishable** <note>original Greek: αἰσθητοῦ, κινητοῦ καὶ φθαρτοῦ (*aisthētou, kinētou kai phthartou*)</note>. The fact that we are now circumscribed in a certain natural place comes mostly from our weakness and imperfection; **hence place is said to perfect the things located within it**, according to **Aristotle** (*History of Animals*, Book 8) and **Scaliger** (Exercise 250). But transfigured bodies are spiritual and entirely perfect, as **Bullinger** <note>Heinrich Bullinger (1504–1575), a Swiss Reformer and successor to Zwingli.</note>—

By citing theological and philosophical authorities, the author suggests that the resurrected state is more akin to the existence of angels, being "spiritual" rather than "soulish."</summary>

—confesses himself in Sermon 9, on the *Apostles' Creed*, Decade 1, pages 31–32. Therefore, they can no longer be **circumscribed** in a natural place according to the manner of mortal and corruptible humans. 

4. Thus the glorified bodies are in God's hand and are sustained by the Lord, so that they do not require any help from the outside, <note>original Latin: *sicut nostra corpora indigent loco ut conserventur*</note> **just as our [mortal] bodies require a place in order to be preserved.** 

5. Only those bodies are in a place **by circumscription** <note>Meaning physically contained by surrounding boundaries.</note> which are **touched by another body**, <note>original Latin: *per circumscriptionem ea corpora, quae ab alio corpore tanguntur*</note> as **Aristotle** notes in *Physics*, Book 4, text 43, and **Fonseca** <note>Pedro da Fonseca (1528–1599), a Portuguese Jesuit philosopher and theologian.</note> in *Metaphysics*, Book 5, Chapter 15, Question 9, folio 1. Now, however, in the Heaven of the blessed there are no such **bodies** or physical objects <note>original Latin: *corpora*</note> by which glorified bodies would be surrounded; for there it is as **John** says in the Apocalypse, chapter 20: **I saw a great white throne and the one who sat upon it, from whose face the earth and the heaven fled away; and no place was found for them.** Therefore, it follows undeniably that glorified bodies are not naturally in a **place by circumscription**, which belongs only to this life, but are in a certain <term>how</term> <note>original Greek: τω (*tō*); here referring to the specific mode of existence.</note> or **"where" by definition.**

# The Ninth Chapter.

## How and in what place the Last Judgment 
### over all people will be held; also in what form 
### heaven and earth will perish in fire, what will 
### follow afterward, and how God will make 
### a new heaven and a new earth, so that 
### the former ones are no longer remembered.

<image-desc>Woodcut ornamental initial letter 'O' with floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

**O**LTHOUGH the majesty, power, and glory of the **Last Judgment** <note>The final and eternal judgment by God of all nations.</note> far surpasses all human thought and understanding, and rests more in experience—as **Augustine** says in *The City of God*, Book 20, Chapter 30—than we are now able to grasp and understand; nevertheless, because the Lord Himself has prognosticated and prophesied in the Scripture concerning this, in what manner He wishes to return and hold the Last Judgment, it is therefore proper for us all to speak with one another about such a form and process of the glorious Judgment of Christ, and to imagine it well, so that we may prepare ourselves for it and stand worthily when we shall [answer...]

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Chapter 9, which focuses on the process and location of the Last Judgment. It follows a discussion on the nature of glorified bodies and the general majesty of Christ's return. The catchword from the previous page ("word") completes the opening sentence regarding the account humans must give to God.</meta>

...give an account <note>The German text uses the phrase "wort geben," literally "to give word."</note> for all that we have done during our lifetime, whether it is good or evil, according to **2 Corinthians 5**. We have already indicated above in the seventh chapter how Christ will be the Judge, in what form He will return, and what <term>Assessors</term> <note>Latin: *assessores*; refers to those who sit alongside a judge to assist in a trial, traditionally interpreted here as the Apostles or the saints.</note> and assistants He will have. Now, however, we must further explain and listen to the process and course of the Last Judgment according to all the circumstances found in the Scripture: how it will proceed and what will follow afterward.

# In what place will the Last Judgment be held?

The Papists <note>A common Reformation-era term for Roman Catholics.</note> are of the opinion that it will happen in the **Valley of Jehoshaphat** near Jerusalem; citing **Gregory de Valentia**, Volume 4, page 2320; **Josephus à Costa**, Chapter 13, Book 4, *On the Last Times*; **Vignierius** in his *Institutes*; **Ribera** on Joel, Chapter 3; and **Maldonatus** on Matthew, Chapter 25. Some hold that it will happen on the **Mount of Olives**; others imagine that the rainbow will be the <term>place of judgment</term> <note>original Latin: *locus judicij*</note> after the prototype described in **Revelation 4**. However, these are nothing but <term>conjectures</term> <note>original Latin: *conjecturen*; meaning educated guesses or interpretations not explicitly stated in text.</note> and human thoughts which have no foundation in the Scripture. For although the prophet **Joel** mentions the Valley of Jehoshaphat in **Chapter 3**, he is not actually speaking of the Last Judgment, but rather of the calling of the Heathen into the fellowship of the Gospel; **Jerome**, Volume 5, folio 87, understands it as referring <term>to every judgment</term> <note>original Latin: *de omni judicio*</note>:

> For everyone, he says, who is judged is placed in the valley which is called Jehoshaphat, that is, the **"Judgment of the Lord."** For he who believes in the Lord shall not be judged. And there He will dispute with them—not by the power of His majesty, but convicting them by the truth of reason—those who divided the people of God and scattered His inheritance among the nations, so that they might compel the servants of God to serve them. <note>original Latin: *omnis enim inquit, qui judicatur, in valle positus est, quæ appellatur Josaphat, hoc est Domini judicium...*</note>

This, however, is certain: that the **LORD** will return in a visible manner in the clouds, where we shall be sent to meet Him in the air, according to **1 Thessalonians 4**. But whether that air shall also be the specific place of the Judgment, we do not wish to speak of <term>assertively</term> <note>original Latin: *assertivè*; meaning with absolute certainty or as dogma.</note>. God alone knows the time and place; and when the hour arrives, He will surely see to the location. With this we should remain content and not indulge in further clever speculations.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Last Judgment from Chapter 9. # Will Heaven and Earth perish in fire before the Last Judgment occurs?

The Papists <note>A common Reformation-era term for Roman Catholics.</note> also have their own notions and unscriptural dreams regarding this matter. For instance, they write concerning the fire—before which Heaven and Earth and all the elements shall melt, according to **2 Peter 3**—that it will be **an elementary fire, not newly created, but multiplied by the power of God and gathered from whatever fire exists in the world.** <note>original Latin: *ignis elementarius, non de novo creatus, sed virtute Dei multiplicatus, & congregatus ex quocunq; mundi igne.*</note> This is found in **Bonaventure**, Book 4, Sentence 47, Article 2; **Josephus à Costa**, *On the Last Times*, page 588; and **Hieronymus Natalis** in his *Evangelical Annotations*, page 4. Or, as **Ferdinandus Vellofillus** writes in his *Observations on Ambrose*, Question 9, folio 11: **a fire of the same species as the elementary fire and our own.** <note>original Latin: *ignis ejusdem speciei cum elementari & nostro.*</note>

Furthermore, they teach and believe that Heaven and Earth will perish in such an elementary fire before the Judgment takes place; see **Thomas Aquinas**, Question 74, Article 7; **Gregory de Valentia**, Volume 4, page 2276; and **Vellofillus**, *Observations*, Question 9, folio 12.

However, it is altogether foolish to judge such mysteries—which no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and no human has ever experienced—according to our own thoughts and understanding. For if we were meant to know these things, the **LORD** would surely have revealed them in His word. But since it is not stated in the Scripture what kind of fire it will be, we do not wish to investigate it improperly. Instead, we simply believe that Heaven and Earth will perish in fire, and we leave the rest to God; He will certainly know how to find the fire. After all, He found a fire <term>for the overturning of the five cities</term> <note>original Latin/Greek: *ad πεντάπολιν (pentapolis) eversionem*; referring to the destruction of Sodom, Gomorrah, and the neighboring cities in Genesis 19.</note>—how should He not also find a fire <term>for the destruction of the whole world</term>? <note>original Latin: *ad totus orbis destructionem*</note>

In the same manner, we judge the sequence of events. Because we do not actually know whether the <term>burning of the world</term> <note>original Latin: *conflagratio mundi*</note> will happen before or after the Judgment. For what is written in the Scripture—that fire shall go before the Lord—is not to be understood as being **about the chronological order, but about the wrath and vengeance of the Judge.** <note>original Latin: *de ordine, sed de ira & judicis ultione.*</note>

And although the <term>Scholastics</term> <note>Medieval university theologians who often used philosophical logic to fill in gaps in biblical narrative.</note> want to prove their dream by claiming that the righteous must be purified through such a fire—**Bonaventure** in his 4th Sentence, Distinction 47; and **Josephus à Costa**, Book 4, *On the Last Times*, Chapter 22—there is nevertheless an eternal silence regarding this in the Scripture. Therefore, we leave it unsearched; we shall experience it on the Last Day. **Then the experience of the things themselves will teach us more than human intelligence is now able to achieve perfectly.** <note>original Latin: *tunc magis docebit rerum experientia, quàm nunc valet consequi ad perfectum, hominum intelligentia*; quoted from **Augustine**, *The City of God*, Book 20, Chapter 30.</note>

<summary>The author critiques Roman Catholic ("Papist") and Scholastic speculations regarding the nature and timing of the fire that will consume the world, arguing that such details are not revealed in Scripture and will only be understood through the experience of the event itself.</summary>

# Will the entire world indeed perish on the Last Day?

Although the Papists <note>A term used by Reformation-era writers to refer to Roman Catholics.</note> hold the view that neither Heaven nor Earth, but only the air shall perish—<margin>Gregory de Valentia, Volume 4, page 2277. Blasius Vega on the Apocalypse, page 870.</margin> nevertheless, God’s Word teaches us exactly the opposite. For in <margin>Psalm 102.</margin> David says: *Your years endure forever and ever; you have previously founded the Earth, and the Heavens are the work of your hands; they shall perish, but you remain; they shall all grow old like a garment; they shall be changed like a cloak, when you will change them.* Likewise, <margin>Isaiah, Chapter 51</margin> also prophesies: *Lift up your eyes toward heaven, and look down upon the earth; for the heaven shall vanish like smoke, and the earth shall grow old like a garment, and those who dwell upon it shall die away likewise; but my salvation remains eternally, and my righteousness shall not be delayed.* Similarly, Peter says in <margin>2 Peter 3.</margin>: *The Day of the Lord will come like a thief in the night, in which the heavens shall pass away with a great crash, but the elements shall melt from the heat, and the earth and the works that are within it shall be burned up.*

From these cited scriptural testimonies, it is sufficiently known and recognized that not only the air, but also Heaven and Earth shall dissolve in fire; and God will make a new Heaven and a new Earth, so that the former shall no longer be remembered. <margin>Isaiah 65.</margin> Therefore, we believe much more in the infallible truth of Divine Scripture than in the deceptive thoughts and fictions of the Papists.

## Will the world then perish according to its substance and essence, or will it only be changed according to its qualities?

Regarding this, there is no small amount of bickering and strife among theologians. For the most part, however, they agree that the world will perish according to its very essence. They prove this from the specific words <term>to perish</term> <note>original Latin: *interire*</note>, <term>to melt away</term> <note>original Latin: *liquefcere*</note>, <term>to pass away</term> <note>original Latin: *transire*</note>, and <term>not to be</term> <note>original Latin: *non esse*</note>. For we read thus in the Scripture: that Heaven and Earth will pass away. <margin>Luke 21.</margin> They will be changed like a garment <margin>Psalm 102, Isaiah 51</margin> the

<meta>This page continues the theological debate regarding the physical destruction of the world at the end of time. It also transitions to a new question regarding whether the Last Judgment should be a source of terror for believers.</meta>

The heavens will pass away and the earth will burn up (2 Peter 3). They will no longer be remembered <margin>Isaiah 65.</margin>; they will be no more <margin>Revelation 20.</margin>. Furthermore, it is also proven by this: that immortality and <term>incorruptibility</term> <note>The state of being unable to decay or perish.</note> belong only to rational humans and angels, and to no other creature in the entire wide world. Since the heavens and earth are to dissolve and be no more, must it not happen according to their <term>essence</term> <note>The fundamental substance of a thing, as opposed to its outward appearance or qualities.</note>, and not merely according to their qualities?

What Luther teaches on this we find in the *Church Postil* <note>A "postil" is a collection of sermons or commentaries on the Bible, intended for use by clergy.</note> for the second Sunday of Advent, folio 32. "You should know," he says, "that just as our corpses are transformed according to their essence, and yet these same bodies are made again according to their essence, so too will heaven and earth on the Last Day—along with all elements and everything everywhere—melt through fire and become powder, together with the bodies of all people, so that nothing but pure fire will be everywhere; and immediately thereafter, everything will be created anew in the most beautiful way, so that our bodies will shine brightly like the sun, and the sun will be seven times brighter than it is now." See <margin>Augustine, *The City of God*, book 20, chapter 16. The Coimbra Commentaries, book 1 on *The Heavens*, chapter 14, article 1.</margin> However, since a definitive decision cannot be taken from Scripture, and thus nothing can be thoroughly concluded on this, we will once again postpone the discussion of this question until future experience <note>The author suggests that since the Bible is not explicit about the physics of the world's destruction, we will only know the truth when it actually happens.</note>; in the meantime, let us be satisfied with knowing that heaven and earth will pass away in fire.

## Will the Last Judgment also be terrifying to the saints and the godly?

For even <term>earthly men</term> <note>Literally "Adamic men," referring to humans in their natural, fallen state.</note> rely on this in their worldly dealings: if they have a gracious judge and favor or goodwill among the <term>assessors</term> <note>Legal advisors or assistants who sit with a judge to provide counsel.</note>, how much more then should we rely on the fact that on the Last Day we will have such a gracious Judge? He does not wish to stifle us but to refresh and raise us up; he does not wish to condemn us, but to save us. Therefore, let the ungodly be afraid, and not the believers; for to the former, Christ's coming and the Last Judgment will be terrifying enough: "For just as the coming of the Son of God brings joy to the good, so it brings destruction to the wicked." <note>original Latin: *sicut enim adventus Filij Dei bonis infert laetitiam, ita malis exitium*</note> <margin>Prosper, *On the Promises*, chapter 17.</margin>

## Will not all people then be judged?

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated or floriated initial 'W' begins this section, likely featuring vines or small figures within the letterform.</image-desc>

**W**e confess not only in our Articles of Faith <note>Referring to the Creeds, such as the Apostles' Creed.</note> that Christ shall come to judge the living and the dead, but the Scripture also gives us a clear and true report of this. It indicates that all and every person who has ever lived, who lives now, and who will yet live in the future until that dear Last Day—small and great, rich and poor, of whatever station or country, of whatever dignity or honor they may be—shall be brought before Christ’s <term>judgment seat</term> <note>original German: *Schöpffenbanck*, an old legal term for a magistrate's bench or a tribunal.</note> and be judged there, as it is written in Matthew 25: "When the Son of Man shall come in his glory, and all the holy angels with him, then shall he sit upon the throne of his glory: And before him shall be gathered all nations: and he shall separate them one from another..."

> God, he says, did not spare the angels who sinned, but cast them down to hell with chains of darkness and delivered them to be kept for judgment.

In the same way, they now entice and drive men to all manner of evil, and <term>not by God's command and impulse</term> <note>original Latin: *& non Dei mandato & impulsu*</note>, as Calvin <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), the French reformer whose views on predestination and providence are being contested here by the author.</note> wishes in his *Institutes*, book 2, chapter 4, sections 2, 4, 5, and Zanchius in *On the Nature of God*, page 352. Therefore, they were cast out of heaven immediately after their fall, Revelation 12, and condemned to eternal damnation, so that they now expect the <term>execution</term> <note>The carrying out of a legal sentence.</note> at the **Last Judgment** hourly and momentarily with great fear and trembling, with great anguish and pain. Then they shall all, with all their accomplices, be banished into the eternal fire, as Christ clearly says in Matthew 25, and hereafter no single devil, or any of the damned...

<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the Last Judgment. <sig>Bb iij</sig>
...[no] ungodly person will be saved, just as <term>Origen</term> <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD), an early Christian theologian who famously speculated about *apokatastasis*, the eventual salvation of all beings, including the damned.</note> dreamed; an opinion held by <term>Zwingli</term> <note>original: *Clinglius* (a variant of Cinglius/Zwingli); Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), a leader of the Swiss Reformation.</note> in *Letters to Oswald Myconius*, book 1, folio 23. The <term>Anabaptists</term> <note>original: *Widerteuffer*; a radical wing of the Reformation that often faced persecution from both Catholics and other Protestants.</note> are also so "merciful" toward the evil spirits that they claim they will finally be saved. ## But will Christ judge people according to their works, or according to their faith and unbelief?

**T**he <term>Papists</term> <note>original: *Päbstler*; a polemical term for Roman Catholics used by reformers.</note> certainly maintain that such a judgment will occur and proceed solely according to **works** (Ruard Tapper, volume 2, article 8, folios 17 and 18; Lindanus, dialogue 1, chapter 3). They claim that according to how much good one has done, he shall receive life and salvation for the sake of such works (Conrad Kling, *Summary of Christian Doctrine*, theorem 11, page 16, and title 169, page 406; Sebastian Vischer, book 5, chapter 14; Stapleton, *Antidote to the Gospel* on Matthew 7, page 80, and *Antidote to the Apostles* on Romans 3, pages 81 and 83; Martin Becan, volume 1, opus 6, chapter 3, page 312).

Because of this, our <term>saving faith</term> <note>original: *seligmachende glaube*; the core Lutheran doctrine that faith in Christ alone, rather than human effort, secures salvation.</note>—of which the Scripture says that it alone, without the addition of good works, inherits and attains salvation and life for Christ's sake—is called a "**proud and haughty faith**" by them, one that can suffer no companions or escorts to stand beside it (Petr Hanson, in *Discovery of the Great Folly, Error, and Ways of the Lutheran Book of Concord*, page 121).

Likewise, some of the <term>Calvinists</term> <note>Followers of John Calvin; the author is grouping them with Catholics here because they also emphasized the "necessity" of good works, albeit in a different theological framework.</note> also teach and write that good works are necessary for salvation (Beza, *Questions and Responses*, book 1, page 78; Zanchius, *Miscellany*, page 256; Joachim Cureus in *Exegesis*, page 35; Piscator, volume 2, *Theological Disputations*, page 133; Goclenius, disputation 9, *Colloquy of Philosophy and Theology*, aphorism 10). 

But God's Word informs us that "no other name has been given to men in which we must be saved" except the name of Jesus (Acts 4). And whoever believes in Him has forgiveness of his sins (Acts 10). For Christ is our righteousness (Jeremiah 23); He is made for us by God for holiness and salvation (1 Corinthians 1); He was given over for the sake of our sins and raised again for the sake of our righteousness (Romans 4). <note>original Latin: *totum igitur gratiæ ejus imputatur*</note> **Therefore, all is credited to His grace.**

<meta>catchword: that (original: ut)</meta>

<summary>The author rejects the idea that devils or the ungodly can be saved (universalism) and argues against Catholic and Calvinist views that emphasize good works, insisting instead that salvation and judgment are based on faith in Christ's grace alone.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological debate over whether the Last Judgment is based on faith or works. The author is providing evidence from Church Fathers (Augustine and Ambrose) to support the Lutheran position that works are merely outward signs of an inward faith, rather than the cause of salvation itself.</meta>

As Augustine says in his second exposition of Psalm 31: "not by our merits." <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD). The author cites him to show that the doctrine of "faith alone" is not a new invention but has roots in the early church.</note> Ambrose <note>St. Ambrose of Milan (c. 339–397 AD), another "Doctor of the Church" whose writings were highly valued by the Reformers.</note> also writes of this with spirited words in Book 1 of *Jacob and the Happy Life*, chapter 5:

> This is what profits me: that we are not justified by the works of the law. I have, therefore, nothing in my own works to boast about, and nothing to brag of, and so I will boast in Christ. I will not boast because I am righteous, but I will boast because I have been redeemed. I will not boast because I am free of sin, but because my sins have been forgiven. I will not boast because I have been helpful, nor because anyone has helped me, but because Christ is my advocate before the Father, and because Christ’s blood was shed for me. <note>original Latin: *Illud mihi prodest, quod non justificamur ex operibus legis...*</note>

Just as we are not saved for the sake of works, so we will not be judged for the sake of works. Instead, the judgment will occur according to faith and unbelief, for no new sentence shall then be established or passed. Rather, what Christ says in John 3—"He who believes shall be saved; he who does not believe shall be damned"—that very thing will be executed and carried out in the final judgment. Thus, the believers will stand at the right hand of Christ and hear the comforting *venite* <note>Latin: "Come," referring to Matthew 25:34: "Come, you who are blessed by my Father, inherit the kingdom."</note>; the unbelievers, however, will be placed like stinking goats at the left hand of the Judge and must hear the sorrowful *ite* <note>Latin: "Go" or "Depart," referring to Matthew 25:41: "Depart from me, you who are cursed, into the eternal fire."</note>, meaning they must go into the hellish fire (Matthew 25).

Although such judgment and the execution of the sentence once passed shall happen according to unbelief and faith, yet because it will be a public trial and faith is not publicly visible to men, nor is unbelief always known, the *deeds* <note>original Latin: *opera*</note> or works will also be brought into the judgment at the same time. Faith alone will absolve and make a person righteous; unbelief will condemn. But works will be the "infallible <term>criteria</term> of both" <note>original Latin/Greek: *infallibilia utriusq; κριτήρια* (kritēria); the author uses the Greek word for benchmarks or standards of judgment.</note>—the fruits and identifying marks of who was a believer or an unbeliever.

For example, when a miser, a usurer, a fornicator and adulterer, a glutton, or a proud person (who has departed from this life unrepentant in such sins) is judged, his misdeeds will stand publicly before the eyes of all the world. From this, everyone will see that he did not have the true faith, nor did he want to have it. On the other hand, the good works of the righteous will also be open to everyone, and from them one will recognize—

<summary>The author uses quotes from St. Augustine and St. Ambrose to argue that salvation is not based on human merit. He explains that while the Last Judgment is decided by faith, works are presented publicly as the visible evidence or "criteria" of that faith.</summary>

## Will the sins of all the ungodly be revealed at the Last Judgment?

Although in this world countless sins remain silent and hidden, nevertheless at the Last Judgment everything will be revealed, as Christ says in Matthew 10: "There is nothing hidden that will not be revealed, and nothing secret that will not be known." For God will judge the secret things of men through Jesus Christ (Romans 2). And in 1 Corinthians 4, Paul says: "Do not judge before the time until the **LORD** comes, who will also bring to light what is hidden in darkness and reveal the counsel of hearts." At that time, the righteous will see before their eyes all the secret and cunning plots of the ungodly, which they practiced and held against pious Christians on earth. There we will learn what horrific sins the damned committed here, and indeed everything will become known in detail, including the manner in which it occurred.

<note>original Latin: "non enim confusè aut summatim aut indigestè peccata cernentur..."</note> *For sins will not be perceived in a confused, summary, or disordered manner,* says Basil in the book *On True Virginity* <note>St. Basil of Caesarea (c. 330–379 AD). This work, traditionally attributed to him, emphasizes that on Judgment Day, the soul is stripped of all disguises.</note>. *But each thing will be known in its parts as they are, just as in a painting. For example, how someone hastening to commit <term>adultery <gloss>adulterium</gloss></term> rose from bed, what steps he took as he walked, by what slower or swifter motion of the body, what appearance he assumed while proceeding, from which side he broke in to violate another's wife; likewise in what order the woman submitted herself for this nefarious act: all these things, I say, will be seen as if diligently impressed in parts upon a tablet; and not only the external deed but even the innermost thoughts.* <note>original: "intimæ cogitationes"</note>

When the sins and misdeeds of all the ungodly [are] thus to everyone... <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "vor" suggests the next page begins with "vor Augen gestellt" (placed before the eyes).</note>

# Will the sins of the righteous also be revealed?

Just as the ungodly will accuse, banish, and condemn one another among themselves, so will the pious and the elect bear witness for one another; one will recount, boast of, and praise the good works of the other, as Christ indicates in Luke 16:9. There, all their salutary, Christian, and useful thoughts, plans, and works will come to light, so that not even a drink of cold water, given out of love to the needy, will remain unpraised or unrewarded, as Christ says in Matthew 10. There it will become known and recognized what every preacher has done for the Church of God, how he sought the <term>salvation <gloss>Seligkeit</gloss></term> of mankind with special earnestness and zeal. There one will see the great sweat and diligence that the dear prophets and apostles had for the propagation of the Word of God; there one will see the earnestness, zeal, and constancy of <term>Athanasius <gloss>Athanasij</gloss></term>, <term>Luther <gloss>Lutheri</gloss></term>, and other dear men of God; there one will see the fire-zealous love that many pious princes and lords bore toward their dear subjects—how they so heartily concerned themselves with the <term>affliction of Joseph <gloss>schaden Josephs</gloss></term> <note>The "affliction of Joseph" is a biblical reference (Amos 6:6) used here to describe the suffering of the faithful or the Church, which godly leaders work to alleviate.</note> and remained steadfast in the pure teaching of the <term>Gospel <gloss>Evangelij</gloss></term>.

Although the works of the righteous will publicly come to light and be praised, nevertheless, their sins—which they committed out of weakness—will not be discovered and revealed in the same way... <note>The text ends with the catchword "bahr," indicating the next page begins with the conclusion of the word "offenbar" (revealed).</note>

## But shall the believers not also be judged?

Indeed, the judgment shall be held over all people. However, because the righteous believe in Christ and therefore have a <term>favorable judge <gloss>favorabilem judicem</gloss></term>, they shall not be judged by him unto condemnation, as the **LORD** himself says in John 5: "Whoever hears my word and believes him who sent me has eternal life and does not come into judgment, but has passed from death to life." For the judgment is of two kinds: the <term>judgment of condemnation <gloss>judicium condemnationis</gloss></term> and the <term>judgment of absolution <gloss>judicium absolutionis</gloss></term>. The unrighteous enter into the former; the righteous enter into the latter. In the former, the sins of the ungodly are revealed; in the latter, the sins of the believers are covered. In the former, the unbelievers are condemned; in the latter, the elect are absolved, and pronounced and publicly proclaimed as heirs of eternal life.

<summary>The text argues that the sins of the righteous will not be publicly shamed at the Last Judgment because they have been forgiven through Christ, contrasting the 'judgment of condemnation' for the wicked with the 'judgment of absolution' for believers.</summary>
<keywords>Last Judgment, justification, grace, Papists, Jerónimo Osório, José de Acosta, forgiveness of sins, Judgment of Condemnation, Judgment of Absolution, Elect</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of the Last Judgment. The catchword "Wird" (Will) links to the start of the new question regarding the procedure of Christ's judgment.</meta>

### But will the Judge, Christ, call each person one by one to give an account, and examine and question them concerning the transgressions they have committed?

Before worldly judges, it indeed proceeds in such a way that long legal proceedings are arranged before them; there is no end or limit to the <term>filing of briefs <gloss>libellirens</gloss></term>, <term>submitting of replies <gloss>replicirens</gloss></term>, and whatever else belongs to legal accusation and defense. <note>These terms refer to the complex written exchange of documents in 17th-century legal culture, which the author contrasts with the speed of divine judgment.</note> But Christ will not hold any long delay with his judgment, just as it is also unnecessary for him to question the accused and bring in many testimonies or produce various accusers, because he is the <term>knower of hearts <gloss>Hertzenkündiger</gloss></term> of all people, and can bring everything that is hidden publicly to the light (Romans 2:16; 1 Corinthians 4:5). He needs to ask no one what they have done; he already knows it all (John 2). When the books are opened, everything stands there (Revelation 20), as Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic.</note> writes in *Sermon 8 on Psalm 91*:

> The judge needs no witness; the truth examines the intentions; the investigation of faults reaches into the hidden parts of the heart, where finally that divine gaze investigates the most secret recesses of minds, and at that sudden brightness of the sun, the laid-bare depths of souls pour out everything—whether good or evil—that they were concealing. <note>original Latin: "Iudex teste non indiget, veritas discutit intentiones, inquisitio culparum pertingit ad abdita cordis..."</note>

Thus also writes Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430), whose writings on the "City of God" and the "Creed" shaped Western views on the end of the world.</note> in *Book 3 on the Creed, chapter 8*:

> He will judge not as an earthly judge, seeking witnesses to convict you, or seeking the truth through torture so that he may punish one who has confessed; for Justice itself sits as the judge, and your own bad conscience is a witness against you. <note>original Latin: "Iudicabit non ut judex terrenus, quæsiturus testes, ut te convincat..."</note>

Just as the Judge, Christ, will not bring extensive witnesses—because the sins of all the world, both secret and public, are entirely manifest before his eyes—so too will he not take up one person after another and spend a long time with his judicial process. Instead, we shall all be caught up together to meet him in the air (1 Thessalonians 4). And then he will quickly and nimbly place the <term>elect <gloss>Außerwelten</gloss></term> at his right hand and the <term>ungodly <gloss>Gottlosen</gloss></term> at his left; the former he will <term>absolve <gloss>absolviren</gloss></term>, and the latter he will <term>condemn <gloss>condemniren</gloss></term> and damn.

Will

<summary>This page argues that Christ’s judgment will be instantaneous and all-knowing, unlike the slow, bureaucratic nature of earthly courts. Drawing on Bernard of Clairvaux and Augustine, it explains that the human conscience and God's omniscience replace the need for witnesses or lengthy trials.</summary>

### But will no mercy be found with this Judge after the verdict has been handed down?

**I**n this world, there is indeed such a perverted state of affairs that many a judge allows himself to be swayed and bent by gifts, favor, and presents; but before the <term>judgment seat <gloss>Richterstuel</gloss></term> of Christ, things will proceed very differently. There, there will be no respect of persons. <margin>Zephaniah 1</margin> Money and goods will help no one. Likewise, absolutely no mercy will be shown toward the <term>impenitent <gloss>Unbußfertigen</gloss></term> <note>Those who have not repented of their sins before death.</note>, for it is <margin>Romans 2</margin> a day of wrath, a day of retribution and rebuke, a day of fury, a day of trouble and distress, <margin>Zephaniah 1</margin> a day of storm and tempest, a day of darkness and gloom, a day that shall burn like an oven; there, all despisers and ungodly shall be stubble, and the <term>Last Day <gloss>Jüngste Tag</gloss></term> will set them on fire <margin>Malachi 4</margin> and will leave them neither root nor branch. <note>This imagery is drawn from the Old Testament prophets to emphasize the total destruction of evil.</note>

> There are neither tears, as <term>Ephraim <gloss>Ephraim</gloss></term> <note>St. Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD), a prolific Syriac hymnographer and theologian.</note> says in Chapter 4 of his book *On the Last Judgment*, nor groans that might turn aside the judge's sentence: there is no delay of time for action: there is no return to life after departure. <note>original Latin: "Ibi nec lacrimæ sunt... nec gemitus, quæ judicis avertant sententiam..."</note>

Thus also <margin>Augustine, Book 3 on the Creed, chapter 8</margin> writes Augustine in *Book 3 on the Creed, chapter 8*:

> That judge is neither swayed by favor, nor bent by mercy, nor corrupted by money, nor softened by repentance or <term>satisfaction <gloss>satisfactione</gloss></term> <note>In a legal/theological sense, "satisfaction" refers to making amends or paying a penalty to balance a debt of sin.</note>: here, while the soul has time, let it act for itself as long as there is a place for mercy, because there <note>Meaning, at the Judgment.</note> is the place for <term>justice <gloss>justitiæ</gloss></term>. <note>original Latin: "Ille judex nec gratia prævenitur, nec misericordia flectitur..."</note>

## What kind of procedure will then be held in the Last Judgment?

**T**he kind of <term>procedure <gloss>Process</gloss></term> the Lord Christ will hold in his judgment has been prefigured for us by the Spirit of God in the Scriptures through parables taken from worldly courts; and indeed, it is indicated that it will occur and proceed in four ways: First, the Judge Christ will make [the judgment] known and manifest to all people—

<meta>catchword: his [suam]</meta>

<summary>This page emphasizes that Christ's judgment is final and immune to the corruption or appeals common in earthly courts. Citing Ephraim the Syrian and Augustine, it warns that mercy is available only during life, as the Judgment is reserved strictly for divine justice.</summary>

—his <term>majesty in the session <gloss>majestatem in sessione</gloss></term> <note>This refers to Christ taking his seat upon the judicial throne.</note>, when he will sit upon the throne of his glory—as a Prince of all kings on earth—and as the Judge of all judges. <margin>**Matthew 25, 1 Timothy 6, Revelation 1 & 19**</margin> **II. Authority in the gathering <gloss>Auctoritatem in congregatione</gloss>**, when all nations must assemble before his judgment seat, and every knee shall bow before him—those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth. <margin>**Isaiah 45:23, Matthew 25:32, Romans 14:12, Philippians 2:10**</margin> **III. Equity in the pronouncing or the proclamation of the final sentence <gloss>Æquitatem in pronunciatione seu sententiæ definitivæ promulgatione</gloss>**, for he will judge the people rightly without any <term>respect of persons <gloss>ansehen der Person</gloss></term> <note>Biblical phrasing meaning "partiality" or "favoritism."</note> and will <term>proclaim <gloss>promulgiren</gloss></term> the final verdict, as it is written: <margin>**Mark 16, John 3**</margin> Whoever believes shall be saved, but whoever does not believe shall be damned.

### What, then, will follow such a delivered final verdict?

**N**othing other than the <term>execution <gloss>Execution</gloss></term>; for as soon as the LORD has proclaimed and delivered such a verdict, the <term>righteous and elect <gloss>Gerechten vnd Außerwelten</gloss></term> <note>In Lutheran and Reformed theology, the "elect" are those chosen by God's grace for salvation.</note> will be taken up into eternal glory without any delay or postponement. They will remain with the Lord for all time <margin>**1 Thessalonians 4**</margin> and will rejoice and triumph with great jubilation and joy, singing and saying: "Now have come the salvation, and the power, and the kingdom, and the might of our God, and the authority of his Christ." <margin>**Revelation 12**</margin> The ungodly and damned, however, will be cast without delay into the abyss of Hell <margin>**Matthew 25**</margin>, where they shall be plagued eternally alongside all the devils. The smoke of their torment will rise from eternity to eternity, and they will feel no rest neither day nor night. <margin>**Revelation 14 & 20**</margin> But as to what the actual state of affairs will be regarding the elect in eternal life and the damned in—

<meta>catchword: the [der]</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the final stages of the Last Judgment, describing Christ's absolute authority to separate humanity and the immediate, irreversible execution of his verdict into eternal salvation or damnation.</summary>

—of Hell. Concerning this, the *Theological Prognostic* <gloss>Theologischen Prognostico</gloss> <note>A "prognostic" in this period was a scholarly or prophetic forecast, often regarding the end of the world or the state of the soul.</note> or Prophecy of the Future State of the New World <gloss>Weissagung vom künfftigen zustandt der Newen Welt</gloss> <note>The "New World" here refers not to the Americas, but to the "World to Come"—the new heaven and new earth after the apocalypse.</note> shall, through **GOD'S** grace, deal with it in detail. Various beautiful, lovely, and powerful questions shall be resolved there, such as: how we will know and name one another in that life; what kind of joy, *Glory* <gloss>Glory</gloss>, and magnificence <gloss>Herzligkeit</gloss> we shall have; and conversely, what great sadness, anguish, and pain the ungodly shall taste and feel in Hell. 

May the gracious, merciful **G O D** and Father now grant us all his blessing and grace, so that we may worthily prepare ourselves for the final arrival of his Son, in order that we may receive him with joy and be placed at his right hand as obedient little sheep <gloss>gehorsahme Schäfflein</gloss>. For the Day of the **L O R D** is near and hastens greatly <margin>**Zephaniah 1**</margin>, and the **LORD** himself says: "I come quickly. Amen. Yes, come, **LORD JESUS**." 

The grace of our **L O R D  J E S U S** Christ be with you all, **A M E N**.

# Flyleaf

->This page is a blank flyleaf or endpaper.<-

-><image-desc>The page features decorative **marbled paper** along the top, right, and bottom edges. Marbling is a traditional bookbinding technique where ink or paint is floated on a liquid surface to create swirling, stone-like patterns before being transferred onto paper. This provides both a decorative finish and a way to mask wear on the edges of the book.</image-desc><-

<note>Flyleaves are the extra pages at the beginning or end of a book that are not part of the main text. They act as a buffer between the printed content and the heavy outer covers. Marbled edges were a common feature of luxury or professional bookbindings from the 17th century onwards, protecting the page edges from dust and providing an elegant appearance when the book was closed.</note>

*Note on the Endpaper:*
The interior of the binding is decorated with "nonpareil" or feathered marbled paper. This traditional bookbinding technique features horizontal, undulating waves of deep burgundy red, rhythmically crossed by fine vertical "combed" strokes. These strokes create repeating, fan-like patterns in alternating lines of white, bright blue, and small accents of yellow.

# Inside Front Cover and Endpaper

->**Decorative Marbled Paper**<-

*Note on the Binding:*
The interior of the binding is decorated with a classic "comb" or "peacock" marbled paper design. This traditional bookbinding technique involves floating pigments on a liquid base and using a comb-like tool to create repetitive, undulating patterns before transferring the design to paper. 

<note>This specific pattern features horizontal wavy bands. The primary color is a deep, rich burgundy red, interspersed with rhythmic arches of blue, gold, and white. The marbled paper is set within a frame of the volume's original brown leather binding, visible at the edges.</note>

***

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page describes the physical exterior of the manuscript's front cover, preceding the interior marbled endpapers.</meta>

# Front Cover of the Volume

->**External Binding**<-

The manuscript is protected by a sturdy **half-leather binding**. The spine and the outer corners of the boards are reinforced with smooth, chocolate-brown leather. <note>A "half-binding" uses leather for the spine and corners—the areas most prone to wear—while using cheaper paper or cloth for the rest of the cover.</note> 

The central panels of the cover are decorated with **marbled paper** featuring a dense, horizontal "combed" or **nonpareil** pattern. <note>The "nonpareil" (meaning "without equal") pattern is created by using a very fine-toothed comb to pull pigments across a liquid base before dipping the paper.</note> This intricate design consists of thousands of tiny, rhythmic waves in shades of deep maroon, cream, muted yellow, and slate blue.

A delicate, **gold-tooled** line <note>Gold tooling involves pressing a heated brass tool into the leather through a thin layer of gold leaf to create a permanent, shimmering decorative border.</note> serves as a crisp boundary where the leather meets the marbled paper, providing a professional and elegant finish to the volume.

<summary>The front cover of the manuscript features a traditional half-leather binding with reinforced brown leather corners and a vibrant, combed marbled paper design in shades of red, cream, and blue.</summary>

<keywords>binding, marbled paper, half-leather, gold tooling, nonpareil pattern, book arts, 18th-century style</keywords>
